# Continuity State

Bootstrap canon is established in `bible/`, `outline/series.md`, and `outline/ending.md`. No chapter prose exists yet.

## Hard canon

- World: Neyra, a late-medieval river-and-road world. Years count from the **Quiet Reckoning**; the story opens in **YR 312, Mudmonth**.
- Protagonist: Sir Aren Kest, thirty-two, a former Lantern Guard captain and current bridge repairer in Merefen. Estranged from Tovan; still idealizes Edric.
- Starting mystery: the Bellweather Sundering, **YR 301, Mudmonth**, erased Bellweather and seven villages. The story opens on the eleventh anniversary, in the same season. The Crown blames Aren’s failed bridge defense.
- The seven villages: Alder Cross, Saltbrim, Wrenlow, Cobbett, Nine Elms, Stonebarrow, Hushwater.
- Deeper answer: Marshal Edric Hunn and the Lantern Council ordered a forced cull to contain the Unwritten Wound; the order sacrificed a living city.
- Gatehouse rule: a living path cannot be reset or forcibly closed while conscious life exists inside it. Closing a route removes Aren’s access, not the world.
- **Local custody rule (added in bootstrap):** roads are opened by named local custodians, not by Aren. Two consenting custodians with paired anchors can open a road without him, and path communities can open roads to each other without the main world. Aren is a translator and temporary common anchor, never a necessary mechanism. The ending must show roads working without him.
- Path capacity: one at Stage 0/First Stitch, then two, three, five, seven, and ten at later stages. The final Unowned Road stage removes exclusive personal access.
- Path costs: roadmarks, memory loss, path bleed, lost time, failed workings, social obligations, and permanent local consequences. No path is a disposable map.
- Crossing budget: about three attempted crossings per season at any stage; **three days of fever after a return, at Stage 0 and at Stage 1 alike** — the first crossing in Volume 01 pays the same three days — growing to a month at Stage 6. A return stitch is consumed by use.
- Final enemy: Edric Hunn, using the established First Witness protocol to activate the One Road. No new final enemy is planned.

## Canon clarifications recorded in this phase

1. **Name change.** The Culling Order field warden was renamed **Sera Dain → Sabin Dain** to remove a collision with Sera Quen. Use Sabin Dain in all prose and state.
2. **Name change.** The Lantern Council chancellor was renamed **Veyra Senn → Iselde Senn** to remove a collision with Mara Vey.
3. **Cast discipline.** At most six new named characters per batch. Every recurring character needs one want unrelated to Aren. No supporting character is killed for shock.
4. **The bell has provenance.** Crown salvage, sold at a Merefen clearance for two pence, bought by Aren as scrap bronze in the opening Mudmonth. It is a damaged anchor, not a key or a summons.
5. **Mara’s archive is the main-world Bellweather Claims Archive** in Merefen’s salt-loft rooms. It is not the living archive inside Bellweather-Living, which is run by Hollis Dree and refuses to surrender copies.
6. **Relationship policy is decided:** one slow-burn relationship, Aren and Mara Vey. No harem.
7. **Aren’s age arithmetic.** Aren is 32 at the opening and was **21** at the Sundering (born YR 280, apprenticed at twelve, Guard at seventeen, commissioned by brevet at twenty-one). This is deliberate: he was the youngest captain in the Guard, which is why Edric chose him, why the Crown can blame a boy, and why Aren believed his own defence for three years. Do not rewrite the Sundering as a child being made to do it alone. He held a real command and really failed it.
8. **The Rusk families are separated.** Jos Rusk, the Hushwater ferryman, survives and lives in Merefen; his claim is the first one Mara Vey personally refutes, and he is wrong about the cause. His wife **Anwen Rusk** (37, who kept the Hushwater ferry’s crossing book) and daughter **Bryn Rusk** (11) are among the erased and are now named outright instead of being left as “the wife and daughter.” The recurring Merefen ferryman formerly called Pei Rusk is **Pei Tarrow** (52, Hushwater survivor’s son, freight boats on the Old Toll channel). He is no relation to Jos Rusk. Do not stage the two men together, and do not let a bystander call Pei a Rusk.
9. **Hanna Vey and Mara Vey are not related.** The shared surname is deliberate common naming from the Sable river country, not a plot relation, not a mistake, and not a reason to rename either of them later. Let Mara notice the coincidence once, early and coldly, and never return to it. No scene may imply kinship.
10. **Toll figures are locked.** Bellweather city: about **four thousand**. The seven villages: about **seven hundred and twenty** (Alder Cross 200, Saltbrim 90, Wrenlow 60, Cobbett 140, Nine Elms 110, Stonebarrow 40, Hushwater 80). The Crown quotes the city figure alone and folds the villages into it; the Archive keeps the two numbers apart on every page and never prints a combined total. In a book whose moral weight rests on exact arithmetic, a character arguing about the dead must use the number actually being argued about.
11. **Dispatch reality, for the record.** The runner selects the first `workspace/**/PROMPT.md` in sorted order that has no `.done` or `.blocked` marker. `state/phase-ledger.json` and `PHASE_SYSTEM.md` are controller-owned, are never written by the writer, and the ledger is not what dispatch reads — so the only protection against a repeated batch is the prompt files themselves. Consequence: **exactly one prompt may exist per batch.** The duplicate `workspace/phase-002-batch-plan/` prompt, which also asked for Chapters 1–10, was deleted during the phase-000 fix pass. `workspace/volume-01/batch-0001/PROMPT.md` is the single prompt for the first batch and pairs with `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0001.md`. The `.wip-conflict` marker in `workspace/phase-000-bootstrap/` only gates WIP-branch resume and is cleared by the runner on completion; leave it alone.
12. **The named dead are records, not cast.** Ferris Oat, Hanna Vey, Corin Slate, Bess Alder, Anwen Rusk, and Bryn Rusk are read aloud, quoted, filed, and argued over; they do not act on the page and do not count against the six-new-named-characters-per-batch limit. That limit applies to characters who speak, decide, or obstruct. A survivor or claimant who acts is a character and does count: Jos Rusk and Pei Tarrow are both cast, not records.

## Canon clarifications recorded in the Volume 01 outline phase

13. **The Sundering's date is the nineteenth of Mudmonth.** The story opens on the sixth of Mudmonth, YR 312, so the eleventh anniversary falls thirteen days later, and the storm comes up on the night of the eighteenth into the nineteenth. Volume 01 opens on the sixth of Mudmonth and closes in the last week of Fallowmonth, about five months of main-world time, matching the series time ledger.
14. **Aren's aunt is dead.** She raised him after Ilyra's crossing and died of the low-quarter fever three winters before the opening, so Aren has no household, rents one room above a cooper on Weir Street, and nobody at home has to be told where he is. The person who holds his tools and covers for him is Tovan.
15. **The bell's economics and dates are fixed.** Aren buys the bell on the first of Mudmonth, YR 312, at the Crown clearance for two pence, **five days** before Chapter 1 opens on the sixth. Chapter 2 is the **eleventh**, the day the Merefen bridge guild's removal notice reaches him, so the notice is dated the day it arrives — eight days before the anniversary, and before any storm. The guild's standing valuation is **six marks** as scrap bronze, because the crack is worth the difference and the roll is right. In Chapter 2 Aren cannot raise six marks and signs a guild bond for the sum with his tool-chest as surety. He refuses Tovan's honest six-mark offer and takes the bond instead, on purpose: the metal buys nothing, a bell in a yard belongs to somebody else, and a bond puts the bell in his name in front of the guild. He has chosen to be the man answerable for it, and he never says so to anyone. The hold he signs in Chapter 1 is good until the guild's next sitting, **the twenty-fourth** — and nothing happens on the twenty-fourth, because a bench cannot act against a man who is not in front of it and Aren is nine days gone. That is the town's own process for a cracked bell, and the watch's sealed order replaces it three days later, on the twenty-seventh, without anybody in Merefen getting to choose between them. The bond lapses when the watch takes the bell, which is a smaller humiliation than the market days the closed road costs.
16. **Return stitches cannot be improvised for free.** Aren rings the bell without having prepared a return stitch, which is the mistake that shapes the batch: he is stranded for six path days, loses **nine** days of the main-world calendar against a Gatehouse warning of three, and returns on the twenty-seventh to a bell already seized. A working time-diverge warning in this series is wrong by days or weeks, never silently by months, and the drift is stated in the prose as a warning that was wrong.
17. **The path's own answer comes from two institutions.** The Bellweather Bridge Guild kept the **counterfoil** — the stub proving a severance writ was sent to Bellweather-Living on the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301, refused on the deck of the arch and withdrawn. The **outgoing writ register** naming the signatory is kept by the toll houses, who are the guild's political opponents. The volume midpoint reveal lives in the toll-house counting room, not the guild hall.
18. **Path-side vocabulary is the volume's argument.** Bellweather-Living maintains its crossing through **stewards**; a steward maintains. The corrupted Crown term is **private keeper**, and a provincial document applies it to Aren during Batch 0001. The legitimate word *custodian* is not used in Volume 01. The word "keeper" fitting Aren is a Volume 01–03 hook and is never explained in Volume 01.
19. **Batch 0001's six names are fixed:** Tovan Kest, Mara Vey, Wenna Koll, Lysa Fenn, Captain Rell, Sera Quen — all six already established in `bible/characters.md` with a want, a flaw, and a function, and named in prose for the first time in this batch. Jos Rusk, Bram Ottery, Halven, Pei Tarrow, Sarn Oyelaran, and Ilvane Quarren are Batch 0002. Bram Ottery therefore **does not carry out the bell seizure in Chapters 8–10**: the seizure is Rell's watch, and its sergeant is addressed by office and left unnamed so the six-name cap is not spent on a man who performs one errand. Bram enters by name in Volume 02, when somebody has to answer in writing. Rell's unlawful seizure remains the fact recorded against him. A full batch-by-batch cast plan is in `outline/volume-01.md`.
20. **Batch 0001 uses two POVs plus one.** Aren in eight chapters, Mara in Chapter 3, Tovan in Chapter 9. The path-side institutions in the batch are addressed by office — the bench, the bench-clerk, the hull-master, the toll-house steward — so the six-name cap is not spent on people who only exist to be quoted.
21. **Two Gatehouse lines in the whole first batch,** one at the ringing and one at the return, both plain and both technically true in a way that conceals the moral cost.
22. **Planted in Batch 0001, owed later.** The bell bought for two pence (Chapter 1); the Merefen bridge guild's removal notice **dated the eleventh, the day it arrives** — before the storm and before any crossing (Chapter 2), which pays off when the watch's sealed seizure order turns out to be dated the same week (Chapter 8); Ferris Oat's brine weights, **held and cited but not filed** — the extract Mara trades for his promise (Chapter 3) — with the formal entry of the named roll of the dead reserved for **Chapter 892**, where it is the first document the Charter names; the bell ringing in the anniversary storm (Chapter 4); the empty iron cradle left on the near abutment after the seizure (Chapter 8), which is **not** still empty in Chapter 50 — the town reclaims its own iron during the Block 5 resolution, bands the crack, and the bell returns to the bridge without ever returning to Aren; Aren's deferred field notes and his three years of repeating the official fever account, disclosed in his own hand in Chapter 10, together with the two omissions he files alongside them. These are the Volume 01 setups named in `outline/ending.md`, and the writer must not add a second version of any of them. In particular, the brine weights are a cited authority in Volume 01 and a Chapter 892 payoff; filing them as an exhibit in Chapters 10 or 48 spends the ending's beat early and must not happen.
23. **Volume 01's cast discipline on the Culling Order.** No named member appears in Volume 01. Sabin Dain enters in Volume 2. The phrase "Culling Order" may appear at most once in Volume 01, in a document or a claimant's mouth, with no member attached to it.
24. **Hushwater Reach exists in the path.** Bellweather-Living has a living hamlet on a channel that is drowned in the main world, and its ferry cooperative keeps a crossing book in a living hand with different names in it. The cooperative counts **ferry hands and hearths, not souls**, so nobody can put a number on the hamlet; the best estimate in play is **near sixty**. That is deliberately far from the **eighty** erased at Hushwater in the locked toll figures, because a living hamlet of exactly eighty beside an erased village of exactly eighty is a signpost and this volume is only allowed a hint. No character notices the gap, and no one explains the coincidence in Volume 01. The thread stays held until Volume 09.
25. **The first crossing's calendar is locked, and so is the fever.** Crossing one opens on the night of the eighteenth into the nineteenth of Mudmonth and returns on the **twenty-seventh**; path time inside it is six days. The fever takes **three days — the twenty-seventh, twenty-eighth and twenty-ninth — and is over on the thirtieth**, which is the day Chapter 10 opens. The thirtieth is not a fourth day of illness and must not be called one; write it as the day the fever breaks. Three days is the figure in the crossing budget above and in `outline/volume-01.md`; `outline/ending.md` states no fever length and is not a source for it. The cost does not grow until Stage 6.
26. **The batch's day map is the only calendar the first ten chapters use.** Ch 1 the sixth · Ch 2 the eleventh · Ch 3 the twelfth · Ch 4 the night of the eighteenth into the nineteenth · Ch 5–7 path time with no main-world clock · Ch 8 the twenty-seventh · Ch 9 the twenty-seventh to the thirtieth · Ch 10 the thirtieth. The thirteenth to the eighteenth is a stated cut: no visitor, no document, no arrest, no further bell sound. **The bell stays on its cradle across that cut because of the twenty-fourth hold in item 15, not because a date was missed.** Volume 01's own time budget in `outline/volume-01.md` now lists the same days.
27. **Two guilds, two names, no crossing between them.** "The guild" in the main world is the **Merefen bridge guild**: a town's craft body of bridgewrights and road-wardens holding the Old Toll crossing in municipal trust, keeping the scrap roll the bell's six-mark valuation comes from. The path's institution is always the **Bellweather Bridge Guild**. They never appear in each other's world in Volume 01, and a chapter must not let "the guild" be ambiguous about which one it means.
28. **The promise Aren makes to Mara is undated.** She asks for a promise that the bell will not be rung, and refuses to put a date on it, so Chapter 4 is a broken promise rather than a technicality. Do not restore the earlier wording, which limited the promise to "before the renewal." The batch owns **two omissions, holding three facts**: to Mara, the promise with no date on it and the night he has already chosen; to Tovan, the reason he refused six marks and signed a bond. Both omissions appear in the written account he files in Chapter 10, which is why the second is what makes the first credible.
29. **The Volume 01 drift ceiling.** The first crossing's warning is wrong by six days and the third by three weeks. `outline/series.md` now allows three days to three weeks of uncertainty at return for Volume 01, and three weeks is the ceiling at this scale. Do not drift further in this volume, and do not drift by months.
30. **Chapter files, batch summaries, and review records have fixed homes.** Chapters go to `chapters/volume-01/chapter-0001.md`, four digits, matching the card labels `### Chapter 0001` in `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0001.md` (`PHASE_SYSTEM.md` is the authority and is controller-owned; this entry records the convention where the writer will actually see it). A completed batch's summary is appended to `state/batch-summaries.md`, and the one-line "Last batch summary" field in `state/current.md` points at the most recent entry. Reviewer findings are persisted under `reviews/`, not left in `logs/`, which is gitignored.
31. **Dispatch guards, standing version of item 11.** Do not create a second prompt for a batch that already has one; if a phase's work tempts a new prompt directory, it is a new batch and not a new copy of this one. And **`state/phase-ledger.json` is controller-owned, is never written by the writer, is stale by design, and is not what dispatch reads** — the prompts themselves are the dispatch list, and `AGENTS.md` says to update the ledger while `.opencode/agent/novel-writer.md` forbids it. The runner's own restore list does not include the ledger, so nothing will undo a careless edit; the only protection is the agent rule and the fact that no one reads the field. Leave it alone: a writer who "fixes" it is the one most likely to break the one-prompt-per-batch protection.

## Canon established by Batch 0001 (Chapters 1–10, prose on the page)

32. **The province's renewal is dated for the second of Greenmonth, not the anniversary.** The nineteenth of Mudmonth is a Sunday and the Bellweather Claims Archive is shut on it with the blinds down. A claim entered before the second of Greenmonth is a claim of the old year; anything after it is entered under a finding that nothing survives and is weaker for a year. The nineteenth is human grief and the second is a clerk in the provincial town copying a finding out of the same book he copied out in 301. **This is the volume's administrative clock and Block 2 runs on it.** The exhibit is dated the thirtieth of Mudmonth, three days ahead of it.
33. **The "three days" is how the warning reaches a roadwarden, and the saying is the same rule.** The Gatehouse's warning at the opening is three days; Volume 01's two-line voice budget is spent on the ringing and the return, so what Aren actually receives is the road crews' saying — *three days to open an old road, three days to come back through it, and a man who has not three days to give should not begin.* **The warning, the saying, the nine days and the six-day drift are all unchanged.** Open question 5 ("what did the warning mean by three, and how is it nine?") stays live and is answered in the middle volumes: nine is six path days plus the three the rule allowed for the crossing itself, and the drift is the six.
34. **Aren crossed in the clothes he left in, and the field notes rode in the breast pocket of that same coat.** They came back with him and they are the physical reason Ch 10's account can be specific about eleven years of silence. He has never handed them over and has never lost them. Do not move them to a chest, a box, or a room.
35. **The bar went through with him and came back.** It is the four-foot iron bar from the Old Toll cradle operation; it is his tool and it is the only object he carried into Bellweather-Living. It lies on the Old Toll setts where he set it down in the fourth second of the return.
36. **The Bellweather fever was real inside Bellweather-Living.** Two hundred and eleven dead of the low quarter in Mudmonth, YR 310, kept in a local ledger. **The number is given to Aren by the ferryman on the north bank**, who also names the woman on Ferry Lane who keeps her shutters shut and the man at the tanner's who has not been out in daylight. **The woman on the gallery is a different person** — she is the one who asks *How would you know?* and who says nobody there has ever wanted to see a stranger's hand. Ch 5 has three separate women in it and Chapter 9 once gave the number to the wrong one; keep the ferryman as the source of two hundred and eleven and keep the Ferry Lane woman distinct from the gallery woman. **The number is stated a second time in Batch 0003, in Ch 27, by Hollis Dree out of the city's own ledger in a public hall; that is a second source and not a substitution, the ferryman remains the first, and nobody else in Bellweather-Living may give it.** The Crown's fever account is therefore a **true thing carrying a false one**, and this is the moral complication the case is built on. It is not the Unwritten Wound, nobody in the path uses any word like it, and the path's ledger and the Crown's four thousand are two different documents about two different events.
37. **The third bell is the crossing-closed bell, and it is the hinge — and it is one bell, not two.** Rung from the crown at night, once and long, to close a crossing. It cracked on the third stroke on the night of the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301, when the Bellweather Bridge Guild refused the severance writ on the deck — so the refusal and the damage are the same event, and the cracked bell is the anchor and the hinge at once. The path's bell stands in the founder's yard on two oak trundles under a tarred cloth, unrecast and unringed for eleven years. **The main world's bell is that same bell:** it was lifted out of the river at Merefen and entered on a Crown clearance roll in **YR 302, the year after the writ**, as *unclassified salvage*, with no city and no river written on it; it was sold at that clearance for two pence on the first of Mudmonth, YR 312; and it stood on the Old Toll cradle until the watch took it. **Chapter 7 adjudicates this on the page**, in Aren's own flat sentence to the bench: nine stone, split lip to shoulder, the founder's mark on the shoulder is theirs and he read it in a Merefen scrap yard before he ever came up the north steps, and it is in two places, and he rang it in one of them, and he cannot make the two agree. **This is settled fact and is not an open mystery, a time-diverge, a ghost, or a twin.** The bell is an anchor, and an anchor may be in more than one place at once; the Crown's blank clearance entry is the proof that the north has had it and does not know where from. Do not stage a second bell, a copy, a replica, or a mistaken identity, and do not let any character explain it inside Volume 01. Nobody in Volume 01 uses the word *hinge* about the bell except the Crown paper.
38. **The path's immediate problem is on the page and is not Aren's to solve.** An eight-inch crack in the **outer ring of the second pier**, river side, that opens in the cold and shuts in the warmth and is not growing. The ring stone is sound; the mortar behind it was raked out from inside by water coming up under the arch. The honest repair is a new cutwater: nine months of the bench's whole capacity against a debt pledged to the ninth of a grandson. The bench has ordered the pier marked twice a year by two men and a clerk. This is the seed of Block 5's failed public oath and must not be resolved before Chapter 41.
39. **The Old Toll sill is spent, and anchor-glass is the gate.** The packing was lifted and set down out of line, and there is a pale, dry, warm cross the size of two hands in the stone where the working struck it from the other side. Two Steppe discs, cut and annealed to bridge pins, **four marks the pair, ready in a fortnight, money before cutting.** No return stitch can be prepared, and no second crossing can be attempted, until that glass is paid for, cut, and set. This is the batch's forward resource and it is a money problem before it is a magic problem.
40. **The town ledger comes to eleven marks and four pence**, itemised in Ch 10 in six lines, of which eleven days' crew pay fell to Tovan and not to the trust. Tovan's own tally in Ch 9 (oak, lime, stone and cartage, nails) is the material line of the same column and the two must continue to agree. It is the smallest number in the book and it is never to be set beside the four thousand.
41. **Mara's roll of the dead is still unbuilt, and its rule is now on the page in her own head:** a roll built out of Ferris Oat's ledger must be entered as a ledger, in its own order, with the brine weights standing beside the names, and never as a speech. A speech can be answered; a ledger can only be checked. **Do not spend this in Volume 01.** The brine weights are held and cited in Batch 0001; the roll is the Chapter 892 payoff.
42. **What the characters do and do not know at the end of Chapter 10.** Aren knows: the city is standing, in debt, in a morning of its own; a writ was refused on the deck; a real fever was used to carry a false one; he was twenty-one and given a partial order and did not reach the inner city; he has been part of the lie for eleven years by saying nothing; a Crown document calls him a keeper and nobody can find the office; and the man who can be traded for glass is named in a slip nailed in a hut on the Old Toll road. He does **not** know what a route signature is, why he can cross, what the First Witness is, what the private-keeper clause is, that Ilyra exists, or that the bell is anything but a damaged anchor. Mara knows the crossing is real, the exhibit is dated, and he chose a night before he signed. Lysa knows the seal is the same on two papers and the office does not exist, and has refused in front of Mara to discuss the eleven-year-old salvage filing she took from him. Rell believes the fever and has written down what he does not believe.
43. **Three dates inside Block 0001's first week are fixed by the prose and must not drift.**
   - **The seizure: first light on the nineteenth of Mudmonth**, the morning after the storm, four men and a cart, by Rell's watch on his own authority. The cradle has therefore been empty **eight** days when he returns on the twenty-seventh.
   - **The sealed order: dated the sixteenth, delivered by courier on the evening of the eighteenth.** Rell has held it nine days on the twenty-seventh, and it predates the storm and the anniversary.
   - **The guild's removal notice: the eleventh**, dated the morning it reaches him, and Tovan witnessed the six-mark bond that day.
   Do not restate any of these as the fifteenth, the seventeenth, the twenty-first, or the twenty-second. **The figure Lysa Fenn puts on the seizure is three days** — from the sixteenth, the day written on the paper, to the morning of the nineteenth — and that is the damning number, because it means the whole thing was done in three days off a document that predated the storm. **Do not let it become eight (the empty cradle on the twenty-seventh) or nine (Aren's absence).** Two state files carried the wrong figure once; it is now three everywhere.
44. **The eleven-year-old salvage filing is now on the page.** In Chapter 10 Lysa Fenn tells Aren, in front of Mara and before she is asked, that she took a filing from him in YR 301, that it is a **different document from the bell**, that two documents about the same man are not the same document, and that she will not discuss its contents in this room. She says it in advance so that he is not surprised when she refuses. **The contents remain unstated and are a late-volume discovery.** Do not have any other character refer to it, and do not let it be confused with the bell's clearance entry of the first of Mudmonth, YR 312.
45. **Aren's and Tovan's ages fix the estrangement's arithmetic.** Aren is thirty-two and left for the Guard at seventeen; Tovan is twenty-five and was **ten** at the time. Tovan's account of the silence is therefore: he wrote at eleven, wrote again at twelve, and got nothing for two years, and did the cooper's roof himself over three summers from fourteen. The batch card for Chapter 9 said "seventeen"; the ages in `bible/characters.md` are load-bearing in two files and win. The emotional content of the beat is unchanged.
46. **Path-side "the death bell"** is Bellweather's bell for a death, rung at the fourth hour and the ninth. It is not a fever bell, and the main world has no such institution: in Merefen the ferrymen ring for the drowned, on the cracked bell, and nobody has rung it since the seventeenth of Mudmonth. Keep the two customs distinct.
47. **The gauge board at Sedge Reach read seven and a quarter on the sixth of Mudmonth** when the ferryman said eleven fingers, and that is the figure Tovan quotes in Chapter 9 about the argument he lost. Bellweather's own board reads nine and a quarter in Chapter 5. Different rivers, different weeks, different men, and the same quarrel — which is the point of the chapter and the reason the reader should recognise it.
48. **The four marks are Tovan's.** Tovan has them, and says so twice in Ch 9. Aren's position at the end of Ch 10 is a shilling a day, eleven days of unpaid wages, a bond for six against a chest worth three and a half, and about a mark and a quarter in hand. The anchor-glass slip wants four marks the pair, money before cutting. **A next-phase card that opens with Aren holding the four marks is wrong** and has already caused one such error; if a batch needs four marks, it has to say whose they are and how they are going to be got. The Batch 0002 Ch 11 and Ch 20 cards have been corrected.
49. **Two figures the batch never prints together.** Four thousand is the Crown's figure for the city and stays the Crown's figure. Seven hundred and twenty is the villages, named by name, counted by name. The Archive keeps the city and the villages on separate pages and Mara states the rule out loud in Chapter 10. **Eleven marks and four pence is the town's bill for nine days** and is never set beside either number. No chapter in Volume 01 may add them up.
50. **Audit note for the next writer.** Batch 0001's chapters and state were passed through a correction pass at the end of the phase: the Ch 7 bell-identity and YR 302 date error, the Ch 9 attribution of two hundred and eleven, the Ch 10 seizure figure and a duplicated block, the surety choice in Ch 2, and the date arithmetic, word count, and four-marks ownership in the state files and the Batch 0002 prompt. **No plot moved, no chapter was renumbered, and the volume's planned beats are unchanged.** Item 37 now states the bell's identity as settled fact rather than leaving it to be inferred.



## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 10

- **Aren and Mara:** a contract, not trust. He has signed an undated promise, broken it, filed the only thing he brought back with no condition and no copy, and put his own eleven-year silence into her file. She has decided he is a better bridge-builder than she is a keeper and that both are the same thing, has told him in advance that she will be less careful with him than he would like, and has refused to let his grief and the town's eleven marks and four pence share a page. She has *not* been told what a crossing costs him, and the account he filed is about his past, not his body.
- **Aren and Tovan:** the batch's refusal is stated and unanswered. Tovan has named the two years of silence and got no reply; he has refused the road, the secret, and the span. He witnessed the bond without being asked. He also saw the clean rectangle in the moss and has told almost nobody. He is the recurring face of the argument that Aren is doing to survivors as well as for them.
- **Aren and Edric:** unexamined devotion, now souring from a distance. No letter, no visit, no appearance in Chapters 1–10 — but the seizure is his office's paper and the *keeper* title is his mechanism, and Aren has said out loud that "being good is how they get you" in his brother's hearing without yet applying it upward. Edric is not named in Chapters 1–10 except inside a seal.
- **Aren and Bellweather-Living:** hostile contact with a bench that will answer a question only for a promise to return, and a ferrywright who has decided he is a cost, not a rescue. He carries their refusal in an unsigned counterfoil and a debt, and he has volunteered for their work twice.
- **Aren and Rell:** a professional's worst case. Rell has written down what he does not believe, has said so in front of witnesses, and is right that the office is real and the office is not findable. This is a live relationship, not an obstacle, and it must not become an ally before the Volume 11 testimony.
- **Mara and Lysa:** a working alliance with a six-week grudge already predicted out loud. They agree on the exhibit and disagree about sequencing, and Lysa has the superior craft for this county.


## Ending constraints

The ending must preserve Bellweather-Living as a living sovereign community, contain but not magically cure the Unwritten Wound, ratify the Charter of Many Roads, keep Aren and Mara’s relationship voluntary and independent, and leave Edric alive for public judgment.

## Knowledge control

`outline/series.md` contains a per-volume table of who knows what. Do not reveal a fact to any character before the volume listed there. This is the primary tool for protecting the central mystery across 900 chapters.

## Canon established by Batch 0002 (Chapters 11–20, prose on the page)

51. **The currency is now closed and is one mark to four shillings** (48 pence), so a mark divides into four exact quarters of a shilling, and every figure in the batch depends on four and not five. **Ch 17 is the reason and the prose does not spell it out:** the factor puts four marks in the yard chest on the sixth and Ch 17 says only that by Thursday morning there would be *nothing in the chest except the other three and a quarter* — the quarter-mark that went on the crew's wages is implied, not stated, so a later writer must not quote a three-quarter mark as though the page said so. Ch 19 gives the glass price as **a hundred and ninety-two pence, twenty-four days at eight pence a day**. Ch 20 makes the glass four marks, of which **Aren pays a mark and a quarter of his own and takes two marks and three quarters** as a wage advance. Do not write a mark as five shillings, as eight shillings, as a hundred and twenty pence, or as ninety-six pence anywhere below this item. **`bible/terminology.md` said 8 shillings / 96 pence and was wrong; it has been corrected to 4 shillings / 48 pence to match the prose.**
52. **The batch's sixth-name cap is spent and closed:** Jos Rusk, Bram Ottery, Halven, Pei Tarrow, Sarn Oyelaran, Ilvane Quarren — all named in prose in this batch, all pre-existing in `bible/characters.md`, each with a want stated in his or her own voice. **Path-side institutions, the guild clerk, the magistrate's clerk, the bench, the toll-house steward, the factor, the boy on the deck, and the eel-man of the wharf road all stay addressed by office.** Do not name a seventh person in Block 3; Block 3's names are Hollis Dree, Alis Merrow, Captain Ruen, Reeve Aldis Fenn, plus two unfilled.
53. **Bram Ottery answered in writing and was punished for it, and is never rewarded for it.** His statement was written at his own table on the night of the second of Greenmonth, brought up and given to the clerk at the seventh hour on the fourth, which is why he says he has had a bad two days about it, entered in a magistrate's record, and **Rell relieved him of the sergeant's post as of that hour, on the ground that a statement made without instruction is not a watch record, and stopped his pay as a man at all until it is disposed of.** Rell will not get him nine and three months of accrual out of the watch fund and says so in a corridor. Lysa Fenn said in a public room that there is **no promotion in this and there will not be one.** Ottery lives in Merefen, is unemployed, and does not appear again in this volume.
54. **The statement's content, which is the chapter's landing:** the order was executed on a paper and not on a belief; the crew did not discuss the fever; the bed of the stone was swept afterwards by a boy with a broom; and from the seventeenth of Mudmonth a man in Merefen had been telling the market that the bell carries the fever and that removing it was right. Ottery heard him at the watch-house door on the morning of the twentieth, the morning after the bell went into the storehouse, and did not write any of it down until the night of the second of Greenmonth, twelve days later, and by then he had a reason to. **That man is the eel-man of the wharf road, fifty-eight, a market pitch, called by his trade, standing in the stair during the hearing, and he said it again when the room turned.** The court cannot un-sell it. His own arithmetic, on the page: *if the bell's the fever and the crown's taken it, then the crown's right and there'll be no fever; if I'm wrong, then somebody's here who's paid for the taking of it.* He is not a villain and is not arrested and is not in the file by name.
55. **Lysa Fenn's finding of the fourth of Greenmonth is a public document with her name at the bottom of it, and it is the batch's durable asset.** Five clauses: the instrument is sealed by the marshal's emergency office, that office has produced nothing, and this court cannot compel it; the court has searched the town charter, the county's list, and the Crown's schedule of provincial offices and found no office of keeper of the Bellweather hinge, no office of road-keeper of Merefen, and no commission; the emergency powers were renewed on the second for one year, on the same terms, **to officers not named in the finding or any instrument**, and the seizure was executed by this county's watch; the object remains in this county's custody, under seal, lawfully; and — the mechanism — any office thereafter acting in the matter is on notice that this court asked and was not answered, **and that the asking is dated.** A private minute at the foot says the finding is not a victory. **What it is worth: nothing until somebody in the provincial town finds it, and he will find it by accident and will decide what it is.**
56. **The hearing's other findings, for anyone who needs them:** the bell was nine stone; it stood on an iron cradle at the Old Toll abutment, the cradle continuous with the stone; **in the week before the storm** the road warden repacked three lengths of the sill beneath it, the same repacking Ch 2 and Ch 11 and Ch 18 give as the autumn work and the work of the week before the storm — **there is no dated repacking and the thirteenth is not on any page**; at the sixth hour on the nineteenth four men of the watch lifted it with a bar through the headstock loop and a cart and swept the bed of the stone, leaving a clean rectangle in the moss; the split is **outward from the sound bow**; the guild's notice of the eleventh had already been issued about it; and no person produced the authority at any time before the hearing. The guild's application to have the bell returned is refused, and **the trust of the Old Toll crossing is not restored**, because a municipal trust cannot be affirmed over a lawful Crown holding on the same stone. The bench of the **Merefen bridge guild** sits on the ninth of Greenmonth and will vote on the bond.
57. **The crown clerk on the fourth was a county clerk and a man worth keeping in mind.** He read the finding without stopping, told the room that a finding is a statement of what a court will entertain and not of what happened, named the memo that is about to be written about Aren and Mara for naming the marshal's office in a room of eleven on the morning a finding was read, and gave Aren the batch's real enemy for free: **the office that holds the county file is the office that will be asked what the file is for.** He is addressed by office and is not named.
58. **The bench mark is a quarter of an inch low and is now in the Archive's book.** The Sedge Reach datum, re-cut by hand in Mudmonth and cut by the hand, not the instrument; Aren took two readings off it on the first of Greenmonth, wrote the figure, and made a mark on the lid of the guild's chest. The guild's man told him a road warden does not put papers in against the bench's own mark. **On the eighth of Greenmonth the keeper of the Archive had the figure copied onto the back of the face-stone cutting contract and witnessed by Tovan Kest as a carpenter**, with the wording *copied from this contract at the second hour of the eighth, T. Kest, carpenter* — because a man in a yard has three offers a month to put his hand on another body's account and this one he refused. A town that re-cuts its own datum by hand in a wet month is a town whose figures cannot be checked, and Mara says in Chapter 19 that it is the same disease as a Crown roll bound out of order.
59. **The Crown's YR 302 clearance entry is out of place in a bound order, in a hand the same age as its neighbours, and is filed unargued.** It sits in the salvage and clearance volume between two entries of YR 304. It says one bell, nine stone by the founder's weight, *unclassified salvage*, a number, an hour, and **no city and no river**. Sarn Oyelaran first called it out of place; Mara took three days to decide she was entitled to *altered*; **she did not publish it and did not put it in front of the magistrate**, on Halven's reasoning — a page shown to a court becomes a claim that a Crown roll has been written to, and the registrar who gave her an honest sentence at no charge is not to be spent to make her own sentence heavier. **Sarn wrote her own attestation, in her own hand as a copyist, with the date, the hour, and the words *bound out of order, in a hand not of this roll*, and no opinion.** It is in a wrapper in Mara's press. **Nobody in Volume 01 explains the entry and no character may.** Do not confuse it with Lysa's eleven-year-old salvage filing (item 44) or with the Merefen clearance sale of the first of Mudmonth, YR 312, at two pence.
60. **Mara's second wall rule, added on the second of Greenmonth: *No combined figure is entered in this office.*** It sits under the original rule (an anchor is recorded on the word of a sworn tradesman, in his own hand, and not on a clerk's word, and not on a witness's word, and not on the word of a man who has an interest). **The rule is now stated out loud, in a room, in front of a court officer, in Chapter 19, where she **refuses** it — *you may not add them, and I am not going to let you add them* — and the same refusal is said a second time in a yard in Chapter 20, where the Crown's four thousand is named in one paragraph and **the seven figures are not recited until the next one, after a beat, and are explicitly called a recitation and not an answer**:** a total is one number and can be checked once and disproved once and nobody has to look at the reason; a name can only be looked for. The magistrate's clerk served the finding of the fourth on **the seven villages by name, in the county's spelling, with each figure separately, `sum not stated` in the total line, and a space left where the eighth figure would go** — and entered `no reason given` on the guild roll in Chapter 20. **The combined figure has never been printed on any page in this series and must not be.** Four thousand is the Crown's and stays the Crown's; the villages are seven hundred and twenty by name.
61. **A registrar's fee is a charge for a search and Halven's position is exact.** The four shillings Mara paid on the second bought a **dated request**, not the ledger. The closed press cannot be opened without a magistrate's order, and Lysa Fenn's order is stamped and **returnable on the fifteenth**, which is eleven days from the fourth. The *Anchors, schedules and dispositions, Alder Vale* book — the only book in that registry that names objects, including a schedule for three bells on the Alder Vale crossing — **is entered on the roll as being in the fourth press and is not on the shelf**, and the gap is about two inches and a half and was not entered anywhere by Halven's own hand. Halven disclosed this in person, unasked, and asked for it to be written down word for word. He is a keeper of a public register and therefore has **no jurisdiction**: he cannot decide what a register means, so he cannot refuse a fee without a magistrate, so he cannot be got at except through a magistrate. He is not a villain and he is not bought in this volume. **Do not stage him as a Rusk, or beside one.**
62. **The anchor-glass is bought, cut, set, and paid for, and the money's provenance is now on the page.** The slip's terms are *two discs, Steppe glass, cut and annealed to a bridge pin. Four marks the pair, ready in a fortnight. Money before cutting.* **Quarren's rack held a pair that came down the Steppe road in the winter at the count price, a year annealed before he ever paid for them, standing since the first of Frostmonth, never sold.** A pair that has had its year needs no year: the slip's fortnight is a price list assuming a man comes in today, and the year begins in the hole, not on the wheel. He sold the rack pair, which emptied his shelf, and there is no fresh Steppe glass north of Merefen before Longlight. **Two discs, dressed by him, set edge to edge across the two lines of the cross, a pin through both into a seat cut by Aren himself in a single block of the fourth course, a bed of lime and oak dust, a collar of oak, and the maker's mark under the near disc.** **Quarren's page** — *setting of anchor-glass, seat in cut stone, and how it will fail* — is out of his own account book, signed, dated, timed, and given away, and he said it was the first customer in thirty years to ask how it fails instead of how it holds. **A scar is a place a thing came through and can come through again: two is easy, three is not a number a glasswright will sell.** Nine shillings is being quoted in Merefen off Quarren's own board and is **more than half his price**, not a third of it: four marks is sixteen shillings, so the lie is over the half. Rusk repeats the slip's price wrongly in Ch 12 and Ch 20 has the man who wrote the slip call it out.
63. **The money is a wage advance against a contract, not a loan and not a bond, and Tovan's terms are fixed.** Four marks out of the yard chest against the month of face-stone cutting; cleared at eight pence a day off the yard rate; taken at the first Friday after delivery and not before; **carried on and not forgiven if the cutting fails**; entered in the yard's book on the eighth with the date, the hour and the contract number, and on the guild roll as a debt and not a surety, with the factor's name as third party. **Aren put a mark and a quarter of his own on the bench, entered as paid and not as credited, so the advance is two marks and three quarters and it is in his brother's book as a wage and not a favour.** Tovan stated in the room that Aren had not asked, so that the town story afterwards would be half wrong. **The second bond against the tool-chest is offered by the guild clerk and refused out loud** by Aren, using the clerk's own sentence from the twenty-eighth of Mudmonth: *a man who signs six and has three and a half is a man who has signed on faith, and faith is not a currency we take.* The bond of the eleventh is still on the roll and the bench votes on it on the ninth of Greenmonth.
64. **A return stitch is prepared for the first time with a witness, and the witness is a record.** Aren's undertaking of the eighth of Greenmonth, read aloud on the bridge and signed by three hands, sets down: the date and hour, the seat, the two discs, the maker's hand, and **plainly that he does not have a person to witness what he is about to do and that a page cannot be asked to turn round.** Tovan Kest signs **the fact that a thing was set and the hour, and refuses in writing to sign anything about a road**, on the ground that he has not believed in one since he was seventeen. The guild clerk puts his hand on a copy; a third copy goes into the Archive's press, outside Crown custody. **This satisfies the fourth term as a durable record and not as a person, and it is the first time in the volume he has prepared a crossing at all.** What he still does not have, and says so on the bridge and then in the street: **a person at the far end.** Nobody in Merefen knows who is holding anything on the other side of the door. The sheer line is the same: two is easy, three is not a number a glasswright will sell, and Chapter 18's notebook already said the sill will take one more and not a third.
65. **The ninth of Greenmonth is committed, and the bill is named aloud.** A market day; the guild bench sits; **the provincial summons of the twenty-ninth of Mudmonth goes unanswered**, which is a gap in a ledger in a building two hundred miles away and, in a year, a fact about him instead of an argument. Lysa Fenn said in advance that a man who does not appear has left a gap and a court that notices a gap will sometimes do something about it. **This is the second of three crossings this season.** The first cost nine days of the main-world calendar against a road-crews' saying that allows three, three days of fever, a market that did not happen, and a bell under a Crown seal that the Crown was within its rights to take. **Nothing at the sill answers him, and that is written as deliberate in Chapters 11, 18 and 20** — no warmth, no fold in the air, no voice, and the working that does work (a promise into a scaffold upright) is named beside it so the reader has the contrast in the same scene.
66. **The Rusk separation is enforced on the page.** Jos Rusk (Chapters 12, 19) and Pei Tarrow (Chapter 17) are never in a room together and no bystander calls Tarrow a Rusk; Tarrow says in his own voice that he has been a symbol for eleven years without being asked, that he has no book, no list and no dead, and that he wants freight. **Tarrow's mother is a Tarrow, of Hushwater, alive, sixty-eight, four miles from Merefen, and asked him to stop coming into town because coming into town is what got her noticed.** Anwen Rusk's crossing book has **eleven lines on the last page, the eleventh in a different hand and different ink — *Persons, 11. Not landed, drowned channel* — and the change of hand is attested by Aren Kest in tradesman's hand on the wrapper, at the cost Mara named in front of Rusk: his name now sits in a Crown file about Hushwater alongside the two instruments that call him a keeper.** Nobody in the batch compares the eight figures in this item to anything. Rusk is alive in Merefen, above a chandler's on the north end, and **he is wrong about the cause and was not argued with**; Mara put the cause in dispute in writing on purpose, because a wrong cause can be disproved in a year and a right cause cannot be proved at all.
67. **The five-day cut is on the page and is empty.** The fifth of Greenmonth was a Monday, the market was cleaned up, no new document came, no new person came, and the bell did not move. **Weekday chain for the block, now fixed: the second of Greenmonth a Friday, the fourth a Sunday and a market day, the fifth a Monday, the sixth a Tuesday, the seventh a Wednesday, the eighth a Thursday, the ninth a Friday and a market day.** The bench sits on the ninth. Do not drift these.

68. **Batch 0002 repair pass — what changed and what did not.** Thirty-five review findings were applied to Chapters 11–20 and six to the state files. **No plot beat was removed, no chapter renumbered, and the planned volume direction is untouched.** The four things a later writer must know because the batch's own state had them wrong: **(1)** Bram Ottery's statement was written on the night of the **second** of Greenmonth, not the seventh (items 53 and 54 corrected); **(2)** the road warden's sill repacking has **no date on any page** — Ch 2, Ch 11 and Ch 18 all say *the autumn, and again in the week before the storm*, and Ch 15's sworn finding now says the same (item 56 corrected); **(3)** the four thousand is never printed in the same sentence, paragraph, or breath as the seven villages — in Ch 13 they are two sentences, and in Ch 20 they are two paragraphs with a beat between and the villages explicitly recited rather than answered (item 60 corrected); **(4)** nine shillings is **more than half** the price of four marks, not a third of it (item 62 corrected). Also now on the page and usable: **Rell put the twenty-seventh of Mudmonth to the court himself and said on the record that he could not tell whether the paper or the road warden was lying** — he is no longer merely correct, he is a frightened officer with a lawful custody and a documented doubt, and Block 3 may use that. **Aren said the ninth aloud on the bridge, to Tovan and to Quarren, before he signed**, and Tovan chose his line in the light of it: no road, yes bridge, the distinction written that night. **No System display survives anywhere in Chapters 11–20**, and none may be added. Chapters 11–20 use `---` scene breaks; Chapters 1–10 do not — match the file you are in, and prefer none.

## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 20

- **Aren and Mara:** a working arrangement with a debt in both directions. He brought her the bell report, unargued and unwitnessed, and she filed it as reported with its own limits written out in full. She put the Hushwater wrapper in front of him knowing what his name on it would cost and told him the cost before she asked, and he wrote the attestation in her room in a tradesman's hand and did not read it twice. **She has told him where his money can come from, in front of a witness, and did not enjoy it and would do it again rather than let a man buy glass with a lien on his own tools.** She has not been told what a crossing costs him in the body; she now knows the date is impossible and has chosen not to say so twice. She keeps a third copy of his undertaking in the press. A copy in her book and a copy in his brother's week-book, dated, three hands.
- **Aren and Tovan:** the batch's refusal survives intact and is now **institutional, not emotional.** Tovan will witness that a thing was set and will not sign a sentence about a road, and he said why: he has not believed in one since he was seventeen and a man should not put his name under a thing he does not believe in. He put a wage advance on the roll **before he was asked and said so in the room**, and took a mark and a quarter as paid rather than credited so it would be a wage and not a favour. He copied a clerk's figure onto a factor's paper and refused to sign it as his own account. The estrangement is not healed; it is *inhabited*. Two weeks of paid work and a stone contract, and a brother who has found out what the yard is for and that it is not the mission.
- **Aren and Lysa Fenn:** she will have to put him nearer a bench and had two days to decide where; she has made herself a party on the record; she stamped the inspection order that gives the Archive its eleventh day; and she told him, in her own room, off the record, that he is the only person in the county who has ever come to a court with a stone instead of a speech, and refused to waste an evening on a man who is about to be told by a glasswright he cannot pay. He is going to fail to answer the summons she told him in advance would leave a gap.
- **Aren and Edric:** unchanged on the page and worse underneath. The office that holds the county file is the one that will be asked what the file is for; a memorandum about this county's keeper is already in motion; the Crown moves a bell anywhere in Neyra and no office in Neyra can say who told it to. Not named except inside a seal, and not to be named in this volume.
- **Aren and Rell:** a professional's worst case, now with a document between them. Rell relieved his sergeant in a corridor and told him the price to his face, and will not get him the accrual. Both of them are now correct on the record and neither has the bell.
- **Aren and Bellweather-Living:** **twenty days and not one word, in either direction, and the silence is now a stated cost rather than a mystery.** He has no person at the far end, has said so aloud, and has gone anyway.
- **Aren and the glassmaker:** a first honest sale and the batch's first account of how the glass will fail. Quarren has been selling glass to men who wanted a promise for thirty years and has just been asked for the failure mode instead.
- **Mara and Lysa:** a working alliance with the grudge still live and now on both sides of a public record. Mara's finding is dated and in a provincial file; Lysa's is dated and in the same file. Neither has told the other what the other is worth.
- **Mara and Sarn:** a keeper who has begun to need other people. Sarn argued for publication and was right about the principle; Mara refused on strategy; Sarn then wrote the attestation nobody asked for, in her own hand, as a copyist. The warrant for a third post is in the day-book and is the first promise Mara has made anybody in four years.
- **Mara and Jos Rusk:** a claimant who has been heard, given a number, told the truth about what his claim is worth, and given a cause that is weaker on purpose. He is not grateful and will not be, and he is owed a hearing as long as the case is open. He is going to find out how to reach five ferrymen and think hard about whether telling them is the same as using them.

## Canon established by Batch 0003 (Chapters 21–30, prose on the page)

69. **The batch's day map is on the page and it is the only calendar.** Main world: Ch 21 the **ninth** of Greenmonth (a **Friday**, a market day, the guild bench sits, the provincial summons is unanswered); Ch 24 the **sixteenth** (a **Friday**); Ch 29 the ninth to the sixteenth; Ch 30 the sixteenth to the twentieth. Weekday chain: the ninth a Friday, the tenth a Saturday, the eleventh a Sunday, the twelfth a Monday, the thirteenth a Tuesday, the fourteenth a Wednesday, the fifteenth a Thursday, the sixteenth a Friday, the seventeenth a Saturday, the eighteenth a Sunday, the nineteenth a Monday, the twentieth a Tuesday. Path time, Ch 22–28: **nineteen days, arriving the ninth of Greenmonth, YR 312**, and the chapters count **days of being there, not calendar dates** — Ch 22 the first, Ch 23 the second to the eleventh, Ch 25 the twelfth, Ch 26 the thirteenth, Ch 27 the fifteenth, Ch 28 the sixteenth to the eighteenth, departure the nineteenth. **Nineteen path days against six of the main world.** **The name is read aloud in the guild hall on the thirteenth day, so every later chapter measures from the thirteenth and not from the arrival: Ch 27 is two days after it and Ch 28's "five days" is counted from the eighteenth and is correct.** Do not reintroduce a Greenmonth date into a path scene and do not let the two calendars be read as the same clock. **Do not drift this chain** — the sixteenth is a **Friday**, and the Market Square board line *Road warden in the cart on Friday* means the cart, which came on the sixteenth.

70. **The bell's business in Merefen is closed on the page and must stay closed.** The bond of the eleventh was **carried on the ninth**: the tool-chest at three and a half against six, the balance on the road warden's wages at a shilling a day, **entered as a debt of his and not of the guild's, entered as proposed by him and entered as withdrawn by him before the vote.** The minute carries the reeve's statement that the surety was not in front of the bench and the clerk's statement that he proposed it and left. **He is on the guild roll — a road warden is elected in this room in Frostmonth — so in a year he will have a vote on a question that is not about him, and he has noticed that.**

71. **A vote taken in a man's absence is a vote about a bond and not about a man.** Reeve Aldis Fenn's minute says so, and Aren Kest says so aloud in a room, and the guild clerk enters *no reason given* as the guild's standing joke. **Two new phrases on the Market Square board, both without a rule over them:** *Ask Kest, he was gone* (under the first line) and *Road warden in the cart on Friday. Asked nobody anything*. The board now has **two ruled lines and three unruled** and nobody wipes them.

72. **The Crown letter is on the page and it is undeliverable by design.** Dated the **twelfth of Greenmonth**, brought by a man off a provincial post, to the town trust, and addressed *To the road-keeper of Merefen, or such officer as now holds that office, in the matter of the Bellweather hinge.* The trust is in abeyance, the reeve refuses to receive it because a reeve who receives a paper addressed to nobody has admitted there is nobody, and it goes into a drawer in the toll-house — where the other Crown paper in Merefen is — **chalked on the outside in the toll-clerk's own hand: the date, the second hour, *Addressed to the road-keeper of Merefen. No such office in this county. Not received. Not answered.*** The toll-clerk's reasoning is the durable part: a Crown's letter in a drawer is a rumour; a Crown's letter in a drawer with a date and an hour on the outside is a fact a man can look for. **Nobody has broken the seal and nobody will.**

73. **The middle of the volume turns on a press-mark, not on a wax.** A creditor's register records, against each tender, **which press cut the die, which die was in the press, and which impression of it that season**, because wax is rubbish in eleven years and the press-mark is cut into the page. The line for the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301 carries **press four, die eleven, impression two of the season**, and the counting house keeps a **seal-book** of its own wax impressions of every die in its use, and in the middle of that book, on a leaf nobody was surprised by, is **a hand laid flat on a book** — the same device as the grey wax on a post on the Old Toll bridge, the wax on a watch storehouse door by the fish weir, and the instrument Lysa Fenn put into her finding of the fourth. **Aren recognises it and does not say so in the room.** This is a trade fact and it is a fact a man can check.

74. **The outgoing writ register, as it now stands on the page.** Kept by the toll houses of Bellweather; four hundred leaves, five columns, about nine hundred lines; every line is a *tender* — a thing offered in satisfaction of a debt, with the day, the amount, the offerer's name, and a column for what came back. The line for the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301 reads: *a writ severing the crossing at Alder Vale and its dependent places, and the removal of the persons found within the sealed limit, and the handing of the arch to officers of that office, and the tolls of the arch released to that office for the term of the severance; tendered on the fourteenth and tendered against the arch; returned on the twentieth; withdrawn on the twenty-second.* Witnessed at the tender and the return by one hand; **entered at the withdrawal by a third hand, which is the only hand in the book that is a fair copy**, and a frightened clerk writes beautifully. The day's column **headed the twentieth, which is the day the tender came back and not the day anything stood there**, reads: **two hundred on the deck, four days stood, crews both banks at nothing, and one load of flour carried off the north quay entered as ours because the man who took it was in a Crown cloak and there was nobody on the quay who could say otherwise, and it is entered.** The four days is a duration entered under a day, and the span it describes is the **boats** standing up from the eighteenth to the twenty-second, not the tender: the tender came back on the twentieth and a recall came up on the twenty-second. In the column headed *tendered by*: **Edric Hunn, marshal.** The marshal is **alive** and has an office four days off, and Merrow says so in the room, and that is the most frightening sentence in the batch.

75. **The evidence exonerates and indicts on the same four lines, and the indictment is a one-line refusal.** A writ dated the thirteenth, read on the fourteenth, giving five days counted from the day before it was read, expiring on the night the city refused it; tendered the same day it was read; a recall already in the cabin of a second boat with a name on the outside of it, because nobody writes a recall in advance unless they know the date of the thing they are recalling. And, in the same book and the same night: **Aren Kest was on that deck in a Crown's cloak at the second hour, and he had sent a rider up on the seventeenth for a written extension covering the arch, and it was refused in one line on the eighteenth.** He had the copy in his hand at noon, in a cell, on the nineteenth. The man was twenty-one, had been in the valley since the eleventh, and had a written order for a bridge four miles inside the valley. **He did not hold the deck, and the finding said a captain lost a bridge, and both are now in the record and neither is a lie.**

76. **A name in a creditor's book is a war, and that is why the page does not leave the city.** The toll houses' condition, which the guild's man accepts: the day a name is in a creditor's book the book begins asking what the name was owed, and the answer is a Crown war, and forty-one houses and a bench and an abbey manage the only crossing in a country of four days and would not survive one. **Four conditions, entered by the counting-house clerk before the book was opened:** no copy of anything; a fair copy in the counting house's own press, unsigned, not produced in court; **the fact leaves, the line does not**; and **no motive.** The fourth condition is broken in the open — Aren says *on purpose* about the two boats and the old clerk stops him and enters the arrest against him, with the day and the hour. **The name is said aloud exactly twice in the whole batch, both times in Bellweather-Living, both times in a room, and never in a notebook and never in Merefen.** What goes north is a fact in two hands on a date: that a creditor's register in Bellweather has a line for the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301, with the press-mark of press four, die eleven, and the tender and return and withdrawal dates.

77. **The path's terms for the return are now an agreement, and it is a document with named terms.** Entered in the guild hall's book on the eighteenth day, read back twice: **a consent with conditions; a named person, Sera Quen, the ferrywright at Hushwater Reach, who keeps the road; a visible road, meaning a date, an hour, a place and the name of a person on this side standing in a book kept in the hall; a promise with a date on it, being the day the second pier is marked in the middle of the year at the lowest water by two men and a clerk; and a promise by the named person that the consent is hers and not the bench's and cannot be argued with in this hall.** The fourth term: **any paper out of the north addressed to this city comes to her hands and is answered by her or not at all.** The fifth, hers: the fourth camp keeps its name on the gate bridge approach for as long as there is a camp, and a stranger does not decide what the camp is for. **Two witnesses, Sera Quen and Captain Ruen, and one record, the bench-clerk's entry.** This is the first time in the volume that anything in Bellweather-Living is a document rather than a favour.

78. **The counterweight is named and it is a price, not a favour.** Aren Kest states, in the hall, that the road crews' saying is three and a man who has not three to give should not begin; that he spent one in Mudmonth; that he is in the second; and that **the third is pledged to the bench's date**, which falls in the middle of the year, when there may be nothing left. And then the second half, which the bench records as the price: **the seat he goes out of and comes back through has been used twice, the glasswright wrote in his own hand that a scar takes two and that three is not a number he would sell to a stranger or a friend, there is no fresh Steppe glass north of Merefen before Longlight, and Aren has no money and no maker — so somebody has to cut and set him a new seat before the date, and he cannot promise to be there.** If he is not there, she writes *not here* in the book. **The seat limit is not softened anywhere in the batch: two is easy, three is not a number a glasswright will sell.**

79. **The agreement's other half is Aren's own written extension of the Chapter 10 account, and it is a statement of a man and not a finding.** He says four things that are in the sworn account and then a fifth that is not, and the fifth is that he was on the deck of the arch on the night of the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301, in a Crown's cloak, at the second hour, with two hundred people on it who had not been ordered there, and that he has never said it in any room. He then puts **the field book** — the notes that have ridden in the breast pocket of the coat he crossed in, and have not been out of it since — on the guild hall table, and the clerk enters the hour and the fact that it is a statement and not a finding and that Aren is **a witness against himself in this matter and against the account his own office has kept since the thirtieth of Mudmonth**, and that the bench is not asked to believe him and is not asked to forgive him, and that it is entered because the bench has no column for the other things and has refused to invent one. **The account is not filed a second time as a new document. It is extended, out loud, in his own voice, in front of the city.**

80. **The second pier is still eight inches and is not growing, and the work done on it in the batch is maintenance, not repair.** The ring stone is sound; the mortar behind it was raked out from the inside by water coming up under the arch; the honest repair is a new cutwater and nine months of the bench's whole capacity against a debt pledged to the ninth of a grandson. The joint is repointed in **clay and a little lime to the width of a thumb, left an inch and three-quarters, on the local method, over Aren's objection and with his dissent minuted** — because a hard pointing in a moving ring is a crack somewhere he cannot get at. **The crack opens in the cold and shuts in the warmth and is measured twice a year by two men and a clerk.** Nothing in this batch may be read as a repair. Block 5's public oath is untouched.

81. **The Bellweather Bridge Guild's fourth camp is on the page and it is a road surface, not a road.** A hundred and forty paces of the north bank, three drying floors, a rubbing floor, a pitching floor and a store, run by two guild men and a woman who has cut setts since she was nine, entered in the hall's book as a works of the guild **with his name in the wrong column on purpose**, so that a bench can claim a road it has built. **The gate bridge approach is being laid to the fifth bay; the third bay of the gate bridge came out on the sixteenth day and a third of it is not there**, worse than the sounding rod he had described to a counting house. The camp's fifth term is that a stranger does not decide what the camp is for.

82. **The bell's counterparties in Bellweather-Living and what each of them wants, all of it unrelated to Aren.** **Alis Merrow**, toll-house steward, wants the northern trade: three houses shut since YR 301, four feet of paving, a hundred and twenty marks of uncollected toll, a city that cannot mend its own bridge. He is a creditor, not an ally, and he uses a foreign stranger as an argument and says so. **Hollis Dree**, the city's toll-clerk and archivist, will not surrender a copy of anything to the main world, and the reason is a reason. **Sera Quen**, ferrywright and steward at Hushwater Reach, maintains and is answerable to the people who use the crossing. **Captain Ruen**, barge escort, grateful, with a daughter of fifteen who worked out that there is a road. **The bench-clerk** has kept a list of the four men in a hundred years who were offered the refusal and could not be believed with it, and the fourth died in a cart two years ago. The city counts **ferry hands and hearths and not souls**, and the best estimate for Hushwater Reach is **near sixty**; the erased Hushwater in the main world is eighty. **No character in this batch prints the two figures together or notices the gap.**

83. **A woman with a crossing book and a reason, and the sentence that goes with it.** Hushwater Reach's book is a working book in a living hand; the second page carries **eleven crossings against nine names**, the tenth being the woman who keeps it and the eleventh her mother, who does not cross on a working day any more. **She will not let it be copied**, and the reason, in her own trade's words, is that a copy is a second thing that somebody can lose, and after eleven years one of them will be in a court in a country that has a finding saying the channel was never there, and the argument between them will be about her hamlet and not about him. The same reason, in the same terms, is why Hollis Dree's office gives out nothing. **It is the same answer as the keeper of the Bellweather Claims Archive's rule about one set of records outside Crown custody, and Aren works that out on the water on the way back up the channel and it is the only thing he writes in the notebook that is not a figure.**

84. **The Archive's new holdings and the second claim number, with the ordinals settled.** The wrapped shelf register of the Crown's fourth press, attested in a copyist's hand: the *Anchors, schedules and dispositions, Alder Vale* is entered among **two hundred and nine** other volumes and rolls and is not on its shelf, and against it, in the press register's own hand, **the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301. Removed.** — no name, no office, no authority, no destination, no fee, no initial of the clerk or the man who told him. **Nobody in Volume 01 explains it and nobody may.** Entered on the office's own account, **against nobody, and with no opinion**: an application for the production of records in the matter of Alder Vale, resting on two facts only — that a bound Crown order contains a page out of its order in a hand not of the roll, and that the volume entered on the press register as containing the schedule of Alder Vale's anchors is not on its shelf. **A date can be looked for; a total can be disproved once.** The Stonebarrow fragment is the sixth claim number this office has entered in four years, the application the seventh. **The brine weights are not filed and are not mentioned.**

85. **What a crossing costs, entered as a document on the sixteenth of Greenmonth, and the thing it is a document about.** The Archive's own account, in the fair hand, so that he is told it by a paper and not by his own body: on the twenty-seventh of Mudmonth a man returned and was in a fever three days, over on the thirtieth, entered from a watch statement and not from his own mouth. And then, in the file, on his word and not her observation: **that the cost of a crossing to him is in the body and not in the calendar; that it is greater the better he becomes at the work on the far side; and that there is no arrangement of his hours that makes it less.** The bill is the crossing, because the crossing is the only thing he is good at and it costs more the better he gets at it. He has stopped paying the price alone and has started making it a document other people are responsible for, and the cost of that is that he can no longer be the only one who can be harmed by it.

86. **Aren's relationship to the Crown is now documentary and not personal.** His position at the end of Chapter 30: a provincial ledger with *absent* against the ninth; a bench that has to decide in a year whether to restore a trust over a lawful Crown holding; a Crown letter in a drawer chalked *not answered*; a magistrate's finding in a county file that says an instrument was lawful and the office behind it could not be named; a sergeant he has never spoken to whose unasked statement he has used twice; and a clerk two hundred miles off who will be asked what the county file is for. **Edric is not named in Merefen anywhere in this batch and does not appear.** The name exists in a book in a city four days off and in the reader's head and nowhere else.

87. **What this batch deliberately did not do, recorded so a later writer does not "fix" it.** It did not name a seventh or fifth character — the woman in the hood is the woman on Ferry Lane from Chapter 5, whose shutters have been shut since the thaw, and the girl on the steps is the girl from Chapter 5, and the old and young clerks in the counting house, the man with the mallet, the woman with the string and the woman with the crossing book all stay addressed by trade. It did not total four thousand and the villages, and it did not print the two Hushwater figures together. It did not explain the bell, the YR 302 clearance entry, the roadmark, or why he can cross. It did not resolve the second pier. It did not give Aren money. It did not file the brine weights. It did not use a Gatehouse line, a panel, a system display, a seam, or a warmth in the palm; the three touches of the sill in this batch all get nothing, and the working that works is still shown by timber and a hand and not by a light. It did not have him notice his own problem for free: the mortar, the notebook on the seventeenth day, and the account in the filing room are the three places it is earned.

88. **The three Hushwater women are three women, and the chapter now says so out loud.** (1) **The woman who keeps the crossing book** at Hushwater Reach, about fifty, a thumb wet with ink, who refused Aren a copy in Ch 23 with *a copy is a second thing that somebody can lose* and who told him the counting-house rule; she is not on the bench, was not asked to come, and is not in the hall. (2) **Sera Quen**, ferrywright and steward, a ferrywright since she was fourteen, **younger than he expected**, iron dust in the creases of her hands, who named a person and a camp and wrote the five terms. (3) **The woman who has cut setts since she was nine** at the fourth camp on the north bank, who is a sett-cutter and not a Hushwater woman at all. Ch 27 now carries an explicit clause separating (1) from (2) so that a later writer does not merge them; **do not name (1) or (3).**

89. **The claim numbers Mara entered in Batch 0003, with their hours.** The Stonebarrow fragment against nobody at the **ninth hour of the sixteenth**; **the application for the production of records against nobody, on her own account, at the eleventh hour of the sixteenth** — the seventh claim number this office has entered in four years, the fragment being the sixth; and the office's own sheet headed *the cost of a crossing, as known to this office*, **at the ninth hour of the sixteenth**, in the day-book and not in the press, handed to him standing up in a room. **All three are on the sixteenth, which is a Friday**; there is no claim number entered on any other day in this batch, and Ch 30's reference to one entered on her own account must be read as the Friday.

90. **The Crown letter's chalk, as it now stands on the drawer.** One line in the toll-clerk's own hand, and it is a sentence and not five words: *Crown letter, twelfth of Greenmonth, YR 312, second hour. Addressed to the road-keeper of Merefen. No such office in this county. Not received.
