Not answered.* **The word *no* stands three times in it** — no such office, not received, not answered — and Ch 30 now says so, so that a later writer does not try to count the words.

91. **Batch 0003 review repair pass — what changed and what did not.** Sixteen findings were applied to Chapters 21–30 and eleven to the state files. **No plot beat was removed, no chapter was renumbered, and the planned volume direction is untouched.** The four things a later writer must know because the batch had them wrong: **(1)** **the sixteenth of Greenmonth is a Friday**, not a Tuesday, and two state files and three prose spots said Tuesday — the board line is now *Road warden in the cart on Friday*; **(2)** **the name is read in the guild hall on the thirteenth day**, so Ch 27's "five days" and "eight days" are now **two** and Ch 28's "five days" (from the eighteenth) was already right and was left alone; **(3)** **the sworn account was filed on the thirtieth of Mudmonth, YR 312, six weeks before Ch 28**, and Ch 28 had dated it a year early in two places; **(4)** **the guild clerk's entry in Ch 21 is the undertaking of the eighth of Greenmonth and it is the *second* time *no reason given* has gone on a roll that month** — the third is the ninth page, entered on the **tenth** at the yard in Ch 29, which is the live reading and which Ch 30 repeats. Also fixed: Ch 26's tangled paragraph at the arrival has been rewritten on page facts, Ch 26's doubled instruction to the clerk and its repeated clause are gone, Ch 26's unclosed quotation and the two mid-paragraph close-quotes in Ch 27 are closed, Ch 26's four-day span is now attributed to the boats and not to the tender, Ch 30's second witness is **Tovan Kest, who is now in the room when the account is sworn** so that the chain of custody is on the page, Ch 24's closing is back inside Mara Vey's point of view, and Merrow's "six days" in Ch 25 is now **twelve**, matching the twelfth day of the chapter. **Recorded rather than changed:** Hollis Dree states the two hundred and eleven in a public hall in Ch 27 out of the city's own ledger (item 36, second source, not a substitution), and the Ch 29 Crown letter carries the address only and not a body recording non-attendance, which is dropped on purpose (item 92).

92. **The Chapter 29 card beat that was cut on purpose, so it is not re-added.** The card called for the Crown letter's *body* to record that the road-keeper of Merefen did not attend on the ninth. **The delivered letter carries the address only**, and the reason is that the non-attendance is already carried by a better document: the **provincial ledger with *absent* against the ninth**, which is the whole point of it and is what makes the word a fact about a man in a year rather than an argument. A second document saying the same thing in a Crown's hand would weaken the ledger and would put the town in the position of answering a paper it cannot answer. **Do not restore the body of the letter.** If a later batch needs a Crown hand on the non-attendance, the place for it is the provincial clerk's ledger and not the letter.


## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 30

- **Aren and Mara:** the batch's real movement, and it is a change of custody rather than a change of feeling. She entered a claim number on her own account against nobody and told him the cost of it before she signed; she entered the cost of a crossing on her own paper, dated, in the fair hand, so that it was on paper before his body told him; and he told her, in her own filing room, what a crossing costs in the body, which is the one thing he had been managing her about for two months. **She was not relieved by it and had thought about not being relieved for six days and said so in advance.** He still does not promise to stop and cannot promise not to. The relationship is now a working arrangement in which both of them hold documents about the other.
- **Aren and Tovan:** the refusal is intact and the bill is attached. Tovan took a wage advance he was not asked for, entered a Crown letter chalked *not answered*, took work he does not like from a creditor and said so in the yard before anybody could ask, and **learned in a market from a carrier that the yard has more in it than it has had in four years because a man comes down a street and looks at a thing that is wrong and says a figure out loud.** The yard's only defence is that it is paid for a contract and not a favour. He asked no question on the road up to the Old Toll and will not, and he put his brother in the room at the back of the yard that is kept for a man who is ill. The estrangement is not healed; it is two brothers holding the same piece of paper about a seat cut square in a bridge.
- **Aren and Bellweather-Living:** a document, not a favour. He has an agreement with a named steward, a visible road, two witnesses and one record, and he has put his last crossing of the year and an uncut seat on the table. He has been told in a hall, by an archivist, in front of two hundred people, that he is owed nothing and that being believed is not a thing that lasts. **He has a debt to somebody who is not the guild, which is Alis Merrow, and a consent that is not the bench's, which is Sera Quen's.**
- **Aren and Captain Ruen:** an answer given in the wrong order on purpose, and a man who asked for it. Ruen is grateful and says so and asks anyway, and is not comforted, and is right. He has a daughter of fifteen who now knows there is a road, and a garboard fastening with Aren's work in it that has not opened in two years, and he has said nothing to anybody about either for two years.
- **Aren and Edric Hunn:** a name in a book four days off, in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, and a press-mark that puts the marshal's office and the watch captain of Merefen in the same hand-laid-on-a-book device. **Nothing about it is in Merefen's record, in Merefen's notebooks, or in his own coat, and he has said in public that he cannot carry the page and will not write the name down.** Edric is not named outside that one room.
- **Mara and the Archive's own position:** she is now a party who has entered a number against nobody, and a woman who has been right four times and has started to price it and noticed. Sarn Oyelaran attested the shelf register in her own hand as a copyist, and is the person who wrote it down, and is beginning to be needed rather than useful.
- **Reeve Aldis Fenn and Lysa Fenn:** the reeve has minuted that a vote about a bond is not a vote about a man, has costed the town at four or five marks a year and a market and has said so in a room without being thanked, and has refused to receive a Crown letter addressed to an office that does not exist because receiving it would be an admission. The magistrate's finding is in a county file two hundred miles off. **The two sisters' minutes do not agree with each other and were written three days apart, and the reeve has said in public that this is the honest state of the town.**

## Canon established by Batch 0004 (Chapters 31–40, prose on the page)

93. **The batch's day map and the weekday chain, and the two errors in the Batch 0004 prompt itself.** Ch 31–32 the **twentieth and twenty-first of Greenmonth** · Ch 33 the **twenty-eighth** · Ch 34 the **second of Longlight** · Ch 35–36 the **ninth and tenth** · Ch 37 the **fifteenth** · Ch 38 the **eighteenth** · Ch 39 the **twenty-first** · Ch 40 the **twenty-second**. Weekdays, anchored on the **audited** fact that **the sixteenth of Greenmonth is a Friday** (item 91, and the Market Square board line *Road warden in the cart on Friday* depends on it): **20 Tue, 21 Wed, 28 Wed, 30 Fri; Longlight 1 Sat, 2 Sun, 9 Sun, 10 Mon, 15 Sat, 18 Tue, 21 Fri, 22 Sat.** The Batch 0004 prompt said "the twenty-eight a Tuesday" and gave the Longlight weekdays as 2 Fri / 9 Fri / 10 Sat / 15 Thu / 18 Sun / 21 Thu / 22 Fri. **Both are arithmetically incompatible with the audited Greenmonth chain and both were rejected; the prose follows the chain above and the prompt is wrong, not the chapters.** The lowest water of the year is **on or about the twenty-sixth of Longlight, a Wednesday**, which is the bench's marking day and the day the third crossing is pledged to.

94. **A crossing with no owner is a crossing with no crew, and a crew is not an owner.** This is Batch 0004's most reusable idea and it is Toban's, in his own voice, on the Old Toll deck. A **trust in abeyance means the crossing has no owner; a crossing with no owner has no crew, because a crew is hired and paid by somebody**; and a crew "can be hired by anybody with a box and can go home at noon" and "never once decided anything about this bridge." The distinction: **the owner is the thing that answers, and answering is the expensive part.** Nobody in Merefen had ever had to make the distinction because for two generations the owner was a bench, and a bench has a book and therefore has an answer. The trust's instrument gives the bench power over a crossing and no power over a person, which is why Chapter 37's absent man is entered as a protection and not as a default.

95. **The tolls of the Old Toll crossing are suspended, and the direction bars correspondence and *entertainment*.** **Dace Millward**, 61, a creditor in this town thirty-one years, **holder of the tolls for two years by purchase from an abbey in Rainmonth** (not nineteen years — the trade is thirty-one, the holding is two). The Crown's letter is dated the **twentieth of Greenmonth, YR 312**, arrives at the **seventh hour**, is addressed to *the holder of the tolls of the Old Toll crossing* and does two things: **suspends the tolls from the first of Longlight**, and directs that the holder "shall not permit, and shall use his office to prevent, any passage, **entertainment**, or correspondence" between any person claiming to hold or have held the crossing and any other person, on the crossing or elsewhere in the county, upon the subject of the crossing. **The town is not required to be told of the second half of it** (item 101). Millward's figures, fixed: **nineteen marks a year** in a good year, **eight marks and ten shillings** the winter half, and a wharfage that needs four hundred yards of road. He obeys, says so, wants the obedience on the chalk, and the toll-clerk writes it: *Crown letter, twentieth of Greenmonth, YR 312, seventh hour. To the holder of the tolls of the Old Toll crossing. Suspension of the tolls from the first of Longlight. A direction as to correspondence. Received by the holder. Held in the drawer. **The tolls are suspended by the Crown and not by this town.*** It then carries three witnesses' additions, including *read aloud in this office, the seventh hour, in the presence of the holder, the toll-clerk of this office, and Aren Kest, road warden of Merefen*, and Aren Kest's hand went under the clerk's and not over it.

96. **The quarter-inch datum, and the guild's man's argument for putting it on Crown's paper.** The Sedge Reach bench mark was re-cut **by hand three times in thirty-five years** — Mudmonth of YR 312 (the sixth), Mudmonth of YR 297, and the twenty-eighth year — every time because the instrument was away, every time in a month when the mark was proud, and **always downward**, so every level in the county taken since the twenty-eighth year is out by not less than a quarter of an inch **in the direction that makes a deck look sounder and a crown look higher than it is**. **The quarter of an inch is in the stone and the nine inches is in the heads:** a man who takes a level off that mark writes a round number, because a bench minute says *re-cut in hand* and not by how much, and the next man takes it off him and rounds it again because he cannot check it either, and nobody alive will find a nail's width on a mark a bench has dressed by hand. **Four generations of men rounding it in their own favour is nine inches, and it is the heads that go on building for a hundred years.** This is the chapter's thesis and it must not be re-derived as a physical figure: **the stone has lost about a quarter of an inch in thirty-five years and the arithmetic of the records degrades faster than the stone does.** The three figures are in Aren's notebook, which is in a coat, and that was the position until Chapter 34: **the guild's man refuses the guild's roll and consents to the county's schedule**, on the argument that "a thing that is ours is a thing we are answerable for and a thing we cannot be corrected in. **So keep the figures you can be got at.**" The figure is now on the county's sheet of the second of Longlight, in a remarks column, with a date, an hour, and a witness, and says on its face that the bench was asked for eleven days and declined. **The guild's man still refuses the guild's roll and must go on refusing it** — the bench is not wrong and has not been alleged to be.

97. **The Crown's four jobs, and the column headed *keeper*.** Longlight is bridge season and there is more work in the county than in four years. Four jobs: a **schedule of the season's inspections** (forty-one structures, a penny a mile and a shilling a day and the county's cart — **taken**), a **courier** carrying a sealed Crown packet four days each way (**refused**), a **copying post at three shillings a week** in the county survey office, initialling Crown returns (**refused**), and a **culvert at Four Elms** for a farmer (**taken**, a shilling a day and the farmer's materials). The reason, in his own hand: **a schedule is a record and a post is an office**; "a schedule asks me what I saw. A post tells me what to do next." **The county's schedule has a ruled column headed *Keeper* on every line** — an ordinary administrative column, ruled by the county's surveyor — and a man who signs in it on forty-one structures becomes, when the book is copied into a provincial office, a keeper of forty-one crossings. He cannot correct the form and he cannot correct the market, and the reason he cannot correct the market is that explaining the other sense of the word would mean explaining a column heading on a county sheet in a market. The one job he takes is signed at the foot of the sheet with the reason in his own hand **and with the statement that one of the two reasons is the money**, "because a man who takes Crown money for a month and cannot say in his own hand why is a man who has already agreed to something and does not know it."

98. **Petitioning, subpoenaeing and petrifying, in that order, and why the third works.** Mara's chapter, in a filing room, in her own words, and it is the batch's most portable piece of machinery:
- A **memorial** is a paper a person writes who cannot sue. It asks for nothing in particular and it dies of its own weight in about five years. **This office answers memorials by being dull at them and never argues with one in writing.**
- A **petition** is the same paper with a man's name on it and a court at the head. It is a door, and a door has to be answered.
- A **subpoena / returnable order** is a paper with a date on it. It names nobody's wrongdoing, it can be made without a summons, and its whole force is the date. **The first one came off a provincial post on the twenty-first of Greenmonth, stamped on the eighteenth, returnable on the ninth of Longlight, with a recital naming an office the county cannot produce.**
- **Petrification** is a **writ of enquiry into custody**, and it is the one that works. **The writ does not want to succeed.** It is returnable on the **ninth of Rainmonth** and is worth nothing on the ninth of Rainmonth, and what it wants is for the ninth of Rainmonth to arrive: **pending the enquiry the office may not move, copy out, or part with any record held by it.** Nobody is hurt, nothing is taken, nothing is shut, and a record office cannot make a copy of anything for thirty days. **The bar takes effect when the writ is stamped and not when it is served or returned**, and the stamp was the second hour of the ninth of Longlight — so **Mara's entire margin was the six days between the twenty-first and the twenty-seventh of Greenmonth and she spent them on Chapter 32's schedule of holdings, and nobody told her they were the margin because the writ did not exist yet.**
- **A printed space is the most frightening object in this county**, because a blank gets ruled through and dated and a ruled line is read as an answer, and the person who answers for it is the one who signed the return. Petch's grandfather's rule.

99. **Rowan Petch, 26, four years in the filing room of the provincial court, and the particular.** Mara's exhibit wrapper of the thirtieth of Mudmonth carried, in a small hand in the margin, *the seal is that of the marshal's emergency office, that office having produced nothing to this court.* The provincial court holds a copy of Mara's file and not the exhibit, and a clerk writes a **particular** for every exhibit the file does not hold. Petch wrote the particular **on the ninth of Greenmonth** and in it **put the words *the marshal's emergency office* into a file of the county's superior court** — which is the office the finding of the fourth of Greenmonth formally asked for and was not answered, on a dated question. He disclosed it to Mara **of his own motion, unasked, on the twenty-first of Greenmonth**, asked for his disclosure to be entered in her day-book with the hour, and **asked for it to say he did it on his own motion and not on a fee**, which is the sentence a clerk needs written over his name and which somebody else always has to write. He has been in Merefen **three times**: the twenty-first of Greenmonth, the ninth of Longlight, and the eleventh. He paid nothing and is owed nothing; the only thing he wants is to have been careful once while somebody was watching. **He appended his own memorandum to the endorsement of the Archive's return, of his own motion, not entered in the file, because "there is nowhere else in this building to put it."**

100. **The bench minute of the fifteenth of Longlight, and the gift the county gave the town for free.** The bench of the Merefen bridge guild **sat on a Saturday, on the reeve's calling, and the Saturday is minuted.** **Aren Kest was entered absent — at his own request, made on the thirteenth, not served, and the bench recorded that it had been informed he stands named in a provincial ledger in respect of the ninth of Greenmonth and had declined to consider it, and no reason is given.** (This is the **fifth** *no reason given* in a guild roll in four months; the clerk said in Greenmonth that he hoped somebody would find them all and read them together, and has begun writing the dates in the margin beside them.) Tovan said the true thing and did not vote because he is a party, in a public room, and the clerk entered that it was the **second** time a man had stood up in that room and said the true thing and declined to vote on it. The resolution, carried by eight hands and the reeve's: **the Old Toll crossing is kept in repair by the bench out of its own materials and its own men's time, and not out of the trust, and not at the charge of any person holding tolls, and not on account of any person named in any paper of the Crown** — and the bench records that it is not the owner and does not claim to be. The Crown's superior court had, on the ninth, sent a returnable order to the bench requiring it to name the officer holding the object; **the county refused to execute it on the eleventh** ("no officer of this county has authority to execute an order of the superior court, and the addressee is a bench of a town's guild and not an officer of anything") and **returned it**, and in returning it had to write the matter into a printed space, which put **the office of keeper of the Bellweather hinge into a Crown's clerk's hand in Merefen, on the back of a refused paper, with a date** — and the town read it out in a guild hall and minuted that it had taken no notice of it. **A Crown's refusal to use its own court against a town's bench is now a document the town can read out loud and be right about. It is the only piece of Crown paper in Merefen that helps the town, and it got there by accident.**

101. **Captain Rell, the commendation, and the instruction.** The Crown's office nailed up on the watch-house door, dated the **eighth of Longlight**, a commendation of the watch of Merefen with a grey seal and the hand-laid-on-a-book device, praising the execution of a lawful instruction in the matter of an object **"held in use among a company of persons"** — a phrase out of the seizure order of the sixteenth of Mudmonth, about a bell on a cradle that four hundred people walked over every fourth day, read in a provincial office as *in the middle of people*. **Rell is not flattered: he calls it a man being picked, nails it up himself with four nails, and says the notice is the only one he has put on that wall in nineteen years.** The instruction, dated the **ninth of Longlight, second hour** — the same hour, in another building, a writ of enquiry was stamped on the Archive, and the coincidence is in the letter and not noticed — received on the **tenth at the second hour** on the county's own horse and carried out on the **tenth at the fifth hour**, sealed, from the marshal's office: the object is held lawfully and the office is content it continue; **the watch shall prevent contact between the object and any person, and prevent any person approaching, examining or speaking with any person concerning it**; and **the watch shall prevent the assembly of any body of this county in respect of the object, and shall report.** It is **not a threat, names no law, requires no court, and the town is not required to be told.** Rell carried it out in one day, moved the bell at the fourth hour **from the storehouse by the fish weir into a room in the watch house itself**, did not renew the storehouse seal and left its door open in the day, and **told Aren on a step in the rain that he had made the chain of custody unbroken on purpose and that a record that is unbroken is a thing somebody can use.** He was relieved of a sergeant and of nine months and three weeks' accrual and has not been thanked; **his defence is that the one gap in the chain is nine minutes long on the twenty-seventh of Mudmonth and a man who was under an arch and told four hundred market people a city was standing.** The instrument **is returnable on the fifteenth of Longlight and Rell fills in the return himself, with his own hand, and has to write the office of keeper of the Bellweather hinge into a form, because the form asks what matter the object is held in respect of.** The bell is now in a room with a lock and a man in it at all times, **which is a Crown's room by the end of a year whatever anybody says.**

102. **Edric Hunn's first letter, and the fifth piece that is not in the chain.** It arrives at Merefen on the **eighteenth of Longlight, fourth hour**, in **county brown paper** with a **county postmark** and a **grey wax seal of a hand laid flat on a book** — a letter does not need a seal, and the seal says the sender wants the first person who opens the envelope to know. It is **eleven hundred words, dated the eighth of Longlight**, and it is **accurate**: it accounts for every day, every room, every hand and every paper since the first of Mudmonth, including the bell report of the seventh, the account filed beneath the finding on the thirtieth, the bonds and the notice and the valuation, the four conditions and the arrest entered against him for saying a motive, the passage in the hall on the eighteenth day of the other world, the field book on the bench's table, and the four-hundred-leaf register with its press-mark. **It contains no offer, no restoration, no rank, no shield for the Archive, and no access to a record.** Its one act of generosity is the four sentences that kill eleven years: *At twenty-one years of age you were the best man in the room. That is not a compliment; it is a load-bearing fact.* The whole letter is written in the third person about him; **exactly one sentence in it has a subject, a verb and a second person in it — *You will have no further contact with it*** — and it requires no court. It closes by asking, courteously, **for the list of those to whom he has spoken of the matter.** **The letter contains no person's name but his own and the sender's** — every other participant is *a man of Hushwater*, *a woman without a name*, *a road warden of Merefen*, *a creditor's man* — and Aren works out in a quarter of an hour that the only book in this county with that in it is the Archive's attendance column, and that Ch 32's petition's second column is a list of four descriptions and no names. **The names are the last thing the office does not have, and the letter is the Crown being entirely honest with him for the first time in eleven years.** His reply is five hundred words over four days, in the flat hand, and it is **not a refusal and not a defence**: a receipt by seal, date and hour; **the date** (the middle of the year, at the lowest water, and nobody in this country will be able to tell him where he is until he is there or is not, and that is deliberate); **a fact** (the object is lawful here, the magistrate's finding is in a county file, he signed six marks and cannot pay, and he is not going to get the bell back); and **a condition** (what the instruction costs: a bench that cannot meet, a toll-holder's winter half, a man with a bar to be watched in a county of nine men who watch roads — and, unasked for, **the dated question of the fourth of Greenmonth**). He gives no names. He signs with his name and no title. **The letter went to the post on the twenty-second at the seventh hour in county brown paper with a tallow seal, and reached the provincial town on the twenty-fourth, and a provincial clerk's memorandum of the twenty-fifth asks who else he has told, and answers itself: not this county; the question requires another office, and that office has a woman in it, and that office has a form.**

103. **The fifth piece, and why it cannot be lodged.** The Crown's letter is the only document in his possession that is the other side's own account of him, and it is signed and sealed. He cannot put it in the Archive's press (a writ forbids the office to part with records, and a Crown's own case against him in a room over a salt merchant is what the writ was written for); he cannot give it to the magistrate; he cannot put it in a guild roll. **It stays in a locked room over a cooper and there will never be a chain of custody on it, and he has written that down in the fourth piece.** **He did not show it to Mara**, and the cost of that is exact: she would have to enter it within a year and a day or refuse to, and a keeper who applies her own rule to a man she has an opinion about is finished, and he knows she will find out inside a week — and she finds out on the day it arrives, off a postman going past her own window at the fourth hour — because a wax seal the size of a crown piece is not a thing you can walk up a street with twice a day. **He was right about the speed and had no way at all to do anything about it, which is the point.** **This is the second time in a month he has decided what a keeper of records may not be told, and she had written him a line on the third of Longlight, dated, saying *do not write to me about this*, which he read as permission and which was the opposite.**

104. **The report, and the two readings, and the trade fact that makes both of them useless.** **Harl Mounce**, 34, a barge mate of fourteen years on the northern road carrying stone; laid four days on a towpath at the toll-house at Gallow Ford on the **second of Longlight** while two men and a boy with a book took a number off a mark cut in the coping of the **second pier**; a steward of thirty years told him **eleven**, and **eight** at the last marking, in a wharf conversation, and neither number is in the book of any person. He is not a liar and he has the sequence, the date and the words wrong, in the direction that makes it worse, and he said *going* four times and the steward never said it once. Mara entered it on the twenty-first at the fourth hour, as reported, with **five limits**, and the fifth is the load-bearing one: **the same description — a mark cut in coping, two men and a book, a rule, a number twice a year, the second pier — is a description of a Crown's office at a bridge in this country, and is also a description of a bench marking a ring on a pier twice a year at the lowest water in a city this county's own finding says was never built, and no person has said the two are the same bridge and no person in this county can say they are not.** She therefore **did not ask him which**, and entered the day and the hour at which she decided not to, against nobody, with no opinion. The market's second version, thirty mouths: *Bridge in the other world is going. Kest has been there. Kest is not saying* — and the joke that has hardened round it is **Ask the keeper. The keeper'll have it written down with the hour on it**, which is now the same word doing three jobs in one town: a keeper of records, a road warden, and an office a Crown's clerk read off a roll.

105. **The trade fact that makes the true version worse than the false one, and it is Aren's.** **A ring stone opens in the cold and shuts in the warmth, and a bench marks a ring twice a year precisely because a ring has two readings and not one.** So eight and eleven are almost certainly not the same season, and a crack in a ring that measures eight in the warmth and eleven in the cold **has almost certainly not moved three inches** — and a bench that has entered two numbers a year for four generations and has never once compared them **cannot tell from its own book whether the ring is standing still or going, and has never needed to, because until a writ came down off a river eleven years ago nobody in this country was marking that ring for anything but a bench's own order.** The number he wants is the one four years ago, in either season, and nobody in this country has it. **And the report cannot be acted on by anybody: not by him (no seat, no glass, an instruction, and a woman who will write *not here* in a book), not by a bench (no jurisdiction over a stone four days off), not by a magistrate (can order a production, cannot order a man to look at a ring), not by the keeper of records (who has written down that she does not know).** This is the batch's engine, and it is a man being competent and arriving at nothing.

106. **The chain, the three places, and the rule it obeys.** On the **twenty-second of Longlight** Aren Kest makes **a chain of four pieces in one order with a date and an hour against each**: a sworn account of the thirtieth of Mudmonth with the finding of the fourth of Greenmonth beneath it; the bench minute of the fifteenth of Longlight with the returned order on it; the report of the twenty-first with its five limits; and **the fourth piece, his own account of the nineteen days in the city four days off, in nineteen lines with a day against each, which is in nobody's hands but his and always has been.** The spine of the chain is not his and not Mara's: **it is the phrase *the office of keeper of the Bellweather hinge*, which is in three of the four pieces, is in none of them by itself, and reached Merefen free on the back of a refused paper in a county clerk's hand.** Three places, and he chooses a **building over a person** and says why in a room: **a person can be arrested for a document and an office can only be closed, and a closed office still has a door.** (1) **The toll-house**, Dace Millward's roll and chest, entered in seven lines in the toll-holder's own hand with an eighth of his own, because a creditor's book is a book of what a man is owed and it is the only book in the county a court in a hundred years will order a thing out of. (2) **The Archive's press, as a lending and not a custody** — the chain is the property of a person, the office may produce, exhibit, read and hand it to a court, and asserts no claim to it, and **it will be listed in the writ's answer on the ninth of Rainmonth in a fair hand**, which is the cost Mara named before she agreed to it. (3) **The yard's week-book, as a fact and not a paper**, because **Tovan Kest's rule, said out loud in a gateway in front of four men on the twenty-eighth of Greenmonth, is that nothing that belongs to a man goes in this yard's book and only a thing set.** The second witness to the chain is **Dace Millward, who is not his brother and not a clerk**; the second place-holder is the woman upstairs; and the yard holds the fact that a paper exists and cannot produce the paper, which is the correct division and is the first time the rule has been tested.

107. **The money, closed, and the cost named in somebody else's accounts.** **Aren Kest has eleven shillings and fourpence in a jar on a shelf** on the twenty-second of Longlight — the county's schedule at a shilling a day and a penny a mile, and a culvert at Four Elms at a shilling a day with the farmer's materials, less three weeks of eating; the two shillings for the room comes out of the cutting contract and not out of the jar. **Four marks is sixteen shillings, which is the anchor-glass, and he is four shillings and eightpence short of two discs, and there is no fresh Steppe glass north of Merefen and the Steppe road comes down in Frostmonth.** The figure has not moved in a fortnight and it is the smallest number in the book and it is not the point. **Millward's cost of lodging the chain, said out loud, is that his roll now has four things in it that are not tolls** — a suspension, a direction about correspondence, a town's charter and a chain of eleven sheets — **and a man in a counting house reads that roll once a year in Harvestmonth and has read it for eleven years and has never once stopped at a line, and this year he will stop at four, and the year after it will be three times, because that is what a book does: a book with three things in it that are not business has become a record, and a creditor does not want to be a record.** Tovan's figures, also closed: the wages on a load of nine tons are a fortnight and four days for four men at the yard rate and a boy at half, **two marks, thirteen shillings and sixpence**, against the factor's **four marks** on that load, and there are four more loads before the contract is done. **The Old Toll's maintenance is three marks and about six the campaign, twice a year, seven marks and no farthing**, and the figure in every conversation and in the bench's own resolution is **four marks and six a year**, because four is what goes in a book, and Tovan has caught himself rounding and has stopped.

108. **The batch's six-name cap: four spent, two unfilled.** **Dace Millward** (Ch 31, 35, 40), **Iven Sarrat** (Ch 31, 33), **Rowan Petch** (Ch 32, 35), **Harl Mounce** (Ch 39). All four have a want that is not Aren: a toll-holder who has never once had anybody want a page out of his roll; a factor who has to write *not known* to his house in the month of Longlight; a clerk who wants to have been careful once while somebody was watching; a barge mate who is out of work and has been paid, in four rooms, for saying a bigger thing than he heard. **Two slots left unfilled.** The toll-house steward's name at Gallow Ford was cut on review: the office enters that it does not have it, with the day and the hour and the reason. The guild's clerk, the guild's man with the levels, the salt merchant, the toll-clerk, Wenna Koll, Sarn Oyelaran, Jos Rusk, Reeve Aldis Fenn and Captain Rell are all addressed by office or by established name and cost nothing.

109. **What this batch deliberately did not do, recorded so a later writer does not "fix" it.** It had **no crossing in it, in either direction**; the Old Toll sill was touched twice, on the tenth and the twenty-second of Longlight, and **both times returned nothing**, written as deliberate, with no warmth, no fold in the air, no voice, no seam and no system display. **Volume 01's two Gatehouse lines were spent in Batch 0001 and none of the ten chapters spends a third**, and the phrase *Culling Order* appears nowhere in the block. It did not explain the bell, the YR 302 clearance entry, the removed ledger, the roadmark, the private-keeper clause, or why he can cross. **It did not resolve the second pier** — Ch 40's coda on the far side is a bench at the lowest water doing the ordinary promised marking of the twenty-sixth, with no Gatehouse event of any kind in it: two men and a clerk put a rule on the ring stone, the number is eight inches and was eight inches at the last marking, the woman who keeps that road keeps her hands open and nothing comes, and the only thing the far side spends the morning on is whether the road is a road or a door, which it puts off as the second question. It ends on **About nine people did.** and **the promise that fails in Block 5 has not been made yet and must be made on the page, in a hall, with a name attached to it.** It did not file the brine weights. It did not put the marshal's name in any main-world document before Chapter 38, and **the marshal's name appears in Merefen only in Chapter 38, in a signature on a Crown's letter, and is not entered in any file, press, roll or day-book anywhere in the volume.** It did not total the four thousand with the seven villages, and **the seven villages are not named or counted anywhere in this block** — the only reference in the manuscript is Chapter 19's *sum not stated* and the space left where an eighth figure would go, and Chapter 40 does not carry it — so the rule is held and untested. It did not print the two Hushwater figures together; Hushwater Reach is not in this block at all. It did not give Aren money, and it did not let him see, touch, or act on the report.

110. **Batch 0004 review repair pass — what changed and what did not.** A review of the landed batch returned thirty-eight findings; **twenty-nine were applied to Chapters 31–40 and the rest were recorded as nits.** **No plot beat was removed, no chapter was renumbered, and no controller file was touched.** The substantive changes: **the report of the twenty-first of Longlight was a main-world bridge and Chapter 40 was treating it as the far side** — that is now the chapter's crisis instead of its summary, and Ch 39's **fifth limit** states the two readings in the record itself so that the ambiguity is a fact in the file and not a narrator's slip; **the phrase *Culling Order* was on the page as a meta aside in Ch 38 and is gone**; **Ch 34's market was dated the fourth of Longlight, which is a Tuesday**, and is now the second, with two dependent chronologies moved; **Ch 35's return could not have been filed on the Tuesday before it was due on the Sunday**, and Petch's disclosure is now that the return was **late** — it came in on the eighth, a Saturday, and the writ was stamped at the second hour of the ninth **because** it was not in, which is a better mechanism and puts the Crown's third move where the chapter needed it; **Ch 38's memorandum dated the letter the eighth of Longlight instead of the eighteenth**; **Ch 36 said an instruction received the previous day had been executed in nine days, and that a letter was posted on the wrong day**; **Ch 36 had Aren claiming thirty-one years' experience at thirty-two**; **Ch 31 gave Millward nineteen years' tenure of tolls he had bought two years ago, and two figures for the winter half**; **Ch 33's wage bill did not add up and its maintenance figure contradicted the four marks and six used in four later chapters**; **the Chapter 40 chain said four pieces aloud and then had him write a fourth, and declared there was no fifth piece after calling the letter the fifth** — the chain is now three pieces said aloud, a fourth written afterwards in the room above the cooper and carried down in his coat, and the letter as the fifth that stays where it is; **Ch 40's coda is now marked as leaving Aren, has lost the Crown's figure of four thousand for the living city, and no longer spends Block 5's failed oath off-stage**; **Ch 34 now ends on the provincial form with the printed space rather than on a landscape**; **Ch 40's nine-paragraph closing inventory was cut by half** and the Chapter 31 opening echo removed; and the batch's money now agrees between Chapters 34 and 40 at **eleven shillings and fourpence** with the two shillings for the room coming out of the cutting contract. **Recorded rather than changed:** the Archive's income is now **four marks a year of the town's money** of which **eleven shillings is the second floor** (a pound is not Neyra coinage and three pounds of rent against eleven shillings was impossible); *"the middle of the year"* for the bench's marking day in the twenty-sixth of Longlight is **inherited phrasing from the Chapter 28 agreement** and is the bench's own phrase for its low water, and must not be changed in one block and not the other; **Lysa Fenn has no page in this block**, which is deliberate and is carried into Batch 0005 as an instruction.
 

## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 40

- **Aren and Mara:** the batch's real movement, and it is a reversal of the movement of Chapter 30. In Chapter 30 he told her what a crossing cost in the body and gave her a document; in this block **she priced being right for the first time in four months and said so out loud**, entered a claim against herself on her own account, made a second keeper, sent a town's charter out of her building to a creditor, told a watch captain a fact he could only get from her, and then **took a paper off a filing room table and put it into a press as a lending and listed it, in advance, in the answer she is going to give to a writ in a court on the ninth of Rainmonth** — which is the cost of being right the fifth time, and she named it before she agreed to it. **He did not show her the Crown's letter and she did not ask, and she has worked out that he has it and has written down the fact of its delivery and not its contents in the day-book with the hour on it, and the reason is a rule on her wall: a copy is a second thing that somebody can lose and a fact is a thing that can be looked for.** He came up her stair with four things and no paper and she told him that was the most careful thing anybody had done in that building in four years and then told him to go away and write the fourth one. They are not closer and they are not further. They are the only two people in this county who are both holding documents about the other.
- **Aren and Tovan:** the refusal is intact and has been given an institutional form. **The yard's rule now exists: nothing that belongs to a man goes in this yard's book, and only a thing set** — invented in a gateway in front of four men on the twenty-eighth of Greenmonth because of a stair rail, tested in Chapter 40, and the test produced the correct division, which is that the yard holds the *fact* that a paper exists and cannot produce the paper. Tovan has entered his brother's unasked handrail in the yard's own book, in his own hand, with a date, an hour and a rule over it, **and has not told his brother, and is not going to.** He has said the true thing in a public room and not voted, for the second time in five months, and has said that the man who says the true thing in this room has to live in this town. He has caught himself rounding a figure for four weeks to be believed sooner and has stopped. He has learned that his brother's freedom is the reason his gate has anything behind it and has stopped defending the gate. **The estrangement is not healed; it is two brothers holding the same two pages of the same book and neither of them has mentioned it.**
- **Aren and Captain Rell:** a working relationship that is neither an alliance nor a war, and it is the batch's most careful piece of craft. Rell is the Crown's man in this county and is frightened, correct and unrepentant, and has been shown to be right on every count by a Crown's own commendation he nailed up himself. He told Aren on a step in the rain that he had made a chain of custody unbroken on purpose, and he asked him for one thing in trade terms and got it. He asked him not to come up the street again, and then told him that every man standing still in this business is standing still because he went up, and that he is the first man Rell has met who can see that about himself, and that he has no idea what to do with it. **They will meet again and it will be about a form, and neither of them is going to enjoy it.**
- **Aren and Edric Hunn:** **the devotion is over and it ended in a letter and not in an argument**, because the letter was accurate and the accuracy came from a list with no names in it and the list came from the Archive's own method, and because the one generous sentence in it — *you were the best man in the room, and that is a load-bearing fact* — is the reason a valley died, said kindly, by the man who used the room. He wrote back in the trade's terms with a date, a fact and a condition and no refusal and no defence and not one name. **He is now known to the office in a shape that fits him exactly, and what the office has stopped asking for is whether he will stop, and what it has started asking for is who else he has told.**
- **Aren and Bellweather-Living:** **thirteen weeks, and not one word, in either direction, and the silence has become an account rather than a mystery.** There is a mark in a diary that will be taken on Wednesday at the lowest water by two men and a clerk, and a woman who keeps that road will write in a book that he was not there, and there is a crack in a ring four days off that is either eight inches or eleven or neither, and a report that may be about a bridge four hundred miles the other way, and the seat has been used twice, and there is no fresh Steppe glass north of Merefen before Frostmonth. **He has a third crossing of the year in him and four shillings and eightpence, and the crossing is still being saved, and the reason it is still being saved is that a chain of four dated pieces is in a creditor's chest and a press and a yard's book, and nobody in Bellweather-Living knows that any of it exists.**
- **Mara and the Archive's own position:** she is a party under a writ, she has a second keeper, she has a lending she must disclose, she has a claimant's name in a provincial file, and she has begun to price being right. **She has also, for the first time in four years, been wrong in public about a thing that is her whole life** — the town has attached a report about a bridge in another world to her office through a joke, and the only correction available to her is a document nobody reads, and she has entered the fact that she does not know and the hour at which she decided not to ask.
- **Reeve Aldis Fenn and Lysa Fenn:** the reeve has called an extraordinary bench on a Saturday and got a resolution out of it, and has minuted that she did not vote because a reeve does not vote on the guild's own matter and has wanted to for nine years, and has told the crown clerk to come in a room and be right. **Lysa Fenn has no page in this block at all**, and her finding of the fourth of Greenmonth is carrying more weight than any document in the county, and the reeve's minute and the magistrate's minute still do not agree and were written three days apart.
- **Mara and Sarn Oyelaran:** Sarn is now a **keeper of records with a key and a warrant in the day-book**, and she refused to write the one sentence the whole case wants, out loud, in a room, and gave the reason in trade terms. She is the person the office is beginning to need rather than use.
- **Mara and Jos Rusk:** he has his name in a provincial file as an attendee, twice, in his own name, and she told him before anybody else and did not apologise. He is not grateful and is not going to be, and he has said that he will not tell the five ferrymen until winter and will write to her first with a date, and that a paper cannot stop what he is about to do, and that this is the argument for it.


## Batch 0005 pre-write resolution — the Fallowmonth chain, entered before the prose and not to be drifted

111. **Longlight runs to the thirtieth, as Mudmonth and Greenmonth do, and Fallowmonth opens on the first.** Carried forward from the audited chain in item 93 (**the twenty-sixth of Longlight is a Wednesday**, which is the lowest water of the year, the bench's marking day, and the day the third crossing is pledged to) and from **Chapter 40, which is a Saturday on the twenty-second of Longlight**. The chain is therefore closed: **Longlight 22 Sat, 23 Sun, 24 Mon, 25 Tue, 26 Wed, 27 Thu, 28 Fri, 29 Sat, 30 Sun. Fallowmonth 1 Mon, 2 Tue, 3 Wed, 4 Thu, 5 Fri, 6 Sat, 7 Sun, 8 Mon, 9 Tue**, and the rest of the month on the same frame (**10 Wed … 16 Tue, 17 Wed, 18 Thu, 19 Fri, 20 Sat, 21 Sun, 22 Mon, 23 Tue, 24 Wed, 25 Thu, 26 Fri, 27 Sat, 28 Sun, 29 Mon, 30 Tue**). **Fallowmonth the second is a Tuesday** — the day he comes up out of the Old Toll sill, and the day the second pier of the great arch goes. **The first Friday of Fallowmonth is the fifth**, which is the yard's factor-money day in the new month. **Do not drift this.** Path-side weekdays are the path's own and the two calendars are not one clock: the far side's own day-names may be used in a Bellweather scene and a report that carries one of them into Merefen is therefore *wrong about the day* while being right about the night, which is the batch's fourth report and its last pull.

112. **The third fever's dates, resolved.** The established pattern is fixed twice: **item 25** and **Chapter 30** — *begins on the night of a day, breaks on the third day, three days, never a fourth.* First: night of the twenty-sixth of Mudmonth, three days, over on the thirtieth. Second: night of the fifteenth of Greenmonth, three days, broke on the eighteenth at the sixth hour. **Third: he comes up out of the sill at first light on the second of Fallowmonth, the fever runs the second, third and fourth, and it breaks on the fifth of Fallowmonth at about the sixth hour.** The Batch 0005 prompt's phrase *the twenty-second to the twenty-fourth* is a **slip in the prompt** for the second to the fourth of Fallowmonth and is rejected, for two reasons: Chapter 40 is a Saturday on the twenty-second of Longlight and he was in Merefen on that day, and Chapter 49 opens on the ninth of Fallowmonth, which requires the fever to be four days over. **Three days is the figure, on every page.**

## Canon established by Batch 0005 (Chapters 41–50, prose on the page)

113. **The day map of the block, in both clocks, and it is now closed.** Main world: Ch 41–42 the **twenty-sixth of Longlight, a Wednesday**; Ch 43–44 the twenty-seventh of Longlight to the **second of Fallowmonth**; Ch 46 the crossing at the fourth hour of the **first of Fallowmonth** and the return at the first light of the **second**, a Tuesday; Ch 47 the second of Fallowmonth, the night of the first into it and the morning after; Ch 48 the second to the **ninth of Fallowmonth**; Ch 49 the ninth to the twenty-ninth; Ch 50 the twenty-second to the **twenty-ninth**, the last week of Fallowmonth. Weekdays (item 111): **26 Longlight Wed · 27 Thu · 28 Fri · 29 Sat · 30 Sun · 1 Fallowmonth Mon · 2 Fallowmonth Tue · 9 Tue · 22 Mon · 24 Wed · 27 Sat · 28 Sun · 29 Mon.** **Path side, Ch 41–47:** the arrival is the **twenty-sixth of Longlight, YR 312, and the chapters count days of being there.** The scour was read on the **twenty-seventh**; the second examination, the offer of the bench seat and Merrow's third want on the **twenty-eighth and twenty-ninth**; **the oath renewed at the tenth hour of the twenty-ninth**, the bench-clerk's question at the ninth hour of the **thirtieth**; the scheduled crossing on the **first of Fallowmonth**; the narrower oath on the **second**. The city's six months and twenty days is a figure in a notebook and in two wrappers, not a calendar, and **no path-side chapter may date itself by a main-world day after the arrival.**

114. **The drift, stated on the page as a warning that was wrong and not a surprise, and not to be widened.** The road crews' saying is three days out and three back. **The Gatehouse's own figure for a held road, given to him twice and never in writing, is three weeks.** He was out **six days** in this world's calendar and **six months and twenty days** inside. **The warning was three weeks and it was out by three weeks, and the three weeks is on this side and not on his:** a bridge in that city was shut on a Wednesday night and the word of it came down Weir Street on the twelfth of Fallowmonth and could not say which night. **Three weeks is the Volume 01 ceiling (item 29, `outline/series.md`) and the batch does not exceed it. Do not drift by months in any later volume without a new ledger entry.**

115. **The third fever's dates, and the pattern is now closed.** Begins on the night a crossing returns; **three days; never a fourth.** First: night of the twenty-sixth of Mudmonth, over on the thirtieth. Second: night of the fifteenth of Greenmonth, broke on the eighteenth at the sixth hour. **Third: night of the first of Fallowmonth, the second, third and fourth, broke on the fifth at about the sixth hour.** The prompt's *twenty-second to the twenty-fourth* was a slip and is rejected (item 112).

116. **The two-disc seat took its third use on the afternoon of the twenty-sixth of Longlight and failed, and the glass is gone.** The pin went, the bed of lime was crushed to gravel, and a wagoner prised the two used discs out on the twenty-seventh and carted them away and said they were broken, and Tovan Kest believed him because they were broken. **There is no third seat in this county and there will not be one, and the Old Toll anchor from the twenty-sixth onward is a dressed stone and forty yards of tarred hemp through the abutment on a leather splice cut out of a cooper's bin, set on a bed of lime and oak dust.** Quarren's page stands: *a scar takes two and three is not a number he would sell.* **The answer to "somebody else has to pay for it" is that there is no third seat; the road is a line and two hands, and the bench paid for the ordinary work out of its own chest as a fact and not a paper.** The bench's maintenance specification is **two leaves** and its **eighth line** — in the guild's man with the levels' own hand — is *that a seat so cut will be used and will fail, and that the number of uses is not stated here, because it is not a thing this bench may know.*

117. **The road is a line and two hands, and a stitch is a person and not a page.** Two ends of a tarred line, a date, a hand at each end, and a witness at each end who is not the person keeping the road. **If the witness is not there the road does not open, and it does not fail, and nobody is to say afterwards that it failed** — that distinction is Sera Quen's and it is the volume's whole argument in one sentence. The three terms at the fourth hour of the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth, off a slate, and **the only System display in Batch 0005:** *Road, held: one. Carryover: one, a hand's habit and not a working. Return: made before every crossing, by two hands and a date, and a witness at each end who is not the man who keeps it, or it does not open. Answer: the far side decides; you cannot make a city answer; that is the term and it is not a defect.* **Nobody in Volume 01 learns why he can cross.** His roadmark, the warmth in the sill, and the drift are facts he reports and cannot explain, and he says so out loud on a bridge in Ch 49.

118. **A Bind is a trade working, not a charm, and the volume's failure is in that sentence.** A man who knows a load path can put his intent into a material and the material will take it, which is why four generations of men have sworn things into stone. **A Bind puts the load where the load path says it goes, and the load path of the great arch on the night of the first of Fallowmonth went into the second pier, and the second pier was standing on a shelf of gravel with two notches in it, and it took nine feet of crack in one line.** He stopped in the second minute. **The only sentence in the notebook about it: *I did not take it out because it was going to fail. I took it out because it was mine.*** No supporting character was killed. **A hull-hand of the Hushwater cooperative is hurt at the fourth rib and a stranger nobody in the city can account for is hurt across the back and the shoulder; both live; both are carried to the abbey; both names are in the bench-clerk's book, and the stranger's is the only document in that world that holds it.** The stranger is **unnamed on the page and named in the book**, and the man with the mallet refuses to name the cause in a hall.

119. **The scour, the cutwater, the oath, and the narrower oath — the whole of the block's engineering, now fixed.** The **second pier** stands on a shelf of rock eleven paces long at the top and about four at the water, entered in a book in the hall since before the flood of the second year. At the lowest water of the year there is a **scour** on the river side: four feet across, two feet deep, eleven feet of it under the footing, **four and a half tons of stone in the river two hundred yards downstream making a bar that will be a town's problem in four years.** The honest repair is a **new cutwater: nine months of the bench's whole capacity** against a debt on the arch pledged to the ninth of a grandson — **and the debt is hollow, because the writ of severance released the tolls of the arch for the term of the severance, the severance was refused and the writ withdrawn on the twenty-second of Mudmonth, YR 301, and a withdrawn writ releases what it tendered, so every pledge made against those tolls after YR 301 was made against nothing.** That fact was read out in a hall in Batch 0003 and is now said again by Merrow because there is a hole in the river and a creditor has a grandson of nine. **The oath of the crossing is four lines, is a hundred and forty years old, and is a promise about who answers, not about a stone:** *held; the arch shall bear; the tolls, rents and dues shall be taken and kept to the uses of the crossing; the stewards shall answer for it and shall answer first.* Renewed at the tenth hour of the twenty-ninth of Longlight before two hundred persons, with the clause *the bench has not been able to name a date by which the promise will be discharged and is asked by no person to name one.* **The narrower oath, made on the fourth camp's drying floors on the second of Fallowmonth in six hours and nine sides of minute, is four lines and is a promise about a book, about who answers, and about a door:** mark the crossing twice a year at the two low waters and read the mark back and enter it; where a ring, a pier, a cutwater or a footing has moved, or where the bed of the river has moved, **say so in a hall, with the number, the hour and the name of the man who read it, before doing anything else**; **do not hold a promise about a stone and release every person in the city who has been asked for one**; and the stewarding is held by **a named person at each end of the road, and when those two cannot both be there the crossing does not open.** Two of its lines are Sera Quen's. The repair is **stood in the open** — forty tons of stone out of the river bed on a barge, in daylight, in front of about two hundred people on the bank.

120. **The two stewards, the one witness each, and the channel.** **Bellweather-Living: Sera Quen, ferrywright, and the man with the mallet, a bridgewright of about sixty on that bench for thirty-one years**, who put their names at the foot of a sheet at the ninth hour of the first of Fallowmonth, in that order, neither signing the other's line, answering the bench-clerk's question of the thirtieth. **Merefen: Tovan Kest, carpenter, who is not the man who cut the seat.** **Independent witnesses: Reeve Aldis Fenn at the Merefen end, and the toll-clerk on the north steps at the far end**, who refused twice and gave the reason the second time — *a man you know is not a witness; a man you have never met is.* The day is written at the head of a book on the north bank, in the roll of the Merefen bridge guild, in the day-book of a filing room over a salt merchant, and in two slates. **The first thing across it is a toll dispute about nine tons of paving stone**, argued in two languages for eleven minutes on the twenty-fourth of Fallowmonth and settled on **line ninety-one of a crossing book**: *a cart on wheels is not a man, and a man who comes up the steps is not a cart.* **It is a dispute and not a treaty, and about nine people in Merefen were told afterwards that it was about a load of paving stone and did not believe it and were right not to.**

121. **The bell's return, and the authority for it — the town reclaims its own iron and nobody gives it permission.** **Captain Rell carried it out himself on the twenty-fourth of Fallowmonth at the fourth hour, in daylight, in front of about three hundred people, with two men of the watch and a barrow, making the chain of custody on the roll and reading it out as he did it**, on three grounds, all of them the town's: (1) the bed of the stone at the abutment is a **defect in a parapet** upon a crossing the bench is keeping in repair at its own cost by a resolution of the fifteenth of Longlight, and a repair is not an assembly in respect of the object; (2) the iron is carried on the **bench's own scrap roll of the eleventh of Mudmonth at six marks as unclassified municipal salvage, no mark of ownership entered, no person claiming**; (3) **no office has produced any authority for the taking of it in eleven months, the asking being dated the fourth of Greenmonth and unanswered**, and the Crown's own finding of that date found the custody lawful and **did not find the metal to be anybody's.** Rell's own four lines are in a county file and end: *if any officer of any office above this county thinks that wrong, he may come and take it off this bridge, and I will put it in his hands myself, and I will say in a room that I did it.* He was not instructed and was not asked; the bench's minute records that he was invited to dispute it at the ninth hour on the twenty-third and did not come. **The band is the town's work** — iron off a gate-post at the end of Weir Street, six inches wide, forty-one rivets, set by the guild's man with the levels, and his sentence is *it'll hold as long as the crack does, and the crack is not a crack, it's a split.* **The old split is re-sealed with dull red oathlight from both sides at the same hour by the Bellweather-Living steward's hands on the far side of the scheduled crossing**, not by Aren, who was not on that deck and cannot be, and the trade's rule is that the sealing takes two hands of one trade and the word put into it is the word a bench uses when it says it will answer first. **Nothing in the bell breaks again in this volume. It is rung by a boy of fifteen, the steward's apprentice at the Merefen end, and by the ferrywright at the far end, and it is not Aren's and is not his to ring.**

122. **The claim, the name, and the eighth ruled line.** The claim was entered on the **seventh of Fallowmonth at the eleventh hour, against nobody, on the office's own account**, and it is **the eighth claim number this office has entered in four years** (the seventh was the sixteenth of Greenmonth, also against nobody). **The name of Edric Hunn is on a separate sheet under a separate number with no opinion attached, and the reason for the separation is written once: a claim and a name may be read together by a court that wants to and by nobody else; a keeper who enters a name and a claim in one sentence has made the name a party, and a party can be served.** Four places: the press (a custody), the toll-holder's chest (a lending, eleven sheets, the day and the hour in his own hand), the yard's book (a fact and not a paper, with a rule over it), **and a lock-up on a canal wharf in Harvestmonth, entered as a transaction and not a favour, at a farthing of salt, because a document left with a person is a thing a person can be arrested for and an office can only be closed.** **The writ of the ninth of Longlight was discharged on the twenty-eighth of Fallowmonth on an endorsement, at the second hour, in a form, and the office's answer is in a county file two hundred miles off with no blank in it.**

123. **The villages, the eighth line, and the four thousand.** Served on the eighth of Fallowmonth **by name, in the county's spelling, with each figure separately** — Alder Cross two hundred, Saltbrim ninety, Wrenlow sixty, Cobbett a hundred and forty, Nine Elms a hundred and ten, Stonebarrow forty, Hushwater eighty — and **`sum not stated` in the total line, and the form is ruled for eight places and the eighth place in the Crown's finding of the second of Greenmonth is the city, so the eighth line is ruled and empty and the reason for leaving it empty is entered under the total and was copied in the hand of a county boy of nineteen who asked whether it would still be on the sheet in a year.** **It is still empty at the end of the volume and Mara Vey notices it on the twenty-ninth and writes down that she looked at it and wanted to fill it.** The Crown's four thousand is the Crown's and is never printed beside the seven figures and is not added to them. **Hushwater Reach's nearest estimate in the path is near sixty and the erased Hushwater in this county is eighty, and the two were never printed on the same page and no character noticed the gap.**

124. **The batch's discipline, recorded so a later writer does not "fix" it.** **The phrase "Culling Order" appears nowhere in Chapters 41–50** and may not appear again in this volume. **No Gatehouse Order member is named and Sabin Dain does not appear.** **Volume 01's two Gatehouse lines were spent in Batch 0001 and none of these ten chapters spends a third. The word "Gatehouse" appears once in the batch — `chapter-0047.md`, in a notebook entry, giving the Order's own figure for a held road as a standard of measurement — and that is a measurement and not an appearance; a later phase auditing this must not read the zero as a claim about the word.** **Exactly one System display in the batch** (the slate in Ch 49) and no warmth, no fold, no voice and no seam is displayed anywhere; the warmth of the sill is a physical fact reported by a man who cannot explain it. **The brine weights are cited in the claim and are not filed and are not an exhibit, and there are now two lines about them in the day-book, four lines apart, on the second and the seventh of Fallowmonth, and the pair is a thread and the roll built out of them is Chapter 892's.** **The batch's names: no new name at all, and four unfilled slots of the six-name cap** — a correction recorded in the Batch 0005 review pass, because three files had asserted otherwise. **Edric Hunn is not a new name: he is first named in `chapter-0026.md`, he is a signature on the Crown's letter from Chapter 38, and the batch-0005 prompt itself calls him already established.** What the batch gave him is **his first page and his first room**: spoken in a room, entered in a file, and argued over for the first time in this volume, at the county court on the twenty-seventh of Fallowmonth, on his own request, in a room with a clerk in it. **No character is named who was not already named in Chapters 1–40** — a hull-hand of the Hushwater cooperative who is hurt in Ch 47 is addressed by trade and is not named, the stranger who is hurt is named in a book in another world and not on the page, and the man with the mallet, the salt merchant, the sett-cutter and the toll-clerk on the north steps are all addressed by trade. **Write it this way: Volume 01 added no new names in its last batch, and its total across five batches is four of the six-name cap unfilled on the final block.** **The Crown's office has stopped asking Aren to stop and has started asking who else he has told, and the answer is refused in a minute.**

125. **The coda of Chapter 50, on the page, and it is the volume's last image.** **On the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth, in the last hour of the low water, a man is on the Sedge Reach deck with a tool bag at the fourth course counting load points out of habit** — nine under the first bay, eleven under the second, the hour and the state of the deck in a notebook — **and he does not go to the Old Toll, four hundred yards up the road, which he has not looked at since the twenty-fourth.** At about the fifth hour, with the tide making, **a note goes out of that county and it is not a good note** — a bell off its note since the third of a long-ago year — **and it is answered inside a second and a half and the answer comes back through the rain.** **Nine people in a lane off the Sedge Reach approach heard the two of them together and came for something else and stayed for the sound.** **The bell is rung by a boy of fifteen on the near abutment, the steward's apprentice at the Merefen end, and by a woman with iron dust in the creases of her hands on the far end, and it is not Aren's and he does not ring it. His left palm was warm — the fourth time in five months, on a day that is not the twenty-sixth of Longlight and not the second of Fallowmonth.** **Nothing about this is displayed, explained, or displayed to him; it is a physical fact on the page, and the man in it cannot say why and has stopped looking for anyone to say it to.** **The verse's second hole is in the same notebook and is a bigger one: two notes have become one and the one that is left is the higher, and the higher is the one Ilyra used to end on, and he had it in his head for nine months and did not know it was there until about the fourth hour of the second of Fallowmonth in a room above a cooper with a fever on him.** His mother Ilyra's fate remains intentionally unresolved and is not touched.

## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 50

- **Aren and Mara:** the batch's other real movement, and it is a disclosure and not a document. **On the twenty-second of Fallowmonth at the seventh hour, in a filing room over a salt merchant, with a second keeper present, he put his left hand flat on the table, palm up, which he had not done in front of anybody in eleven years, and said the one thing about himself he has never said to her** — that he has crossed three times, that he could not say why if he tried, that he looked for two days in a lane like a man looking for a job, and that on the morning of the second of Fallowmonth the stone of the fourth course of the Old Toll sill was warm to the hand and has been three times in five months and never once in Mudmonth. **She wrote what he said and not one word more, read it back and made him listen to it, entered it as his statement and not her observation under the second rule and not the first, and then wrote four lines of her own under it and made him listen to those as well** — the last of which is that this office is not going to ask him again, and that the not asking is entered so that in eleven years nobody will be able to say she took it out of a man who was ill. **She asked him one question and not two**, and it was *if it costs you something every time, and it costs more the better you get at it, then who pays it, and is the answer still you*, and he said yes, and added that in about a year it will stop being him and he intends to be a tradesman at a rate of pay and worth four times what he is worth now. **He told nobody else, and there is a line in a notebook in a coat on the twenty-fourth of Fallowmonth that says so and says why.** In Chapter 50 he refuses a shield for her office **on her behalf and with her not in the room**, and does not take it himself: *Give it to the bench, in a form, with a number on it, and let her enter it against no person in her own hand.* **She has wanted that shield since the ninth of Longlight, has not asked for it, and is not going to.** They are not closer and they are not further. **They are the only two people in this county who can each be held to a document about the other, and there is now one in a filing room that says he cannot say why he crosses, entered on his word alone and on no instrument at all.**
- **Aren and Tovan:** the yard's rule held, it produced the third place, and the batch turned the whole arrangement over. **On the morning of the second of Fallowmonth Tovan Kest came the last eight yards on foot and got his brother down off a staging, and then did the thing the yard's rule exists for**: he opened the yard's book on the parapet, flat on the stone, upside down because that was the correct way round, and **read out loud in daylight what was in it, in his own hand, with a date and an hour — that the deck came up a quarter of an inch and set down on the other side of itself and that the moving and the setting down were two movements and not one, and that the two pieces of glass are not in the seat and there is nothing to throw.** He said he had written nothing else and was not going to write anything else, and that there is no Crown's mark and no paper in his book and one road in it, and that he wrote that road in Greenmonth because his brother came down Weir Street and told him where he was going and did not ask him for anything. **He asked no question about a city four hundred miles off, or a page, or a woman on a line, or a bench that swore an oath about a stone five days before the stone came down, and he carried him to a cart, and neither of them said another word for any of it, which the chapter calls the correct amount.** In Chapter 46 he held the near end of the line with a reeve at his shoulder; in Chapter 48 he was in the cart because his brother could not be got down the stairs and he told a ferrywright four hundred miles off: *It is holding. I have got one end of it in my hand.* **He is now the Merefen steward of the channel, and he is not the man who cut the seat and does not want to be, and the woman on the north bank entered that in her book in case anybody comes in a year and says the north sent somebody.** The estrangement is not healed. It is a brother who read his own book out loud in daylight and then did not ask.
- **Aren and Captain Rell:** a working relationship that is neither an alliance nor a war, and the batch's most careful piece of craft, and **Rell has the volume's best scene in it in the last week of Fallowmonth.** He answered the third question and then the fourth in order — *the order* — and did not argue with either, and the fourth answer is that a Crown's letter naming an office went out on a post on the morning a bed of stone was full of people in another world. **On the twenty-fourth he carried the bell out himself, in daylight, in front of about three hundred people, with two men of the watch and a barrow, on three grounds that are all the town's, and made the chain of custody on the roll while he did it and read it out as he did it, and he was not instructed and was not asked, and he was invited to dispute it on the twenty-third and did not come.** His own four lines are in a county file and offer, in writing, to be the man who takes the iron off the bridge if any officer above the county thinks the whole thing wrong. **He filled in the column, he was right on every count, and he is the reason the bell is on a bridge in this county.** They will meet again and it will be about a form, and neither of them is going to enjoy it.
- **Aren and Edric Hunn:** **the devotion ended in a letter in Longlight; what replaced it in Fallowmonth is that two men who are each right have been in a room with a clerk in it, twice, and the second time it was a list of four things on a table.** He came on the twenty-seventh with a minute and asked to be recorded, and offered rank, eleven years of money, a legal shield for the only office in this county that has ever answered a question put to it, and a writ of production into a city of four thousand people. **He refused it in ninety seconds, item by item, on the record, in his own words, and took one piece of it and only one**: the money, **as a wage on a schedule with a date, and not as back pay, and he will not let anybody in this county call it back pay.** The shield was taken and not for himself — *give it to the bench, in a form, with a number on it.* The inspection was refused with the city's losses read out in a room. **The request for the list of those he has spoken to was refused in the same minute in the same words: the offices, the dates and the numbers, and not one name.** Edric said in the doorway that he has been refused by a county, by a trade, and by a man better at the thing he trained him in than he was in the year he trained him, and that he has been right about all three, and that it was the first time in a year anything he had done was right and cheap. **Rowan Petch wrote that down and would not leave it out and was thanked for it in a corridor in plain words, which is the only thank you that office has ever given anybody.** The Crown has stopped asking whether he will stop. It is asking who else he has told, and the answer is on a record two hundred miles off.
- **Aren and Bellweather-Living:** **a bench of nine offered him a seat, a wage out of the guild's own box and the fourth camp, and he refused in a hall in eleven seconds, and the reason the man with the mallet gave for the refusal is the best reason anybody has given him for anything in the volume**: a foreign man who cuts setts on the fourth camp is a foreign man on the fourth camp, and a bench is a thing that is pointed at and not a thing that is pointed with. **He put a bench's own question into its own minute — *does this bench hold the arch, or does it not?* — as a question with a date and a man on it, and declined to answer it, because nine seconds of a stranger's arithmetic in a city is a thing that goes into a market in six weeks.** He spent the night of the collapse in the rain on a scaffold with nineteen men he had not met, and the stone came down on him and on a hull-hand's rib and a stranger's back. **The narrower oath he was given credit for refusing to promise is four lines about a book and about who answers and about a door, it is in nine sides of minute on a drying floor, two of its lines are the ferrywright's, and it releases every person in that city who has ever been asked for a promise about a stone.** **He is not the mechanism, he translates, the far side decides and you cannot make a city answer, and that is the term and not a defect.** He has said out loud on a bridge in daylight that he knows why the fourth course opens when it opens and that there is nobody in this kingdom he could say it to, and that this is not a mystery any more. **It is a trade.**
- **Mara and the Archive's own position:** she is the first publicly accountable person in this county and she is also the only person in it who is in a provincial file by name. **A claim entered against nobody on the seventh, the name on a separate sheet under a separate number with the reason for the separation written once, four places, the writ discharged on the twenty-eighth on an endorsement in a form, the answer in a county file two hundred miles off with no blank in it.** A second form with a column headed *Keeper* and no date on it **answered with a number instead of four names**, and the reason written out in full.
