The brine weights **cited and not filed, and the two day-book lines about them are four lines apart.** The eighth ruled line in a served schedule is the city's and **it is still empty, and she looked at it on the twenty-ninth and wrote down that she wanted to fill it, and that line is the last line in the day-book in the year and the only one in four years with nothing against it and no reason on it.** **She has not been asked for the shield and refused one; she has been handed one by somebody else who refused it on her behalf and gave it to a bench in a form, and she is going to have to enter it against no person, and she knows that.**
- **Reeve Aldis Fenn and Lysa Fenn:** **two minutes in the same county court, four days apart, and neither of them has conceded a point.** Lysa Fenn read the four documents together in her own room on the sixth, at the tenth hour, with five people in it, **and she chose the five herself, and the choosing is the chapter**; she made the reading go past the end of each document, because the end of each document is the only part anybody defends; she put the three questions and then the fourth, which was the order; and she entered the Crown's two instruments lawful on the ninth, in four lines, in nine minutes, on the record of that court for ever, for the next woman who sits there in nine years. **In the marshal's room on the twenty-seventh she said the sentence she said in Greenmonth, in the exact procedure, and this time it was not procedural.** The reeve has called an extraordinary bench on a Saturday and got a resolution out of it and **does not vote on the guild's own matter and has wanted to for nine years**; she held a sheet and a rule at Toban's shoulder at the fourth hour of the first of Fallowmonth; and she is now the independent witness at the Merefen end of a channel, having said yes once and meant it.
- **Mara and Sarn Oyelaran:** Sarn is the second keeper who was in the room on the twenty-second when Aren made his statement, and **she carried a true copy of the writ itself down Weir Street on foot in a plain wrapper with no seal on it at all, because a piece of Crown paper in a yard is a piece of Crown paper in a yard**, and the price of that delivery is the yard's book getting a Crown instrument in it. **She is the person the office needs rather than uses, and the batch gave her the thing to refuse: she will not write the one sentence the whole case wants, out loud, in a room, and she gives the reason in trade terms.**
- **Mara and Jos Rusk:** **he has no page in this block and that is deliberate.** His five ferrymen were to be told in winter and he was to write to her first with a date, and **nothing in Fallowmonth moved him, and the writ's answer in a county file two hundred miles off is the first thing in eleven years that has arrived at that office on somebody else's initiative, and it is not hers, and she knows the difference.**

## Canon established by the Volume 01 close (Chapters 1–50 read as a finished book; the Volume 02 outline is built on this)

**How to use this section.** Items 1–125 above are the record of the prose. Items **126–146** are the record of what the *volume* turned out to be, and they are what `outline/volume-02.md` is written against. The volume-close document itself is `outline/volumes/volume-01-close.md`; the read is not duplicated here, only its findings. **Nothing in 126–146 may be contradicted by a later volume, and nothing in it re-derives an earlier item.**

126. **The volume-02 ledger figures, and the reading of the series time ledger.** `outline/series.md`'s rows are **per-volume main-world elapsed, not cumulative**: the eighteen rows sum to one hundred and forty-three months, which is the twelve-year span the series states. **Volume 01 is five months (the sixth of Mudmonth to the last week of Fallowmonth, YR 312). Volume 02 is seven months of main world and runs the first of Embermonth, YR 312, to the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, YR 313 — two hundred and eight days, just under seven months.** The **longest path interval is nine months and six days in the County of Ash**, preceded by an interval of **twenty-three days**. Two crossings: main-world absences of **twenty-six days** and **eighty-eight days**, one hundred and fourteen days together, so **the volume is more than half absence.**

127. **The drift for Volume 02, costed, and the drift for Volume 01 is not to be contradicted.** Volume 01's ceiling: a Gatehouse figure of **three weeks** given twice and never in writing, out by **three weeks** on the twenty-sixth of Longlight, with the three weeks stated on the page in Chapter 47's notebook as a warning that was wrong and not a surprise. **Volume 02 writes that figure down for the first time, printed in a marginal instruction of the Office of Provincial Registration, which cannot say whose figure it is, and the figure does not change: three weeks.** Crossing A: absence twenty-six days, error **five days**. Crossing B: absence eighty-eight days, error **sixty-seven days**. **The warning has not failed; the man has, and the error scales with the work, exactly as the cost sheet of the sixteenth of Greenmonth said it would.** The road crews' saying of three days each way is unchanged. **The error ratio is about three-quarters of the absence, not a constant. A later volume may widen it; a Gatehouse warning may not silently be wrong by months.**

128. **The County of Ash is a different kind of community from Bellweather-Living and the difference is structural, not a change of scenery.** A clay county on a river called the Kiln, eight villages and a market town, **Kilnholt**, seat of the assizes. **Nine hundred and forty-one hearths on the roll and two thousand four hundred and fifty people; two thousand, nine hundred and six tiles fired on the roll to date — four hundred and fifty-six more tiles than there are people, and somebody has been fired twice, and nobody has ever counted, because a county counts fires and does not count itself.** **The population is exact and printed, because Chapter 61 is a subtraction done out loud in a kiln shed: 2,906 − 2,450 = 456, and an approximate population cannot be subtracted from anything.** It has **no bell and never had one, because nothing was ever refused**; it has no word for *refused* that does not also mean *lost*; its roadkeeper is called **the hand** and is a tile-cutter; a road in Ash is edged in fired tile and the kerb is a record. **Its founding act is the decision to fire everything, made in YR 308, four years after the severance, and that act protected the county for nineteen years and is the disease.** **No Ash figure is ever summed with a Bellweather figure, and the three Ash quantities — hearths, people, tiles — are never added together.**

129. **The softening, and its physician's finding, and what the finding is for.** The county's word: **a person is going back to being clay**, losing the order of their day, then their trade, then their hearth, then their own name. A soft person cannot be witnessed by anybody including themselves, and Ash's law requires two witnesses to stay on the roll. **Hesper Lound's finding, four years old and ignored: the softening travels from a mark to the person who reads it and from a person to the marks of that person's household, and fastest along the marks that are read most, and the marks read most are the roll marks, because a clerk reads the roll every morning. The cure is therefore not fewer records; the cure is records that do not agree with each other.** Three copies, three hands, three places, none authoritative alone, a person restored when two agree and removed when two agree otherwise. **This is a medical finding, not a moral argument, and it is the seed of the resolution and the answer to the volume's question.**

130. **The List of Persons to be Struck, and the rule about it.** About two hundred names, eleven months old, kept under the county's own law by the rollkeeper, and the offer is to **burn** it in the market square at the assizes in front of witnesses, because a struck person cannot be read and a person who cannot be read cannot carry the softening. **The instrument is legal, signed by three magistrates, offered by a decent man who is right about the fever, and it is a cull administered as a kindness. No character in Volume 02 may call the magistrate a villain and no character may be able to say he is wrong about the fever.**

131. **A struck person in Ash is struck from the count, not from the roll, and the difference between those two things is the county's whole moral life.** A struck person is alive, employed, and **is not consulted**: cannot witness, cannot be witnessed, cannot be married into a hearth, cannot be buried in the county's ground, cannot be named at a kiln. **Nobody in the county can state the difference.** Ostry Vaile's sentence, which is the volume's argument: *a person who is struck has to be able to answer for himself, and if he answers he goes back on it, and if he cannot answer then he was never on it and we have been keeping a list of things.* **He is right and has been right for eleven months, and nobody has put it in a form, because a form requires a column and the column is *struck* and there is no column in the world for *answered*.**

132. **The provincial office, and the form, and the clause — named, and the people in it are not.** **The Office of Provincial Registration**, of the Lantern Council, in the provincial town two hundred miles off. It holds the press registers, the fourth press, and a provincial file containing a woman's name. **Form R.C. 4, Return of Contact**, ruled columns: *Object*, *Date*, *Person having knowledge*, *Keeper*, *How often*. Undated on the copy that came. **A form with no date on it does not have to be answered and therefore does not have to be tried.** The marginal instruction on the reverse, in the Office's own hand: *where no office of keeper is found in the county schedule, the person having knowledge shall be entered under the column headed Keeper, and the entry shall not be construed as the creation of an office.* **This is the private-keeper clause on the page in an office's hand, and nobody in Volume 02 understands it, and Volume 02 may not let anybody understand it. Volume 03 is where Aren learns what it is being read as.** The same instruction carries **the three-week figure as a standard of measurement.** **The Crown's escalation in Volume 02 is in kind, not in paper: a return on the Archive's own record asking for a count of persons rather than names — lawful, no court, no party, and impossible to refuse without refusing a number. This is how a road gets closed without a keeper ever being named, and it is the answer to Edric's question, and it is made by Mara Vey, and nobody notices until Sabin Dain's three-line dated reply proposes a count for the county as a whole.** **Name the office and the form. Do not name the people in the office, with one exception: Sabin Dain, 33, an officer of that office whose title on her own form is an aid to the register, who is not a member of anything, and who wants that no family in this country is ever again removed from a record by a name nobody entered.**

133. **Sabin Dain's want, in her own voice, and the fact that she is Volume 02's mirror of Mara Vey.** *That no family in this country is ever again removed from a record by a name nobody entered.* She is disciplined, she gives reasons in the order she acts, and she is right about almost everything. **The volume says once on the page that she is a mirror and never again.** She has told no lie and offered no threat; she came because a form with no date on it does not have to be answered and her office has sent nineteen to this county and had an answer to none. **She is the vector, and the reader should be able to see it by Chapter 55.** **She is not a member of the Culling Order in this volume and uses no such phrase; she joins an order in Volume 04 and refuses the Charter in Volume 17, and the reader is given Volume 04's turn by her having been right in Volume 02 about unnamed things.** The phrase *Culling Order* is spent once in Volume 01 (Ch 12, Rusk's mouth) and appears **once more in Volume 02, in Ch 54, in Nell Tace's mouth, with no member attached, and nowhere else.**

134. **Halven's trade, landed.** The marshal's emergency office offers him **an endorsement on the same printed instruction that carries Form R.C. 4**, which discharges the writ of enquiry on the reeve's office and ends the question of the gap on the fourth press shelf. The price is the ledger. **He takes the shield and sells the *Anchors, schedules and dispositions, Alder Vale* to the Office of Provincial Registration — the volume that is not on the fourth press shelf, removed the nineteenth of Mudmonth YR 301, no name, no office, no authority.** The endorsement is dated, has a number, and **cannot be withdrawn, because the office that gave it has no procedure for withdrawing an endorsement and will not build one inside a quarter.** He discloses it to Mara unasked, in his own hand, in a form that cannot protect him, asking that the disclosure say he did it on his own motion and not on a fee; his want is *I want there to be one document in the world in which it says I did it*, which is what makes him viable in Volume 11 and Volume 18. **Nobody is charged and he keeps the post in Volume 02.**

135. **What the ledger contains, and it is a schedule and not an answer.** A schedule for **three bells on the Alder Vale crossing** — two entered removed with no office against either, the third entered *retained* — **and the retained one is the bell on the Old Toll abutment in Merefen.** And a schedule of dispositions whose county column runs past Alder Vale to **eleven other counties, eight of which are counties with a boundary oath on a stone**, and one of which is **Ash, with a disposition word that is the word the county of Ash uses for a person who has been pressed into a tile and fired. A Crown office did to Ash in YR 305 exactly what Ash is now doing to its own people, and nobody in Ash knows it, and nobody in the room in Merefen is sure what the word means.** **A Crown office did to Ash in YR 305 exactly what Ash is now doing to its own people. The ledger does not answer why anyone can cross and is not permitted to.**

136. **The second severance, four years later, in a county no one in Merefen could name, and what it turns out to be.** The writ register's index: **nine entries, eight of Alder Vale, one dated YR 305.** The column headed *returned* is **empty** for the ninth and a separate column headed *answered* carries a date **eleven days after the tender — a clerk's answer and not a river's.** **The writ at Alder Vale was refused. The writ at Ash was complied with, and the compliance is a document, and the document is in a book in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists.** The name is not on the index; the register does not leave the city; the four conditions say it is not produced in court and has not been asked for and will not be. **The ninth line carries press four, die eleven, and a later impression of the season — so the reader can check the hand by a press-mark and Aren cannot, because the index has no name.** The four people in two countries who can see it are **Alis Merrow, Aren Kest, Mara Vey, Rowan Petch.** **In Volume 02 the second severance does not open: it is entered by Alis Merrow into a record of his own counting house, in his own hand, at his own cost, in his own city, in a matter in which no court in Merefen has jurisdiction — the first document in the series held by a person in a path, and a creditor's press is the worst place in the world to keep a roll of persons and the only place where nobody can quietly take it out of the book, and he says so in a hall and asks that the argument be minuted where a Crown's man will find it in about nine years. The two halves are put side by side in Chapter 894.**

137. **The stage and the shape of the Second Door, as the terms the book gives them.** Two routes. **Two carryovers, and he cannot tell which path gave him which:** the hand's habit of reading a handrail in the dark (eleven years old, not a working), and **a way of standing** — weight slightly back, hands loose, a ferrywright on a slip — which he has been doing since the ninth of Embermonth, which Mara notices before he does, and which she does not ask about. **One message, and it must be a list, because a list can only be checked. One object, under a pound, and it must be something the far side can use and the near side can do without. And the second route does not answer when it is called: the Second Door carries no second schedule, the far side decides, and there is no procedure for a far side that has not decided.** So nine months of Ash come down on a named day, in a fair hand, being a list, **and he does not learn what happened on the day it happens.** At Stage 1 in Chapter 30 the same silence cost him eleven days and the book treated it as a mystery. **At Stage 2 it costs him the middle of his own case, and it is a choice: a route that answers on demand is a route somebody has to stand at a bridge for at all hours, and he is the somebody.**

138. **The costs of the second crossing, in the body, and the melody rule.** He loses **the exact sequence of an ordinary morning with Tovan Kest** — six or seven things in an order, a particular Tuesday, eleven years of it — and can do every one of them and cannot do them in that order and does not know the order, and **Tovan will not re-teach him his own brother and says so in a yard in his own words.** He cannot say which path gave him the stance. **The first-crossing loss of one verse's remembered pitch remains the only fixed melody cost in the series so far, and Volume 02 adds nothing to it. The verse's two holes are a wound and not a clue, and Ilyra is not named, not mentioned and not guessed at in Volume 02.**

139. **The relationship breach, and its instrument.** Working trust breaks because **he conceals the cost of the Second Door crossing and Mara finds it with a date**: he gives her two path dates in the main-world frame, in one morning, in a working room, and a bridgewright would not have noticed and a keeper of records cannot not. **She does not ask, because she wrote on the twenty-second of Fallowmonth that this office is not going to ask him again, and the not asking was a protection and is now a wound, and the reason she writes down is that the not asking has changed from a protection into a thing she is doing to him, entered in a day-book and not in a file, and she has noticed the change and has noticed it a year late.** **This is the series' Volume 2 relationship milestone and it is not resolved in Volume 02 and is not resolved in Volume 03; Volume 04's chosen intimacy is bought with it.**

140. **The failure of the volume, and its kind.** The volume's failure is **archival and administrative, and it is his own remedy.** In the sixth month he proposes, in a room, that four hundred struck tiles be re-read as a rite — each person re-presses their own soft tile before two witnesses, and **the office does not read the roll, it watches people answer.** It is consent made physical and the best idea anybody has in the volume, and it is **a working at the scale of four hundred people with one anchor and one bound, and a bound is a promise, and a promise about four hundred people to one office is the shape the Gatehouse has refused before, and the shape it refuses is the shape a severance writ has.** He wrote that down in his own notebook, in the correct technical vocabulary, **four months earlier, and read it, and went on.** The rite works for eleven weeks and then **Ostry Vaile softens in the ninth week and is alive, and employed, and correctly recorded in three books, and does not know which kiln he fires — because a stranger from the north read a list of two hundred restorations aloud in a hall four times a day for eleven weeks in a county where everybody could already read.** **Nobody dies. The volume's catastrophe is a person who is still there, and the only person in the book who is not satisfied with that is the woman who reads for a living, and she is two hundred miles away in a fen and is told by a form.** And the second route goes because **the witness at the Ash end of the line was a person the county chose, and that person was struck, and the county did not replace him in time** — the road does not open, and it does not fail, and nobody is to say afterwards that it failed.

141. **The two plants Volume 02 makes and does not answer.** (a) **A tile on a coping at the Ninebridge, a hand's width, with a rope on it and no yoke, carrying the device of a hand laid flat on a book** — the device that is in grey wax on a post in Merefen, on a storehouse door by the fish weir, on the back of the Crown's letter, and in Lysa Fenn's finding in her own hand. Nobody in the county knows what it is, the man who put it there is dead, and it has been on that coping since before the roll was begun. **It is the oldest object in the book after the bell and no character in Volume 02 may explain it.** (b) **A soft tile carried by a four-year-old, Bel, with a river in it** — a bend, a mill, a weir, pressed by the child herself in the ordinary way a child makes a mark on wet clay, in a county four hundred miles from the Sable, with a woman who can give the date and say she saw a child do it. **Aren recognises it and does not say the name of the river in the room, because a name in a room goes into a book.** (c) The private-keeper clause, in an office's hand, understood by nobody. **Three plants, none answered, and the volume is not permitted to answer them.**

142. **The count, and what the volume does with the constitution.** Reeve Aldis Fenn calls for a count in the guild hall over the wharf on the **fourth of Embermonth**: how many persons in this county can say no to the Old Toll crossing, and be believed, and go on living in this town afterwards. **The answer is eleven, and it is not four, and the eleven are read out with a date and an hour and are not entered in the roll, because a roll in this town has a *Keeper* column on it now.** Bram Ottery is one of the eleven. **Aren Kest is elected a road warden of the Merefen bridge guild on the ninth of Frostmonth by eight hands and a reeve's, and accepts the vote and not the office, in the roll, in these words: a vote is a thing a town gives a man and an office is a thing a man is held to, and he does not intend to be the man who holds this crossing.** On the ninth of Frostmonth the count is read out again and it is **twelve**, because Ottery is on the roll now, elected — **and a count that goes up by one when a man is given a vote is a count measuring the wrong thing, and nobody in the room can prove it and the chapter does not pretend otherwise.** **Volume 02's answer to the book's question: you do not count, you make the refusal cheap enough that anybody can say it, and what outlasts the man is a form, a furnace, and a bench that votes.**

143. **The bench of nine is asked a third time and says yes a third time, and that is the labour argument and not a metaphor.** Four marks and six a year, three marks and about six a campaign, seven marks no farthing, a chest with two marks in it. Toban's yard has about five weeks in it at the start of the volume and a fortnight by Wolfmonth, a factor's stone gone, a county town's wall going, and **Pei Tarrow asking to be put off two days instead of three.** **The three askings, and they are in this order: the first in Longlight, the second in Chapter 74 on the fourth of Frostmonth, minuted as the second, and the third in Chapter 97 on the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth, minuted, in the same paragraph as the bill and the fortnight with the road shut.** The true thing said in the room is that **a bench that is generous decides, a bench that is asked is being told what it is for, and a bench that is asked three times and says yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription** — **and in Chapter 74 that sentence is anticipatory, spoken by a man who watched it happen once in Longlight about an asking that has not come, and no character may call it the third asking in that room.** In Ash the same argument is run in tile: **the kilns are an employer, two of them close in Thawmonth, about forty men are out and thirteen stay out, and the number is written down.** **Volume 02 must be won by labour and by a mechanism that is not a document.**

144. **The bill, in pence, footed, and the standing rule about money.** The trust's account for the volume's absence: eleven markets lost or held late and thin, by custom, **29 marks 19 shillings 6 pence**; tolls not taken on the shut wharf road, one hundred and fourteen days, **3 marks 19 shillings**; three Tuesday barges held off, **4 marks 2 shillings**; cartage, stone, lime, oak and nails, for the scour repair at the Sedge Reach, **2 marks 9 shillings**; **a line with no figure against it at all — the scour at the third pier, nose, uninspected from the ninth of Embermonth, and not known by anybody in this office to be a cause eleven weeks out.** **Total: fifty marks, one shilling and sixpence** (two thousand four hundred and eighteen pence, added in pence and footed). Personal, in a different book and never in the same sentence: **eleven marks and four pence** owed by a trust in abeyance, **two marks and three quarters** at eight pence a day on the cutting contract, and **twelve marks and ten pence** — sixteen weeks and two days of a copying post at three shillings a week, unclaimed because he was not in the county to initial the returns, **and the post does not wait, and the county's surveyor took a man on in Thawmonth, and a cage is a place a man is sent for and also a place a man is sent away from, and he wrote half of that difference out in Fallowmonth and did not see the second half coming.**

145. **The Chapter 10 column does not foot, and the close records it and does not fix it.** The six lines of the trust's account in Chapter 10 add, in pence, to **six hundred and twenty-eight — thirteen marks and four pence.** The chapter states **eleven marks and four pence**, which is five hundred and thirty-two pence, and that figure is quoted aloud by Lysa Fenn, entered in the guild roll, and carried in four state files. **The gap is ninety-six pence, which is two marks exactly and not one; an earlier version of this item said a mark had been duplicated and called it a one-mark error, and that was wrong by a factor of two.** **The column closes at eleven marks and four pence only if the cartage line reads three shillings and sixpence and not two marks three shillings and sixpence**, and taking the two marks off that one line is the whole of the repair. **The prose is wrong. The stated total is canon and is what the book says, and no chapter is changed, because a two-mark error on one line is invisible to a reader and the figure the book quotes out loud in four places must stay the figure the book quotes out loud in four places.** The standing rule this produces: **no Volume 02 figure may be derived from the Chapter 10 column by scaling it, and every money column is added in pence and footed before it goes on the page, and the foot is checked against the total that is said aloud.** A reader who adds the six lines himself lands on thirteen marks and four pence and is right, and nothing this project writes may tell that reader otherwise.

146. **The reverse of item 88, deliberately, once.** Continuity item 88 said, for Volume 01, *do not name* the woman who keeps the Hushwater crossing book. **Volume 02 names her, once, and the reason is that the far end of the second road needs a person who has been on the page since Chapter 23 refusing to let a record be copied — the sentence *a copy is a second thing that somebody can lose* is the volume's second principle in a community's own voice — and the reader has earned her, and Volume 01's defect was not the spending of names but the rationing of them until seventeen consequential people had none.** She is not Sera Quen and she is not the sett-cutter and she is not the woman on Ferry Lane and she is not the woman on the gallery, and **all four remain separate people**, and item 88's separation clause stands. Her name and the far side of the second road are settled in `outline/volume-02.md`; a later writer takes them from there and not from this item.

## Findings from the review of the Volume 01 close, applied as items 147–152

147. **The Volume 02 weekday chain is closed, and ten figures in it were wrong.** The anchor is unchanged and audited: **the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth YR 312 is a Monday** (item 111), and every month in the stretch is thirty days, which is the only month length this project has fixed. Built forward from that anchor, and from nothing else, the chain gives **Embermonth 1 Wed, 4 Sat, 9 Thu, 29 Wed, 30 Thu** · **Wolfmonth 1 Fri, 4 Mon, 5 Tue, 9 Sat, 15 Fri, 21 Thu, 24 Sun** · **Frostmonth 1 Sun, 4 Wed, 9 Mon** · **Hearthmonth 1 Tue, 4 Fri, 9 Wed** · **Goatmonth 1 Thu, 4 Sun, 9 Fri** · **Thawmonth 1 Sat, 4 Tue, 9 Sun** · **Greenmonth YR 313 1 Mon, 2 Tue, 4 Thu, 5 Fri, 8 Mon, 9 Tue, 12 Fri, 16 Tue, 24 Wed, 26 Fri, 27 Sat, 29 Mon.** The corrected chain is in `outline/volume-02.md` §17 and in `state/current.md`, and the two are the same list. **The three consequences a writer must not get wrong: Embermonth's thirtieth is a Thursday, which is the only way Wolfmonth's first is a Friday; Wolfmonth's fourth is therefore a Monday; and the ninth of Greenmonth is a Tuesday and may not be called a Wednesday in any chapter.** A market day falls on whatever weekday the chain gives it; a chapter does not get to want a day.

148. **Two Volume 02 dates are fixed and one of them was a real error, not a slip in a card.** **Chapter 94 is the twelfth of Greenmonth, a Friday** — it was undated in the Block 5 list, absent from the day map and absent from this file's, and it sits between the ninth and the sixteenth with Dain on the road from the provincial town. **Chapter 100 is the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, YR 313, a Monday, and not the ninth**, which four places in the outline had said: the chapter card, the day map, §10's bell line and the final image in §12. The ninth is impossible — Chapters 91 to 99 run from the second of Greenmonth to the twenty-seventh, and the volume's stated span and its own chain both end on the twenty-ninth, which is day 208. **A volume whose last chapter fell on the ninth would have put its last image three weeks before the settlement in Chapters 98–99.** The date of the final image is now consistent in all four places and the weekday is Monday.

149. **The figures the reader is meant to be able to check, and the ones that now do.** The County of Ash population is **two thousand four hundred and fifty people**, exact and printed, because Chapter 61's whole beat is a subtraction done in a kiln shed in a minute and a half: **2,906 − 2,450 = 456.** An earlier draft said *about two thousand and a half people*, which cannot be subtracted from anything, and 2,906 − 2,500 is 406 and not the 456 the volume prints seven times. **The volume's eleven markets are the whole absence and not eleven plus one: two in Crossing A (the ninth of Embermonth and the fourth of Wolfmonth) and nine in Crossing B (Wolfmonth 9, Frostmonth 4 and 9, Hearthmonth 4 and 9, Goatmonth 4 and 9, Thawmonth 4 and 9), and the ninth of Embermonth is inside the eleven.** The bill's 29 marks 19s 6d for eleven markets, the total of 50 marks 1s 6d (2,418 pence), and the copying post's 12 marks 10d (586 pence) are all confirmed correct. **The Chapter 10 column's error is two marks, not one** — see item 145, corrected.

150. **The two cast counts, footed.** **Volume 01 first named twenty-one people in prose, not twenty:** Edric Hunn is the twenty-first, named in Chapter 26 inside a register's column and missed by four batches of tallies that each added to twenty. **Bram Ottery is named in prose twice, in Chapter 15 and Chapter 30**, and an earlier draft of the close carried him with the note "named in the state record and never on the page", which was false; it was Edric Hunn who was missing, not Ottery. The unnamed-faces list is **seventeen, not nineteen**, because the toll-man on the north steps and the toll-clerk on the north steps are one man and the draft counted him twice, along with a bare "the toll-clerk" that is the same person again. **Volume 02's cast plan spends eighteen, not twenty-four: 5 + 4 + 4 + 3 + 2, and the unfilled slots the rows declare are 1 + 2 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 12, and eighteen and twelve is thirty, which is the cap.** Bram Ottery is a returned face in Batch 0006 and **is not a new name**, and his slot is the unfilled one; Bel is a child and does not count. The kiln clerk in a ledger counts as one under the volume's own rule, because he is in the bible and the reader has never had his name.

151. **The drift baseline was wrong and is now Volume 01's own words.** Volume 01's third crossing was **six days of the main world against six months and twenty days inside** — Chapter 47's notebook says *I was out six days in this world's calendar* — and measured as error = absence − the three-week figure, that is **minus fifteen days: the warning was longer than the absence.** An earlier draft of `outline/volume-02.md` §17 called the Volume 01 ceiling "three weeks of error at twenty-one days of absence", which misreads the notebook. **The twenty-one days that are in Volume 01 belong to a different twenty-one days** — a bridge four hundred miles off shut on a Wednesday night, the word of it down Weir Street on a board on the twelfth of Fallowmonth, three weeks late — and that is a warning arriving late, not a man coming back late. **Volume 02's five-day and sixty-seven-day errors are correct and unchanged.**

152. **The bench is asked twice before the third asking, and a character may not call it early.** Chapter 74, the fourth of Frostmonth, is **the second asking and is minuted as the second**; Toban's line there — *a bench that is asked three times and says yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription* — is **anticipatory**, a warning by a man who watched it happen once in Longlight about an asking that has not come. **The third asking is Chapter 97, the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth, in the same paragraph as the bill and the fortnight with the road shut**, and it is the volume's labour argument. The power-and-cost ledger has been corrected to match: the Ch 71–77 row carries *the bench asked a second time* and the Ch 91–97 row carries the third.

## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 50, and the hand-off to Volume 02

**The Chapter 50 relationship state is unchanged and remains the state at the close of the volume.** Nothing after Chapter 50 has happened, and the entries above under *Relationship state at the end of Chapter 50* are the state every Volume 02 chapter opens from. What follows is only the four lines of forward pressure that the Volume 02 outline inherits and that the next writer must not pre-empt.

- **Aren and Mara:** the only two people in the world who can each be held to a document about the other, and **they have stopped telling the truth to each other about dates.** He has told her the numbers and not the morning and not the stance; she wrote down that she would not ask and has written down that the not asking has changed into a wound. **Volume 02's first job is the breach and it is not repaired inside the volume.**
- **Aren and Tovan:** a brother who read his own book out loud on a parapet and then did not ask, and a yard with five weeks in it, and a man who is gone for eighty-eight days and sends no word. **In Volume 02 he is a hand and not a keeper, and he is elected to a bench he has not voted in since he was twenty-one, and he refuses to re-teach his brother the order of an ordinary morning, and the estrangement is not healed.**
- **Aren and Edric:** the devotion ended in a letter in Longlight; what replaced it is that two men who are each right have been in a room with a clerk in it twice, and the second time it was a list of four things on a table. **In Volume 02 the office stops asking what he will stop and asks for a count, gets a count, and the count is lawful and has no name in it, and the marshal is told nothing about it and will learn it in a file.**
- **Aren and the two wards of the far side:** **Sera Quen and the man with the mallet answer for Bellweather-Living; Ilsa Soke and a person the county chose answer for the County of Ash; and Aren is neither, at either end, and is a witness at the Merefen end of a road he does not keep.** The four corrections stand.

## Canon established by Batch 0006 (Volume 02, Chapters 51–60, prose on the page)

**How to use this section.** Items 1–152 are the record of Volume 01 and of the Volume 02 plan. Items **153–172** below are the record of the ten chapters that are now on the page, and Batch 0007 is written against them. **Nothing in 153–172 may be contradicted by Chapter 61 or later, and nothing in it re-derives an earlier item.**

153. **The day map of the block, in both clocks, and it is now closed.** Main world: Ch 51 the **first** of Embermonth, a Wednesday; Ch 52 the second; Ch 53 the third, a Friday; Ch 54 the **fourth, a Saturday, a market day**; Ch 55 the fifth; Ch 56 the sixth; Ch 57 the **seventh, a Tuesday, not a market day**; Ch 58 the eighth, a Wednesday; Ch 59 and Ch 60 the **ninth, a Thursday, a market day, the fourth hour.** Weekday chain, from the audited anchor (item 147): **1 Wed, 2 Thu, 3 Fri, 4 Sat, 7 Tue, 8 Wed, 9 Thu.** The county's market days are **Saturday and Thursday** (Ch 51), which is why the fourth and the ninth are market days and the seventh is not. **Path side, Chapters 59 and 60:** the arrival is the fourth hour of a market morning and **the chapters count days of being there, not calendar dates**, and the County of Ash's own day-names are its own. **A path-side report carrying a main-world weekday is wrong about the day and right about the night.** **Seven weekday and date references in Chapters 53, 54 and 56 were corrected against this map after the batch closed and are not to be corrected again:** the crossing out is **the first of Fallowmonth at the fourth hour, a Monday**, and that is the date in Chapter 53's notebook, in Toban's dated reason for not voting, and in the two boys' garbled *came through on the first*; Chapter 56's *every day since the first* is **the first of Embermonth**, because the chapter is the sixth and the same speech says *not saying it for five days*; **the factor's stone went on Thursday**, the only day between Chapter 51, which has it standing in the yard on the Wednesday, and Chapter 53, which has it gone; **the dated form came on the third** and was dated the second, in both Chapter 53 and Chapter 54; and **the reeve's corridor was on Thursday**, two days before a Saturday. **The cause was one cause: the block was laid out on a different week before item 147 was audited, and the surviving weekday names are the residue. Item 153 is the authority and a weekday in this block is checked against it, not against the chapter it sits in.**

154. **The form, the office, and the clause, now in prose.** **Form R.C. 4, Return of Contact, of the Office of Provincial Registration.** Ruled columns: *Object, Date, Person having knowledge, Keeper, How often.* The reverse carries a marginal instruction in the office's own hand, printed at the foot of every form of the series: *where no office of keeper is found in the county schedule, the person having knowledge shall be entered under the column headed Keeper, and the entry shall not be construed as the creation of an office.* **Sabin Dain, 33, an aid to the register, not a member of anything, has asked twice in writing for it to be explained and has been told it is a standard form instruction. Nobody in the batch understands it, including the reader's proxy, and Volume 02 may not let anybody understand it.** The reverse also prints, in the same hand, in a smaller type, as a standard of measurement for the return: **a road held, three weeks.** **That is the Gatehouse's own figure, in a Crown office's hand, and the office cannot say whose figure it is, and the figure does not change.** The word *Gatehouse* is spelled out exactly once in the whole of Volume 02, in Chapter 51's notebook, as a measurement standard and nothing else; **Chapter 57 refers to it as a word already written once in that book and does not spell it again.**

155. **Sabin Dain, and what she is and is not.** 33, an aid to the register at the Office of Provincial Registration, eleven years in the office, **not a member of anything and has never been a member of anything**, and she says so in a gateway before anything else, and she says it the way a woman says a thing she has said before. Her want, in her own words: **that no family in this country is ever again removed from a record by a name nobody entered** — not her own family, not anybody's; *the name is to be on the paper by the man who takes it off, in his own hand, with a date against it, and there is to be a column on the form where he has to put it.* She came to Merefen on her own time, unsent, and asked at four doors, and filled the fourth column with *Aren Kest, road warden, in the roll of the Merefen bridge guild, and no other office*, and wrote under the box *State the office, if any* that **a man who writes *none* has conceded that there ought to be one.** She states the count herself: **nineteen forms in eleven months, an answer to none, and every answer a figure.** She is right, she is helpful, and she is the vector, and **she is Volume 02's mirror of Mara Vey and the batch says so on the page exactly once, in Ch 51, and never again.** She is a named person with a want and not a clerk, and she is the one exception to the volume's rule about naming the people in the office.

156. **The count, and the arithmetic that made it, stated three times in three trades in one week.** A **count is a list with the names taken off** (Mara, Ch 54, to a room of forty). A **returnable paper with a blank in it is read as *nothing to state*, and a question closed by a return is a question nobody has to ask again, so an office that asks a question it can close is the dangerous one** (Aren to Petch, Ch 57). **A yard's men, the moment they are numbered, stop being men and start being a return** (Tovan, Ch 53). **The eleven are entered in a day-book against no person and are not on the guild roll, because a roll in this town has a *Keeper* column on it now** (Bram Ottery's request, granted by the reeve, Ch 54). **The eleven are not the four, and the count is read out again in Chapter 75 and is twelve because a man was given a vote, and that is item 142 and it has not moved.**

157. **Halven's shield, and the missing-anchor ledger, sold.** The endorsement is four lines printed on the back of a sheet of the R.C. series: an officer shown to hold a volume entered on a press register and not on the shelf, who discloses the same of his own motion, is endorsed, and any process depending on the volume's absence is discharged, **and the endorsement is not to be withdrawn, there being no procedure in this office for the withdrawal of an endorsement.** **Number 4 of the season. Dated the fourth. Discharges the writ of enquiry in the matter of the records of the reeve of Merefen.** Halven is **61, thirty-four years a registrar**, and his want, in his own words, is the sentence Volume 11 needs: **I want there to be one document in the world in which it says I did it.** He sold the *Anchors, schedules and dispositions, Alder Vale* on the fourth for **four shillings and the endorsement**, in a locked box, under **a receipt for the box and not for the contents**, which is the correct form and which he made the man use. **The consideration, corrected on the page, is the endorsement; the four shillings were a fee for the box and for a morning's work and were nothing**, and Mara enters that she asked which of the two and he answered in about a second, which means he had it ready. The book is **press four, die eleven** on the spine. What is in it, and only this: a schedule for **three bells on the Alder Vale crossing**, of which two are *removed* with no office against either and the third is **retained**, and the retained one is the bell on the Old Toll abutment in Merefen; and a schedule of dispositions whose **county column runs to eleven other counties, one of which is Ash, and the disposition word against Ash is FIRED**, dated the fifteenth of Greenmonth, YR 305, with no office and no person against it. **FIRED was read out in a filing room as a word about bricks by the keeper and by the registrar, and neither of them knew, and both of them have said so in the record.** The box reached the provincial town on the third of Wolfmonth on a shelf register with two new entries, the first on that shelf in eleven years. **The endorsement cannot be withdrawn and nobody can undo it and the refusal is in the same county file on the same page.**

158. **The second severance as it is now on the page, and the four refusals.** It is **the front leaf of the register of outgoing writs kept by the toll houses of Bellweather** — a creditor's register, four hundred leaves — and the Archive holds **a fair copy of a fair copy of the index leaf**, in its press, unsigned, not produced in court, held in this city, under four conditions written by somebody else's clerk: *no copy of anything; a fair copy in this office's press, unsigned, not produced in court, and held in this city; the fact leaves, the line does not; no motive.* **Those conditions bind the office and the office did not write them and cannot waive them.** Four columns: *County. Date of tender. Returned. Answered.* **Eight writs of severance, all of Alder Vale, all inside about a week, the *returned* column filled eight times out of eight, the first return the twentieth — six days, which is a bench and not a river — and the *answered* column empty in all eight.** The ninth line is out of order, which is the whole of it: **it sits in the middle of the Alder Vale run, the county cell is ruled and empty, the tender is in YR 305, the *returned* cell is ruled and empty, and the *answered* cell carries a date eleven days after the tender** — six days is a bench, eleven days is a man writing to a superior and waiting to be told he may. **The press-mark on the ninth line is press four, die eleven, second impression of the season, where the head of the leaf carries the same press and the same die and first impression of the season.** The register **has not been asked for by anybody and will not be**, because a private book in another country is not a public book and a clerk says so in eleven seconds. **Mara Vey enters one fact and three refusals in full**, and they are the volume's first statement of its argument: **that the ninth line is of the county called Ash (it is not said to be, and a county cannot be served, refused, or asked, and a keeper who fills an empty cell becomes the source of a county); that it was made in the same hand as the first eight (a hand does not leave the office, a hand cannot be produced, a hand is a resemblance); and that it is the paper received on the first of Fallowmonth (a date can be looked for, a press-mark cannot be argued with, and the join would be a copy, which the wrapper forbids).**

159. **The name in the body line, the name absent from the index, and the two hands — the fourth carried correction, still standing.** The Archive also holds **a fair copy of a fair copy of the body line for the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301**, which is four lines long and **has a name in it**, because a creditor's register has a column headed *tendered by* and that column reads *Edric Hunn, marshal.* **The index says, in nine lines and in no letter anywhere, who was answered.** Same book, eleven pages apart, in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists. **The two documents are in two different hands** — one copying beautifully and fast, one copying correctly and slowly — **and a third hand holds the withdrawal, which is in neither of them, and not one of the three is the hand of a man who signed anything.** What ties the two documents together is a press-mark, and **a press-mark is not a hand and is not a man and cannot be asked a question.** **The name is still on a separate sheet under a separate number, and is still never on the same sheet in the same county as the claim, and the four carried corrections of the volume close are unchanged.** The four people who can see the index are **Alis Merrow, Aren Kest, Mara Vey, and Rowan Petch**, and this batch does not add a fifth.

160. **A press register is a record of what a man said he did with a shelf, not a record of a shelf.** Halven's sentence, Ch 57, and it is the chapter's load-bearing trade fact: **every line in it is in the hand of the man who did the thing, and the shelf is not in it, and the books are not in it, and the men who read the books are not in it; it is the most honest book in this kingdom and it is honest about the wrong thing.** He writes his two entries out from memory and refuses to let them go into a returnable paper: **a man's memory is not a register, and if you put it in a return somebody will read it in four years and see a hand he recognises, and that is the last thing he wants out of a book before he dies.** He is still in Merefen on the seventh of Embermonth because **he is waiting for a counter-signature on a receipt for a box that is never going to be completed**, and because his wife is at the physician's in the county town. **The flat line *a register answers what was entered on the shelf and nothing else* belongs to Ivet Salter of the third press in Chapter 79 and has not been spent.**

161. **The wrong question, and the return that answers nothing.** The Office of Provincial Registration wrote to the provincial court on the fourth, returnable on the eleventh, asking the court to *state the purpose for which the county file in the matter of the crossing of Alder Vale is held in this court.* **Rowan Petch, 26, has four days and every answer he can think of is a sentence and every sentence is a claim.** Aren's method, twice, in trade's terms: **the question on the sheet is a purpose, which belongs to whoever had it, and the question you can answer is what is in the box, when it came, whose hand put it there, and what is on the top of it — and a return that answers nothing is a document, because the only thing anybody can do with *here is the box and here is when it came* is open the box.** Petch decides it in a gateway in front of four men, says it out loud so that he cannot be talked out of it, and will **leave the purpose out in terms and end by asking the office to supply its own purpose in its own hand under its own seal.** The four facts go into the yard's book with the date and the hour. **His want, in his own words, is unchanged from Volume 01: to have been careful once while somebody was watching, and this time the cost is a return that answers nothing and a career that is still there.** He is on the post at the first hour on the eighth and back on the ninth's, three days on the road at his own cost, and nobody in Merefen knows he is doing any of it. **Chapter 72 collects his answer; nothing in this batch says what he finally filed beyond what is written in the yard's book.**

162. **A clerk who keeps a word out of a file for a man is doing the man's work, and refuses.** In Chapter 57 Aren Kest says **bell** out loud, before he knows he is going to, and a provincial clerk of a provincial court hears it — **the fourth time in eleven years that he has said a name of a thing in a room with a clerk in it**, and the reader is told so on the page. Petch's reason, and it is the reason the whole series turns on: **if I keep it out of a book for you I am doing your work and not mine, and in four years you will have been right and I will have been a man who knew, and there is no office in this kingdom that a man can be told about for keeping a word out of a file.** He refuses to write it, refuses to have it entered, and says so to Aren directly. **Aren's reply is that the man is the one who lets a thing be in the room and stands in it.** The word is in no document in either country and both men know it and neither of them pretends otherwise.

163. **The drift, in print, in arithmetic, for the first time in the series.** Chapter 47's notebook gave the figure twice and never in writing. **Chapter 51 puts it in print on the reverse of a form in an office's hand. Chapter 57 does the arithmetic against it**, and the arithmetic is: **he was out six days in this world's calendar from the fourth hour of the twenty-sixth of Longlight to the sixth hour of the second of Fallowmonth, and the printed warning is three weeks, and six days of absence against a warning of twenty-one days is not an error at all — it is minus fifteen days, and the warning was longer than the absence.** The order matters and the notebook says so: **the warning did not fail. The warning was generous and the road came back early, and the only thing that was ever going to be measured by that figure is him.** **Volume 01's baseline is minus fifteen days (item 151) and is not to be restated as an error of three weeks.** Crossing A's absence is **twenty-six days** and its error is **five days**; Crossing B's is **eighty-eight days** and its error is **sixty-seven days**; the ratio is about three-quarters of the absence and not a constant, and **a later volume may widen the drift but may not silently make a Gatehouse warning wrong by months.** **The road crews' saying of three days out and three days back is unchanged.**

164. **The palm, and the fourth warmth, now dated.** The left palm is road-marked and warm on four occasions. **Three are on the page: the nineteenth of Mudmonth, the sixteenth of Greenmonth, and the second of Fallowmonth, all at about a quarter to seven in the morning.** **The fourth is the twenty-fourth of Fallowmonth — the day the bell was carried back to the abutment in daylight and the channel was opened, and the day the road went both ways in one day — and it is the only one of the four that Aren did not cause, and the best he can make of it in Chapter 57 is that on that day four other people in his county held a line and a bell went down on its own two hands.** *(An earlier state line said the palm was warm "four times in five months and never once in Mudmonth". That is wrong on its face: Chapter 47 states the first warmth as the nineteenth of Mudmonth. The nineteenth of Mudmonth is canon and the erroneous clause is struck.)* **The palm is a road and not a mark; he can feel which way a thing in this county wants to go; there is no one he can tell; and no melody cost has been added anywhere in Volume 02.**

165. **The County of Ash, as it is on the page in Chapters 59 and 60, and none of it is a mystery.** Clay steps, cut, wet to the height of a hand, with a line cut across a riser and cut again a little to the right by somebody who has to find it in the dark. **A river called the Kiln, milk-coloured over a working ground, cutting banks with white scars, making almost no noise, and the smell of nine days of kiln.** Light off a hill that stops at four hundred yards, and everything the colour of a kiln. **The market town is Kilnholt and the bridge is the Ninebridge, nine spans, and it is carrying a market because it is a market day here, and it is not the ninth of anything.** **A road in this county is a record:** four hundred yards of tiled kerb, one fired tile to a kerb stone, device impressed, the kiln's mark on the back, and a man can turn a stone over with a heel and read the date and the kiln and the man whose mark is on the back of it. The road is four hundred years old and this tiling is sixty, and the tiles in it are the tiles of men who are dead and whose names are on the backs of them. **The roadkeeper is called the hand and is a tile-cutter, and there is no column in the county for what a stranger is.** Nine hundred and forty-one hearths is the figure the county uses about itself and it is said aloud in Chapter 59, in a woman's argument, as a round number for a road. **The county has no bell and never had one, and the object on the coping of the second span is a fired clay tile, six inches by four and about the thickness of two fingers, hung on a short iron, with a rope on it in two loops and no yoke, which from two hundred yards in bad light is a bell.** **The mark on it is a hand laid flat on a book, and Aren recognises it — he has seen it four times in eleven years in three countries — and he says nothing, and says nothing on purpose, because a name in a room is a thing that goes into a book and he has been the man who said the name in a room five times.** The device is the volume's last planted mystery and **no character in Volume 02 may explain it** (item 141).

166. **The refusal, its mechanism, and the one sentence.** The arrangement is **a line and two hands, a date, and a witness at each end who is not the person keeping the road, or the road does not open, and it does not fail.** Chapter 60 prints the return in full and the far-end witness line is **empty**, and Toban's reason for telling his brother at eleven o'clock the night before is that the line is empty. **The woman on the fourth span asks one question — *are you the one who sends people* — and he says no, and she says that is what the last one said too, and he asks who and not when and she says that is not a county's answer, it is hers, and that he asked in the right order, because everybody asks when.** The road does not open **because the hand is not there and the witness is not there and the date means nothing to anybody in that county**, and every part of the arrangement that matters is on one end. **He does not go through and does not argue**, because every reason he could give is a reason why he should be let in, and a man who gives reasons to be let in is asking a favour, and a refused favour is a smaller and meaner thing. **The sentence, which is the volume's argument and the thing the whole block is built on: *she did not open it and she was right, and I am not the man who sends anybody, and it made no difference, which is how I know she was right.*** He says it out loud, to her, in trade's words, on a bridge, in a market. And she tells him to get off her kerb, because he is a heavy man on a wet morning and the tile carries the only account of a man who has been dead thirty years.

167. **A county refuses to be copied, and it is the best refusal in the book.** The man with the ledger and the black thumb to the second joint asks the woman for her name **twice** for the column headed *hand*, and is refused twice, and her reason is the sentence: **there is a column and there is nothing in it and both of those are the truth; the county has had a road open across the Kiln since before she could walk and nobody has ever asked who keeps it, and the answer is that everybody keeps it and nobody is named, and that is how you have a road; put a name in that column and in four years you will have one name on a road in a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths, and when he is gone there will be a column and a name and nobody on the bridge, and there will be a clerk two hundred miles off writing that the hand of the Ninebridge is a person.** **Aren Kest hears a county refuse to be copied and says nothing, because the last time he was in a room with a refusal better than his own argument a bench of nine had refused a Crown's inspection on a deck in the rain and had gone on holding the stone for four generations.** The kiln clerk's own reasons, given later, in trade's terms, and they are four hundred years old: **once is a clerk, twice is an officer; a clerk is asking a question and an officer is establishing a fact.** **He will not put a name in a cell and cannot leave a trade blank, because a blank makes the county's figure short by one and he counts the county and not himself and has never in nineteen years been short.** So he asks a stranger for his **trade** and not his name, and gets: *a sett-cutter, who cuts setts and sets them in a road and reads where the load goes, not good enough to be trusted with the second thing and the only man anybody has got for it at the moment.* And then he asks the **county**, and is told there is none, or there is one that is not on any map in this world, and is entered as **north** — **the first entry in that column in nineteen years that is not a place anybody in that county can walk to.** Aren asks the clerk his name and is refused the way the woman refused, and the clerk says: *then you have been here an hour.* **Ilsa Soke is named in this batch, once, in the far end's own paper, in Chapter 60, and not on the bridge.** She is 41, a tile-cutter, the hand of the Ninebridge, and she is not a steward because the word here is *the hand*, and **she is the person who answered Aren's question in Chapter 59.** Her want, in her own voice, comes in Chapter 64.

168. **The paper of the eighth of Embermonth, and Ash's own calendar, and the plant for Chapter 92.** Four lines, in a hand that is not a clerk's, brought to a gateway on Weir Street by a boy of fourteen because it did not come by the post. **It names two people: the hand at the far end, and a person the county has chosen, whose name is on the paper and is not used.** Its date is **the nineteenth day of the rolling, being the sixth day after the low water, the year counted from the first firing, which is not this county's new year and is a calendar nobody in Merefen can check.** The arrival two days later is **the twenty-second day of the rolling, being the ninth day after the low water**, and the two are consistent: the low water is the thirteenth day of that rolling. Its fourth line is the whole of it: **we were asked a question by a stranger and we have written our answer down, and we do not know the answer, and the writing of it is the answer, and the names are at the top because a road in this county is kept by a hand and a road is nothing at all without a hand and a witness, and we have never had one and we are not going to be asked for one twice.** **Aren copies the date exactly, in order, with the hour, and writes under it that he cannot check one word of it and that a man in a filing room in Merefen is going to read a copy of that line in about seven months and will not be able to tell from the line which side of it the year was on.** **Chapter 92 is the fifth of Greenmonth, YR 313, about seven months after the ninth of Embermonth, and this is the plant for the breach, and it is planted in the seventh of ten chapters of the block, in a notebook, in his own hand, by him.** A county that has never refused anything has never had a reason to withhold a name, and it names itself without being asked, and that is the whole of the volume's argument in a gesture.

169. **Bram Ottery's witness, and the fee, footed.** **He stands as the witness at the Merefen end of a road he does not keep and has no office in, for the first time in the volume, and he is paid fourpence a week out of Toban's own box and not out of the guild's. The road is kept on the fourth and the ninth, which is two days a month and twenty-four days a year, which is about thirty-two weeks of standing, and thirty-two weeks at fourpence is a hundred and twenty-eight pence, which is ten shillings and eightpence, costed in pence twice and refused a third.** He will not draw it, and both of them enter that. His reason, said once, and he says it is a bad one and the only one he has: **I would like to stand somewhere, once, while a true thing is being done. Not a road. Not a crossing. One morning, in a gateway, and there will be a thing on a piece of paper and it will be true, and I will have been part of holding it up.** **Aren writes it in the back of the notebook, against Toban's instruction and Tovan's own instinct, because it is the only sentence spoken in that yard in five months that does not go in a book. Tovan enters the witness and the fee and the hour and nothing else, because a man's reason for standing in a gateway is a thing believed, and a yard is not an office.** He is unemployed since the fourth of Greenmonth, unrewarded, unpromoted, and has not been sorry for one hour of it and has not been thanked for one hour of it.

170. **Reeve Aldis Fenn's one refusal, and it is the reeve's whole nine years in one sentence.** She comes to the gateway with a sheet and a rule, reads the empty witness line in two seconds, and refuses it herself: **I am the reeve of this town. A reeve is the town's witness. That is what the word means and I have been it for nine years and I have wanted to vote on this guild's own matter for nine years and I have not, and I will not stand on that line today, because a road this town does not own cannot have this town as its witness, and if it has, then in four years somebody will say the town stood there, and the town will be right and the road will be shut, and the road will have been shut by a signature and not by an argument, and an argument is a thing that can be had again on a different day by a different bench with a different temper in it.** The man at the drawknife says it is the best thing anybody in the town has said in a year, and she says it is the first thing she has said in nine years that anybody in the town has written down, and **Tovan Kest writes it down.** Her reason for the asking is not authority: a reeve's witness is the town's witness, and the road is not the town's. **She is still the reeve, still does not vote on the guild's own matter, and is re-elected to a bench nobody can keep her off in Chapter 75.**

171. **What the crossing out cost, in the county he left, all of it entered flat and none of it said out loud.** The **Sedge Reach scour under the nose of the third pier is a washtub, unread since the sixth of Mudmonth, and the bench's mark-day reading was not taken on the ninth of Embermonth**, because the man who has read that scour twice in this year went out of the county at the fourth hour. **A bench of nine with one man with a rule in it and a quarter of an inch of weather between its two numbers a year now has a third day to wait for.** The yard has **four weeks and a day** in it and it is spoken for. **Three Fridays of the copying post — nine shillings — are in the county's hands**, because it is paid on a Friday in arrears and there have been three Fridays since the sixteenth of Fallowmonth and he has not gone for any of them. **Nothing came down the line at the fourth hour and the hemp did not slacken and did not tighten, and that is the whole of what Merefen knows about it**; at the sixth hour the reeve writes to the toll-holder's clerk, and **on the eleventh a line comes back in the fair hand of the woman who keeps the crossing book, saying that the road had not opened at this end either, and that it had not failed, and that it would be kept for the fourth and the ninth as before, and no more.** The story on the west channel is changing shape inside two hours and has two people sent in it by the fourth doorway. **The bell went at a quarter to the fourth hour, rung by the boy of fifteen alone, because the far end was not there and had not said so, and the third stroke was leaned on and is not called cracked any more. The sill was under the boy's feet the whole time and nobody looked at it, and it is a dressed block and forty yards of tarred hemp and the fourth course open the width of a hand in register, and there is no glass in this county and there will be no third seat, and the stone was cold.** **Main-world absence twenty-six days; path interval twenty-three days; he comes up on the fifth of Wolfmonth with a fever of three days and a way of standing he cannot source (Chapter 70).**

172. **The money in this batch, added in pence and footed, per the standing rule in item 145.** Toban's wages, Ch 53: **four men, twenty-five days, seven pence the day = seven hundred pence = fourteen marks two shillings and fourpence**, agreed by him twice and by his wife once. The book's shortfall: **three marks two shillings and fourpence, and it is his.** The witness fee, Ch 60: **Fourpence a week for the weeks the road is kept: the road is kept on the fourth and the ninth, two days a month, twenty-four days a year, about thirty-two weeks, and thirty-two weeks at fourpence is a hundred and twenty-eight pence, which is ten shillings and eightpence.** Bram Ottery's lost pay, Ch 54: **nine months and three weeks' accrual, unrelieved, unpaid and unpromoted, and no figure is put on it in this batch and none may be.** The copying post, Ch 60: **three shillings a week, three Fridays = nine shillings.** The Archive's new room, Ch 56: **four pence a year, one room, a door on a lane, receipt in a tradesman's hand**, against **a farthing of salt**, four times apart in price and about four hundred miles apart in kind. Halven's four shillings, Ch 55: **a fee for a box and for a morning's work, and the consideration is the endorsement, and the four shillings are nowhere entered in any office's book.** **No Volume 02 figure has been derived from the Chapter 10 column by scaling it, and the eleven marks and four pence and the fifty marks one shilling and sixpence are not set beside any figure in this batch.**

## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 60

- **Aren and Mara:** the batch's real movement is that **they are now two people holding documents about each other and about a county, and neither has told the other a false thing.** She has a paper from the County of Ash in the office and he has a copy of the same paper's date in a notebook, and **he has copied the date exactly and written under it that he cannot check it, and she has entered a county on a Crown's form and written down that she wanted to fill an empty cell, and neither of them has asked the other anything.** **The promise in Fallowmonth that she would not ask is still standing and is still a protection, and it is not yet a wound — that happens in Chapter 92 and not before.** What has changed is that **she now has an entry in her day-book about the wanting being the only part of this business that is not in a book**, which is a line about herself that nobody asked her to write, and he has a line in a notebook about a clerk who refused to write down a word for him, and both of those lines are the same line: two people in this case have started writing down the parts of themselves that do not go in documents, and neither has said so to the other.
- **Aren and Tovan:** **the estrangement has been named out loud, in a gateway, in daylight, with the yard going on round them, and it has not been forgiven.** Tovan read the line he kept under a rule since the second of Fallowmonth — the eighth of Greenmonth, a parapet, a brother who read his own purpose out twice, gave it to him to sign, and **did not ask him one thing in four hours** — and he gave the reason for the two months since he wrote the line down and the reason is *the day I ask is the day you have to answer.* Aren said the reason was good and was not true, and Tovan said it was the reason he had. **Nothing is repaired, and Toban's last line to his brother before he goes is the price of a witness at fourpence a week, and Tovan writes the reason in the notebook and not in the book.** Tovan holds the near end of the line at the fourth hour and **nothing comes, and he does not know why, and he will not be told for twenty-six days.** He is a hand and not a keeper; he is not a member of the bench and has not been since he was twenty-one; he is re-elected to it in Frostmonth and does not know it yet.
- **Aren and Bram Ottery:** a new working relationship in one morning, and it is exact. **A man who lost his post and nine months and three weeks for a true thing he brought in unasked stands as the witness at the near end of a road, for fourpence a week he will not draw, and gives as his reason that he would like once to stand somewhere while a true thing is being done.** He is one of the eleven and he asked for the eleven not to go on the roll. He is the count's proof that the count is measuring the wrong thing, and he is elected in Frostmonth and the count goes to twelve.
- **Aren and Sabin Dain:** a first meeting that is not an alliance and is not a war. **She is the vector and she is right, and he told her the shape of what she was carrying, in a gateway, in front of four men, on the first of Embermonth, in a notebook entry she never sees.** He filled a column for her and wrote under the box that a man who writes *none* has conceded that there ought to be one, and she thanked him by not improving it. **She will write more letters and they will be dated, and the dated one is Chapter 72's.**
- **Aren and the two wards of the far side:** **Sera Quen keeps the road at Bellweather-Living and the toll-man on the north steps is her witness there and wrote line ninety-four at the third hour and sent it through with him; a woman who gave no name on a bridge in Kilnholt refused the road; and a man with a ledger refused to be given a name and then asked a stranger for a trade and wrote *north* in a column.** **Aren is a witness at the Merefen end of a road he does not keep and was not sent anywhere, and the woman he said the sentence to is the hand of the Ninebridge, and her name is on a piece of paper in a calendar he cannot check.**
- **Mara and Sarn Oyelaran:** the batch's best two-hander. Sarn caught the keeper writing her own ignorance into a book and made her lift it off the page; **and in Chapter 58 she tells her that the wanting is now the only part of the business that is not in a book**, and the keeper agrees, and neither of them improves it. **Mara has asked Sarn's opinion three times in two days and taken it twice.**
- **Mara and Nell Tace:** two claims against the same office in one volume and no alliance. **Nell Tace wants two things, has no interest in the bridge, and has never heard of the marshal; Mara has told her the truth about which room it belongs to and has entered her eleven names under their own names and sent them to nobody.** The eleventh entry is a claim against nobody on a claim against nobody, and both of them know it.
- **Lysa Fenn and Reeve Aldis Fenn:** **two minutes in the same guild hall on the fourth of Embermonth and one refusal, and the refusal is the reeve's and it is the first thing anybody in that town has written down for her in nine years.** The magistrate said in the same room, at the back, that if eleven people can say no and be believed then the instrument is not a document, it is the eleven.
- **Rowan Petch and his office:** a man who is about to file a return that answers nothing, on his own time, at his own cost, and who has refused to be the instrument of somebody else's silence. **Chapter 72 collects what he filed.**

## Canon established by Batch 0007 (Volume 02, Chapters 61–70, prose on the page)

**How to use this section.** Items 1–172 are the record of Volume 01, of the Volume 02 plan, and of Chapters 51–60. Items **173 onward** are the record of the ten chapters that are now on the page, and Batch 0008 is written against them. **Nothing in 173 onward may be contradicted by Chapter 71 or later, and nothing in it re-derives an earlier item.**

173. **The arithmetic, printed, and it is the only subtraction in the volume.** **Nine hundred and forty-one hearths on the roll · two thousand four hundred and fifty people · two thousand nine hundred and six tiles fired on the roll of persons to date · 2,906 − 2,450 = 456.** The column foots on the page in Chapter 61: the count off the wall of two kilns is given as **1,680 and 1,226, which sum to 2,906**; the division in fours is given as **726 fours and two over** for the tiles and **235 fours and one over** for the hearths, **and the man with the ledger takes the hearths' one and not the tiles' two and says which is which: "Two over on the clay and one over on the fires, and the one over on the fires is the one tile in this county that has not got three brothers and has not had since the first firing." Both remainders are on the page, they belong to different quantities, and neither of them is the subtraction this chapter is for — a reader who conflated the two over with the one is reading the division wrong.** The people figure is said out loud by the man with the ledger for the first time in nineteen years, in a room, because a stranger asked, and he then says four hundred and fifty-six and stops and does not say what it is.** Aren does it in a minute and a half, out loud, and a room of nine stops. **The four hundred and fifty-six is a floor of a surplus and not a best estimate, and no figure anywhere in the volume may be got from it by scaling it**, and Chapter 66 says so out loud in the notebook and refuses to give the remainder a number. The three Ash quantities are never added together except in Chapter 61, in a kiln shed, by a man who does not go on afterwards. **The Ash *population is exact* because an approximate one cannot be subtracted from anything**, and it is exact everywhere: 2,450 in Chapters 61, 62, 66 and 70.

174. **Ash's calendar, and the twenty-three days, and they are now closed.** A **day of the rolling** is a day of a county's clay-digging and firing season; a rolling kiln is fired nine days and takes a cord of wood a day; there are **two low waters a year**; the **year is counted from the first firing**; and **the low water is the thirteenth day of the rolling**, which is item 168's figure and is consistent with the paper of the eighth of Embermonth (nineteenth day of the rolling, sixth day after the low water) and its arrival (twenty-second day, ninth day after). **Chapters 61–69 count days of being there and are dated by days of the rolling in Chapters 61, 62, 63, 66, 67, 68 and 69:** Ch 61 the first day = **the twenty-second day of the rolling, being the ninth day after the low water**, which is the same day as Chapters 59 and 60; Ch 62 the second to the fourth day = days 23–25; Ch 63 the fifth = 26; Ch 64 the sixth = 27; Ch 65 the seventh to the tenth = 28–31; Ch 66 the eleventh = 32; Ch 67 the twelfth = 33; Ch 68 the sixteenth = 37; Ch 69 the nineteenth to the twenty-third = 40–44, **with the twenty-second day the sittings and the twenty-third the last day on the far side.** **The card numbers the chapters to the nineteenth day and the four remaining days are carried in Chapter 69's tail;
