the path interval is twenty-three days and a later writer must not re-derive it as nineteen.** **No path-side chapter dates itself by a main-world day, and no main-world weekday is used anywhere in the block** — the two calendars are never read as one clock. **Ash does have weekday names of its own and they are on the page twice as the county's own: a Monday in Chapter 61 (the first day, a Monday's walk and a Friday's count) and a Friday in Chapter 62 and Chapter 65 (the clerk's ledger entry).** Those are Ash's and are not the main world's, and item 153's reason still holds — a reader cannot check Ash's calendar, so **a later writer may not build a new Ash weekday chain on them, and the chapters in this block date Ash by the rolling and not by a day of the week.** **Chapters 59 and 60 and Chapter 61 are one day, and the block is one continuous twenty-three days, and Chapter 70 is the fifth of Wolfmonth, a Tuesday, main world, twenty-six days of absence, the fever running the fifth, the sixth and the seventh and breaking on the eighth.**

175. **The Roll of Persons, the tile, and the law, as they are now on the page.** A **seal-tile, six inches by four, fired**, four faces and a back: **a device pressed with a die** (about forty dies in the county, older than most of the men who use them), **a name written in wet clay with a stick**, **a hearth**, **a trade**, and **on the back, pressed by the person themselves in wet clay, their own right hand** — and it is the only part of this county's records that is anybody's at all, and the mark is a hand and not a signature and cannot be forged by anybody who has not had the hand. **A person is on the roll if two other persons witness them AND their tile agrees with the clerk's entry — both, not either.** Quist has struck a name nine times in nineteen years, eight of them dead, and the ninth is her own second-year mistake, uncorrected on the page and corrected every morning for eighteen years. **A person who is struck is struck FROM THE COUNT, NOT FROM THE ROLL, and nobody in the county can state the difference**: Quist can say it four times and cannot give a reason, and her reason for never asking is that the two words are the same length and sit next to each other on the same page. **A struck person is alive, employed, and not consulted: cannot witness, cannot be witnessed, cannot be married into a hearth, cannot be given a name at a kiln, cannot be buried in this county's ground** — and the ground is north of the low water, in the peat, where there is no ground and no grave, and the marker carries the kiln's mark and **no name on it, because there is no name to put on it, and a man who puts a name in a marker in the peat puts a name in a book somewhere.** **The law's silent engine: a person who softens cannot be witnessed by anybody including themselves, and the law requires two witnesses, so the softening removes people by operation of the law and nobody decides it and nobody has to, and Quist's pride is that she has never had to be the one.**

176. **A struck person is given a soft tile, and a soft tile can be re-pressed by anybody, and that is the whole mechanism of the household horror.** A **soft tile is a mark in clay not yet fired; it is carried for a child until the seventh year, and there are about four hundred soft tiles in the county at any one time, lying on boards under wet cloths in racks in the cutting shed at the north end of the Ninebridge, which belongs to the Ninebridge kiln.** A soft tile goes **out of true** — it lies in a wet board for seven years and dries and wets and dries and the face creeps and the mark walks off the edge — and re-pressing is the ordinary repair, done on a Saturday, by the person, in about a minute. **A person who is struck is given one of the same tiles a child carries, and nobody in the county can say what it is for, and that is the county's whole moral life in an object.** **Ostry Vaile's mother was fired twice: the first time fired, the second time in the winter with no fire at all, on a board on a shelf in her daughter's kitchen, re-pressed with a thumb and a wet cloth by Vaile's sister's husband in the morning before he went to work, because it had gone out of true in about three weeks and he was fixing a board on a shelf; nobody asked her; Vaile did not ask her either; and after he had fixed it she had nothing that was hers, and it took Vaile a fortnight to understand that and he has not been able to put it in a sentence since.**

177. **Re-pressing a soft tile, the two failures, and the second use for the hand.** The rule: **do not press the tile, press the dish. If you press on the mark you take the mark out; if you press around the mark the dish comes up against the mark and the mark stands up in it, and a mark that stands up in a dish can be read at four feet on a wet morning.** It is a thing a child does and it takes a grown man four days, and the reason is not strength: **the difficulty is that every trade Aren has been in was taught to him by a shortage and the answer here is to be less than the thing, and a man has too much hand, and there is no such thing as too much hand in any trade he has been in.** The two failures are both fatal to the person and both are a stranger's hands: **pressed in the middle, the mark smears and the tile's mark and the clerk's entry do not agree and a person who has done nothing whatever is off the roll that evening; pressed too hard, the knuckle comes up on the back where the kiln's mark goes, and a tile that cannot be traced to a kiln is a tile a man who wanted to be on the roll could not be kept off it, and the only people in this county who would want to be on it are the two hundred and six.** **He gets it on the fourth day, on about the fortieth board, and nothing happens when he gets it, and what his hand is doing is a hand setting a thing true against a thing out of true** — the motion of Chapter 6 and Chapter 10, the fourth of the four times it has come back without asking — **and it is on the right side of the world for the first time and it belongs to a county and not a city.** He gets no strength, no stage, no rank and no authority. **He gets a second use for the same hand, which is the whole of Stage 2, and nobody in either country has taught it to him and nobody in either country knows he has it.** A Bind is not put into anything and the word is not used in the batch; the one sentence about it is *a Bind does not travel and a motion does*. The four pages of the notebook at the end of Chapter 65 and the four at the end of Chapter 69 are the two documents Batch 0008 must not contradict.

178. **The four lines of county law, the list, and the calendar of the burning.** *Where a list of persons to be struck from the count has been made under this act, and has remained unexpended for the space of one year, the assizes may cause the list to be burned in the market square at the sittings, in the presence of two magistrates and of such witnesses as the assizes shall call; and thereupon the striking of every person named in the list shall be thereby dissolved; and the clerk of the roll shall destroy the tiles of every person so named, and shall enter the destruction in the roll, and not the name.* **Signed by three magistrates, and the third signature is nine months old and the man has been dead since the eighth of the last rolling, and no character in the batch says one word about it, and the reason is that a signature is a signature.** **The List of Persons to be Struck: two hundred and six names, eleven months old, in a locked press at the assizes, of which the clerk of the roll holds the only other key.** The law wants a year; the year is up at the sittings; **the sittings sit at the end of the rolling, which the magistrate says is a fortnight off and the kiln-hands say three.** Burning the list dissolves the striking, and therefore the roll is wrong, and therefore **two hundred and six fired tiles are destroyed**, and therefore a house keeps its fire and loses its name, **and the magistrate's own sentence is that a fire with no reason anybody can give for it is not a worse thing than a name that cannot be read.** He is right about the fever and his figures are on the page: **forty-one people off the roll with the softening in the four years before the list, and a hundred and twelve in the eleven months after, by month nine, eleven, nine, thirteen, fifteen, seventeen, twenty-one, twenty-four, and no place in that column where it turns.** The list is read: by the clerk of the roll every morning, by the physician, and by the man with the ledger because he cannot leave a column unread; the magistrate has asked all three in writing and has no power over any of them and has not told the rollkeeper that he asked. **The list is not burned, at the sittings on the twenty-second day, because the clerk of the roll said no in the magistrate's own room, and her reason was not the fever and not the finding: two hundred and six tiles broken and wedged back is a day and a half of a wedging shed, and in about eleven years somebody who is not her is going to have to fire a roll for a person who has been struck and put back, and she would like the county to have spent the day and a half.** That is the first refusal in this county that works, and it is a trade reason, and it stands.

179. **Hesper Lound's finding, as it is now on the page, in his words.** Four pages, written in the third year of the softening, **in a drawer in a table in a room over a chandler at Kilnholt, and shown in nineteen years to one person, once, for nine minutes, in a room with the door shut.** *(a)* The softening enters by the mark and not through the eye: a man whose day has lost its shape, and the order of the day goes first, then the trade, then the hearth, then the name; and a man who is read carries it into the marks of his household, and a mark read rarely has not yet been read at all, **and a person is safe only until somebody in that house opens a book.** *(b)* It travels fastest along the marks that are read most, and the marks read most in this county are the roll marks, **because a clerk reads the roll every morning and does not know that she is doing anything.** *(c)* **Therefore the cure is not fewer records** — fewer records is what a frightened office does, and a record that is not read cannot be corrected, and a record that cannot be corrected is not a record, it is a claim, and this county has been living on claims for four years. *(d)* **The cure is records that do not agree with each other:** three copies, three hands, three places, none authoritative alone, a person restored when two of the three agree and removed when two of the three agree otherwise, the third being allowed to be wrong, and **no single office able to do either.** The last clause is that the difference is the person: in Quist's office a person says nothing, in his a person says everything, and the record that disagrees with the person is the record that is wrong, **and there is no such thing in this county as a record that is right and the person who is wrong, and he has looked for one for four years and has found about two hundred men for whom it would have been very useful.** His want, in his own voice: **the only thing wrong with the County of Ash is not the fever, it is that nobody has ever read a physician's finding out loud.** He has about forty pages in the drawer and three of them have been read by somebody. **The finding is a medical finding, not a moral argument, and it is the seed of the resolution. It was read out loud once in this batch, in a room with a table, and it was misunderstood twice — cleverly, by the rollkeeper, and kindly, by the magistrate — and the week was asked for after both.**

180. **Ostry Vaile's sentence, and the missing column, and the shape of the blank.** **A person who is struck has to be able to answer for himself, and if he answers he goes back on it, and if he cannot answer then he was never on it, and we have been keeping a list of things.** Said in nineteen seconds to a room of nine men at a firing floor, having been said once before in a shed in the autumn in almost the same words. **He is right, he has been right for eleven months, and no character in the county can put it in a form.** The reason is not authority: **a form requires a column; the fifth space on the struck person's form is headed *struck* and takes a name, a date and a witnessing hand, and it is about the size of the kiln clerk's two fingers, and there is no sixth space and the sheet is the same width it was the day the roll was begun, and nobody in the county has ever met the man who ruled it.** **A column is ruled before anybody knows what is going in it, and somebody ruled five and stopped, ninety-four years ago, in a room in the assizes, in an afternoon, with a knife.** The notebook's entry, in his own hand: **a cell that is ruled and has never been filled in is not the same thing as a cell that is blank because there was nothing to put in it, and there is no way to tell the two from the cell, and both look exactly the same, and a man who is given a cell has to be told which one he is looking at by somebody, and in this county the somebody is the sheet, and the sheet does not say.** **That entry is the same shape as the eighth ruled line in a served schedule in Merefen four hundred miles away, and no character in this block may say so and no character in this block knows it, and Chapter 87 is where Ash says the argument out loud and it is not said here.** The want of the struck, in their own voice and it is the volume's: **not money — to be asked.** And Vaile's other sentence, the anti-Archive position, which is the most defensible sentence anybody says in the volume: **he would rather be left alone and uncopied.** The book does not argue with it.

181. **The five terms, on a tile and in a book, and they are Ash's and not his.** Pressed in a hand that is not a clerk's and not a die's, in three sizes of writing because line two is forty-one words, with a broken piece of a saw, fired in a rolling a week old, and the same five lines in a book at the assizes. *(1) The road is not to be made to carry a person. (2) No name is to be read out on the far side of the line by anybody from the north, and no name is to be written in the line's book at the north end. (3) The north is not to send anybody who can be asked to come and look. (4) A person who is struck may answer, and if a person who is struck answers, the person is restored. (5) The road is kept by a hand at each end and a witness at each end, and the witness at Ash's end is chosen by Ash.* **A tile cannot be copied out of — to take a mark off a fired tile you chip it, and a chipped tile is a permanent account of a man having wanted a copy — and a book can be copied, and a copy in a book is a thing a court can have. So the pair is a thing nobody can quietly take out of the book and a thing everybody can be shown in the same room, and the tile is the one that cannot be lifted.** He notes on the bridge that the county has understood that in four years and a woman in a fen has understood it in eleven months, **and he does not make the comparison for her, because he has no business in it and he is going to be in a fen with a fever in about four days.** **Term four is the whole of the volume and term four is what kills the road, and the mechanism is on the page and is not magic:** to restore a struck person the roll must be re-read, at the person, with the book in the room, the tile on the table, and two witnesses whose names are in the book — **so term four is a room, with a book in it, and two hundred people to go through it one at a time, and the whole of it resting on one book in one room in one building.** He gets the tile half and the hand of the Ninebridge gets the room half and neither of them puts the two together.

182. **The notebook entry of the nineteenth day, in the correct technical vocabulary, four months early, and he does not act on it.** Set out in the trade's own words: **anchor, the roll** — one book, one room, one building, the only thing in the county that does not move; **witness, the person answering and two whose names are in the book** — and the rule in this trade is that the witness is whoever would have refused, **and here there is nobody who would have refused, and the keeper of the roll said no in one word in front of him, and the reason she would not refuse is that she is the anchor, and a witness that is also the anchor is not a witness, it is a second reading of the same hand;** **counterweight, the county** — nine hundred and forty-one hearths, a week of wedging, forty men losing a day; **bound, the promise that these two hundred are the people**, and a bound has to be the narrowest thing you can make it, the width of the split, the length of the timber, and this one is the whole of the county. **A bound is a promise, and a promise about two hundred people to one office is a shape he has had refused before him by a man in a room in another county, on a paper about a number of persons in a place that could not be asked a question, and he did not ask the reason twice and has been sorry for it for nine weeks; he has since found that there is an office and a form and a column with a word at the top of it and he has half of the other half of that, and he has decided in writing, on the fourth day of a week in a clay county four hundred miles off, to leave it, and has entered the decision so that a clerk in Merefen can see that it was a decision and not an oversight — and he does not know what the clause is and is not permitted to find out in this volume.** The figure: **he does not know what a person weighs in a working; he has set a deck for forty tons on a hand's width of oak twice in his life; he is going to say two tons each; two hundred of them is four hundred tons; and four hundred tons into a bed of river gravel at Ninebridge is on the right side of four hundred and is the correct order of size for a thing he does not understand — and A DOCUMENT'S LOAD IS NOT IN TONS, I KNOW THAT, I HAVE WRITTEN IT IN TONS ANYWAY, THAT IS THE SENTENCE TO READ IN FOUR MONTHS' TIME, and in four months, or thereabouts, which is the same thing at this rate, and I have not done the sum because the sum is in two calendars and I have not got both of them, and the one I have got does not have a week in it.** And the line that makes it the volume's most damning page: **I am going to build it anyway, and it is the second time, and the only new thing in the book today is that it is the same mistake both times and noticing it has never once stopped me, and a man who has noticed a thing twice is not being careful, he is being consistent.**

183. **The tile on the coping, re-planted on a late page and not explained, twice.** Shown at four feet in daylight in Chapter 65, on the fourth of the four days he was given at the shed, and again in his hand in Chapter 69 on the twenty-third day. **Six inches by four, about two fingers thick, fired grey clay, with a rope on it hanging in two loops and no yoke, on the coping of the second span at the Ninebridge; the mark on the face is a hand laid flat on a book, which he has seen four times in eleven years in three countries and can name in any light.** **The rope is about a year old and the tile is older than anybody alive who has ever touched it, and the man who put it there is dead and the man who might have known did not know.** It was taken down for one morning in Chapter 69 and put back on the coping. **No character in this batch explains it and no character in this batch explains it on the last page.** Two costs are on the page and both are his: **in Chapter 65 she asks him what it is and he says "I don't know" and she says "Good," and the chapter ends on his admitting that he has lied to the only person in the county who has thanked him for anything, and that a thing said out loud and believed, and believed wrongly, is a debt and not a release;** and **in Chapter 69 he does not name the mark because he has it in a notebook from a paper of the eighth of the last month and has never met the woman it belongs to, and if he uses it now the refusal she gave him on the first morning turns out to be a thing he was holding the answer to since the morning before it, and there is no worse way to lose a refusal than to arrive at it already holding the key.** He tells her it is a good tile, that the face is true, and that it should stay on the coping. **Chapter 85 is still available and is still the chapter that names the bell's absence out loud; nothing in this batch spends it.**

184. **What Aren was asked for, and what he said, and the seven days.** **Colm Arreld asked him what he thinks, in a room with a stove and a clerk, on the fifth day, and it is the first time anybody in this book has asked him for an opinion instead of a service.** He said *I would want to know what happens to the tile*, and then he had nothing, and the reason he had nothing is that the magistrate is right about the fever. **In Chapter 67 Hesper Lound asked him what he makes of the finding, and Aren refused**, and said why in the sentence the rest of the volume is built on: **if I tell this room not to burn that list, I am telling a county with a fever in it to keep a list, on my word; I am the man who puts lists into counties by arguing about them in front of witnesses; I have been doing it for eleven years and I am not going to do it again in a hat.** And the refusal the other way, which is the block's: **he is the reason anybody in that room has to be careful, and he has been the reason for about a year and a half, and he has never once been in a room where he could tell.** **In Chapter 68 he asked the county for seven days, with a price, and said in the room before they agreed that the price is not enough:** the list is not burned on the day it is a year; in the seven days every one of the two hundred and six is asked once, by somebody who is not the office, in his own shed, by a person who works with him, and he is not asked what he wants; the answers go in the asker's own hand and go nowhere anybody can produce them; the tiles are counted at the kilns by the people who made them. **Asking a community for time instead of taking it is the first time in the book he has done it and it is the most expensive thing he does in the volume.** Lound says the third term is wrong, and gives the week anyway, and says the man has got it partly from the man who is going to tell him it is wrong. **Quist says no twice, and says she will not pretend she thinks it is right.** Arreld grants it and says the wages out loud first and gives three reasons, two of them wages and one of them *I would rather be a man who waited and was wrong than a man who was right and could not do it again*, and asks the clerk to enter all three as reasons and not decisions for the first time in nine years. **What the seven days actually produced is in Chapter 69: about two hundred and six people asked, once, by cord-cutters and ash-drivers in their own sheds, in about a minute and a half a house, the answer written in the asker's own book and shut, no copy made, and it is the only work in this volume that nobody has ever written down, and the man from the north carried boards.**

185. **The copying post, the surveyor's line, and the three Fridays, and what is not resolved.** The post is at three shillings a week and was taken on the sixteenth of Fallowmonth against a schedule of forty-one inspections every line of which carries a ruled column headed *Keeper*, and it pays weekly in arrears. **Three Fridays of it are in the county's hands — nine shillings — from the seventeenth of Fallowmonth, and on the fifth of Wolfmonth the county's surveyor sent a line, not a letter, dated the third, saying the post is not held and a man is to be put in it, with no name, no day, and no mention of the three Fridays, and there is no post for the nine shillings to be in.** It is entered in Mara Vey's day-book against no person with the hour, and **it is not resolved in this batch and may not be: the post does not wait, the surveyor takes a man on in Thawmonth, and the twelve marks and ten pence of Chapter 97 is the bill for sixteen weeks and two days of a post that was not there to be initialed.** Tovan said the true thing about it in a filing room and was refused an entry for it, and Mara entered the want and refused the thing he actually asked for, in those words.

186. **Toban's yard and the bench's third number, both costed in the chapter and neither resolved here.** **Four weeks and a day on the ninth of Embermonth is twenty-nine days; less the twenty-six days Aren was gone is three days; and on the seventh a man came in off the Fallow Elms road with a house falling down, paying in wood and not money, and Tovan took it, and the yard has eleven days.** Tovan did the sum in the yard at about the second hour and put it in the yard's book with the hour **and without his brother's name, for the first time in a year, because the yard's book holds a thing set.** The Sedge Reach: **the scour under the nose of the third pier is a washtub and has been since the sixth of Mudmonth, the honest repair is eleven tons of stone and a fortnight with the road shut, and it has not been read since the ninth of Embermonth; the bench set the reading for the ninth and it did not happen, set it again for the twenty-fourth and waited until dark and it did not happen then either, and it is eleven days past that, and a bench of nine that has named a day twice and had it twice is going to name a third, and the third will be taken alone by the guild's man with the levels and Tovan will have to believe it.** The mark on the ring is a quarter of an inch low and the guild's man still refuses the guild's roll. **None of this is resolved in this batch and none of it may be.**

187. **The fever, the stance, and the thing in the notebook that is not in a form, in the state they are now in.** **He comes up at the first light of the fifth of Wolfmonth with a fever of three days — the fifth, the sixth and the seventh, breaking on the eighth — and with a way of standing that is a ferrywright's on a slip: weight a little back, hands loose at his sides and not in his pockets, and he has been doing it since the ninth of Embermonth and has not noticed.** **Mara Vey noticed it before he did, has watched a man do it on a slip from her window for four years, and is the only person in the county who would have seen it. She is not going to ask him and she has written down that she knew for a month before she did anything about it.** **The three dates her entry turns on, and they are the only three: the office decided on the twenty-second of Fallowmonth not to ask him again; she noticed that the not asking had changed on about the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth, which is a month before she wrote it down and is also the first line on that page; and she wrote it on the fifth of Wolfmonth, which is about two months after the promise. Those are the spans and a later writer may not lengthen any of them. The four lines came into her outer room on the twenty-ninth of Longlight and she copied them on the second of Fallowmonth, which is why she has been unable to make head or tail of them for about two months, and the Crown's letter has been in a locked room over a cooper since Longlight and is the other thing she is not asking about.** **He gave her every number he has got and in that order: twenty-six days of absence from the fourth hour of the ninth of Embermonth to the morning of the fifth; a path interval of twenty-three days; the printed three-week warning; an error of five days; and the fever.** He also told her, unasked, that he has known since the third day of his time there that the man at the kiln in the fourth line of the second answer is the man, and did not know until the sixth hour of the fifth that the man was in the room, and asked for that to be entered with the hour. **He has not told her about the morning with Tovan and may not until Chapter 91.** The palm is not stated to be warm in this batch and no warmth in a coping is stated twice, both times as cold: **in Chapter 59 on the clay steps and in Chapter 69 on the way down, and the second is a fact he writes down about a hand and a habit four months later and does not know is a fact yet.** **No melody cost was added anywhere in this batch and none may be.** No stage was claimed and none awarded.

188. **Cast, and the count, and what was spent.** **Four new names first named in prose in Batch 0007 — Pernel Quist, Colm Arreld, Hesper Lound, Ostry Vaile — and two slots unfilled, as the card allows.** **Bel, four, of Kilnholt, is named in Chapter 64 and is a child and does not count against the cap, and she is a child at a board doing the check round on soft tiles, and her own tile is pressed wrongly on the third day and re-pressed correctly on a stranger's word of one word, and nothing whatever in this batch looks at what is on it.** **Chapter 86 is where the soft tile with a river in it is shown, and it is not shown here, and no character in this batch describes any mark a child has pressed.** Ilsa Soke is a returned face named on the page in Chapter 60 and **is not named in this batch**; she is *the hand of the Ninebridge* and *the hand*, she does not correct him, and the reason is given in Chapter 64 and again in Chapter 69. **The kiln clerk is addressed as the man with the ledger throughout and his name is withheld on the page by design and must not be given in this volume.** **The volume total is now nine of eighteen, and the unfilled slots are three of twelve.** Nobody in the batch dies. Nobody in the batch is killed. No unnamed person is arranged into a death for effect.

189. **The Batch 0007 review repair pass — the twelve findings applied, and the four that had already been written into this file.** Twelve findings were applied to Chapters 61–70 and three to the Batch 0008 prompt, and **four of the errors had been canonised in this file, in `chapter-summaries.md`, in `batch-summaries.md`, in `open-threads.md` and in `current.md`, so all four are corrected in the prose and at source.** **No plot beat was removed, no chapter was renumbered, no card was broken, and the planned volume direction is untouched.** The things a later writer must know because the batch had them wrong:

- **(1) The drift is absence against warning, never path interval against warning.** The error is **main-world absence minus the printed three weeks: 26 − 21 = 5 days**, and 88 − 21 = 67 for the long crossing (item 163). The path interval is a different number — twenty-three days — and 23 − 21 = 2 is not the error and means nothing. **Chapter 70 said *Path interval twenty-three days against a printed warning of three weeks, and I have done the sum, and the sum is five days*, which was wrong in its derivation and right in its figure; he now names the absence and the warning as the pair and says on the page that he did not sum the pair he says out loud first.** Volume 01's is still minus fifteen days (item 151) and the printed warning is still three weeks.
- **(2) The blank at the bottom of the form is ninety-four years old and not a hundred and forty.** Ninety-four is canon (item 180) and is now the only figure in both paragraphs of the Chapter 66 entry. **A hundred and forty does three separate jobs in this batch and none of them is years** — it is a tile figure in the Chapter 66 notebook, three times, deliberately, and he says he is aware of what he has done. A writer who finds *a hundred and forty years* in an Ash scene is reading a draft.
- **(3) Chapter 61's two divisions leave two over and one over, and the man answers for the one.** 2,906 in fours is 726 fours **and two over**; 941 in fours is 235 fours **and one over**. The *one tile in this county that has not got three brothers* is the **hearths'** single remainder, and the chapter now says which remainder he means. An earlier state text recorded it as the tiles', which would have let a later writer correct the prose back into an error.
- **(4) Mara's not asking is about two months old and her page is about a month old, and they are not the same figure.** The promise is the **twenty-second of Fallowmonth**, the noticing is **about the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth** (which is also the first line on that page), and the entry is the **fifth of Wolfmonth**. **"Four months" was on the page four times and could not be true of any of them, and the Chapter 69 notebook's *"in four months, or thereabouts"* is a different thing entirely and is untouched** — that one is the deliberate Chapter 92 calendar breach, in his own hand, and is not a duration and is not to be tidied.

Also applied, and not load-bearing but each of them is a figure a reader can check: **the twenty-ninth of September in Chapter 70 is now the twenty-ninth of Longlight** — there is no Earth month anywhere in this book and there was one instance of one; **the surveyor's line came on Monday and is dated the third**, because the nearest Saturday is the second and a line cannot be dated the day after it arrives, and the Batch 0008 prompt's hardening of the third as the writing date stands; ***that was nine days ago, and it is eleven days now* is now eleven and eleven**, because the twenty-fourth of Embermonth to the fifth of Wolfmonth is eleven days; **the list is two hundred and six names and not a hundred and forty**, and the three signatures belong to the four lines and the sentence now says so; **Chapter 62's third day is the twenty-fourth day of the rolling and not the twenty-fifth**, and the population figure was said in a kiln shed on the first morning and not "yesterday"; **Chapter 65's four days are the seventh to the tenth and the chapter now ends on the tenth**, which is what makes its own notebook's *ten days* true and hands Chapter 66 an undisputed eleventh instead of two chapters fighting over one; and **a smeared mark takes about four years to become a grey nothing, on the woman's own figure in Chapter 64, and not a hundred and forty days.**

**And one reconciliation rather than a change.** Ash's own weekday names are on the page twice — a Monday and a Friday, both the county's own — so item 174's old flat statement that no path-side weekday is used anywhere in the block was over-broad as written. It is rewritten to keep the rule that matters (no main-world weekday in a path scene) and to forbid the real risk, which is a later writer building a new Ash weekday chain on two chance mentions. No prose changed for this.

## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 70

- **Aren and Mara:** the batch's real movement is that **they are now two people who have each decided in advance what they will not ask, and only one of them has said so out loud.** She wrote in Fallowmonth that the office is not going to ask him again; he wrote in Greenmonth on a cost sheet that the error scales with the work and told one person. **On the fifth of Wolfmonth at the eighth hour she wrote in the day-book that she is not going to ask him about the way he stands, and she entered the reason, and the reason is that the not asking has changed from a protection into a thing she is doing to him: a protection is entered in a file, and this is entered in a day-book over a weigh-house, and she has noticed the difference, and she has noticed it late, and about two months is how long it took her to write it down instead of doing it.** He gave her every number he has, in the order a reporter gives numbers, and asked for nothing, and told her one true thing he was not asked for, and she entered his figures on the office's own account against no person and wrote under them that the office did not ask why, and that the reason it did not ask is entered elsewhere and has a date on it. **She asked him one question, about a document, and apologised for it in plain words, because a man who is asked a question wants one he can take.** He told her the one question he has for her since Thursday and did not ask it, and the reason is that he has used up his asking in a clay county. **The two path dates in the main-world frame have not happened. He came back with a day off a county's own calendar, copied it exactly, in order, with the hour, and wrote under it that a man in a filing room is going to read a copy of that line in about seven months and will not be able to tell from the line which side of it the year was on, and he has now said the same thing a second time about a sum in two calendars and a week.** **Nothing has been lied to. That is the volume's standing danger and Chapter 92 is its date.**
- **Aren and Tovan:** **the estrangement is named a second time, in a filing room, in a different county, and it is not healed.** Tovan shut a door in that room that he has not shut in four years; he would not let his brother out of a chair and would not help him and said both were on purpose; he brought the yard's figures and the bench's third number and the surveyor's line because the batch's world is a labour world and those are the only three things in it; and **he did the sum in the yard at the second hour and entered it with the hour and without his brother's name for the first time in a year, and said out loud that not one word of that arithmetic is about whether he forgives him.** He refused Mara an entry for the thing he actually wanted, she entered the want and refused the thing, and he said: *I have watched four years of it from the other side of a street and I would like one line in it in my brother's hand that somebody wanted.* **Nobody has re-taught anybody anything and nobody has said the word brother.**
- **Aren and the hand of the Ninebridge:** the block's central relationship, and **it is conducted entirely in trades and entirely without her name.** He calls her *the hand* and she does not correct him, and the reason she gives is that a job can be asked to turn up and a person cannot. She gives him work on the terms **that he is useful and not a visitor**, and the term is kept: he carries boards, he re-presses tiles, he asks nobody anything for nine hours, and **he tells her no name, no opinion, no argument and no reason, and that is the only way the two hundred and six got asked in this county.** She warns him about the county and the rolling and the kiln-hands and the cutting-bench man's suspicion, all of it correctly, and once about herself: *I do not lie to a man about what he is going to have to stand in a room with.* He lies to her once, about the coping, and she thanks him for it, and the chapter says that is a debt and not a release. **He does not use the name on the paper of the eighth of Embermonth and the reason is given on the page: arriving at a refusal already holding the key is the worst way to lose it.** He does not use it in this block and **may not use it in this volume.** She asks him the question she asks everybody, at the end, and it is the same question she asked a stranger on a bridge on the first morning: **are you the one who sends people** — and it is asked because he has just asked the county for time, and time is the only thing in that county anybody ever wants from her.
- **Aren and Pernel Quist:** two professionals who are each right, four days, and no alliance. **She gives him the law in four sentences, the five exclusions, and the argument for the tile — the account of a person that does not need them — and she gives him the drawer, out loud, without showing it to him, and her conclusion from it is correct and wrong and she has had four years to arrive at it.** She lets him look along forty-one shelves with a lamp and then says she has not looked a third time in nineteen years because a woman who goes looking for a thing she is going to find finds it as a person who wanted it. **She says no to him twice, in his own room, with a physician in it, and says she will not pretend she thinks it is right, and she does the thing he asked for anyway; and she says no to the magistrate, in his own room, with no stranger in it, and it is the first refusal in this county that works, and the reason she gives is a day and a half of a wedging shed and a roll she would like still to exist in eleven years.** He has not been given a form and has not asked for one.
- **Aren and Colm Arreld:** **the volume's decent man, and the first time in this book that a man with power has asked Aren for his opinion and the first time he has found he has nothing.** Arreld reads the four lines of his own law off the table by heart, defends a clause that may be a mercy and may be a cruelty, and has read the physician's finding and disagrees with it, and then reads it the way a kind man reads a true thing: as the same claim in another language, and offers to put a physician's name on his own instrument and asks a stranger to help him. **Aren sees the file the sentence will be in in about two seconds and does not say the sentence, and the reason he does not say it is on the page and it is the batch's central professional fact: every sentence he says in that room is a thing a magistrate can pick up and put on a fire.** He asks for seven days and gets them, and **the seven days are cheap, and he knows he has been let off cheaply, and Arreld gave them for the wages and not for the argument and minuted the reason in those words.**
- **Aren and Hesper Lound:** four pages, forty in the drawer, four years, one reading, one refusal. **Lound's want is that the finding be read out loud in a room with a table, and he got it from a stranger, and he used him as the instrument because a stranger has nothing to protect.** He is wrong twice in the block — the third term of the seven days, and the magistrate's reading — and right about everything else, and he gives the week anyway and says the man has got it partly from the man who is going to tell him it is wrong. **He is also the man who said the softening enters by the mark, and he said it first, four years ago, in a drawer, and nobody in the county read it out loud until a sett-cutter was in the room.**
- **Aren and Ostry Vaile:** one scene, one room, and the two sentences that are the volume. **Vaile's sentence is the answer and it is the most dangerous sentence anybody said to Aren in five months, and Aren recognised both of those in about a second and a half and told nobody and will not tell anybody, and the reason is on the page: once it is in a form in his own head it gets into his own hands.** Vaile also said the sentence the book cannot argue with — *I would rather be left alone and uncopied* — and Aren tried the counter-argument honestly and Vaile answered it with the number four. **Vaile's mother was destroyed in a winter by a man fixing a board on a shelf, and that is term four's other half and it is already on the page before term four exists.**
- **Mara and Aren, and the day-book:** the last line of the block is in a day-book and not a file, on a page that has had nothing against it and no reason on it since the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth, and the three entries on that page are now: *I wanted to fill it and did not*; *I wanted to fill it because I think I know*; and *I have seen a thing about a man that he has not told me and I am not going to ask him, and I know I am doing it for me and not for him, and I am not going to pretend that I have not noticed that I have stopped noticing.* **A keeper's own wants are the only part of the business that is not in a book, and they have now been entered in one, and the entry is about a man, and that is a first.**

## Canon established by Batch 0008 (Volume 02, Chapters 71–80, prose on the page)

**How to use this section.** Items 1–189 are the record of Volume 01, of the Volume 02 plan, of Chapters 51–60 and of Chapters 61–70 with its review repair pass. Items **190 onward** are the record of the ten chapters that are now on the page, and Batch 0009 is written against them. **Nothing in 190 onward may be contradicted by Chapter 81 or later, and nothing in it re-derives an earlier item.**

190. **The day map of the block, closed, and the two of Chapter 70's facts that were not free.** Main world: Ch 71 the **ninth of Wolfmonth, a Saturday, a market day, the fourth hour** · Ch 72 the **fifteenth, a Friday, not a market day** · Ch 73 the **twenty-first, a Thursday, not a market day** · Ch 74 the **fourth of Frostmonth, a Wednesday, a market day, the bench and the second asking** · Ch 75 and 76 the **ninth of Frostmonth, a Monday, a market day**, the election at the fourth hour and the count in the afternoon · Ch 77 the **fourth of Hearthmonth, a Friday, a market day, the crossing out at the fourth hour.** Path time, Chapters 78–80: **the first twenty-two weeks of the nine months and six days, the fourth week, the eleventh week and the twenty-second week, dated only by the rolling, the two low waters and the year counted from the first firing, and no main-world weekday is used anywhere in them.** **The county's market days are Saturday and Thursday and the ninth of Wolfmonth, the fourth of Frostmonth and the fourth of Hearthmonth are markets because the chain gives them to be; the fifteenth and the twenty-first of Wolfmonth are a Friday and a Thursday and are not, and no chapter in this batch wants a day.** Two dates that are facts about Chapter 70 and were not moved: **the copying post pays weekly in arrears and the surveyor's line is dated the third of Wolfmonth** (Ch 71 and Ch 76, both of which call it the third), **and the yard job off the Fallow Elms road was taken on the seventh of Wolfmonth, a Thursday**, which is the same Thursday the chapter calls Thursday, and the ash-wood came in on it in three carts. **A state file said the seventh of Fallowmonth and the page said Thursday inside the ninth of Wolfmonth, and the page is right: the only Thursday inside this block's week is the seventh.** **Arithmetic of this block a reader can check: the twenty-first of Wolfmonth to the ninth of Frostmonth is eighteen days and the chapter says eighteen; the fifteenth of Wolfmonth to the ninth of Frostmonth is twenty-four days and the chapter says twenty-four; the thirteenth to the seventeenth of Wolfmonth is four days; **the seventeenth of Wolfmonth to the ninth of Frostmonth is twenty-two days, and the chapter and the five state locations that carried twenty-five were wrong and are now twenty-two**; the ninth of Wolfmonth to the ninth of Frostmonth is thirty-one days; the fifth of Wolfmonth to the ninth of Frostmonth is thirty-four days; **and a letter dated the twelfth of Wolfmonth arriving on the fifteenth is three days, which is the same three days Mara Vey says is the only fact in her business she cannot do anything about.** the ninth of Wolfmonth to the ninth of Frostmonth is thirty-one days.**

191. **The two eighth ruled lines, and they are not the same line, and nothing in this batch joins them.** (a) **The guild's maintenance specification of two leaves**: the first leaf is the work and the second is the guild's standing conditions, **of which there are eight**, and **the eighth is a seat** — *that a seat so cut will be used and will fail, and that the number of uses is not stated here, because it is not a thing this bench may know.* **In Chapter 74 the guild's man with the levels does not read the eighth, gives his reason in his own words, and has it entered with his reason and not the bench's**, and the reason is that a leaf that is reprinted is a leaf that has had a line taken off it, and in nine years somebody will want to know what the line said, **and that there is no seat in this county and the number of uses of a seat that is not there is not a thing this bench may know either.** (b) **The served schedule of the eighth of Fallowmonth** has **eight ruled lines and the eighth is the city's and is empty**, and in Chapter 76 a hand is laid flat on the weight over it for about four seconds as a way of not doing it. **The two are in different chapters, in different rooms, in front of different people, and no character in this batch refers to the other or wonders about either. The city is still not on the served schedule at the end of the volume.**

192. **The count, and the two numbers, and the fact that they are not the same count.** **Eleven on the fourth of Embermonth at the fourth hour, read out in a hall in the presence of forty persons, with a date and an hour, entered in the day-book against no person and not on the guild roll because a roll in that town has a *Keeper* column on it now. Eleven again in the return that went to the province on the ninth of Frostmonth. Twelve in the guild hall on the ninth of Frostmonth at about a quarter past four, because Bram Ottery was put on the roll and elected and the roll went from thirty-one names to thirty-two.** The reeve reads both and enters both with the date of each and enters **that they are not the same count, because one is a count of persons and one is a count of names on a roll, and if a clerk in two hundred years adds one of these to the other it will be a good sum of two true things and a lie.** **A count that goes up by one when a man is given a vote is a count measuring the wrong thing and nobody in the room can prove it and neither Chapter 75 nor Chapter 76 pretends otherwise.** **Chapter 76's return is in full and the four that are in a file are all in the figure and none is named, and the column headed *Keeper* on the sheet that went is empty and no reason is entered, and the reason is not entered because entering a reason in that column is a thing the form's own printing tells a person to do.**

193. **The office's three lines, dated the thirteenth of Wolfmonth, and the whole of the Crown's escalation in kind.** *Received. The office notes that a count of persons is not a list and proposes a count of persons for the county as a whole.* **Three lines, signed, in a woman's hand and not a clerk's, dated at the place of issue the thirteenth of Wolfmonth — a Wednesday — which is four days after Toban's nil return of the ninth went out of the yard in Weir Street.** It came into the Archive on the seventeenth, was entered in the day-book against no person with the hour, and **was not read until the ninth of Frostmonth, twenty-two days later, because it asked nothing of that office and named nobody in it, and a paper a keeper has not read is a paper she has answered.** **Aren Kest is the only person in the book who could have said what it is. On the ninth of Frostmonth he is twenty-five yards from the door and will not be in the county in three weeks, and the reason nobody sent for him is a binding agreement made at a bench in a guild hall over a wharf on the ninth of Embermonth, whose only two holders in this county are two men, one of whom is not a member of any bench and the other of whom does not vote.** The day-book entry says, in full: a count of persons who have had knowledge of a declared object is a different thing from a count of the persons in a county; this office has no instrument that can touch a figure for a county; the county's schedule of provincial offices has been searched and does not carry one; **and this office never asked for a figure for a county — it did not occur to me, it occurred to them on the thirteenth of Wolfmonth, and I am the only person in this county who has read both documents and I do not see anything in them.** **Sabin Dain's second letter is dated the twelfth of Wolfmonth, arrived on the fifteenth, is signed, asks for a figure of persons, carries the four words *how the figure was compiled* under the figure, and is returnable on or before the ninth of Frostmonth — the same returnable date as the instrument at the county court, which is the office's one date in this county.** The three-day gap between a thing's date and its arrival is on the page in Mara's own mouth as **the only fact in her business she has never been able to do anything about**, and it was three days in Fallowmonth and it is three days in Wolfmonth and it will be three days in Greenmonth.

194. **A count is a list with the names taken off, and a nil is a figure, and both are now on the page three times in three trades in one block.** **Chapter 71, a yard:** the form is a ruled table wanting a county, then the hundred, then a figure, then *how the figure was compiled*, and **there is no column and no box for a name anywhere on the sheet and no date on it**; the answer gives the number of the lines (nine), the hour (the fourth), a description of the man and the fact that his name was not asked for, **and against the space for a figure, nil — "nil is a figure, and I enter it because it is true and because it is the only figure in this county that cannot be asked about a person."** **And the count returns of that office are undated, and an undated form with no date on it does not have to be answered, and an instrument that need not be answered and cannot be refused is an instrument that can be put through any door in the kingdom without asking the man at the door first; nobody in the batch says that the office knows this and Tovan suspects it.** **Chapter 73, a court:** a returnable paper has a party; this one names no person and no body; it names a county; **a county is not a party and cannot sue or be sued and has no officer in this kingdom that can take a return; therefore the paper has no party, and a returnable paper with no party at the end of its own stated period has not been answered and has not been refused, and it expires — and refused is an act and an act can be appealed.** **Chapter 76, a filing room:** eleven, with the *Keeper* column left empty. **The three are the same answer to the same instrument in three trades, and Tovan Kest says in a gateway on a market day that the answer is the answer somebody else is making in a book about a different set of things, and that he does not know who and has not asked, and that he did not get it off anybody and whoever is making it did not get it off him, and that neither of them has ever seen the other's face, and that if the two of them did get it out of the same place then there is a third thing that knows it and neither of them has met it. He does not name the other person, does not give a gender and gives no distance. That is the card's withholding and a later writer may not put a name, a sex or a number on that other person in Volume 02.**

195. **A court can beat an instrument with the form's own construction, and the price of every good answer is that the body giving it can be sued.** Lysa Fenn's finding of the fourth of Greenmonth, five clauses, stands with the fifth as the mechanism: **any office thereafter acting in the matter is on notice that this court asked and was not answered, and that the asking is dated, and the asking is dated on or before the fourth of Greenmonth, which is the date of the finding, and the instrument at her desk on the twenty-first of Wolfmonth **is dated the twelfth of that month, arrived on the fifteenth, three days on the road like everything else, and has been on her desk since the fifteenth without an answer on it.** **A paper cannot arrive before it is dated, and an earlier draft had it arriving on the eighth of Greenmonth and dated the tenth or eleventh; the dates are now the twelfth and the fifteenth, which keeps the mechanism intact and keeps the volume's three-day post rule.** **The mechanism beats an instrument of a later date because the later instrument is dated after the asking and the fifth clause does not require anybody to have read it; what it requires is only that the act be dated, and the act is dated, and there is not one clerk in that building who has ever turned to the second page of a finding of a county court.** Her fear, in her own book, in three numbered parts, and it is the volume's last move stated by the person it will happen to: **she has built the best answer in this county out of the fact that this court is not a person, and in about four years somebody with a good head and a clean sheet is going to make this office a person, and a person can be a party and can be in default and can be fined and can be shut, and the answer dies; and there is no procedure in that office for withdrawing anything, so an endorsement once given stands for ever, while a magistrate can only be removed by a petition of the county, which takes two years and a majority — a woman can be removed in an afternoon by a man in a room, and she has nine of the other kind behind her and would take four more.** **Marda Oakes, 45, of the county town, a member of the county council, is on the page in Chapter 76 and wants the Archive made a body corporate, and the reason is given in her own voice and half of it is deliberately unsaid.** Her want, as it stands: **an office with a door can be sued, and a keeper can only be dismissed, and a town is safer with things it can sue.** **Nobody in that room says that sentence and Chapter 76 does not say it either and the reader can.** What is on the page instead: that a county council can write to a keeper and be told no, and that a woman who said no can be told she said no, and that the county then has no remedy; and that the finding of the fourth of Greenmonth is **a notice, and a notice does not stop anybody, it only stops people who read them, and there are four of those in this county and three of them work for this office**; and the reason she stops mid-sentence, given out loud: **you are not an office yet, and when you are one, everybody who helped you will be a person who helped you.** **She asks no question about the file, which is the first time in about a year anybody has come into that room and not wanted a name out of it, and she leaves a card with a name, a county and a room and no seal and no office, and it is the only time in four years Mara Vey has entered a card against a person.**

196. **The counting post, the three Fridays, and Rowan Petch's return, all as they now stand.** **The post is gone; the county's surveyor sent a line dated the third of Wolfmonth saying it is not held and a man is to be put in it, with no name, no day and no mention of the three Fridays, and the nine shillings has no post to be in. It is not resolved in this batch and may not be: the surveyor takes a man on in Thawmonth and the twelve marks and ten pence of Chapter 97 is the bill for sixteen weeks and two days of a post that was not there to be initialed.** **Rowan Petch, 26, went on the county post at the first hour on the eighth of Embermonth, was three days on the road at his own cost, came back on the twelfth and filed on the twelfth, and was three days back by the fifteenth.** The return is printed in Chapter 72 in full: four facts about a box, and **the court cannot state the purpose for which the file is held, and the court does not decline to state it, and if the office holds a purpose then the office is the holder of it and is the only body in this kingdom that can state its own purpose in its own hand under its own seal, and this court requests that it do so, and will then answer it.** **The line *I have met both* is in the second position on purpose, because a sentence in the first position is the point and a sentence in the second position is an address, and a man in a hurry reads an address.** **His want is unchanged and in his own words: to have been careful once while somebody was watching, and being careful when nobody is watching turning out to be the cheapest thing a young man can do with his life.** **And the bomb, in a slow fuse: on the twelfth, in the outer room, a clerk reading a column out loud got to a county and said *this one, the file, has a person in it now*, went on to the next county, and four seconds later said a name. It is Sabin Dain, 33, an aid to the register, eleven years in the office, not a member of anything, who told Petch so twice in a corridor. The office has entered her into the county file in the matter of the crossing of Alder Vale as the person having knowledge, in her own hand, on the fourth of Embermonth. A name in a county file is a person who can be produced. Mara Vey looked at the name, asked for no further fact about the woman, entered that she had not asked, and gave the reason: a keeper who holds five facts about a person begins assembling them, and a keeper who assembles facts about a person is a person with an interest in a person.**

197. **The eleven-year-old salvage filing, refused four times and in writing once, and the fifth time promised.** Lysa Fenn took an eleven-year-old salvage filing from Aren Kest in YR 301 and has **refused to discuss it three times in advance, on purpose, so that he would not be surprised when it came; and in Chapter 73 a request in writing and signed, received on the eighteenth of Wolfmonth, asks the court to state whether it holds any record relating to a declared object in the matter of Alder Vale in the year three hundred and one in the matter of salvage, and she refuses it once, in writing, and puts the reason on the file, because a filing of eleven years standing in that matter is the only account a certain person has of a certain thing and this court is not going to be the office that tells a stranger it is there.** She tells him in the room that this is the fourth time, that if it comes a fifth time she will answer it, and that she has told him now so that the fifth is not a surprise either. **He was told three times in advance on purpose, is not surprised, says nothing, and says nothing twice, and both times correctly. A later writer may make somebody ask about the fact of the filing in writing once more and be refused, and may not make anybody ask about it more than once in the whole of the case, and the contents of the filing are not explained in this volume.**

198. **The bench, the three askings, the second one, and the sentence that is now on the page about all three.** **Four marks and six a year; three marks and about six a campaign; a chest with two marks in it; the second of the two visits voted in Longlight is the fourth of Frostmonth and it is a Wednesday.** The work, footed: **four marks three shillings and fivepence, being 233 pence — lime 72, sand and oak-dust 36, tarred hemp 80, nails and cramps 18, three days' labour 27 — against two marks in the chest (96) and two marks of subscriptions promised in notes (96), so four marks in hand and short by forty-one pence, which is three shillings and fivepence.** **The second asking is the materials, and it is minuted as the second, and it is to be found by subscription and not by rate, because a subscription is a thing a man can refuse in person and a rate is a thing a man finds out about in a quarter, and the whole of the subscription goes in the market book with the names and the figures so that in nine years a man may go and see whether this town was asked or was told.** **The third asking is Chapter 97, on the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth, in the same paragraph as the bill and the fortnight with the road shut, and no character in the Chapter 74 room may call it the third, and none of them does.** **Tovan Kest's true thing, said for the fourth time in six months, standing, with the level case down on the table: a bench that is generous decides, a bench that is asked is being told what it is for, and a bench that is asked three times and says yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription — and he has watched it happen once, in Longlight, four hundred miles off, in a city whose name he will not say in a hall that keeps a roll, where a bench of nine said yes in the first month of a winter, yes again in the third, and yes a third time in the eighth, and by the ninth it was voting on a list made somewhere else with every vote unanimous and recorded as unanimous, and a book in that city has four years of unanimous minutes in it and not one disagreement in the whole of it.** **It is anticipatory and the day has not come, and he asks on the page that it be minuted that he said it while it was still early. And the minute ends: *The sum is short by three shillings and fivepence.*** **The ordinary work and its trade fact: a chisel that is warm rings and a man can hear whether he has found a natural bed or has made one, and in a hard frost the iron is dead, and the fourth course of the Old Toll can therefore honestly be re-set once a year by nine men who are not a bridgewright's crew.** The disputed course is at the fifth line of the first leaf: **the man at the fish weir, 61, wants the opening at the joint above the dressed block left open the width of a hand, in register, for his own reason and not the town's — a dressed stone in a splice is a lie about the fact that the stone moved, and there should be one place in this town where a man can stand on a bridge and look at a mark and know the mark is where the thing was — and the guild's man with the levels shows him, by holding the rule himself for two hours, that it is not a weep but a bed of shells gone out of the second course about eleven years ago; the bench does the work, courses dressed and re-set and the opening left, and the man at the fish weir asks that the foundry's man vote against it out loud so that it is in the book that somebody disagreed, and he does.** **The finding of that hall is the man at the fish weer's own rule in tradesman's words, arriving from outside the guild, and it is not Aren's rule and no character connects them.**

199. **The election of the ninth of Frostmonth, the guild's *Keeper* column, and the sentence the volume ends on.** **The roll of the Merefen bridge guild is nineteen hands long with four columns and the third is headed *Keeper*, put there three months ago because a form came down from two hundred miles off with a column with the same word at the top of it; a column headed *Keeper* wants somebody against it, and a bench that appoints its officers in private has a form and an answer that do not match, and on the ninth of Frostmonth the bench votes for the first time in eleven years, and the reeve has the reason entered, and the reason is a column.** **A reeve has a casting vote when the bench is level and a casting vote is not a vote, and that is the mechanism of her nine years' silence and it is on the page, and she has said the same sentence in the same words every year because a woman who improves her own reasons is a woman who has noticed she is being asked twice.** **The reeve was re-elected, eight hands, she did not vote on her own election, and the minute is the shortest in the roll and the *against* column has the word *none* in it for the first time in nineteen years.** **Bram Ottery, 47, is on the roll, four for, four against, one abstention, the reeve's casting vote, and the roll stands at thirty-two; his reason for asking is a vote and not an office, and the guild clerk reads the yard's entry twice and enters that the arrangement of the witness's fourpence a week and the hour and not the reason is **two months** old and has not been improved on — **the ninth of Embermonth to the ninth of Frostmonth is sixty-one days, which is two months and neither four nor a year** — and is not re-litigated and is not improved on.** **Aren Kest is elected road warden of the Merefen bridge guild, carrying the Sedge Reach, the wharf road and the winter work, by eight hands and a reeve's, four and four with the casting vote, and says: I accept the vote and not the office. A vote is a thing a town gives a man, in a room, with a date on it. An office is a thing a man is held to. I will come when I am wanted and I will read anything put in front of me and I will say the true thing and I will not vote and I will not hold the crossing, and I do not intend to be the man who holds this crossing.** **He is on the roll and elected and will have a vote on a question that is not about him, and he notices two seconds late that a refusal had cost him nothing — that the price was on the man afterwards and the price was a bench that had learned his name was worth a vote — and he says nothing, because a man who argues in a room that he ought to be disqualified from a vote has asked the room to do his arithmetic.** **The Sedge Reach is the crossing the office carries and it is a different bridge from the Old Toll, which is four hundred yards up the road and where he cut a seat once and paid for it out of his own money and is not the man of.**

200. **Reeve Aldis Fenn's second refusal, in the same words, and the one thing she did that she has not done in nine years.** **On the fourth of Hearthmonth the bench has before it one item of ordinary business with a date on it — that the road be kept, as it is kept, on this day — and it carries, eight hands and the reeve's, and the reeve voted, and the bench-clerk enters *the reeve voted* without comment and reads it back and nobody asks him to.** And she is in the yard at the same hour with a sheet and a rule, because everybody looked at her when the witness line was read out, **and she refuses the fourth line in the words of the ninth of Embermonth, word for word, and asks that the reason be minuted in the words she is about to use and not in better ones: a road this town does not own cannot have this town as its witness, and if it has, then in four years somebody will say the town stood there, and the town will be right and the road will be shut, and the road will have been shut by a signature and not by an argument, and an argument is a thing that can be had again on a different day by a different bench with a different temper in it. She will hold the sheet.** **The man at the drawknife said nothing the second time, and the chapter says why: hearing it twice is a different thing from hearing it once. Her own addition, minuted: a man may do one of the two and not the other, and it took her nine years to find out which of the two she would rather do, and it is not the one she thought when she started.** **She is now the independent witness at the Merefen end of a channel and she has declined to sign as one.**

201. **The crossing out of the fourth of Hearthmonth, and the arrangement, and the one sentence that is not about a road.** **Two hands, a date, a line, and a witness at each end who is not the person keeping the road. The line is made up at the third hour in nine minutes with the hour against each of the nine and the splice at this end is the one splice in that yard, cut in Fallowmonth out of a cooper's bin. Merefen's end: Tovan Kest the hand, Bram Ottery the witness, the toll-holder's clerk with the line's book, and the reeve with a sheet. The five terms are read out at the reeve's request from a fair copy and the second line is read twice at the boy's asking, and the second line is forty-one words long and it is the reason the meeting is quiet.** **The gate is not opened from Merefen. There is no gate at this end and nothing whatever happens at this end until the far end decides to open it, and the gate is the five terms, and it took about four minutes in a gateway in Weir Street in a hard month with four men and a boy and a reeve with a sheet and a rope doing nothing at all.** **The far end of the line is the hand of the Ninebridge and a person the county chose, and no name is read out on the far side by anybody from the north and no name is written in the line's book at the north end, and the name on the paper of the eighth of Embermonth has still not been used, and the not writing went into the yard's book as a fact and not as a want.** **Nine months and six days.** He comes up on clay steps and the road opens, and it opens because a county wrote down what it wanted and a town wrote down what it wanted and the two did not have to want the same thing. **The one sentence in the yard that was not about a road, said by Tovan at the gate with his hand on the rope: the yard is a fortnight, and when you come back it will be a fortnight or it will not be, and if it is not then I will have had to put a man off, and it will be Tarrow, and I will not be able to pay him and he will not be able to ask me, and I am telling you now so that on the day you come back and I say nothing about it, you will not think it was your brother.** And at the end: **the bell at the Old Toll is rung by a boy of fifteen alone, leaned on the third stroke, and there is no bell at the far end of that bell and there never has been, and about four hundred people in a county that has never once refused anything in nineteen years went to work without one of them ringing a bell, because there is no bell in that county and nobody there has ever had a reason to know it.**

202. **The ash-wood in Toban's yard, and the coincidence that is a coincidence.** **Eleven cords of ash came in on the seventh of Wolfmonth, a Thursday, in three carts as the price of a wall off the Fallow Elms road, which pays in wood and not in money, and on the ninth of Wolfmonth the man at the drawknife says, with a sett in his hand and his eyes on the end of it, *that is good ash,* and that a trade four hundred miles off would give a good price for it by the cord, and nobody asks him what trade, and nobody in the chapter acts on it, and nobody in the chapter knows why it matters.** **Ash is the fuel of a thing that has not been built yet and is not in this county, and the man at the drawknife splits one cord into a corner and stacks it higher and drier off the floor, and Tovan Kest sees him do it and does not ask him why, and it is on the record that he did not ask. No Ash figure, no kiln figure and no hearth figure is on the page anywhere in Chapters 71–77, and the wood is not a plant anybody may pull on: it is a price, and a price in a yard is a fact about a yard.**

203. **The yard's arithmetic, on the page, with the hours, and the four days.** **The yard is at a fortnight on the ninth of Wolfmonth. The book's three figures: eleven days at the second hour of the fifth of Wolfmonth, entered without his brother's name, the reason being that a yard's book holds a thing set; fifteen days at the fifth hour of the seventh, the wall off the Fallow Elms road re-spoken for four days because the timber is not coming till the eighteenth and a man who takes his men off a wall in Frostmonth does not get them back for the spring; and fourteen days at the first hour of the eighth, because Pei Tarrow, 52, asked at the grindstone to be put off two days instead of three, which is one day, and a fortnight.** **Tovan Kest reads all three out in the gateway with four men in it and says: a yard that gains four days without anybody being able to say where they came from is a yard that has stopped counting, and a yard that has stopped counting is a yard that is being run on somebody's memory, and I have been on the other side of that in this county and I did not enjoy it.** **Pei Tarrow's asking is not a plea and he says why in his own voice: he has a barge on the channel and a market day, he has been called a symbol for eleven years and has no book and no list and no dead, he wants freight, and he does not want the day off the board — he wants the day off the yard's board and to be somewhere else.** **Gwil Trewhella, 39, a carrier on the western channel, of Toban's crew and not Toban's, comes in at half past three on a market day and gets the same answer: the number of lines in the week's book, the hour the last form was read, and a wage figure that is a wage book and not a book of persons, and the whole of Toban's argument that a road is a promise and a promise is not a trade and a yard cannot answer for one. His uncle kept the west lock above the mill for thirty-one years, and the only thing anybody ever asks you about a lock is when it will be open, and if you have not got a name and a day you have got a habit, and a habit is not a thing you can put in a letter. Nobody offered him a day and nobody promised him a name.**

204. **The three weeks in the County of Ash, the four men out of it, and the two numbers on the board.** **The county's price, footed in pence on a board in the ash-driver's hand and copied by the boy with the slate, including the blanks, which is what that boy does:** *two hundred and six to be put back, each requiring one tile with the person's own hand on the back · one tile in three is fit · six hundred and eighteen into the kiln · about forty fit to a rolling · fifteen rollings wanted · the county has done five in a season in every year for nineteen years · three times the tiles. Nine days to a rolling and a cord a day, so a hundred and thirty-five cords wanted against forty-five cut in a season, so ninety cords more than this county has ever cut in one year. Ash at the kiln gate, two shillings and sixpence the cord: ninety cords, 2,700 pence, fifty-six marks and one shilling. Cutting: about forty men, one day, five pence the day: 200 pence, four marks and eightpence. The whole of the extra season: 2,900 pence, sixty marks one shilling eightpence. Men whose day is lost if it is done in the digging: about forty. Men out if two kilns stand idle for a rolling: about forty.* **And the last two lines are not the same and the man at the cutting bench has it entered that they are not to be added, because a man who loses a day and a man who loses a season are not the same man and a room that adds them has been told a story it likes.** It is on a page headed with a number nobody had ever put at the top of a page before, **and it is the first page in that county about people and not about tiles, and the second line on it is the first entry in nineteen years in that office against a man by name that is not a removal.** **Nobody in that room said *we are right* or *you are wrong*, and both of those are free, which is why the room costed a thing instead.**

205. **The price, said out loud, and it is the volume's labour argument in a county's own trade.** **The ninety cords is not the price. The cutting is done in the same weeks as the digging, and a man cannot be in the ash with a saw in the fourth week of the rolling and have his own hearth burnt down in the same week, and in this county a hearth is what a fire is, and a fire is what a person is, and there is no column in this county for a person.** And then the sentence: **a county that fires three times the tiles has to cut three times the wood in the same weeks as the digging, and about forty men lose a day, and the county cannot pay forty men for a day it has not got, and a county that cannot pay a man for a day does not go and beg — a county that cannot pay a man for a day puts him on a list.
