And the kilns can do that sum in a morning and the magistrate can do his in a year, and they are the same sum, and one of them has a law attached to it and the other one has a price, and a price is not a law but a price is the one that gets kept.** **Magistrate Colm Arreld is not in the room and has left a paper in the county's own book, three lines, which is the third instrument he has refused to make and the first he has refused in writing: he is not giving this room an instruction and will not be asked for one in a room of two hundred people, because the last instrument he made is on the table untried, and a magistrate who makes a second thing before the first has been tried is a magistrate who has decided the first was wrong.** **And the rollkeeper refused to read the roll out in the room, for the second time in nineteen years, and refused to give the county's figure of nine hundred and forty-one hearths, and both refusals are right, and the reason for the second is that the moment that number is said out loud in a room where the struck are standing it is a count of persons, and the county has never once had a count of persons.**

206. **The kilns close, and a charged rolling cannot wait, and the wedging shed.** **The ninety cords were not cut, because there is no office in this county that can put forty men in a wood for a day and pay them for it. A rolling kiln that is charged is nine days of clay and cannot wait; it goes back into the wedging shed, which is the scarcest room in the county, and which is the room the rollkeeper kept a list out of, in a corridor, nine years ago, for a day and a half — and she says so herself, in four sentences, to about twenty men, in the eleventh week, and says the reason she gave in that room was the correct reason on that day and reasons do not stay correct, and that she is not resigning, and that a rollkeeper who resigns is a woman who has said the county's records are not worth a room, and this county has had four of those in nineteen years and the county did not stop and the county got a new woman each time.** **Two rolling kilns stop in the twelfth week: the one at the north village fired out a half-charged rolling, nine days of wages for a kiln that will not be paid for nine days, and the one at the east village was not charged and was left.** **About forty men out, and the ash-driver will not go past forty and will not write a number in a week he has not counted. The two live kilns go to half in the fifteenth week and take twenty-seven of the forty. The other thirteen do not go anywhere, and that is the only figure in this business that everybody in the county has agreed on without an argument, and the reason it is agreed is that it is the ugliest one.** And the reason, in the county's own words: **a struck man, an employed man, and a man whose kiln has shut are three different people and the room knows it and does not say so. A struck man is on a list and can be put back and has a tile in a box. An employed man has a hearth, a fire, a wage drawn at the end of a week, and two witnesses whose names are in a book, and is the only one of the three who can be asked a question in a room. A man whose kiln has shut is none of those and is not on anything at all, and there is no column in this county for him and there never has been, and a man who is not on a column cannot be put on one, and a man who cannot be put on one cannot be given a vote, and a man who has not got a vote is not a witness, and a man who is not a witness cannot be restored.** **Six of the thirteen are six lines with nothing against them and no reason on them, in a keeper's hand, with the date and the hour.**

207. **The three hands, the third of them four hundred miles off, and the two letters.** **The three hands are the rollkeeper's office at the assizes (one book, one room, one building, and it does not move); the hand of the Ninebridge (not an office, and a shed at the north end of a bridge with about four hundred soft tiles on boards under wet cloths and four hundred yards of fired tile set one to a kerb stone with the kiln's mark and a date on the back of each, so that a man can turn a stone over with a heel and read who cut it); and the press of a creditor's counting house in a city that is not on any map in this kingdom, at a farthing of salt, on a shelf whose price is not money.** **The price of the shelf is the eleven sheets: a chain of eleven sheets has lain in that counting house for eleven years for a creditor who did not want them, and a shelf cannot be given away for nothing because a shelf is the only thing in a counting house that cannot be argued with. The eleven sheets are four hundred miles off and no one in that county can go and get them, because the county's second term forbids a name at this end of the line and the roads do not join. A promised hand is not a hand.** **So in the seventeenth week the county wrote to the only office it had ever heard of that keeps press registers, with no name in it, and asked **fifteen** words: *Will a press hold what is put in it, and refuse to give it up?* **The letter did not ask for the register; the office decided the answer to that question was its own register and sent a copy unasked, and that is why a fair copy of a whole leaf is in the same bag.**** The answer came in eleven days to the Ninebridge and not to the assizes, **because the assizes is an office and an office is a thing a Crown's man can ask a question of, and the Ninebridge is four hundred yards of kerb and a shed with a door on the side of it.**

208. **The flat line about registers, spent once, here, and the two new press names.** **Ivet Salter, 61, of the third press, thirty years, in a small flat hand: *Yes. That is the whole of what a press does. It holds what is entered on the shelf and it refuses nothing and it gives nothing up, and a court cannot take a thing out of a press that belongs to somebody; a court can take it out of one that belongs to an office, and the difference between those two is whether there is a person at the end of it, and the person is what a register is for. A register answers what was entered on the shelf and nothing else. If you want a book that can be asked what a thing in it is for, then you want a court, and a court will give it to whichever of you asks loudest, and that is not a defect in the court. That is the trade.*** **This is the only appearance of that sentence in the whole of Volume 02 and it is why the third hand cannot be a registry press and must be a creditor's: a registry press is an office and can be taken out of; a creditor's press is a person's goods and cannot.** Chapter 57's version of it is Halven's and Chapter 72's is Petch's and Chapter 79's is Salter's, and they are three different sentences about three different things and only Salter's is a definition. **Sennet Roil, 28, a clerk in the fourth press of the provincial registry, who did not ask to be sent it and has a grandmother's habit of copying a whole leaf, made the fair copy above it, and a whole leaf means the four ruled cells at the foot with nothing in them, and a copyist who leaves the blanks off is a copyist who has made an argument, and her grandmother was a copyist for forty years and was struck off a parish roll once and never once said so.** **And in that fair copy there is a county cell that is ruled and empty, on a line with a number and a date on it, and a man four hundred miles off reads it on a board at the low end of a rack with a wet cloth in his hand and stands there for about a minute and a half and writes one line in the back of a notebook and says nothing to anybody: a cell, ruled and empty, in a copy of a shelf register in a press **two hundred miles off**; I do not know what it is; I have written down that I saw it and that I did not say anything, because a man who says a thing about a cell in a shed full of people is a man who has got a shape and no fact, and last time I acted on a shape with no fact under it I put a bell in a file four hundred miles off and I have not finished paying for that. And the woman of sixty-one has written that a register answers what was entered on the shelf and nothing else, and that is the whole of her office, and she says it as though it were a small thing.** **This cell is the same shape as the fifth space on a struck person's form that has no sixth, and as the eighth ruled line in a served schedule four hundred miles the other way, and it is the same shape and it is the same blank, and no character in this batch may say so, no character in this batch knows it, and the two documents are never in one room.**

209. **The argument minuted, and the bench's own powerlessness, and the third hand being a letter.** **Alis Merrow's argument, read out in the long room at the assizes by the magistrate himself and entered whole at the officer's request: that a creditor's press is the worst place in the world to keep a roll of persons and the only place in the world where nobody can quietly take it out of the book; that he has been arguing that for eleven years in a hall; that he is now the man who is proposing it to his own city; and that he asks that the argument be minuted and that the minute be put where a Crown's man will find it in about nine years.** **The bench tried to put the minute where a Crown's man would find it and could not, and the reason it could not is the volume's subject said out loud in a room: there is no Crown's man in this county, there is no office, no schedule, no writ, no fee, no seal, and no county in a column with a disposition word against it; a county that is not on any map in the kingdom cannot be written to or asked or served, and the county's own second term says that no name is to be read out on the far side of the line by anybody from the north; and **there is nowhere in the County of Ash that a Crown's man will ever find a minute, and that is not because anybody here has hidden anything, it is because there is nobody here to look.** So the request can only be granted where he is: **he has to put it in his own city, in his own book, in his own hand, at his own cost, and then it will be in the world, and we cannot help him and we cannot stop him, and if he does not do it then the argument is a thing a stranger said in a shed in a country that is not on any map, and in about nine years that is worth nothing at all.** **The bench entered its own powerlessness in a book in a minute, which is the first time any body in that county has done it in nineteen years, and the reason it did it is that the argument was about where a minute goes.** **The magistrate says the other half, which is the volume's: he has had a physician's finding in a drawer for four years and a list with a law on it for nine years and has not acted on either because each is one instrument and every instrument he has made has had one good idea in it and the good idea has been a mark in the ground for the fever; he has been right about the fever eleven times and wrong about the names every time and has known it for two years; a stranger came off a road in the first week of a rolling and gave the county a second instrument, and he is glad of it and has been ashamed for about four years and both are true.** **Pernel Quist does not sign, and her two reasons are both hers and neither is a matter of principle: if she is one of the three then every restoration in this county is a thing a woman in Kilnholt did — and she has known that since the sixteenth day of a rolling, when a stranger asked her a question in a room with a stove in it and she said no in one word and he wrote down the reason, and the reason was that she is the anchor; and the third hand is four hundred miles off and behind a price, and a roll signed by two hands and a promise is not a roll in three hands, it is a roll in two hands with a third hand written on the end of it in a fair copy, and in about four years a magistrate is going to ask which of the three it is and the answer will be that one of them is a letter, and she will not sign a thing that has a letter in it and has been right about that every single time in nineteen years. And: **I am not signing this week.** I have not said no. Do not put it in the book that I said no, because I have said no twice in nine years and both times I was wrong, and a book is a worse place to be wrong than a corridor and a better place to be right, and I have not decided which this is.** It is the fourth page of the minute and the longest thing in it and she corrected one word and the word was *worse*. **She has not signed and Chapter 89 is where she signs, and the reason she gives there — a roll that can be wrong is not a weaker roll, it is a roll that has to be read twice, and a roll that has to be read twice cannot be read by one man in a morning — is NOT spent in this batch and must not be spent in Chapters 81 to 88.**

210. **The second notebook entry, four months after the first, in the same vocabulary, in tons, and he does not act on it.** **Chapter 80, the twenty-second week, in the back of the notebook, in the flat hand, on a low stool at the low end of the rack: he does not write down what the rite is for, because he has not worked it out, and a man who writes down why a thing is good in the same book as what the thing is has written a sentence instead of a working — and that sentence is the one he wrote in a filing room in Longlight and Petch refused to hold for him, and it is the shape of the sentence that put a bell in a file four hundred miles off.** The parts, in the trade's own words, and **the anchor, the witness, the bound and the counterweight are the four from the nineteenth day of the first time here, and the witness line is identical and he says so: a witness that is also the anchor is not a witness, it is a second reading of the same hand, same as the last time, I have written that sentence twice now in two counties and it has been right twice.** The counterweight now also carries **six men out of a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths whose names are six lines with nothing against them and no reason on them, and there is no column in this county for what happened to them and there is not going to be one.** The bound is the same and he says the same thing about it. **The figure is the same: two tons each, off a barrow of setts, with no reason of any kind; two hundred of them is four hundred tons; four hundred tons into a bed of river gravel at Ninebridge is a thing I can point at and say there, that is what this is; a document's load is not in tons, I know that, I have written it in tons anyway, twice, in two counties, and the second time I have also written down that I noticed the first time, and noticing it did not stop me then and it has not stopped me now, and a man who has noticed a thing twice is not being careful, he is being consistent.** **And the calendar, planted for the third time in his own hand: four months, or thereabouts, which is the same thing at this rate, and I have not done the sum because the sum is in two calendars and I have not got both of them, and the one I have got does not have a week in it, and I wrote a line about that exact sum in this book the last time I was in this county and I have not read that line since and I know what it says without reading it.** And then: **I am going to build it anyway. That is written down as well, because I have written the mistake down before and read it and gone on, and the only new thing in this book today is that it is the same mistake both times and noticing it has never once stopped me.** **The last line of the last chapter of the block is nearly the same sentence as the last line of Chapter 69, and it is the same man doing the same thing in a different county: Closed the book. There are about four hundred soft tiles in this county between here and Kilnholt and one pair of hands and mine, and I am going to do the bottom of the rack next, and it is the best work I have had in five months, and nobody in this county is going to hear a word about any of the above from me, ever, in a room, with a clerk in it, ever again.** **The reader knows. Nobody in the County of Ash knows. Chapter 84 is where he finds out that he was right, and Chapter 84's cost is that the remedy is his and the damage is his and the method was correct and it is still him deciding for a room — and a later writer may not have him notice before Chapter 84.**

211. **The seal on the letter, and what was done with it.** **The four sheets from the counting house of the tolls of Bellweather-Living came to the Ninebridge in the sixteenth week, brought by a person who carried papers and did not stop in that county, and the envelope bore a seal with a device on it that is a hand laid flat on a book, and the hand of the Ninebridge looked at the seal for about four seconds and then cut it off with a knife and dropped it into the pit, and did not say why, and did not have to.** **The letter is dated in a form that county has no word for, being a month and a year counted from something that is not the first firing, and the hand of the Ninebridge read that date out loud once in a shed and then said, *We cannot check one word of that*, and nobody asked her to check it, because she is the hand of the Ninebridge and a hand does not get asked. No character in this batch says what the device on that seal is, and no character in this volume may: it is in grey wax on a post in Merefen, on a storehouse door by the fish weir, on the back of a Crown's letter, in a magistrate's finding in her own hand, and on a fired clay tile on a coping in that county, and it is the volume's last planted mystery and Chapter 85 is still available and does not explain it either.**

212. **Cast, and what was spent.** **Four new names first named in prose in Batch 0008 — Gwil Trewhella, Marda Oakes, Sennet Roil, Ivet Salter — and two slots unfilled, as the card allows. The volume total is now thirteen of eighteen, and the unfilled slots are five of twelve.** **Ilsa Soke is a returned face named in Chapter 60 of Batch 0006 and is NOT named in this batch; she is *the hand of the Ninebridge* and *the hand* throughout Chapters 77 to 80, and he asks her one question on the fourth of Hearthmonth and gets the answer he already had.** **The kiln clerk is addressed as the man with the ledger and his name is withheld by design and must not be given in this volume.** Everyone else in the batch is addressed by trade: the guild's man with the levels, the guild clerk, the bench-clerk, the man at the fish weir, the foundry's man, the man at the drawknife, the man in the leather apron, the man at the cutting bench, the man at the loading end, the ash-driver, the cord-cutter, the man from the provincial office, the toll-holder's clerk, the boy of fifteen, the boy with the slate, the boy of thirteen, the clerk of the assizes, the clerk of the provincial court, the carrier who was described as about thirty and was not asked his name. **Nobody in the batch dies. Nobody is killed. No unnamed person is arranged into a death for effect.** The tile on the coping is on the page once in this batch, in one exchange, on the fourth of Hearthmonth, **and it is not touched, not explained, not recognised out loud, and the man who has known what it is since the first morning says nothing and asks about it instead, and is thanked for the answer, and the debt is not this batch's to settle.**

213. **The Batch 0008 review repair pass, and every figure a reader can now check.** **Forty-three findings applied across Chapters 71–80, the five state files and one next-phase prompt, in two passes: twenty-eight on the first, fifteen on the second, of which four were defects the first pass had introduced itself and one was a figure the first pass had invented in a state file. No plot beat removed, no chapter renumbered, no card broken, no controller file touched, and no beat in the next-phase prompt touched. The batch is **38,796 words.** The load-bearing corrections of the first pass, in the order a later writer will trip over them. **(1) The weekday chain had two broken entries.** With Wolfmonth 1 Fri, the twelfth is a **Tuesday** and the seventeenth is a **Sunday**; the chain and the state files said Friday and Friday, and **Chapter 72 had already inherited the error onto the page in a woman's mouth making a virtue of a three-day post discipline.** Chapter 72 now says Tuesday and the day-book entry still says it is three days, which it is. **(2) A paper cannot arrive before it is dated.** Chapter 73's instrument had been on the magistrate's desk since the eighth of Greenmonth and was dated the tenth or eleventh; it is now **dated the twelfth and on the desk since the fifteenth**, which keeps the fifth-clause mechanism, keeps the eighteen days to the ninth of Frostmonth, and adds a fourth instance of the volume's three-day post rule instead of denying it. **(3) Chapter 76's interval is twenty-two days, not twenty-five** — (30 − 17) + 9 — and the figure was wrong in the chapter twice and in five state files; **all seven are now twenty-two. And the same chapter's *back in this county four days* is thirty-four days.** **(4) The yard's book misdated its own key entry.** The wall off the Fallow Elms road was re-spoken on the Thursday of that week, which is the **seventh of Wolfmonth**, and the book's second line said the fifth; it says the seventh, and items 190 and 202 are corrected with it. **(5) Toban's answer in a gateway no longer names, genders or places the other person who is making the same answer**, and a later writer may not supply any of the three. **(6) Chapter 79 no longer puts the *struck* blank in the same sentence as the register cell**, and the *struck* blank stays where it lives, in Chapters 66 and 73, in a different country, in a different month; **no two of the volume's blanks are now in one room or in one sentence, and that is true of the prose and not only of the intention.** **(7) Chapter 79's geography was two counties at once.** The provincial registry's third and fourth presses are **two hundred miles off**, in a town this county has never had a reason to send anybody to, and the notebook entry now says two hundred miles; it said four hundred, which is the Ash distance. **The narrator's sentence that had put the same press *four days off a cart road* — about thirty-six miles at the cart rate in `bible/terminology.md` — was the same error wearing a different hat and is gone; one press, one distance, and it is two hundred miles.** **(8) Chapter 77's far end is in Ash**: Tovan holds this end, **his brother takes four fathoms of it and goes out of the county, and the far end is four hundred miles off in the hand of somebody he has met once.** **(9) Chapter 80 no longer says he refused three times.** **(10) Eight, not eleven**: *I have not got a trade for it* is eight words, and the minute and every state file say eight. **The two counted quantities the prose states — the fifteen words of the question the county wrote and the forty-one words of the county's second term — are both right, and the first was *nine* and is now fifteen.** **(11) No real-world month and no main-world weekday in a path chapter.** **(12) The hand of the Ninebridge is forty-one**, and *useful and not a visitor* is placed in **his first week at the door of a cutting shed** (Chapter 64) instead of on the first morning. **(13) The aphorism is the rollkeeper's**: *a keeper who puts a want in a cell where she has a fact is a mouth and not a cell* is Mara Vey's and was in Pernel Quist's mouth with a false attribution; Quist now has her own sentence, about a woman who would like the county to be the shape of her wanting, and the credit for Mara's line is not stolen. **(14) Vaile's line parses against the board the reader has been given.** **(15) Chapter 78's drafting artifact is gone** — a chapter reference had survived mid-sentence — and is replaced with the shed and the change of shift and the eleventh day of a rolling, which is where Chapter 66 put it. **(16) Chapter 73's recap paragraph is deleted** and Lysa Fenn no longer claims to have said the *how many* sentence to a carpenter and a clerk, which she had not. **(17) Chapter 76's knowledge boundary is framed, not deleted**: the three paragraphs about the notebook, the binding agreement and the crossing-out are an **outside-narrator passage, marked as such, between two rules**, and the chapter returns to her room for the served schedule. **Mara Vey is not given Aren's documents and the breach is still Chapter 92's.** **(18) Marda Oakes reads the two writs**, of the ninth and twenty-ninth of Longlight, and the finding separately, and no longer claims a Halven disclosure never served on a county council. **(19) The witness-fee arrangement is two months old in Chapter 75** (the ninth of Embermonth to the ninth of Frostmonth is sixty-one days) **and Chapter 74's yard figure is gone**, because it was *eleven days and a fortnight by Friday*, twenty-eight days after Chapter 71's fourteen, and a chapter may not quietly have four days appear. **(20) The month that was not a month**: *the fourth month* is gone from Chapters 73, 74 and 75, replaced by **Longlight** and by **the first hard frost of the winter**; one use survives in Chapter 68, which is Batch 0007, where it means Longlight, and it is to be read that way. **(21) Chapter 79 is no longer seventeen lines of report**: the wedging shed is a room with a bank and a floor and about twenty men in a doorway, a boy of nineteen comes down the road with his apron over his arm and is not put on anything, and the six are asked for as a column and refused. **The figures are untouched: two kilns in the twelfth week, about forty out, twenty-seven of the forty taken by the two live kilns in the fifteenth week, thirteen who do not go anywhere, six unaccounted.** **(22) Chapter 72's Petch was two days in a coach and one on foot** and does not say he slept in a coach and walked. **(23) Chapter 77's terms, the splice, and Toban's second refusal are untouched, and the yard's book still holds things set and not things believed** — which is why nothing Pei Tarrow said in Chapter 71's gateway is in it, and that omission is now on the page as a decision.

**The second pass, and what it found. Read this before trusting anything above.** The first pass closed every finding it was given and introduced four new defects of its own; all four are closed. **(a) The first pass's own new text in Chapter 79 had the narration trapped inside an unclosed quotation — five sentences of third person standing syntactically inside Pernel Quist's speech — and had her say *two kilns coming off this month*, which is a main-world unit in a path-time chapter. Both are gone: the speech is two speeches with a narrating beat between them and *inside the month*, and a hod is put down and the other kept so that the staging holds. The first pass had also opened the wedging-shed scene with a *third space on a form with no sixth space*, which put the struck blank back into Chapter 79 by the back door; **that is struck out of the new scene, and Quist refuses the six because this county has never built a shape for a man who is not on the roll and not on the list, which is her own reason and touches no other document.** **(b) Marda Oakes was called *he* four times in Chapter 76 by Mara Vey**, in a chapter that introduces her as a woman and in which no second person enters the room. She is she. **(c) Chapter 71's new Pei Tarrow scene contradicted itself about an hour** — Tovan said he was not in the yard at the third hour and Pei then arrived about the third hour, which is also when Gwil Trewhella comes in. Tovan now says he was not in the yard *when I did the sum*, and Pei comes in a few minutes after Trewhella has gone out. **(d) The first pass's state record invented a figure** — *twenty-five for the eight words in the minute* — and there is no such figure in Chapter 80. Also closed in the second pass: **Chapter 73's recap, which the first pass had corrected from *four* written refusals to *a second time*, now says what the chapter and this file say — three refusals in advance, a fourth in writing, and the fifth answered. The unreconcilable ordinal month survived the first pass as *the fifth month of this year*; the finding of the fourth of Greenmonth is in Greenmonth and the chapter now says so. Chapter 77 no longer gives a tally of how many times a page like that has been made in the yard's book, because nothing in the batch supports any tally; it names the last two pages instead. Chapter 77's Ash arrival line said he had not been in that county since the first of Fallowmonth, which is Volume 01's third crossing and contradicts the same chapter's own first line about twenty-six days of absence; it is the ninth of Embermonth. Chapter 73 said a fact had grown heavier *in May*, and Neyra has no May. Pei Tarrow's new line about a list he was put on in his forty-first year, about a bell, is gone: it was new canon that nothing in Chapters 12–17 or in this file supports, and the line now says what item 203 already records, that he has never been on one. The claim in `state/current.md` that no main-world weekday is used anywhere in Chapters 61–69 or 78–80 was false for Chapters 61–69 and is now stated as what it actually is: the rule is that a main-world weekday has no business in a path-time scene, it is true of Chapters 78–80, and the Ash people's own Monday and Friday in Chapters 61–69 are their own and may not be carried into Merefen or built into an Ash chain. The two leaks the first pass closed in Chapter 79 were still sitting in item 206 and would have been re-imported by a Batch 0009 writer, and are now corrected there too. The state file's *sixth blank* was followed by five and is now *the same blank is on the page five times*.** And **one factual error in this phase's own next-phase prompt was corrected — the two hundred and fifty leagues for four hundred miles, which is wrong by half — because the prompt would have carried it into Chapter 81, and no card and no beat in it was touched.**

## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 80

- **Aren and Mara:** the batch's movement is that **she returned a figure of eleven to the province on the ninth of Frostmonth and he was twenty-five yards from the door and was not sent for, and neither of them knows that the other would have understood what the office's three lines are.** He is four hundred miles off inside a clay county in his fifth month and cannot be reached; she has the three lines in her own day-book with the hour on them and has entered that she does not see what they are, **and the two of them are the only people in the world who could each have said what the other needed to hear, and they are not in the same room, and that is not an accident of the plot: it is a binding agreement, a bench minute, a letter in a file, and a crossing that opened on a market morning.** **Nothing has been lied to. He gave her the numbers on the fifth of Wolfmonth and this batch adds no word between them at all — there is no meeting, no letter, no message, and the last thing she wrote about him is on a page in a day-book and the last thing he wrote about her is that he is not going to tell anybody in a clay county a word about any of it.** The not asking is unchanged and the wound is unchanged and **the Chapter 92 breach is now planted for the third time in his own hand and is not resolved and may not be.**
- **Aren and Tovan Kest:** **named out loud, in a yard, on a market day, at about half past four, for the first time in four years** — *there is a road in this county that was **refused** on the ninth of Embermonth at the fourth hour and nothing came, and it did not fail, and I do not know when it will open and I have not asked, and I have a brother in it and I have not sent him a word because I do not know the address* — and he told four men in a gateway that if either of them ever tells anybody in this county that he said it he will deny it in a form. **And the sentence at the gate of the fourth of Hearthmonth, which is the whole of the brother beat in this block: the yard is a fortnight, and when you come back it will be a fortnight or it will not be, and if it is not then I will have had to put a man off, and it will be Tarrow, and I will not be able to pay him and he will not be able to ask me, and I am telling you now so that on the day you come back and I say nothing about it, you will not think it was your brother.** The estrangement is not healed, nobody has said the word brother in a scene, and the arrangement of four months standing — the fee and the hour and not the reason — has not been re-litigated and has not been improved on.
- **Aren and Lysa Fenn:** a single morning and the best two-hander in the block. **He went because she sent for him, to give an account of a Crown's man in a gateway in the words he used there, and he was in the room while a woman beat an instrument with the form's own construction in nine minutes and then said out loud that she was frightened of her own answer, and he watched her be delighted and frightened in the same hour and understood, in about the length of a fire ticking eleven times, that this is what competent looks like when it is not him — and he said none of it, and the reason is that a sentence said in that room would be in a file in nine years in somebody else's hand, and she has the finding and the paper and the refusal and does not need him.** **And the fourth refusal about the eleven-year-old salvage filing, and the promise of a fifth, given in advance so that the fifth is not a surprise either. He has not asked her one question in eleven years that he could not have asked out of curiosity.**
- **Aren and Reeve Aldis Fenn:** two chapters and a single line between them. She did not vote on her own election for nine years and on the ninth of Frostmonth she used a casting vote twice and on the fourth of Hearthmonth she voted with her hand on a thing in her own guild, **and he watched a man who had no vote in that room become a man with a vote in it in eleven seconds and say the thing that is in the roll, and she minuted that the bench does not know what to do about it.** She is now the independent witness at the Merefen end of a channel and has declined to sign as one, and he was not in the room when she declined.
- **Aren and Reeve and the road:** the fourth of Hearthmonth is the first morning on which all three of the things this volume is about are on one page — a bench voting, a reeve refusing to sign, a man going out on a line with a witness who is not the hand — and **nobody in that yard connects any of it, and the yard's book holds things set and not things believed, and the one sentence about the brother is the one thing in it that is not entered in any book at all.**
- **Aren and the hand of the Ninebridge:** **the block's central relationship, and it is still conducted entirely in trades and entirely without her name.** He calls her the hand and she does not correct him. **On the fourth of Hearthmonth she offers him one question and he asks about the tile on the coping, and she gives him the answer he already had and tells him she has never asked anybody to take it down, and tells him that he has known what it is since the first morning and did not say, and says that this is the whole of what has happened between them in four months and she would like it to go on being the whole of it — and he says agreed.** That is the whole of a reconciliation in this volume and it is a professional agreement about a lie, and **he is carrying boards and re-pressing tiles and asking nobody anything for twenty-two weeks, and he has not been re-taught anything and nobody has taught him anything, and the second use for the hand is the same second use and it is now five months old and it belongs to a county and not to a city.**
- **Aren and Pernel Quist:** **two professionals who are each right, and in this block the fourth time they are in a room together she is the one who gets refused and he is the one who refuses nothing, and both of them know it.** She refused to read the roll out and refused to give the county's figure in a room of two hundred and she was right twice. and he will not sign his own two hands into the working and he said **eight** words: *I have not got a trade for it*, and that is true and is the reason and is not modesty. **She has not signed. She said not this week and not no, and the difference between those two is the whole of what she has learned in nineteen years.**
- **Aren and the magistrate:** **the block's answer to Chapter 63 and 68 is silence, and the silence is on the page and is in a minute in eight words.** **He was asked three times in five months and he is not silent about all three and must not be written as though he were: he ANSWERED the first, in a room with a stove in it, in one sentence, and was right about what happens to a tile (Chapter 63); he FOUGHT the second and won a week (Chapter 68); and he said nothing at all on the third, which was four months earlier in a moot hall in a fen county (Chapter 73), and the reason he said nothing the third time is in a book in a coat that nobody in that room has ever read. And the magistrate has now said out loud, in a room of forty, that he is glad of the second instrument and ashamed of the four years, and **a man who has spent this volume refusing to be a voice has watched a decent man volunteer the sentence he was not going to say, and has not improved on it, and the chapter says he was glad and does not say what he was.**
- **Aren and the rollkeeper of the fourth press and the clerk of the same:** **two people he has never met, one of whom has said the sentence that decides the third hand, and one of whom copied the four ruled cells at the foot of a leaf because her grandmother was struck off a parish roll once and never once said so, and he has read both of their hands in a shed and written one line in a notebook and told nobody.**
- **Mara and Sarn Oyelaran:** the block's best working partnership. **Sarn sees in eleven seconds that a return that cannot be read as a refusal and cannot be read as a compliance is the whole of the trick and that she has not seen it done in nine years; she catches a figure going out with a name column left empty and does not improve it; and she says the sentence that is the block's cold reading of the whole county: everything anybody has done in this case for a year has been a method, and the man with the rule is a method and the man with the churn is a method, and there have been about four things done in four years without a method and one of them is Mara and two of them are dead.** Mara asks her what to do about a woman who came up a stair and wanted an office to be a body, and Sarn says it is a method, and Mara says she knows.
- **Mara and Marda Oakes:** one hour, one stair, and a card. **A woman who has read a form more carefully than anybody in that room, is right about the mechanism, has said the half of her argument that is useful and has refused on purpose to say the other half because saying it early would make her a person who helped her, and then walked down a stair and said, in a doorway, that there is a man in this county who reads forms better than she does and has never once asked this office for anything, and that she has been wondering for six weeks whether that is a very good sign about Mara or a very bad sign about him.** **They have met once. Nobody in that room said the sentence and the reader can.**
- **Mara and Rowan Petch:** **a twenty-six-year-old clerk files a return that answers nothing and comes back and tells her the one fact he has, once, and then tells her not to say anything else about the woman, and she does not.** And he wants one thing and it is not a document, and she refuses to enter it in her day-book because a keeper who writes a man's want in her book has written a document about a person, and she is right and he does not argue.
- **Rowan Petch and the file:** a woman's name in a county file in her own hand, in a matter that Mara's claim number is resting on, and a clerk four hundred yards of counter away deciding that the cheapest thing a young man can do with his life is being careful where nobody watches, and it is not a want anybody would put in a book, and there is now a keeper of records in this case who has told him to his face that it is not hers either.
- **Mara and Lysa Fenn and the two documents:** **two offices, two rooms, two sets of numbers, and one office in this county has a count and the other has a procedure, and neither of them has read the other's paper, and the only thing they share is that both of them have entered the not writing.** And in about four years one of them is going to be made a person, by somebody with a good head and a clean sheet, and nobody in Merefen knows it and the reader does.
- **Aren and the struck:** **a man of fifty-eight said, in a room of two hundred, in the fourth week, that the two lines at the bottom of the board are not the same forty and he has heard the man who wrote them say so, and that he is the one man in the room who is in both of them, and that he has not counted and is not going to, because a man who counts his own name is a man who has begun to be a list, and that if the cutting stops he will lose every day he has got, and that then he will be a man who is struck and a man who is out, and that there is nothing in this county that can tell the difference between those two things or anybody else either — and asked for it to be entered in a book by somebody who was not him, while he was still able to ask.** It was entered, in a keeper's hand, and it is the first entry in nineteen years in that office against a man by name that is not a removal. **He has not been restored. Six men went out of a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths and nobody in the county knows what happened to them and they are six lines with nothing against them and no reason on them, and that is the shape the whole of this volume is about and it is now on the page in a county nobody in Merefen can name.**

## Canon established by Batch 0009 (Volume 02, Chapters 81–90, prose on the page)

**How to use this section.** Items 1–213 are the record of Volume 01, of the Volume 02 plan, of Chapters 51–60, of Chapters 61–70 and of Chapters 71–80 with both its repair passes. Items **214 onward** are the record of the ten chapters that are now on the page, and Batch 0010 is written against them. **Nothing in 214 onward may be contradicted by Chapter 91 or later, and nothing in it re-derives an earlier item.**

214. **The block's frame, closed, and the calendar a writer must not convert.** **Chapters 81–90 are all path time in the County of Ash and are dated only by the county's own frame**, being the rolling, the two low waters a year, the year counted from the first firing, and the county's own twelve months. **The path interval is nine months and six days, it began at the fourth hour of the fourth of Hearthmonth, and Chapter 90 is its last day; the main-world absence is eighty-eight days and the return is the second of Greenmonth, YR 313, a Tuesday, which is Chapter 91 and is not this block.** The county's positions, in this order, are fixed: **Ch 81 the sixth month, the fourth hour of a day in it** · **Ch 82 the whole of the rite, weeks one to eleven, told afterwards** · **Ch 83 the ninth week of the rite** · **Ch 84 the seventh month, and the working-out takes four days** · **Ch 85 and Ch 86 the eighth month** · **Ch 87, 88, 89 and 90 the ninth month, and the ninth of the ninth month is the crossing out and the last day**. **The one arithmetic a later writer must not do: a month of four weeks cannot hold eleven weeks of rite in one month, and the batch does not convert.** It resolves the collision **in the county's own voice and in the volume's own method** — the county keeps a rolling and it keeps weeks and it keeps months, and **it has never once had to convert one of them into another, and a person who wanted to could, and the person who would want to is a person from a county that has a week with a number on it.** **A later writer may not re-derive a conversion between the rite's weeks and the county's months, and may not state a month-length for this county.** **No main-world weekday, no real-world month, and no drift figure is spoken anywhere in Chapters 81–90.** The two crossed lines in Chapter 85 are the county's own and are not summed with anything.

215. **The rite, as proposed and as carried out, and the arrest, and what the arrest is.** **The rite is four sentences: the struck person re-presses their own soft tile in the room, in front of two witnesses, and the soft tile is theirs and nobody else presses it; the two witnesses then press their own hands onto two other tiles, and the two other tiles are the witnesses' and not the person's; the three go onto a board together and the board goes to a shelf; and the office does not read the roll, it watches people answer.** The argument for it in one sentence: **a person can check a rule, and a person who can check is a person who has been asked.** It is proposed in the assizes' long room, at the fourth hour, to a magistrate, a clerk, a rollkeeper and about sixty people, **in a hat**, and the hat is the volume's standing flag and everybody in the book knows what it costs him and he does it anyway; the reason he wears it is that **in a county that has never refused anything there is a difference between a man who comes into a hall and a man who comes down to it, and a hat is the only piece of clothing a bridgewright owns that anybody in that county would recognise as an outsider's, and it is not a lie and it is not a claim and it is the sort of thing a man wears when he has decided to be counted.** A clerk of the assizes looks at it for about four seconds and writes nothing, **because a hat is not a fact.** **Four words into a fifth sentence — the fifth being the reason he is the one saying it, which a woman with a stove warned him about in the eleventh month, and a motive is a thing a man can only repeat — a clerk of the counting house of the tolls of Bellweather-Living, fifty-eight, thirty-one years in that building, not a Crown's man and not in the Crown's service, stops copying a fair copy of the minute and enters the arrest.** The mechanism is the fourth hand: **the county sent a fair copy of the minute because that is what a press on a shelf is for, and the price of the shelf is still eleven sheets.** The entry is in his own fair hand, at the foot of the fourth sentence of the minute, **against a name already on the leaf, being the name the office has used eleven months for that mouth, and it is the second arrest that office has entered against that name in a year, and the first is on the same leaf eleven pages back, entered in a room, with a witness, four words long.** His reason, in eleven seconds, and the whole of the inversion: **a private book in another country is not a public book; there is no officer of the Crown in this city, no register of arrests, no court that sits on one, no form on which a man may be named, and nothing in this kingdom can be got at that book except a man with a key — and a book that cannot be got at is the only place in the world in which a sentence a man said in a room four hundred miles off is safe to keep.** **The two arrests in this case are in the same book: the sentence in the first was spoken in a city that is not on any map, the sentence in the second in a county that is not on any map, the name in both belongs to a man from a third place, and the book is in the first of the three.** **Nobody in Merefen has a copy of either and will not for a long time; the counting house does not produce; the Archive holds a fair copy of a fair copy of an index leaf and would not be able to tell from the index that the book it is a copy of had a second thing in it that was not on the index.** Nobody in the room understands what it means; **Aren Kest understands an arrest in a private book four hundred miles off in about two seconds, the way he knows a load path, and says not one word, and does not put it in the notebook that night.** The arrest is the same document-shape as Chapter 26's and is the same kind of plant; **this is the second one and Volume 02 does not open it.**

216. **The rite worked for eleven weeks, and nobody in the County of Ash noticed anything wrong in it.** **Four hundred and nine boards through one room in eleven weeks, four sittings a day, about nine people in the room at a time, from the second hour to the sixth, and a queue outside in the wet that behaves itself without anybody organising it, because a county organises a queue better than a county organises anything.** Eleven minutes a person. **A press is not a job; it is a thing a four-year-old can do.** The witnesses' names come off the door, said out loud, unchecked about one time in nine, **and the clerk's pen does not move, and the whole of the load goes off the book and onto the room, and nobody in that hall had thought about it and the man who proposed it had written it down twice in two counties and was not going to act on it, because he was not looking at a structure.** **The restorations are read out at every sitting, four times a day, and nobody proposed that and nobody voted on it and there is no minute of it anywhere: it came about because a room with a queue outside it needs to know who is in it.** **It is the only civic work in this volume anybody has ever described as being watched rather than heard.** The reading stopped on the fourth day of the seventh month, in the tenth week, **and the reading had begun on the fourth hour of a day in the sixth month, read by Aren Kest himself, the first sitting of the four, for two days, and then handed to Fennie Loam on the third morning without a word being said about it, in the way a person takes a hod off another person — and the reading did not stop when he gave it up, and that is the only good thing in the business and is also the reason there is going to be nothing to be done about it.**

217. **Fennie Loam, 19, and the reading, and what it is that nobody noticed.** Apprentice at the assizes at three shillings a week, copies the roll, and has been the person in that room who knows how the names are spelt, how the hearths are set out, and what the trades are in the county's own abbreviations, better than anybody alive. She reads it properly: name, hearth, trade, at the speed of a woman reading a row of things she has copied four hundred times, **not one wrong, a comma where a comma goes, no sentence round it, and she does not say *and this one*** — and she gets better, and by the eleventh week the two men on the door are reading the names in with her half a beat behind, **and by the eleventh week a man at the back of the queue is reading them before she gets to them and she slows very slightly to let him keep up, and that is the most generous thing anybody did in that room in eleven weeks and she has no idea she did it.** She is **not to be blamed and is not to be absolved.** Her want, in her own words, in a corridor: she wants her master to have her back for it; she wants to hand in her copy and be told by a magistrate that she was right to read it; **she is told exactly that once, in Chapter 89, by Colm Arreld, in a room, deliberately not minuted, and she asks whether she may write it down and is told she may write *the magistrate said so in a room and he is not minuting it* and not one word more.** And the diagnosis nobody makes, which is the whole of Chapter 84's second half: **the cure is a record a person can contradict, and the fourth term is a struck person may answer, and answering has to be asked for, and there is no column headed *answered*, and a clerk's list of restorations read four times a day is not a consent and is not an asking and is not a contradiction: it is a reading.** The man who proposed the rite has worked that out and has no alternative to offer her.

218. **A record a person can contradict is a record of that person, and a record that cannot be contradicted is a claim about that person, and the founding act of the County of Ash was the attempt to build a claim.** Arrived at by a man counting things in a shed over four days, in his own words: **the founding act was fire everything, made in the fourth year after the register went, nine years to do, and it is the single cleverest act any of them ever took — a record that could not be argued with, a fired tile that cannot be re-pressed, a chip in it permanent, a hand in the back of it for ever and no man can put a word in it afterwards; they had lost a register and they were not going to lose the people, and it protected this county for nineteen years. It is the disease. A county that cannot lose its people cannot hear them either, and a man can obey a thing about himself for nineteen years and at the end of the nineteenth year not one word of his will be recognisable as his own.** The cure is what the county built by accident in four months: **a soft tile, in three hands, in three places, none authoritative alone, a person restored when two of the three agree and removed when two of the three agree otherwise, and the third allowed to be wrong — and it is being read too fast to survive.** Said by Pernel Quist in Chapter 86 in her own trade's voice and from the other direction: **a fired tile is the only record in this county a person cannot alter — not the clerk, not me, not a magistrate, not the county — and that is why this county trusted it for nineteen years, and I have just worked out in a wet shed in about a minute what that means.** The load is on the right side of eight hundred tons and **a document's load is not in tons; he has now written it in tons three times in two counties, and the sentence about noticing it has changed by one word — a man who has noticed a thing twice is not being careful, he is being consistent; a man who has noticed a thing three times is not being careful either, he is being consistent faster.** The bound was right every time; **what he got wrong was the consent: a promise is only as wide as the people who have agreed to it, and he had four hundred and nine of them and not one had agreed to anything, because he asked a hall at the fourth hour and a hall is not a person, and he had the words for a room and used the words for a county.**

219. **Ostry Vaile, 58, restored in the ninth week of the rite, softening in the same week, alive, employed, and correct in three books.** **He is fifty-eight, not forty-one** — forty-one is the hand of the Ninebridge. He is put back in eleven minutes on the ninth day of the ninth week by pressing his own tile, and he is read **eighty-four times in that hall** (four sittings a day, six days a week, from the ninth week to the last day of the eleventh; the four villages had nobody put back in them in the ninth week, so the whole of it was in Kilnholt), **and the man with the ledger puts four hundred and nine and four times a day on a slate together in the ninth week and then takes the slate off again with the back of his hand in about four seconds, because it is arithmetic about marks and not about people and he has never had a reason not to.** The softening is on the page as a **sequence in four ordinary things that are not the same kind of thing**, and the reason the man watching does not assemble them is the reason he has been careful for nineteen years: **he has trained himself to see where a load goes, and a person going back to being clay has no load path; there is nothing in it to trace and no strain and no crack and no stone moved, there is a man at a firing floor and the man is a man.** **The order of the day:** one crooked sagger ring, a finger out of true, stacked at the bottom instead of the top for six days, put right twice, and then on the sixth day not put right, and the man at the loading end goes and stands at the other end of the floor — four seconds, no decision, nine years without telling anybody. **The trade:** he walks onto the charging floor he has not stood on in nineteen years and does it better than the man who does it, and the man of thirty-one with a tile and a hearth and a wage is told there is no work for him at that kiln, and nobody decided it, and a kiln is a floor and there is one of each job. **The hearth:** two hundred and fourteen is called twice at the change of shift and the shift changes, and eleven men know and none of them says a word, **because a man who softens cannot be witnessed and this county's law requires two other persons to witness him, and the moment a man says it out loud he has said a thing that cannot be got out of the room.** **The name:** a man off a boat who has been laid off in a winter asks him which kiln he is at, and he says *that is the one I am at*.** The roll reads *Ostry Vaile. Hearth two hundred and fourteen. Trade: kiln-worker, Kilnholt*, the mark on the back of the tile agrees with the entry, it was pressed by him, it stands up in a dish and can be read at four feet on a wet morning, and **three books in three hands all of them right about him.** **Kilnholt has four kilns and the fourth column of a tile in this county has never once had to say which of the four, because a tile is of a person and a kiln is not a person and the roll is of persons; there is no kiln named on the roll of this county.** Hesper Lound puts a hand on his wrist for about a second and writes nothing, and the room sees kindness and the reader knows it is the law, **and the reason is that a physician who enters a soft person in a book has entered a person the law has already removed by operation, and there is no column and there is not going to be one.** **Nobody in this volume goes into his house**, and the reason is in the law and not in anybody's hardness: **a man who softens is not a man anybody calls on, and the reason is that a call is a thing you note down.** The last line of Chapter 83: **nobody in that room said one word, and the room was right, and the roll was right, and the tile was right, and there was nothing whatever in the County of Ash that was wrong.**

220. **He stopped the list alone, in a room, without asking, and it was the eleventh time in the book, and being right was sufficient.** **The fourth day of the seventh month, at the first hour, before the first sitting.** He does not ask the magistrate, the clerk, the rollkeeper, the hand of the Ninebridge or anybody. He puts his hand flat on a sheet in a girl's hand, says *I am going to take this off you*, and takes it, and says that there is no more reading out and that everything else in that room goes on exactly as it is. **The room stops for about two seconds and looks at about nine other people to find out whether the man who took it has any standing, and there is about one second in which the whole of the eleven weeks hangs on Pernel Quist, who is not in the room, and a clerk of the assizes, who is, and who does not say anything, because a clerk of an assizes cannot stop a thing happening in a room and can only enter one and can only enter a thing he is asked to enter.** **It is the eleventh time in the book and it is the cheapest of the eleven and it is the one that worked.** She does not fight him: *was I doing it wrong?* — and then, *then you can say what the right thing is, because I have been reading that list four times a day for eleven weeks and you have not told me one single time what it is for.* **He has no alternative, and there is not one in the county, and there has never been one and there is not going to be one.** The refusal that follows is the block's first and it is **professional and not civic**, and the hand of the Ninebridge says so in those words: **a clerk's hand is a thing you go through, not around, and a road is not a road unless everybody in a county can go and look at it, and that is what took nine years and about eleven men; and you have done the same thing in a morning, in a room, in a hat, on a girl of nineteen, and you did not ask her, and you are right; and being right is the whole of what has happened today; and a man who has spent eight months not asking anybody anything in this county is not being careful, he is being cheap; and being useful is a thing a man does for somebody, and this morning he was not useful to anybody in that room.** She also gives him the one thing nobody in the room wanted: **you were right about this county twice, or three times, and I have wanted to tell you so for about nine weeks, and I would have given a year's wages for it.** And the fourth refusal's meaning, stated in her own words and in the third person, which is new in this volume and is the guardrail: **she had said no to about four people who wanted her to be the hand of something else in nine years, and she said yes to witnessing a stage in about four seconds, and the two are the same act and she did not notice at the time and noticed at about the second minute.** The notebook's own accounting, and it is the load-bearing page of the block: **being right was sufficient; four times he was right, three of them somebody else paid, the fourth he paid, and he did not know he was paying; a man who has been refused twice stops asking for a thing and starts building it; and the bound is not what killed a man, and he has spent four days on a stool proving that he was right, and the proving cost him the belief that a man who is right about a structure can therefore build one that is right about people, and there is no such man and there is not going to be one.**

221. **There is no bell in the County of Ash, and the absence has a shape that only the man who sweeps can see.** Named out loud in Chapter 85, by **Garrick Dene, 64, of Ninebridge, who has swept the coping of the second span for forty-one years, came into the county at twenty-three with a broommaker's certificate, has swept nine bridges, and is the only person in the County of Ash who sweeps.** The facts: **nine spans and not one bell, and not a bell-cote, and not an iron stock, and not a staple, and not a hole in the parapet where one used to be, and no mark — and when a bell is taken off a bridge there is a mark: a socket scar, or two bolt holes, or a paler rectangle of stone where the soot stood, and you cannot sweep a place clean for a year and you do not need to because every man in the county can see it from the road and they all say nothing because there is a reason and the reason is a man in a coat.** There is nothing to say about it, and about a hundred and forty people cross that span on a market morning and not one of them has ever looked at the wall above the coping on the dry side, **and he has swept it four times a day for forty-one years, and they are not looking because there is nothing to look at.** And: **that is what it is like to sweep a bridge in a county that has never had a bell on it — you get used to it, you do not stop noticing, you just stop having anybody to tell.** The word: **a bell went in the county he came from for a fire, for a death, and for a refusal** (it is said in eleven words and the county is not named and no more about it is said), **and a county that has had that has a word for it, and this county's word for a thing that was asked for and not given is the word for a thing that got lost — one word for both — and he has worked that out over nine years and said it to two men and both of them were right.** The stewardlessness, **named from the bottom for the first time in the volume and not by the person it belongs to**: **where he came from the word for the man answerable for a bridge has a duty inside it, and the duty is that there is a thing about that bridge somebody is entitled to ask him; and a hand that cannot be asked is not a hand, it is a person holding something; and there has not been one single thing on that bridge in nine years that anybody in this county could have asked anybody about, so there is nothing to answer for, so nobody is answerable, so you cannot have the word, so they have got a good word instead, and a hand is a thing that holds on and a hand is not a thing that answers — and a man who is answerable for a thing that cannot be asked about is not a man, he is a shape, and they have got the right word for her and it is the right word, and the reason it is the right word is that there is not another one to use.** His want, in his own words: **to be allowed to lift a thing off a coping and sweep under it and put it back — not to take it away, because taking a thing off a coping is throwing it away and lifting a thing off a coping is doing the job, and there is not one stone moved and no water standing on that span that was not standing there when he came, and he would like to go down on his knees on that coping with a broom in his hand and he cannot, because it is not his to lift and the coping is the county's.** His fearlessness is a trade thing and not a courage thing: **fear is what you do with a thing you think somebody is going to ask you about, and a thing you have to work round every day for forty-one years is not a frightening thing, it is a shape** — the comparison is a stone four inches proud on the shoulder of that span, put there in his first year, which he thinks about four times a year and every time as *somebody ought to come and knock that off and put it in true.* He cannot say who put the mark there: he asked a man of about seventy when he was about thirty, twice over two years, and got *his grandfather put it there* and then *I do not know*, and the man is dead.

222. **The tile on the coping: a third close-range showing, a true sentence, and the price of it, and the object is not moved.** **The mark is described only as a stamp off a die this county does not have: *there is a stamp on the face of it and I do not know what it is a stamp of, and it is not a stamp off any die in this county — I have swept around about forty pressed tiles in my working life and I would know a Kilnholt mark or a Ninebridge mark or a north-village mark in the dark, and it is not one of those, and it is not a mill mark, and it is not a guild mark, because this county does not have a guild that presses tiles.*** **No character in this batch names the device and no character may, in this volume or any other.** Garrick Dene has a name for it — *the old mark* — and it is a workman's name and it is not going to be written down anywhere, **and that is the only moment in the whole of Volume 02 in which the tile is treated as ordinary.** **Aren Kest says the true thing.** He kneels, he does not touch it, and he says: *I don't know.* And the price, in the notebook that night, the last line of the third entry: **in the eleventh month she asked him what it was and he said he did not know and she said Good, and in the twentieth day he told her it was a good tile and she knew he was lying and she thanked him for the answer and said she would like it to go on being the whole of it — and that was a protection, because a man who does not know a thing cannot be asked what he has found out; and today a man of sixty-four who has swept round that mark for forty-one years asked him one question and he answered it truthfully, and he is going to be a man who has said I do not know about a thing three times in one county in front of three people, and three people in one county is a county, and in nine years the question is going to be what a man off the road said on the coping of the second span, and there will be two answers, and one of them is a lie.** **He has bought a protection with a debt and he does not know the rate, and the debt is the sentence a keeper of records in a provincial town gave him eleven years ago: a thing said out loud and believed, and believed wrongly, is a debt and not a release.** **The tile is not taken down, not moved, not touched, and not explained, and the eight inches under it are not swept.** The reader has had the device since Chapter 10 and Chapter 85 is the chapter that gives it its scene and it does not explain it.

223. **Bel's tile, a river, and a county that has no such river, and the sentence that is the most frightening thing in the volume.** **A man at a cutting bench turns over a board in the fourth row in the eighth month because the shed has been aired and says *that is a river*, and about nine people in that shed look at a wet board.** The mark: **a bend coming in from the left and turning away to the right; a wheel on the bend with eight spokes, which is more spokes than there are in any wheel in this county; and under the wheel six short lines across a thicker one, which is a weir.** **Bel, four, is at the fourth row and says *that is mine* and then, asked who did it, *I did* — and the man at the loading end says afterwards that it was the only straight answer he had heard in this county in nine years and that what made it straight was that she was not defending it, she was reporting it, in the way a person reports which board a thing is on.** **Nobody touched it, and the reason is given in one second and is the volume's mechanism: a soft tile is the only record a person can change, and nine people who work with clay every day worked out together that not one of them was going to be the last pair of hands on that clay.** The woman who fires at the Kilnholt kiln, sixteen years and not named on the page, **can give the date to the minute — the last spring, the rolling before the low water, a morning when the boards were aired and about nine women were at the loading end and Bel was four for about five months — and can say she saw a child do it, and she did not go over, and she has been not going over about four months, and she said it only when the keeper of the roll asked her a question about a date, because a woman who has been not going over for four months can put a date down in about four seconds once somebody asks her for one.** **Aren Kest recognises it in about a second and a half — the shape a river takes above a mill on the Sable, four hundred miles off, on the mill side of the water, with a weir of six slats — and does not say the name, and does not say the county, and gives the room the longest sentence he has said in a room in this county, which is about himself and not about a road, and is a refusal, in his own words, to do the sixth thing he has done in eleven years.** The hand of the Ninebridge, from the doorway, without coming in: **then let the room have the mark.** Pernel Quist's sentence: **we have been looking at the wrong tile**, and then four minutes, and then the cost: **a child draws what has been in front of her face, and in about three years this board is going to be fired, and the mark is going to be a hand, and the river is going to be gone, and there is not going to be one thing in the world that says a woman of Kilnholt put a bend and a mill and a weir on the only account of herself that anybody will ever be able to take off her — and there is no instrument.** And the notebook's own finding, which is a new shape in this volume: **there is nowhere in the County of Ash to put a fact that is true and about nobody** — not the roll, because a child is not on it for three years; not the bench, because a bench votes and if this were voted about there would be a column with a number against it; not the assizes, because a fact about a person goes into a book in a room and a room is where a thing like that ends up; **and the man with the ledger has been putting such things in a book for nineteen years and his book is a press, and a press is the only thing in the world you cannot quietly take out of, and that is the answer to the question this notebook has been asking for nine months and it was in a shed the whole time.**

224. **The ninth month, the room where a county says out loud that it has no word, and the two sentences, and the physician's four pages read twice.** **A room of about fifty people cannot agree on a word for what happened to them: forty minutes, eleven words, and every one of them is a word for a book — list, book, roll, column, stamp, the year the register went — and not one is a word for a thing that was done to this county by somebody and could not be refused.** The woman of about sixty, on three hearths in this county, two men buried, the fourth column of her tile saying *wife* which is not a trade, **the second entry in nineteen years in that office against a person by name that is not a removal**: **we did not lose our register. We lost our refusal. A register is what you have instead** — and then, *it is still the wrong shape and I am not going to go and I have not got a trade for it*, and she sits down. **The hand of the Ninebridge's road sentence, in her own words, the first time in the volume it is said in a room by the person it belongs to, and neither sentence is a paraphrase of anything said in Merefen**: **a hand answers for a road and a hand can be asked, and those are two halves and everybody always puts them together and they do not go together; she has been the hand of the Ninebridge for nine years and nobody has ever asked her one single question about it, not the magistrate, not the rollkeeper, not the physician, not the bench, not a man off a road; a hand that cannot be asked is not a hand, it is a person holding something; and you have not got a word for the person who answers and you have not got a word for the person who asks, and it is not because this county is poor or cut off or behind a river, it is because there has never been anything in this county that could be asked — so there was nothing to be answerable for, and so nobody had to answer, and so nobody learned how, and that is why there has never been a hand here that had to answer. Not because we are better than other counties. Because nothing has ever been put in front of us.** And then the correction, in the same breath, which is the load-bearing part: **we have had four hundred and nine of them since the sixth month; what we do not have is a word for what we lost; those are not the same thing; and about forty people in this room have got them the wrong way round in their heads, and the two are so different that the difference is the whole of what any of us has done for nine years.** **The physician's four pages are read again and this time nobody misunderstands them, and it is the county's own doing and not a stranger's** — *a county that has been made into marks for nineteen years and has spent four months being read four times a day cannot hear the second page of a finding without understanding it, and that is not a thing about me, that is a thing about what you have been doing for eleven weeks* — **and he puts the four pages on the table and says anybody in the room may pick them up, and nobody does.** And the cord-cutter's two hundred books: **about two hundred people asked once, in their own sheds, in about a minute and a half a house, the answers in the asker's own hand, a book shut, in a box under a bench in his shed, not in this room and not going anywhere, and he has not read it since the fifth month and is not going to; a woman came in the eighth month and asked whether he still had it and he said yes and she said nothing else and went out; and the answer *yes* has already been said out loud in a kitchen in this county twice and he was not there for either.** And the unresolved thing, which is item 44's argument and the physician's objection and the chapter's argument waiting to be said by somebody: **the cure is three copies in three hands and three places, and he would like somebody to explain what the difference is between a thing a man writes in his own hand in his own house and a thing a woman presses into a board with her own thumb — in this county, in a shed, at a cost.** Nobody answers him. **The best civic work in this volume may be a fire, and that is unresolved and is not resolved in Chapter 98.**

225. **Term four goes into the county's own book as a rule, and the county's own answer to term four, and the cost of it, and the two lines are not the same.** **The two hundred and six went on strike in the ninth month and it was over in six days, and what ended it was not a favour and not a wage: the fourth of the county's own five lines, being that a person who is struck may answer, and if a person who is struck answers, the person is restored, pressed into a tile in the first month of his time there and entered in a book at the assizes, and carried for three years and not put anywhere.** The strike costs a rolling: **a charged rolling is nine days of clay and cannot wait, and it went back into the wedging shed on the third day, and the floor of the wedging shed is the scarce part of the scarcest room in the County of Ash, and a room that is the floor of a strike has to be aired afterwards whatever anybody does in it.** Nobody argues about whether they are right; **the cord-cutter tells the bench, in about nine sentences, that the county has had a rule in a book for three years and has been working round it the way a man works round a stone on a shoulder, and that if the bench does not put it somewhere in the next six days they will go home and not come back for a bench to find out why, because they have found out why about four times and the answer is always a room.** The bench adopts it: **in force from that hour, not conditional upon the road, and not conditional upon any office, to be read at the person, with the book in the room, and the person's own tile on the table, and two witnesses whose names are in the book** — the first entry in nineteen years in that office that is a rule and not a decision — **and it records that the county has had the line for three years and has not put it anywhere, and that the reason is not a reason and is that there is no office in this county to put a line anywhere, no writ to answer, no Crown's man and no Crown's schedule carrying this county; and it enters, at the keeper of the roll's request and in her own words and without improvement, that in about nine years a man will ask which clerk decided this and that there is no clerk and there is going to be no clerk, and that a rule with no office behind it is a thing that can be argued with, and that is the whole of the argument for it and not one part against.** A county writes the volume's sentence out in a keeper's mouth without ever having heard it. **The county's own answer, and it is not Aren's: the re-reading is broken up into eight villages and the market town separately, at the same time, over nine days; the roll does not move and nine lists go out; a list is the names of the people in that place, in that place's own order, in that place's own hand, and a person is on it if two people on it say he is, and a person is on the roll of persons on it or not.** About forty minutes a person against eleven. **It is slower per man and better than the rite, and those two things are the same fact**: *the rite took eleven minutes a person and I could do it; this takes forty minutes a person and I cannot do any of it, and that is not a fault in it — the rite can be run by one man in one room and if that man goes out of the county then it stops, and it stops today, and he is four hundred yards off in a shed this afternoon and it is going to be going in nine places without him in it;
