the county's is nine times slower per man and it can be run by anybody in this county who can read and count, and about nine hundred people in this county can read and count, and there is not one of the nine hundred who has to be him, and in about nine years that is the only thing anybody will be able to say about it that matters.* **The cost: nine boards of lists copied by nine men in the one room in the county with a floor that will take nine pairs of boots — a day and a half of that floor, which is nine days of wedging, and the wedging is the spring's clay** — entered in one line in the county's own book, and **the chapter ends on a county that has spent a day and a half of a wedging shed keeping a piece of paper, and that figure is Chapter 68's and Chapter 79's day and a half and it is the same scarce room and a later writer must not add the two and must not re-cost the ninety cords of Chapter 78.**

226. **The road did not open on the ninth of the ninth month, and it did not fail, and nobody is to say afterwards that it failed.** **The witness at the Ash end was a person the county chose in the first month, under the fifth of its own terms, and it opened on the fourth day of the first month of the year because he was there. He was struck in the eighth month, on a list of about two hundred made in the fifth month and not through a room yet; the county's own reading of its own rule took nine days, his place in the order was on the eighth day of them, and the road's day was the ninth.** **A man who is struck cannot be a witness**, and the county had eleven weeks to replace him and did not, and the reason it did not is the reason the county built the thing that emptied the line, and **nobody in that room is going to point that out.** **Cobb Rennick, 52, a tile-cutter at the Ninebridge kiln, nineteen years on the roll, struck in the sixth month and put back in the seventh week of the rite, is the county's second witness-candidate and refuses in nine sentences, and asks for the word *mouth* to be left in the clerk's reading back: *I have been the mouth of this county for four months and I have had my name read out of a sheet until I could not hear it. I want to be a mark and not a mouth.*** His want, in his own voice, is that sentence and it is the whole of Chapter 88's refusal. **There was no third candidate, and the bench does not hold a list of people who have been asked to stand somewhere, and nobody has ever thought of asking it to, because the fifth of the county's own terms says the witness at Ash's end is chosen by Ash and does not say a word about a list.** The hand of the Ninebridge says it before anybody can: **it did not fail. A road is a road. A road does not try. There is a road and there is a day and there is a man who stands at the end of it, and today there was no man at the end of it, and the road was not there, and the two of those are the same size and the same shape and the same absence, and a man who has to write down that a road failed is a man who has found a way of making it somebody's fault.** A bridge four hundred years old with sixty years of tiling has been shut a hundred times and has not failed once, and both times it was shut while somebody stood about looking as though the river had done it. **On the last day of the ninth month a woman of about sixty steps forward off the dry side of the step without a word being said to her and stands at the end of the line, and says *I do not know what I am standing in*, and is told she is the witness at this end and is not the hand and is on the roll with a tile of her own that agrees with the book; and when she asks what happens if she is not there on the day she is told *then it is not there, and it will not be anywhere near it, and nobody in this county is going to say the word failed*; and she says then I will be there on the day; and nobody thanks her; and the clerk of the assizes writes the hour against it in a flat unhurried hand and does not improve it, and it is the only entry in that book in nineteen years that is a person standing somewhere and not a removal.** **The second route is simply not there for the rest of the volume. He is four hundred miles away with a door and no road, and the last nine months come down as one scheduled message on a named day in the form the Second Door allows, in a fair hand, being a list, and that is Chapter 90 and it is not this chapter.** And the notebook's one line about the whole of the ninth month: **I did not call it. Nobody in this county called it. I have spent four months working out how to call a thing that does not answer when it is called, and a man at a cutting bench in this county had worked it out in about four minutes in the fourth month, and what he worked out was that you do not call a road, you stand at the end of it.**

227. **Pernel Quist signs, and her reason, and it is the volume's most professional sentence and it is not spent anywhere earlier.** **She refuses for two days — one day saying nothing, the second asking for the sheet back to see whether the third hand is still a letter — and then signs, and the minute records that the third hand is a letter and that it is a letter because a bench lost a vote about it the day before.** Her reason, in her own words, four sentences: **a roll that can be wrong is not a weaker roll. It is a roll that has to be read twice, and a roll that has to be read twice cannot be read by one man in a morning.** And then the half that is the whole of the volume: **nineteen years I have kept a roll that cannot be read twice, in one room in one building with a box under the table, and it is the only thing in this county that does not move, and I have been proud of that every day of nineteen years, and about four hundred people have gone through a room in nine days because of it, and one of them does not know which kiln he works in and the roll is right about him and it was right about him in the ninth week of the rite as well and it is going to be right about him in nine years — a roll that cannot be read twice cannot survive a person, and this county has just had a person in it for nine days and I have been sitting in a room with a box under the table while it happened, and I have been right, and my being right is the reason.** She asks for both halves entered and untidied: **she was wrong about the third hand in the twenty-second week and she was not wrong about the anchor, and one of those is not going to be improved into the other.** She signs a roll whose third hand is a letter on the ground that the rule permits the third to be wrong, and the clerk reads it back and she corrects the word *weaker*. **This is the answer to her Chapter 80 objection and it is Lound's fourth page, and it is a keeper's mind arriving at a physician's finding four years late, and neither of them knows what the other said.**

228. **The bench wins a reduction and loses a vote, and the vote is the eleven sheets, and a promised hand that costs a man his season is a list.** **The reduction: the price of a tile to a household goes down a farthing, and the reason is not in the minute, because a reason is a thing believed and a minute is a thing set.** **The vote, lost, four for and three against, the keeper of the roll not voting and entering why: she is one of the three hands and a hand that votes on whether it is a hand is not a hand.** The question: whether to send a man of this county four hundred miles off to fetch eleven sheets out of a press in a room with a door on a lane, so that the third hand of the roll in three hands can be a shelf instead of a letter. **The second reason against is the witness — there is no witness at this end and a man cannot walk out of a county he cannot be fetched out of — and the third is the volume's argument in a trade's mouth: a promised hand is not a hand, and a promised hand that costs a man his season is not a promised hand, it is a list; we have had a list, I have been in a kiln shed with a list of about two hundred on it for four years and I have watched what it does, and a list is what a county makes when it cannot pay a man for a day, and a promised hand four hundred miles off that costs one of thirteen men a season is the same list with a nicer name on it, and I am not going to be the man who gives this county that.** The minute ends: *The bench has resolved that no man shall be sent. The bench has entered that it is unable to say whether the third hand is a hand, and that it has resolved not to find out this year.* **The third hand is therefore still a letter at the end of the volume, and the roll is in three hands and two of them are hands, and that is not to be resolved in Volume 02.** The kilns: **two of four re-open on a schedule of two thirds with the hour against it; the one at the east village is not re-lit, in the book, in one line, because there is no wood and the wood is a season; about thirteen did not go back, and the thirteen are thirteen, and the number is in a keeper's hand with the date and the hour on it and not one word of an opinion, and it is the only figure in this county everybody has agreed on without an argument and it is agreed because it is the ugliest one.**

229. **Ash begins to un-fire its own founding act, and the number is 254, and the count is not the other subtraction.** **The man with the ledger counts it himself and it is not 456 and he says so three times and asks the clerk to enter that he has said it three times.** The derivation, in his own words: **there is a hand on the back of a tile and it belongs to the person; a soft tile can be re-pressed by anybody with a thumb and a wet cloth; a fired tile cannot be re-pressed; so a person who has been fired twice has two tiles with the same hand on the back of them, and the two of them are in two different kilns, and the county has never counted them because the county counts fires and a fire is a kiln and a kiln is on a wall.** He got to **two hundred and fifty-four** — two kilns, one village, and about half the wall of a third, **a floor and he will not go past it, and a stack in the back of the Kilnholt shed stays shut because he has a date in the fourth of a month and does not open a stack in the fourth of a month.** **The reason it is not the four hundred and fifty-six is that that one is every tile over the number of people and this one is a count of tiles with a second hand on them, and one of those is a thing about a shortage and the other is a thing about a person; and 456 is a floor of a surplus and it has not been divided, multiplied, worked back from or entered on his slate, and a later writer may not scale it into 254 or into anything.** The cost, footed on a board and copied by the boy with the slate including the blanks: *two hundred and fifty-four fired tiles, broken by hand — there is no mill in this county and there is not going to be one; a tile is six inches by four and about two fingers thick and a man breaks one in about four seconds.* · *thirteen men, five days, at five pence the day: three hundred and twenty-five pence, six marks three shillings and one penny* · *wood for the proof rolling, nine cords, at the kiln gate, two shillings and sixpence the cord: two hundred and seventy pence, five marks two shillings and sixpence* · *the whole of it: five hundred and ninety-five pence, twelve marks one shilling and seven pence.* Checked: 13 × 5 × 5 = 325; 9 × 30 = 270; 325 + 270 = 595; 595 = 12 marks (576) + 19 pence = one shilling seven pence. **It will be two years, and the first of it is now.** The season, in the county's own frame, because this county has no month a clerk in another country could write down: **the standing water, being the time between the second low water and the next first firing, when the Kiln is up over the working ground and there is no clay to dig and nothing worth burning, and it is the only season in which a man who is out of work and a room with a floor are both available at the same time, and it is about four months long and it is the season thirteen men have had since the twelfth month.** The motion: **a hand setting a thing true against a thing out of true, done four times now — a woman's hands on a clamp bar in a filing room over a salt merchant, one night, alone, on purpose left out of true for a year; a man's hands on a garboard on the third slip of a ferry yard in a city four hundred miles off; a thumb in a dish, forty times an hour for five days, at a kiln — and none of the three has said one word about it to any of the other two and none of them is going to, because a movement is not a fact, and if you put a movement in a book then a man in nine years can ask you what it was for, and the answer is that it is the same thing done by three people who never met, and that is not an answer anybody in this county can use.** **This is the slowest, least dramatic and most permanent act in the book and it is the same motion three times, and it is not a symbol and nobody says it is.**

230. **The terms of the Second Door, printed in the Office's hand on the back of a form, and the stage, and the object, and the list.** **The terms have been in a drawer in a shed at the north end of the Ninebridge since the seventeenth week, folded twice, as the wrapper of a form of the R.C. series, and the hand of the Ninebridge has carried boards past that drawer about nine times a day for four months and has never read the small print, and the reason is that there is nothing in this county that is going to tell her what to do with a form, and she asks for that entered.** **The five terms, as printed:** *1. Two routes, and both held, and the holding of one is not the holding of the other.* *2. Two carried over, and the carrier shall not be able to say which path gave him which.* *3. A return, prepared before each crossing by two hands, and a date, and a witness at each end who is not the person keeping the road; and if the witness is not there on the day, the route does not open, and the route does not fail.* *4. One message and one object. The message shall be a list, and a list can only be checked. The object shall weigh under a pound, and the far side shall be able to use it, and the near side shall be able to do without it.* *5. The second route does not answer when it is called. The far side decides. There is no procedure for a far side that has not decided.* — and beneath them, in a smaller type, as a standard of measurement, **which is what it is and is not a term and is not about any of the five: *a road held, three weeks*.** **Term three is not an office and there is no Crown's man who can be asked to fill it in; it is a town four hundred miles off working it out on a bench in nineteen years and putting it in a minute, and the same sentence is now printed at the foot of a form in an office that cannot say whose figure it is, and two people are carrying it about four hundred miles apart in two countries.** **Stage 2 is reached by qualification and not by award, in a shed with a door on the side of it, with the hand of the Ninebridge as the only witness in the county who can be asked and the boy of sixteen copying the page and the blanks under it; four of the five lines were already true and had been for between four and twenty-two weeks; there is no word for a stage in this county and there could not be, because a county that counts fires has not got a way of counting a man; and the only record of it is a page in the back of a notebook in a flat hand.** **The object is half a tile, and the county's own argument for it, in four minutes: a whole tile is the account of a person and a half tile is the account of nothing; a chip is too small to be anything and a whole tile is a record of a person, and about nine hundred half tiles are in two hoppers behind the Kilnholt kiln and are the only thing in the County of Ash nobody is entitled to want; and the half that goes is the half that had nothing on it, because the half that had the hand on it is four hundred yards away in a box in a house in Kilnholt and it stays there.** **It was weighed at the kiln gate on a stone and came to a shade over ten ounces, and the weighing is the only figure in this business anybody asked for, and it was asked for in the only two words anybody in this county has ever used about the north: *to the north*.** And why: **a keeper of records on the other end of that string can hold it and put it in a press and weigh it, and there is not one thing in it that anybody can take out of it, and she can do that for nine years and it will be the safest object that has ever been in her office — and the county has sent it four hundred miles to be safe in a press, and he has got no way of finding out whether it got there and is not going to ask, because a man who asks whether an object arrived has told the other end that the object is worth arriving.** **The message is a bill, four hundred words, in the hand of the man with a ledger, itemised, every line a figure or a date or a trade, checkable line by line by a clerk four hundred miles off — the two hundred and six asked once, the four hundred and nine twice and the second time being the one that counts, the price, and the road, and a line that is a number and not a name — and a list can only be checked, which is the reason and not a decoration, and a county that had been refused nothing in nineteen years worked that out in about four minutes in a shed, and a man who has spent eleven years putting lists into counties by arguing about them in front of witnesses did not tell them one single thing about it.** It is not a message. It is a bill.

231. **The list came down a line on a day the road did not open, and a keeper of records could not put down line nine, and the last line of the block is the reason she wrote the promise down, and the reason is not a sentence about the man.** **It is not the last line of the volume: `outline/volume-02.md` §12's final chapter image carries the same beat, Batch 0009 landed it here, and Chapter 100 may not restate the reason in any form — the new thing at Chapter 100 is the entry, made a second time against nobody with the hour on it.** **Four hundred miles off, on the morning before he comes up, at about the sixth hour, a list comes down a line in a fair hand** — on the fourth, which is a day the road is kept, **put in at the north end by a hand at that end with a witness at that end who is not the person keeping the road, and it is the only thing that has come down that line in three months, and it came on a day when the road did not open at either end, because a message is not a route, and the county does not know that and put the list in on the day it was going to be kept.** Mara Vey reads every line in about fifty minutes, writes nothing, enters it in the day-book against no person with the hour, in the ordinary form, with a claim number against the office and against no person and no opinion, and reads it a second time at about the seventh hour and **finds nothing to argue with and one thing she cannot put down.** **Line nine of the last hundred words, a number and not a name: of the four hundred and nine, one has not been spoken to since the ninth week of the room at the assizes; the other four hundred and eight have been; he is at a kiln; his hearth has a fire in it on the nights this county can see it from a road; the clerk of the roll does not know which kiln and neither does he; it is entered here as a number and not as a name because the second of the county's own terms is forty-one words long and the county has kept it, and because this county has no column for it.** It is the same man as the four lines copied exactly into her own fair hand about six months ago at the eleventh hour against no person, entered ever since and looked at about nine times, and **it is the ninth time in four years that a record has arrived in that room about a person who cannot be found because he cannot be asked, and the difference this time is that the record is not asking her anything.** She cannot put it down, and her four reasons, written on the back of the list in pencil and then rubbed out with her own hand **because a keeper who writes four reasons on the back of a document has made a document about a person:** one, a keeper who enters a fact about a person begins in about a year to assemble facts about that person; two, **she cannot get a claim number for it, because a claim is against a person or a body, and a county is not a party and cannot sue or be sued and has no officer in this kingdom that can take a return, and the man who could tell her which county it is is four hundred miles off and cannot be asked, and she wrote that reasoning herself into a day-book about six months ago and it is correct and it is the reason she sent the second answer to nobody then and the reason she is not going to send this to anybody now**; three, **the office's own second rule, that no combined figure is entered in this office, and she has two figures about one man and neither has a name in it and she can prove neither against the other, and if she writes one of them under the other she has made a roll of one person out of two counties, and a roll of one person is the thing this whole case is about**; and four, the promise. **And the last line of the block, and the reason the promise was written in Fallowmonth at about the tenth hour on a page that has nothing against it, and the reason is not a sentence about the man: because a keeper of records who asks a question has already decided that the answer is not in the records she keeps, and a keeper who has decided that has stopped being a keeper and started being a person who wants something.** She enters the line as nothing and enters the not writing as the opinion entered, for the fourth time this year, and the page has got a number on it and not a name. **The not asking is now a choice and the choice is the wound, and the difference between a protection and a thing a keeper is doing is about a decision, and she has noticed the change and has written the day she noticed it and it is late.** And: **the second route does not answer when it is called, and the volume ends with a route that will not answer and a man four hundred miles off with a door and no road.**

232. **Cast, and what was spent, and the guardrails, in one place.** **Three new names first named in prose in Batch 0009 — Cobb Rennick, 52, a tile-cutter at the Ninebridge kiln; Fennie Loam, 19, an apprentice at the assizes who copies the roll; Garrick Dene, 64, of Ninebridge — and three slots unfilled, as the card allows.** **The volume total is now sixteen of eighteen, and the unfilled slots are eight of twelve, and Batch 0010 is two names and four unfilled, which makes eighteen and twelve, which is the cap of thirty.** Bel is named and is a child and does not count. **Three people in this batch are addressed by trade and are not named on the page, and all three deliberately:** the woman who fires at the Kilnholt kiln, sixteen years, because a county with no column for a person has not got her in one; **the woman of about sixty who closes the argument in Chapter 87 and stands at the end of the line in Chapter 90, whose fourth column says *wife* and who is the second entry in nineteen years in that office against a person by name that is not a removal, and nobody in that room had asked her name until the clerk did;** and **the clerk of the counting house in Chapter 81, fifty-eight, thirty-one years, who is not a Crown's man, not in the Crown's service, and has never been asked to be anything, and whose eleven seconds of reasoning are the whole of the chapter's second half.** **The kiln clerk is addressed as the man with the ledger and his name is withheld by design and must not be given in this volume.** Everyone else in the batch is addressed by trade: the clerk of the assizes, the boy with the slate, the boy of sixteen who rings a bell, the man at the cutting bench, the man at the loading end, the ash-driver, the cord-cutter, the kiln-man who called a hearth twice, the man who was the charger, the man off a boat off a kiln at the north village, the man on a kiln floor at the fourth hour. **Nobody in the batch dies. Nobody is killed. No named person is arranged into a death for effect, and the only death in the batch is the man of about seventy who may or may not have had a grandfather who put a mark on a coping, and he has been dead and it does no work.** No arch goes down, no glass breaks, no seal is broken on a bell. **No melody cost is added anywhere in this batch and none may be, and the word does not appear in it.** The word *Gatehouse* is not spelled out anywhere in this batch. The phrases *Culling Order* and *custodian* do not appear. The word *Bind* does not appear and nothing is put into any material. **The private-keeper clause is not mentioned, referred to, explained or noticed by anybody in this batch**, and Chapter 90 — the only chapter in the volume in which the back of an R.C. form is turned over — says that there is not one person in the County of Ash who has ever read the back of a form and does not say what else is printed on that reverse. **The two eighth ruled lines do not appear in this batch at all and no character refers to either.** **No two of the volume's five blanks are in one room, in one chapter or in one sentence, and no character says that any two of them are the same: the fifth space with no sixth is in Chapter 83 alone, the fired-tile claim is in Chapter 86 alone, and the six lines with nothing against them and no reason on them are in Chapter 79 and are not touched here.** The city is still not on the served schedule. **No main-world weekday and no real-world month is spoken anywhere in Chapters 81–90**, and no Ash weekday is used either: Chapter 82's two and Chapter 83's four were cut after the first draft for that reason and are the only ones in the batch.

233. **The Batch 0009 card and prompt deviations, recorded so that no writer re-derives them as errors.** (1) **Ostry Vaile is fifty-eight, not forty-one**; forty-one is the hand of the Ninebridge's age and the prompt slipped. (2) **Both arrests in this case are in the same book in the same city** — the prompt's *in two countries* is corrected on the page to: two arrests, a sentence spoken in a city that is not on any map, a sentence spoken in a county that is not on any map, a name belonging to a man from a third place, and the book in the first of the three. (3) **The Chapter 80 notebook was written in this county, at the low end of a rack at the Ninebridge, and no league figure is printed anywhere in this batch.** (4) **The rite's eleven weeks and the county's months are not convertible, and the batch says so in Chapter 82 in the county's own voice rather than resolving it:** a month of four weeks cannot hold the rite in one month, and the rite's ninth week is in the seventh month and Aren's four days follow immediately. **Do not re-derive a conversion.** (5) **The rite is read by Aren Kest himself on the first two days, the first sitting, and then handed to Fennie Loam on the third morning, which is the corridor scene in Chapter 82 and the reason the guilt is his and not only hers; and the reading did not stop when he gave it up.** (6) **The un-firing is scheduled in the standing water, the county's own name for the season between the second low water and the next first firing, because this county has no Hearthmonth.** (7) **The distance from the County of Ash to Merefen is four hundred miles, not two hundred**, and two hundred miles is the distance to the Office of Provincial Registration and to the provincial town. (8) **The only filing room in the County of Ash is an unlocked drawer in a shed at the north end of a bridge, and that is where Chapter 90 puts the form, and the chapter says why.** (9) **The clamp was straightened by Mara Vey's hands in Chapter 10 and Chapter 14, not by Aren's**, so the Chapter 89 echo names who did which: hers on a clamp bar, his on a garboard of a ferry slip, and a man of fifty-eight with a thumb in a dish. (10) **Vaile's own figure is eighty-four sittings, and the four hundred belongs to the list and not to a man**, and the man with the ledger puts the two on a slate together and takes it off again in about four seconds. (11) **A day and a half of a wedging shed in Chapter 88 and a day and a half of a wedging shed in Chapters 68 and 79 are the same scarce room and the two are never added, and the ninety cords and the price column of Chapter 78 are not re-summed, extended or printed in this batch.** (12) **The 456 is not re-derived anywhere in this batch and is not scaled into 254**; it is named once, in Chapter 89, in a trade's mouth, as a figure the man with the ledger said out loud in the first month and has not used.

234. **The Batch 0009 review repair pass, in one place, and what a later writer must not undo.** **Ten findings, all applied: eight to the ten chapters, one to two state files' arithmetic, one structural. (Two chapters were left alone and one pair of chapters was touched twice, so the count of files is smaller than the count of findings.) No plot beat removed, no chapter renumbered, no card broken, no date the batch is built on moved, no next-phase prompt's beat touched, and no controller file touched. The batch is 40,772 words, and it is the same ten scenes it was.** The load-bearing corrections, in the order a later writer will trip over them. **(1) Meta-language, thirty-three instances, all removed from the prose and put into the room.** Chapters 81–90 carried *this volume* thirty-three times, *the volume's argument*, *the whole of the volume*, *this chapter*, *the chapter*, and two passages that stepped furthest outside the fiction: **Chapter 82's second paragraph, which told the reader that a reader trained by eleven months of this book would spend the following forty pages waiting for a character to say something, and Chapter 83's fifth, which told the reader that a reader who had been waiting eleven months for a man to die was going to find that this volume did not do that.** Both are now about the room and the people in it. **The density was the finding and not merely the words: Batch 0008 carries 46 such references across thirty chapters and Volume 01 carries about a dozen across fifty, and Batch 0009 carried 32 across ten, so the batch roughly doubled a rate the manuscript had been holding down for ninety chapters.** **(1a) Two unpaired bold openers, in Chapters 84 and 86, each inside an italic notebook entry and each never closed**, so the emphasis ran away to the next `**` in the file; both are closed where the author put them, and no word was added or removed. The rule now recorded for the rest of the volume: **no narrator may use *this volume*, *this chapter*, *the reader*, *the author* or *this book*, and no chapter may comment on its own length, on the block, or on what the reader is being led to expect. A notebook entry in Aren's own hand may say *this book*, because the notebook is a physical object in his bag; that is the conceit and it predates Volume 02.** The three surviving *this book* instances in the batch are all of that kind: two are in Aren's hand in Chapter 84 and one is a private book in a counting house in another country in Chapter 81. **(2) One main-world month name had leaked into a path-time chapter.** Chapter 88 said the line opened **on the fourth of Hearthmonth**, and its own next paragraph said **the fourth day of the first month of the year**, and continuity item 226 and the whole of Batch 0009's frame use the county's second form: **the County of Ash has no Hearthmonth and the batch says so twice in its own voice.** The chapter now says the county's form in both places, and the two paragraphs agree, and **the guardrail that no real-world month is spoken in Chapters 81–90 is true of the prose again.** The one month name left in the batch is **Fallowmonth in Chapter 90, and it is correct there: it is Mara Vey's own earlier entry in a main-world day-book, four hundred miles off, in the last movement of the chapter.** **(3) The batch's word count was wrong in the state files, twice, and one of the two errors was a figure carried over from another volume.** The writer phase recorded **40,778 words and a Chapter 90 of 5,827**; the chapter files give **40,772 and 5,847** after this pass removed twenty-six words, and **5,827 is Batch 0004's Chapter 39.** The per-chapter column is now **3,779 / 3,757 / 3,955 / 4,678 / 3,277 / 3,628 / 3,667 / 4,144 / 4,040 / 5,847** and it foots to 40,772, and the four-batch column is **151,365** and it foots. **(4) The next-phase prompt did not exist and a state file said it did.** `state/current.md` asserted in three places that `workspace/volume-02/batch-0010/PROMPT.md` had been created by the writer phase, **and no such file existed, and Chapters 91–100 had no prompt anywhere in `workspace/`.** The prompt now exists and is the only one for that block, and it carries the ten cards, the binding guardrails, the closed day map, the cast cap of two names and four unfilled slots, and one correction this file must also record: **`outline/volume-02.md` §8's Chapter 100 card says two thousand nine hundred and six tiles in a kiln in Kilnholt and *four hundred and fifty-six of them are going to be ground back into clay in Hearthmonth*, and the prose of Chapter 89 supersedes it — the number being ground back is two hundred and fifty-four, arrived at by turning tiles over and looking for two with the same hand on the back of them, and the 456 is a floor of a surplus that has not been divided, multiplied, worked back from or entered on any slate.** The 456 may not appear in Chapter 100 as a count of tiles being ground, and no main-world month may be spoken in a sentence about that county's work. **The outline was not edited; the prompt and this item are the record, exactly as the two hundred and fifty leagues was handled at the end of the Batch 0008 repair.** **(5) The review's own findings were written only to a gitignored log**, which is the failure mode `reviews/README.md` exists to prevent, and **a durable record is now kept at `reviews/volume-02/batch-0009.md` with the disposition of every finding.** **(6) Nothing else was found, and the things that were measured and found sound are recorded so a later reviewer does not re-open them:** **no duplicated paragraph and no sentence of sixteen words or more appears twice in the batch**; the ban list is clean, with no *Gatehouse*, no *Culling Order*, no *custodian*, no *Bind* and no melody cost; the money foots, and the un-firing's column is **325 + 270 = 595 pence = twelve marks one shilling and seven pence** at forty-eight pence to the mark and twelve to the shilling, with thirteen men at five pence for five days and nine cords at thirty; the two eighth ruled lines do not appear in the batch at all and no two of the volume's five blanks are in one room, in one chapter or in one sentence; **the standing guardrail that a chapter may state both a weekday and a market day and may not reconcile them is unchanged and is inherited by Block 5, where the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth is a Monday and a market day.**

## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 90

- **Aren and Mara Vey:** **the block added no word between them and it added the one thing the volume has been building.** He is four hundred miles off on the last day of the ninth month with a bag and a half tile in it and a line of tarred hemp going out of a gateway; she is in a room over a weigh-house with a four-hundred-word bill in a fair hand in front of her and one line of it she cannot put down, and the line is about a man at a kiln who has not been spoken to since the ninth week, **and she has had four lines about that man in her own hand on a desk for about six months and has never been told anything and is not going to ask.** **The not asking has changed from a protection into a choice and she has written that it has changed and the reason, and the reason she writes in the day-book is the office's reason and not his: a keeper of records who asks a question has already decided that the answer is not in the records she keeps, and a keeper who has decided that has stopped being a keeper and started being a person who wants something.** **That is the last line of Volume 02's Ash material and the last line of the block, and the prompt's requirement is met: the reason is not a sentence about the man.** Four reasons are written on the back of the list in pencil and rubbed out with her own hand in about four minutes, **because a keeper who writes four reasons on the back of a document has made a document about a person**, and the third of them is the office's own second wall rule and it is the volume's whole subject: **if she writes one figure under the other she has made a roll of one person out of two counties, and a roll of one person is the thing this whole case is about.** He is on his way up. He will arrive on the second of Greenmonth, a Tuesday, at the first light, with a fever of three days, and he will give her the numbers, because he has never not given her the numbers, **and what he will not tell her is the middle of his own case, and it will be found with a date in Chapter 92 and not with a question, and the plant for it is now in his notebook four times in his own hand and is not resolved and may not be.**
- **Aren and the hand of the Ninebridge:** **the block's central relationship, and it is conducted entirely in trades and entirely without her name, and it has changed shape three times in nine chapters.** She gave him work on the terms *useful and not a visitor* and he has been useful for nine months and has asked her nothing. In the eleventh month she asked him one question and he lied, and she thanked him for the answer. In the sixth month he stopped asking about her street without being asked and did not explain it. In the seventh month **he took a page out of a clerk's hand in a room and she told him the price of it in about nine seconds, standing on a bridge with a roll of tools at her hip, and refused him, and it was a professional refusal and not a civic one and she said so in those words, and she also gave him the one thing he had not been told: that she had wanted to tell him he was right for about nine weeks.** In the eighth month she took a form out of a drawer and read the back of it because he asked her for a room with a table in it, **which is the first thing anybody in this county has ever asked her for in nine years**, and then she answered a question about the object he had not been asked, and she made the object in about four minutes, and she argued for it in a way that beat his. And in the ninth month **she witnessed a stage, in about four seconds, having said no to about four people over nine years who wanted her to be the hand of something else, and she said afterwards that the two are the same act and that she did not notice at the time and noticed at about the second minute.** The whole of what has happened between them in nine months is now: **a tile she kept on a coping for forty-one years, a form in a drawer for four months, a refusal on a bridge in about nine seconds, a stage in a shed in about four seconds, and a sentence about a hearing that is the whole of her want in her county's own voice and which he was in the room for and is not going to be told about, because she is a hand and a hand does not get asked and a tile-cutter is a person nobody has to explain herself to.** **Nothing has been repaired and nothing has been broken that was not already broken, and both of them would say that they are professionals, and both of them would be telling the truth, and the reader should not take it.**
- **Aren and Tovan Kest:** **not in this block at all, in either country, and the whole of the brother beat in this block is a sentence said on a market morning four hundred miles away in a gateway and not acted on: the yard is a fortnight, and when you come back it will be a fortnight or it will not, and if it is not then I will have had to put a man off, and it will be Tarrow, and I will not be able to pay him and he will not be able to ask me, and I am telling you now so that on the day you come back and I say nothing about it, you will not think it was your brother.** The estrangement is not healed, nobody has said the word brother in a scene, **and what Chapter 91 spends is the price of the return, which is that the man coming up cannot do the order of an ordinary morning any more and has not been able to do it since the fourth hour of the fourth of Hearthmonth, and he does not know what the order was, and his brother will not re-teach it and will say so in a yard in his own words.**
- **Aren and the magistrate (Colm Arreld):** **the block's answer to the Chapter 63 and Chapter 68 questions is that he did not ask once in nine months, and Arreld noticed.** He was four days away with a boundary when the rite was proposed and left a paper saying he would not give that room an instruction. He is in the room in Chapter 81 and says nothing that goes into a minute. He is the one who adopts term four as a rule. He is the one who refuses to minute the sense of the ninth month's room and says why in eight words. He is the one who tells a girl of nineteen once, in a room, deliberately not minuted, that she was right to read it. **And what he says about himself in Chapter 80 is now paid in a form he did not have: the list was the only instrument he had, and a stranger came off a road in the first month and gave the county a second one, and he is glad of it and has been ashamed for four years, and in the ninth month the county has a rule and a roll in three hands and about thirteen men out, and he is glader and more ashamed than he was, and he has not said so out loud, and a magistrate who is gladder and more ashamed at the same time is a magistrate who is going to be glad about a second thing in about a year and that is Chapter 98.**
- **Aren and the rollkeeper (Pernel Quist):** **the volume's best two professionals, and in this block he says eight words to her once more — *I have not got a trade for it* was Chapter 80 — and she says no to him twice in a corridor and then signs a thing she said in the twenty-second week she would not sign, and the whole of the movement is that he was right about the anchor and she was wrong about the third hand, and neither of them knows what the other wrote down.** She asks for a rule to be minuted with no office behind it. She asks for both halves of her own history entered and untidied. **She has never once been on a road and she is going to be the keeper of a document that four hundred miles off in a fen has to decide whether to enter, and that is Chapter 92 and it is not resolved here.**
- **Aren and Fennie Loam:** **four minutes in a corridor and a page out of her hand, and she is the only person in this volume who has ever worked him out and said so to his face, and she told him that the reading was a job and that he had stopped it for the wrong reason and that being glad about it was worse than arguing.** She does not know until about a year later that she was the reason. She is told once, in a room, by a magistrate, that she was right to read it, and she asks whether she may write it down and is told what she may write. **She is nineteen and she is the fastest mark in the County of Ash and the volume does not know that and she does not know that, and both of those are Chapter 84's whole argument, and she is not to be blamed and is not to be absolved.**
- **Aren and the struck:** **a man of fifty-eight who has lost the order of his own day and a man of fifty-two who has had his name read out of a sheet until he could not hear it, and the second of them said it out loud in a kiln shed in nine sentences and asked for the word *mouth* to be left in the minute, and neither of them is a plea and neither of them has asked the stranger for anything, and the stranger has not been able to help either of them and did not try.** A woman who has not spoken in nine months pressed for four minutes and eleven people said *yes* together. A man of fifty-eight went on strike for six days and the county's own fourth term ended it. **The struck's want is unchanged and it is not money and it is not a record: it is to be asked, and the volume has now shown what asking looks like when it is done four times a day by a stranger, and what it looks like when it is done once by a man in a shed, and both of them are marks and the physician's objection to both of them is on the page and is not resolved.**
- **Mara Vey and the second answer:** **the four lines she copied into her own fair hand about six months ago have now been answered by a line in a list from a county four hundred miles off, in a different hand, in a different country, four months later, with no name in either, and the join between them is that both are about a man at the Kilnholt kiln who cannot be found because he cannot be asked, and the join is not provable, and she is the only person in the world who can see both halves of it, and she has entered the second half against no person with the hour on it and has entered the not writing as the opinion, and the reason is written in full in a day-book, and the reason is about her office and not about him, and she wrote that reason in Fallowmonth and it is the last line of the volume's Ash material.**

## Canon established by Batch 0010 (Volume 02, Chapters 91–100, prose on the page)

**How to use this section.** Items 1–234 are the record of Volume 01, of the Volume 02 plan, and of Chapters 51–60 through 90, including Batch 0009's review repair pass, which is item 234. Items **235 onward** are the record of the ten chapters that closed Volume 02, and the Volume 02 close is written against them. **Nothing in 235 onward may be contradicted by a Volume 02 close repair, and nothing in it re-derives an earlier item.**

235. **The block's frame, closed, and the method that carried it.** **Chapters 91–100 are all main world, in the County of Merefen and in a fen, and they run from the second to the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, YR 313, which is day 208 of the volume and the volume's last day.** Weekdays, from item 147 and from nothing else: **the second a Tuesday · the fifth a Friday · the ninth a Tuesday, a market day · the twelfth a Friday · the sixteenth a Tuesday · the twenty-second a Monday · the twenty-fourth a Wednesday · the twenty-sixth a Friday · the twenty-seventh a Saturday, a market day · the twenty-ninth a Monday, a market day, the lowest water of the year.** The county's market days are Saturday and Thursday and the chapter does not reconcile them. **The path time is over: Aren came up on the second and no chapter in the block re-crosses, and there is no crossing in either direction anywhere in the ten.** The fever is **three days, the second, third and fourth, breaking on the fifth at about the sixth hour**, and the pattern is right for the third time. **The method that carried the Ash material into this block, and it is the only method the guardrails allow, is that it arrives as a fair copy down a line, itemised, and as four lines copied in a keeper's own hand, and there is no narrator in any of the ten chapters who has stood on a bridge in that county and no chapter in which anybody is standing there.** The consequence, which a later volume must not undo: **Chapters 95, 98, 99 and 100 all contain rooms in two other countries and none of them is staged in either of them.** A counting house, a county bench, a creditor's press and the coping of a bridge are all on the page in a fen county as printed things. The one line that carries the rule: **a minute that says a thing was read out is a fact about a room four hundred miles off and not a document in this one.**

236. **The return, the numbers, the concealment, and the third decision.** **The second of Greenmonth, at the first light, out of a bag with grey clay in the seam and a half tile in it.** He gives her every number before she asks, because he always does, and the numbers are the ones the case has: **the path interval nine months and six days, the main-world absence eighty-eight days, the error against the printed three weeks, sixty-seven days, and four pages of his own account in his own hand about the record and not about the road, in which she makes him go and get the book for two figures and corrects one sentence and he agrees and does not argue.** **Chapter 91 is the only chapter in the block that states a drift figure and it states it three times, in speech twice and once in a notebook, and it is the same figure all three times: eighty-eight days of absence minus twenty-one days of printed warning, which is sixty-seven.** The path interval is nowhere subtracted from the warning and the figure never appears again in the block. The prediction he made in Greenmonth in a cost sheet is entered as a prediction, worth about a third of what it is worth entered afterwards. **The concealment is two things and both are in a notebook in a coat and neither is in a form:** the loss of the order of an ordinary Tuesday morning with Tovan Kest — six or seven things, a room over a cooper, a yard on Weir Street, eleven years — which he can do and cannot put in order and does not know the order of; and the stance, weight a little back, hands loose, not in his pockets, which is the second of the five printed terms and which the form says he will not be able to say. **This is the third time he has decided what a keeper of records may not be told. The first was a letter and it cost this town a bench-clerk's afternoon and two nights' sleep and him a year. The second was a mark on a coping and it cost a man of sixty-four the answer to a question he was entitled to ask and him a lie for nine years, and the lie was the only protection the truth had.** The third has not cost anybody anything yet.

237. **The half tile, and why there are two of them, and where the first one is.** **The object of the fourth term is a half tile: a fired tile, six inches by four, broken across the middle, the half that is on the table has nothing on it at all — no device, no hand, no name — and it weighs a shade over ten ounces, and the weighing is the only figure anybody asked for.** It is the account of nobody, and the reason it was chosen is the county's founding conviction: a whole tile is a record of a person and a whole fired tile cannot be re-pressed, so a whole tile is a claim about a person, and a claim is the disease. **It went north on the fourth of the ninth month of the County of Ash's own year, in the bag of the hand of the Ninebridge, on a day the line was kept, and he has no way of finding out whether it is in a press in Merefen and is not going to ask, because a man who asks whether an object arrived has told the other end that the object is worth arriving.** The one in his bag on the second of Greenmonth is a **second**, made by himself in a shed at the back of the Kilnholt kiln with a cold saw, with a saw mark on the back of it that the woman who fired that kiln has never seen and never will, and the reason there are two is that **he could not think of a way to ask whether the first had arrived, and a man who makes two of a thing to avoid asking a question has already decided what the answer is.** She weighed it on the salt merchant's stone, which is in the room and is not a record, and put it in the press, and the two half tiles are the only objects in the case that a court can be served with and take nothing out of.

238. **The breach, and the reason it is a breach and not a scene, and the reason it is not repaired.** **The fifth of Greenmonth, the seventh hour, in a room over a weigh-house.** He says, in the ordinary way and in the middle of other things, that the clerk of the assizes in the County of Ash sent the schedule of the eight village re-readings on the eighteenth of the ninth month of that county's own year, and that the town was not on the schedule because the town has the room with the floor in it. The fair hand writes it down, with the word *county* beside the month. **Then the fair hand goes to the day-book and compares the entry against it, and in about eleven seconds finds that two dates in his account are dates of that county and not of this one, given in the wrong frame, twice, in one morning, without his noticing. A bridgewright would not have noticed. A keeper of records cannot not.** **The finding is not arrived at by asking him anything, and that is the whole difference between the two people in the room.** He tells her to write it down. She refuses, and the reason is in her own trade: **a date is the only instrument she has that she cannot stop using, and a date in a day-book in her own hand is a document about a man, and in about nine years a person may come into that room and read a page about the day she noticed a thing about him.** **And then she does the thing that makes it a breach: she rules a clean sheet, writes the twelve months of that county's year down one column, and tears it up in front of him.** The reasoning, in her own words and it is the office's own rule applied against itself: **two ends and a middle do not make a conversion, they make a number she made up; every line under it would be a line she had invented; the invention would be indistinguishable from the work; and in about nine years a man in a room would take a figure off it and use it, and he would be using her, and he would not know that is what he was doing.** A county four hundred miles off counts the year from its first firing and **she is not going to write that county's year down on a page in this room, and if the day ever comes when somebody in this kingdom has to know what day it is in Kilnholt, they can go and stand on a bridge there and ask.** That is entered with the hour as a thing she did on purpose, and it is the first of her three unnecessary entries this year that anybody could have called timidity. **The reason she gave in Fallowmonth for not asking is not spoken aloud in this chapter and is not written again, and the new thing on the page is the change itself: the not asking was a protection and is now a wound, and a wound she is choosing not to open is worse than a question.** She noticed the change on the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth, YR 312, which was the seventh day of the promise, and wrote it down on the fifth of Wolfmonth, which is what Chapter 70 has, and the promise of the twenty-second of Fallowmonth is **two hundred and twenty-three days** old at the fifth of Greenmonth, YR 313, and the lateness is in the seven months since and not in the noticing. **She does not ask him. That is the breach. It is a working trust breaking over a thing she refused to do. The volume does not repair it, Volume 03 does not repair it, and Volume 04's chosen intimacy is bought with it.**

239. **The three blanks in this block, and which of the five each one is.** **Only one of the volume's five blanks is in this block, and it is the third, and it is found with a date and not with a question.** The **county cell** in the ninth line of an index of a register of outgoing writs, ruled, on a line with a number and a date on it, with nothing in it, no reason on it, and no space on the leaf for a reason — found in Chapter 92 at about the eleventh hour, in a press, by a keeper who was not looking for it, who could not say when she started looking at it, who lost an hour of the afternoon and entered only the hour, **and who did not ask him about it either, and the same not asking, and the chapter does not say why.** The fifth space headed *struck* with no sixth is in the County of Ash and is not in this block. The four ruled cells at the foot of a provincial shelf register are in Chapter 79 and are not in this block. The eighth ruled line in the served schedule is in Chapter 58 and Chapter 98 and is a different blank. **The six lines in a county's book with nothing against them and no reason on them are in Chapter 79 and Chapter 80 and are not in this block.** **No two of the five are in one room, in one chapter or in one sentence anywhere in Volume 02, and no character in either country says that any two of them are the same.** A later writer may not put them together.

240. **The second count, and the figure of one thousand six hundred and nine, and what a count is now.** **The ninth of Greenmonth, the county town, at the fourth hour, in a room over a court with the shutters up, and about eleven people in it, and the clerk of the county council — fifty-eight, twenty-two of them as her clerk — putting a chair at the table for a magistrate before anybody said anything.** The second count, the county as a whole, is **returnable on the ninth of Greenmonth** and is answered by the Bellweather Claims Archive with **a figure and no names: one thousand six hundred and nine, being the number of persons who have come through the door of that office in four years and a half, taken out of its own day-book, which records every person who comes through a door and the hour they come.** The return carries a claim number against the office and nothing against any person, and three paragraphs, of which the third is four words long: **entered against no person, no opinion.** **Lysa Fenn beats the instrument in nine minutes with the form's own construction and the construction is this: a count of persons is a return of a figure; a figure is not a person; no person is in default because no person is named; a thing against which nobody is in default is not refused and is not enforced; therefore the instrument is spent the moment it is answered, and there is no second date on it and nothing in this kingdom that can require another answer.** **The figure cannot expire; the instrument does, and that is the whole of the danger.** **And she is frightened of her own answer, and says so in a room, and refuses to have it minuted: every argument she has ever had with that office is built on the fact that this court is not a person, and a court cannot be sued, refused, injured or made a party, and in about four years somebody is going to make it a person, and the first county in this kingdom to build one will not be a madman, it will be a woman of forty-five who has spent eleven years being unable to make a document do anything and who is right about the mechanism.** **And the count is now the mechanism by which that office can be closed without anybody's name ever being written down anywhere, because a body with a figure of persons against it can be described entirely in figures and a description in figures can be acted on without a name.**

241. **The Archive is a body corporate, and the form, and the sentence nobody says.** **The same day, the county council, by resolution: that the Bellweather Claims Archive, being an office of this county that holds records relating to persons, be and is hereby made a body corporate, with a door, a number, and the capacity to sue and to be sued.** It is carried on a form with a printed space headed *Function of the office*, and **the space has been filled in the founding's own words, of which there are thirteen, and the space above the ruled line has been left empty, and the reason it has been left empty is that on a form of that series the space above the ruled line is for the name of a thing and the space below it is for what the thing is for, and the form has no way of being wrong about that and everybody who fills one in a hurry gets it the wrong way up.** The words in the filled space are the founder's, from a deed of four years and a half ago: **for the keeping of what this county cannot be shown it has lost.** **A body corporate can be sued. A keeper can only be dismissed.** Nobody in the room says that sentence. The chapter does not say it. **Lysa Fenn says it out loud once, at about the sixth hour, in a yard, to one person, and it is the last thing she says that day, and she has repeated it twice since in exactly the same words, and Marda Oakes does not answer it and goes to find the cart.** Marda Oakes, 45, of the county town, eleven years on a council, has wanted this since the eighth of Embermonth and is right about the mechanism and wrong about the office; the clerk of the council read the resolution and the two writs and the finding of the fourth of Greenmonth back and improved nobody's sentence, wrote *no dissent recorded* at the foot, asked whether anybody wished to dissent, and nobody said the word. **The resolution and the four-leaf holding in a counting house four hundred miles off are exact opposites made in the same fortnight and neither of them is wrong, and the moment somebody joins them one of the two has to be the wrong one, and two people in a room over a weigh-house each decided not to join them and neither of them ever found out about the other.**

242. **Sabin Dain asks to be recorded, and says the sentence, and the four instruments, and is refused the column.** **The twelfth of Greenmonth, the county town, at the fourth hour, on foot from the county post, in a plain good coat, with a leather bag.** She did not come to be answered; she came to be asked, and she says in the first nine seconds, before anybody has offered her a chair, that she is an aid to the register at the Office of Provincial Registration, **that she is not a member of anything and has never been a member of anything, and that if anybody ever tells you otherwise in a room you should ask them for the number of the list** — and the clerk of the county council enters it, against a person, with the day, the hour and the number of the form, **and reads it back and she corrects one word, *number* was not *figure*.** **Being recorded is the most frightening thing anybody can do to an officer of that office and it is the only thing that makes an officer answerable, and she has been nearly asked four times in three counties in eleven years and has spent nine months afterwards wishing in each case that somebody had gone on asking.** Her want, unchanged and not improved: **that no family in this country is ever again removed from a record by a name nobody entered** — not whose family, not what they did; the name on the paper by the man who takes it off, in his own hand, with a date against it, and a column on the form where he has to put it. **The sentence, at the fourth hour, standing up, to about nine people including two clerks: the office is not asking you to tell us anything; the office is asking you to confirm a figure; a figure has no name in it; in four years there will be a number in a file with no name attached to it, and that number will be about this county, and nobody will be able to say whose hand made it — not because the hand has been concealed, because a figure does not need one; and it is not coming because anybody in that office thought of it in a room with a clock on the wall, it is coming because it is cheaper than names, and cheaper than names it is, and there is not one instrument in this kingdom that can make it more expensive.** It is minuted whole with her name spelled with the whole of it. **She describes the four Crown instruments in this county back to the room and asks the one question she came for — does this county intend to use the figure — and gets *no*, with a reason for the minute: a figure is only a number until somebody needs it, and the only thing that makes a number into a weapon is a person who is frightened of what is behind it.** Lysa Fenn is in the room and says the cheapest instrument in the kingdom: **a witness is a figure in a book that answers a question for as long as the book is dry, and every return expires, every notice stops only people who read it, and every finding can be got out of a file in about nine years.** **In a gateway in the rain she refuses to discuss the *Keeper* column, and when he does not press her she says thank you, and the reason she gives is that it is a column on the front of a form and she is not going to discuss it with a man in a gateway. She is not a member of anything and uses no spent phrase. She does not touch the marginal instruction and she does not notice it and no character in this batch does.**

243. **The second severance closes in the wrong country, and what a press-mark can and cannot prove.** **The sixteenth of Greenmonth, in Merefen, from four leaves walked in on the back of a toll-taker in a basket on the Old Toll, who does not ask what they are.** The reading: **the ninth line of the index and the first line carry press four, die eleven and two impressions of one season — first impression on the first, second impression on the ninth — the ninth's county cell is ruled and empty, the ninth is the only one of the nine marked *answered* eleven days after the tender, and the other eight are of a valley in this kingdom and are all marked *returned* and none is marked answered.** The signature on the reading is a clerk of that house who is **the grandson of the only hand in four hundred leaves that was ever frightened**, and the whole of what he says is nine words: **I can tell you the press and I can tell you the die and I have had both leaves in my two hands at the same time under a lamp and I am not able to tell you the hand.** **The finding on the page, in Aren's mouth: a press-mark does not lie about a hand the way a name does.** A press-mark is a fact about an object, cut in the metal, the same shape every time the die is dressed, and two leaves of one book carrying the same press and the same die came out of the same room, and there is nothing in that to argue with. A name is a fact about a person, and a person can be got at, and the getting at him can be refused, and he can be alive or dead, and **Edric Hunn has been alive the entire eleven years, which is the one fact about him nobody in either country has been able to use.** So anybody holding both leaves can set the ninth against the first; **he cannot check the hand and neither can anybody in this county, and it is not because the book is locked — it is because the index has no name in it, and a press is not a clerk, and a clerk is not a man, and a man is the only one of those three that can be asked what he did.** Merrow's entry is in his own hand, on his own paper, at his own cost, on his own motion and not on a fee, **entered as produced by a creditor's office in a matter in which no court in this county has jurisdiction**, and the third leaf is the four-line argument minuted in his own words, **a creditor's press is the worst place in the world to keep a roll of persons and the only place in the world where nobody can quietly take it out of the book, and I have argued that for eleven years and I am now the man who is doing it to my own city, and I would like the minute put where a Crown's man will find it in about nine years, and I do not know how.** **The four conditions on a wrapper are broken, all four, on purpose, and are not written down as broken, and that is how a person breaks a condition and gets away with it: not by denying it, but by not putting it next to the thing.** **The name of Edric Hunn is not written anywhere in this batch and the second severance does not open inside this volume, and the join is not stated once. It opens in Chapter 894, on a form, when a clerk in a provincial office puts two documents side by side, and the reader has been holding both halves for thirty chapters and has not been told.**

244. **Halven's disclosure and the endorsement on one page, and nobody is charged.** **The twenty-second of Greenmonth, a Monday, the second hour.** The disclosure is four paragraphs in Halven's own hand, dated the fifth of Embermonth, YR 312, at the tenth hour, and the four paragraphs are: a registrar of the fourth press in a provincial office two hundred miles off, eleven years in it, sixty-one years old, sold a volume on the fifth of Embermonth that was entered among two hundred and nine others and was not on its shelf, whose removal is dated the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301 with no name, no office, no authority, no destination, no fee and no initial, and which he had known was gone for six years and said nothing for six years; that he sold it to an officer of that office for an endorsement **on the printed instruction that carries a form of a series**, discharging a writ of enquiry on the records of the reeve of this town, returnable the ninth of Rainmonth, dated the fourth of Embermonth, taken in the room he has worked in for eleven years, **on his own motion and not on a fee, which he asked to be the first sentence anybody reads of it**; that what is in the volume is a schedule for three bells of which two are entered removed with no office against either and the third is entered retained and is on a bridge four hundred yards up a road in this county; **and a schedule of dispositions whose county column runs past that valley to eleven other counties, against one of which the disposition word is a word this county does not use for anything else, and I am not going to say the word; I have said it in a room once, to two people, four days after I made this, and one of them wrote it in a book and one of them did not, and I have not been able to find out since which of them did.** The endorsement is **Memorandum of endorsement, the office of the marshal's emergency office, the fourth of Embermonth, YR 312, the writ of enquiry in the matter of the records of the reeve of Merefen is discharged, the officer named as receiving the volume has performed the condition, and the endorsement is not to be withdrawn, there being no procedure in that office for the withdrawal of an endorsement. Number: 4 of the season** — and **the two together are the first document in the case in which the Crown's shield and the Crown's payment for a book are in one paragraph, and that happened because two officers of the same office, in the same year, at two desks nine miles apart, each did the ordinary thing their own desk did and one of them did not know the other existed.** **Halven came two hundred miles on the county post at his own expense and stood by the press for an hour and a quarter and nobody asked him one question, and he counted twice, and asked for that to be entered, and it was entered against a person, and he was told that the reason there is a page cannot be entered.** **Nobody is charged.** The reason is a sentence Halven said out loud in that room in the ninth month to three people and did not have minuted, and Sarn Oyelaran wrote it on the back of a sheet in her own hand: **there is nothing to charge; he did a thing that this office wanted done; a man may be sorry and also have committed no offence, and that is the ordinary condition of everybody in that building, and if we begin to charge the ones who are sorry then in about two years we will have charged the ones who are not sorry as well, because the ones who are not sorry are easier to find and cheaper to try.** **The endorsement stands and is not withdrawn, because the office that gave it has no procedure for withdrawing an endorsement and will not build one inside a quarter, and the clerk who would have to build it has four desks.** The register still has a gap on the shelf at the foot dated the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301 with no name against it, and the unasked fair copy of it and of a shelf register is in a press in Merefen and has been produced nowhere.

245. **Alish Renn, 30, and the correction she is given, and her want in her own voice.** A second copyist in the Bellweather Claims Archive, two years in it, about nine hundred leaves copied out of the press, **on a warrant of her own that Mara Vey signed in the ninth month of this year, in her own hand, with the date on it, which is NOT entered in the press and is in a day-book against a person with the hour, and it is the third warrant of her own in four years and the first for a copyist rather than a keeper, and she entered at the time that she did not know why she had done it.** **The correction, in her own words, and she is right about all of it: a day-book is not a file, and a file is not a record, and a record is not a roll.** A file is a place where a fact about a person goes, and it can be produced, a court can order it, and the person it is about is supposed to be able to see it. A record is a place where a fact goes, and it can be produced, and the person it is about is not supposed to be able to see it and often is not told. A roll is a place where a fact about a person goes and nobody can take it out and everybody can add to it. **Which of the three is this office is the question she has been trying to ask for two months, and nobody can tell her by looking, and the only way anybody in this county is ever going to know is if somebody with a claim comes in and finds out.** Her want, in a room, because there is no other room: **to be a keeper with a post and her name in the day-book.** A permit that is not in the press is a thing a person has in a drawer, and a man with a pair of shears and forty minutes could take a leaf out of the day-book, and that is how half the things in this kingdom have gone missing. **Sarn Oyelaran, 26, has a key, and a warrant for a third post that has been in the day-book since the sixteenth of Greenmonth, YR 312 and has not been signed**, and Mara's reason for not signing it is entered in a day-book and not said in a room: **there is one room and two rooms' work, a third post makes three rooms' work in one room, and a woman of twenty-six with a key doing three rooms' work in one room is a woman who will leave, and this office has had four people in four years and a half and cannot have five** — and the other reason, which is the one that is actually the reason: **a person with a post is a person who can be asked whether she should have taken an endorsement, and I am not going to be the person who puts that question to a woman of twenty-six in her second year.** And the thing Renn is right about that Sarn gets out of it in nine seconds: **a name in a day-book against a person is the only thing in that office that is not a record, and it is the only job in the county that is about a person instead of about a document, and Mara did not give it to her and did not explain it for a year.**

246. **The bill of the twenty-fourth, itemised, in pence, and it foots, and the money beside it that never sits in the same sentence.** **The account of the Bellweather crossing trust in abeyance, for the absence of the road warden of this county, being one hundred and fourteen days, being the two crossings and nothing else — twenty-six plus eighty-eight — and the eleven markets are inside the hundred and fourteen days and the ninth of Embermonth is inside the eleven and is not a twelfth market.** *One.* Markets lost or held late and thin, by custom, eleven of them, **twenty-nine marks, nineteen shillings and sixpence, which is 1,626 pence.** *Two.* Tolls not taken on the shut wharf road, one hundred and fourteen days, **three marks, nineteen shillings, which is 372 pence.** *Three.* Three Tuesday barges held off at the Old Toll, **four marks, two shillings, which is 216 pence.** *Four.* Cartage, stone, lime, oak and nails, for the scour at the Sedge Reach, **two marks, nine shillings, which is 204 pence.** *Five.* **No figure against it at all — the scour at the third pier, nose, uninspected from the ninth of Embermonth** — and the clerk ruled the line and left the space and the space stayed empty. *Total: fifty marks, one shilling and sixpence.* **The addition is on the page before the total is spoken: 1,626 + 372 + 216 + 204 = 2,418 pence, and 2,418 pence is fifty marks and one shilling and sixpence, and the empty line is not in the total because there is nothing in it to total, and a clerk who has left a space empty in a column has made a hole in a document on purpose and knows it.** And beside it, in a different book and a different hand and never in the same sentence: **eleven marks and fourpence owed by the trust in abeyance to a man for eleven days' crew pay; two marks and three quarters owed on a cutting contract at eight pence a day, carried if the cutting fails; and twelve marks and tenpence for a copying post at three shillings a week, unclaimed, sixteen weeks and two days, because he was not in the county to initial the returns.** The third is the one with a cage in it: **a post is a cage, and a cage is a place a man is sent for and also a place a man is sent away from, and she wrote the first half of that out in Fallowmonth in four lines in a day-book and did not see the second half coming, and she is not going to pretend now that she did not see it coming.** The post is gone, the county's surveyor took a man on in Thawmonth, and the twelve marks and tenpence is in the county's hands and there is no post for it to be in. **The scour was read on the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth at the seventh hour by the guild's man with the levels, with a man of the yard standing next to him because the bench minuted it: two fingers at the nose, eleven at the tail, a washtub, and it has been a washtub since the sixth of Mudmonth, and the honest repair is eleven tons of stone and a fortnight with the road shut, and the reason there is anybody else in the room is that the man who read it twice before went out of the county at the fourth hour of a market morning and was gone eighty-eight days.** The third reading of the ring: two numbers a quarter of an inch apart and a third number that is not a third number, being the same two with a hole under them. **On the twenty-ninth the county's surveyor paid that man six pence out of a box, for a written report, and told nobody, and it is the smallest thing anybody in this county has paid for in two years and the only one anybody in either country has been glad of**, and the difference between a bench that asks and a man in a box that pays six pence is that one of them happened in a room with a door on a lane and the other happened because somebody was frightened.

247. **The bench asked a third time and said yes a third time, and the yard is a fortnight with Tarrow still in it.** **The fourth asking's pattern is fixed: the bench asked on the fourth of Frostmonth and was given two marks and a subscription book; it asked again on the ninth of Hearthmonth and was given the same out of the same chest in the same words; the subscription book has been open in a market since the fourth of Frostmonth and has four names in it and two of them are the same man twice; and the third asking is the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth and it took about six minutes.** **Reeve Aldis Fenn asked that the minute carry that Tovan Kest said in this hall in the fourth of Frostmonth that a bench asked three times and saying yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription, and that the bench voted for the thing in spite of it, because the man who said it was right and the bench knows it, and a bench that votes for a thing in spite of the fact that it is a subscription is a bench, and Tovan Kest is not a member of it and has not been since he was twenty-one and is not standing for anything.** The bench-clerk read it back, the bench asked a third time, said yes a third time, and entered the work in the roll and nothing else. **The yard is a fortnight on the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth, with Pei Tarrow still in it, the same figure as the fourth of Hearthmonth, written in the week-book on the Monday with the hour against it, and the fourth of Hearthmonth cost nobody a man.
