The four days from the fifth of Wolfmonth are still on the page in four entries in a hand Aren's would know and they are not tidied into one: eleven days on the fifth at the second hour, fifteen on the seventh at the fifth hour with the wall re-spoken for four days, and fourteen on the eighth at the first hour with Tarrow put off two days instead of three.** That page went into the press in the guild hall over the wharf in the ninth of Frostmonth with the rest of the correction, where anybody can come and read it, and eleven people had and two of them were angry with him and one of them said it was the only honest page in the book.

248. **The Tovan sentence, spent, in a yard, in his own words, once.** **The twenty-fourth of Greenmonth, about the ninth hour, a yard on Weir Street, four men in it and a boy of fifteen at a bench-board.** *You stood in a keeper's room on the second of this month and you did five things in the wrong order and I watched all five, and I have got it down to about nine words and I am going to say them once.* **I am not going to re-teach you your own brother.** The fairness he insists on and will not be moved off: **you can still do all of it; that is the part I have got to be fair about, and I would like to be fair about it because you would know in about four seconds if I was not; you can do every one of the six or seven things, I have watched you do them; and you cannot do them in the order, and I do not know what the order was, and if I told you what I think the order was you would do it my way and not yours, and in about a year you would be doing a thing in a yard that is not a morning.** And the reason for not doing it anyway: **I am not going to sit there at about the sixth hour on a Tuesday and say it out loud in a room over a cooper, six or seven times, for a year, because that is how a man re-teaches a person a thing, and I have watched one person be re-taught one thing in my life and it took about four months and it cost both of them, and one of them is standing in front of me with a fever's gap in him and a bag with grey clay in the seam of it.** And the damage, which is permanent in this volume: **I did not ask you to say it. That is the whole of it and I want it written down that I know that and I said it anyway, and I have been keeping that sentence since the fourth of Hearthmonth when I said a different one, and I have had a hundred and ten days with it, and I have decided that the one I said then was worse, because it was about a thing you would lose, and this one is about a thing I am not going to do, and a man can be held to a thing he is not going to do.** **The sentence is spent and may not be improved on, and nobody in that yard has the word brother in a scene, and the estrangement is not healed and is not going to be healed in this volume.** On the fourth of Hearthmonth, at a gate, with his hand on a rope and the yard going on round them, he said a different sentence so that on the day he said nothing about it his brother would not think it was his brother, **and the two hours either side of it in that gateway on the ninth of Embermonth belong to the reeve and not to him, and the eleven o'clock at night in a market four streets off was hers as well, and a later writer may not attach either to Tovan Kest** — and in Chapter 91 he told his brother in a room that he would not tell him, **so that on the day he says nothing, his brother will know he decided in a room and not in a yard, and that is a different thing and it is the only difference there is** — and then he went and shut the door behind him, for the second time in four years.

249. **The settlement: the list is superseded, not burned, and the magistrate's four sentences, and the third hand is still a letter.** **A fair copy of a minute of a bench in the County of Ash, read in a room over a weigh-house on the twenty-sixth of Greenmonth.** **The List of Persons to be Struck is not burned. It is superseded** — the magistrate's instrument withdrawn in a room, with the physician's four-year-old finding read out, and the three hands named, **and the superseding is entered as a fact and not as a defeat.** The magistrate's testimony is four sentences and it is the whole of it: **the list was the only instrument I had; I have had a physician's four pages in the next room for four years and have not acted on them; I am glad to be shown a second instrument and I am sorry that it took four years and a stranger; and I want the county to hear the word stranger from me and not from him, because he is a man off a road and I am the magistrate of this county and it is my county and it is not his.** And then, in the same four sentences, the half that will be argued about for nine years: **I am not sorry about the fever. The physician is right about the fever and he was right four years ago and I have had both of those in the same room and only one of them was true in the way I needed. A list that is not read cannot carry the softening and I was not going to lose this county in the spring to a thing I did not understand. I was wrong about the names and I have never once been wrong about the clay.** **The three hands, named: the rollkeeper's office at the assizes, which is one room in one building and does not move; the hand of the Ninebridge, which is a tile-cutter who keeps the road and has been asked about and has never been asked; and a press belonging to a creditor's office in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, entered as a transaction. A person is restored when two of the three agree and removed when two of the three agree otherwise, and the third is allowed to be wrong, and the bench has entered that it is unable to say whether the third is a hand and has resolved not to find out this year. Nobody in either country resolves the third hand and nobody is going to.** The rest of the fair copy, in a clerk's flat hand: **254 fired tiles broken by hand and the clay wedged back, over two years, at a cost of twelve marks one shilling and sevenpence, in the standing water, being the time between the second low water and the next first firing; thirteen men, five days, at five pence the day; nine cords of wood for the proof rolling at the kiln gate; the softening slowed and not stopped, six boards in the last nine days against about forty in the nine before, entered with the hour and no opinion; Ash's dead stay Ash's, with no claim upon them and none to come, in the shortest line in the county's own book; four hundred yards of the Ninebridge's parapet re-laid in tile in nine days by the tile-cutter who wanted to be a mark and not a mouth; and nobody has read a single name out of a sheet in that county since the fourth day of a month this county does not use, entered as a rule and not as a favour.** **And a woman of about sixty, on three hearths, whose fourth column says *wife*, has written that she does not know whether re-grinding what was fired is a good idea, and that she is going to do it anyway, and that she would like somebody to write down that she said so, in a book that is not hers, and she has asked for it to be sent north and has not been told whether it can be.** The sentence given about it in a fen county, in a room, to three women, is that **it is the first sentence anybody in that county has produced in nine months that is not about what the county has got, and that a county that counts fires has four hundred and nine people put back, a bench, a magistrate, a physician of sixty-three and a man with a ledger who has been right about arithmetic for nineteen years, and every one of them is right about a thing they are going to do — and that is the whole of what a person is, and it is in a fair copy, and it came down a line that does not open on a schedule.** **And what Sarn Oyelaran wrote on the back of a sheet and put in the press face down: a supersession in a county that is not on any map in this kingdom cannot be served on anybody at all, there is no writ, no seal, no office, no Crown's man and no county schedule that carries that county, and there is nobody to be summoned — which means it holds because about two thousand four hundred and fifty people believe in a bench, and a physician's four pages, and a rule with no office behind it, and a woman on three hearths who wrote down that she did not know, and it can be stopped by about eleven men in a room in about a morning, and no form in any office in this kingdom can stop it or the stopping of it.**

250. **The eighth ruled line for the third time, and the brine weights, and the reason that has changed.** **The eighth ruled line in the served schedule of the eighth of Fallowmonth, YR 312 — seven villages by name in the county's spelling, figures separately, `sum not stated` in the total line, eight ruled lines, the eighth ruled and empty, with a reason under it copied in the hand of a county boy of nineteen who asked whether it would still be on the sheet in a year — was looked at for the third time on the twenty-sixth of Greenmonth, in the eighth month after the first look and the seventh month after the second, and was not filled in.** The entry: **I have wanted to, and the wanting is not the same wanting as the last two times, and I am not going to explain the difference because I am the only person who can see it, and a keeper who explains a difference to herself in a day-book has made a document about a person, and there is no person in this and that is the point.** **The city is four thousand by the Crown's figure and has been for eleven years; the seven villages are seven hundred and twenty by name; the two are never added together anywhere in this case and were not added together in this chapter; and the eighth line is the city's and it is still empty, and the city is still not on the served schedule at the close of the volume.** **The brine weights are still cited and not filed, on the second of Fallowmonth, YR 312 at the second hour and on the seventh of Fallowmonth at the fifth hour, four lines apart in the same book, and the two lines are five days apart in that same week and about eight months old at the close of this batch, and are read together every time she opens the day-book.** **The reason is written out in full in this batch for the first time because the reason has changed, and the changed reason is one line: a ledger is a claim, and this office has learned this year, from a county four hundred miles off, that a claim about people is not a record of them.** The roll of the dead built out of Ferris Oat's weights is not built and is not going to be built this year, and the not building is entered, and the entry says that a ledger of the dead built out of a man's brine weights would be the best document this office could ever produce and would be a page on which two hundred names are struck out at once with a total against them, and that **a county four hundred miles off spent nineteen years building exactly that, and it worked so well that about four hundred people went through a room in nine days and one of them does not know which kiln he fires, and there was no instrument in either country that would do one thing about it.** Chapter 892's.

251. **The holding, and the purchase, and Ansel Tarrant, and the column of eleven counties.** **The twenty-seventh of Greenmonth, a Saturday, a market day, the fourth hour, in a counting house in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, and the four leaves read in a room over a weigh-house the same afternoon.** *Received: the roll of persons of a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths, in three hands, of which the third is this house. Consideration: one farthing of salt.* **The holding is made in the country where the register is, and the price is a farthing of salt because a farthing of salt is the smallest thing that office has ever bought anything with and it keeps a column for it, and the man who bought a roll of nine hundred and forty-one hearths has a name in a column and no want stated anywhere, and that is the whole of him.** **He is the salt merchant's brother, entered in the house's ordinary way as a party to a transaction and in no other way and without any opinion, and he is a man who deals in salt on a coast, has wanted a canal wharf for nine years, has been told twice in writing and once in a market that a wharf costs money, does not know what he has bought, and thinks it is a debt. He is not a villain and nothing in this case makes him one.** **And he is the brother of the man who owns the building the day-book is in, and Mara Vey has four pence a year, and the reason she is not going to ask him about it is four words long: I work next door — a person who owns a room cannot be the person who finds out what is in it, which is not a rule anybody has ever printed and is the rule that office runs on.** **The argument is minuted in the creditor's own words, four lines, not improved on, and he signs the last of them: no single office in that county can add a name to that roll or take one off it; there are three, and a person restored is restored when two of the three agree and a person removed is removed when two of the three agree otherwise, and the third is allowed to be wrong. I have been a creditor for twenty-two years and I have never in my life seen a claim that could not be split three ways, and this is the first one I have ever seen that can, and I have built it in four weeks out of my own book because nobody in that county could build it, and I would like the argument minuted and it is minuted, and I would like the minute put where a Crown's man will find it in about nine years and I do not know how; and I will say the rest of it because nobody else in that room is going to say it and I have signed this, and a creditor who refuses a thing of this kind is a creditor who thinks he is a good man.** **The clerk who entered the opinion about putting the minute in the ordinary place is a copyist, and a copyist is allowed to add an opinion to a copy and a steward is not, and he has entered that the steward is a fool, and that is in the document.** **Ansel Tarrant, twenty-one years in that house, is the only new name in this business that has ever been read out in this county, and nobody asked for it.** He is the clerk who signs the four-hundred-leaf register in the hand his grandmother signed it in, **and the entry in the three hundred and first year is the only one in four hundred leaves that was ever frightened, and this is the second.** He asked for the copy to carry the remark and said he does not know why he signed it frightened and that his grandmother did not know either, **and that a book four hundred leaves long that cannot be got at by anybody is the only place either of them has ever been frightened where it was safe to be, and he would like that written down and would like nothing else.** He is entered in the day-book in Merefen **against a person**, with the hour, with a claim number against nothing, because a county is not a party and a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists is not a party, and there is nowhere in this case to state a want. **There is no column for it in a creditor's book, none in a county's roll of persons, and none in a day-book over a weigh-house, and the four things this office has ever had about a person are a number, a figure, a mark on a tile four hundred miles off, and a line in a fair hand that cannot be put down — and the absence of a column is the point and the chapter says so in about four sentences and does not sentimentalise him.** **The fourth leaf is a copy of a copy of a copy of the county column of a schedule of dispositions, running to eleven other counties, and eight of the eleven have a boundary oath on a stone — a long field, a boundary, a season, a stone renewed in public, in a year anybody there can name, by men who are still alive and can be asked what they swore and to what and in whose name — and three of the eleven have nothing in the leaf at all.** **It is a pattern and it is arithmetic and it is not a revelation, it can be seen by exactly two people in the world, a registrar who sold the book and a keeper who has a copy of a page of it, and neither of them can say what an oath has to do with a severance.** It is entered as a question and not as a finding, with the hour, against nobody, no opinion, and the eight are left where they are. **That is the next volume's question and it is the first of the three hooks §11 gives.**

252. **The state at the close of the hundredth chapter, and the tile, and the bell, and the road warden.** **The twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, YR 313, a Monday, a market day, the fourth hour, the lowest water of the year** — which the chapter states and does not explain, and **a later writer may not convert it against Chapter 53, where a character says a wall job would finish about a week before the low of the year; that line is a character being loose about a river and is not a date.** The nose of the third pier is out of the water for the first time since the sixth of Mudmonth. **A road warden of the Merefen bridge guild is on the Sedge Reach deck with a tool bag counting load points — four in the toe of the north pier, four in the toe of the third, two under the cutwater, done twice to see whether he got the same four — and it is a habit and not a working and it goes in no book. His left palm is warm at the fourth hour and he does not need it, and nothing in the sound is his.** **He is not there as the hand of anything: on the roll, with a vote, with a vote on a question that is not about him, not holding the crossing, not voting, said in a hall in Frostmonth that he would come when he was wanted, came on the twenty-fourth and on the deck every working morning since.** **The bell goes at the fourth hour and a half on the Old Toll bridge with its band of forty-one rivets and its dull red seam in it, rung by a boy of sixteen with two years of an apprenticeship who is still not good at it, holding one end of a line, and a carpenter who is a hand and not a keeper, because that is the arrangement, and the arrangement is not Aren's, and the yard's book has the hour and no name in it. At the far end of the line, on a coping on a river this county does not have, a tile-cutter of forty-one and a person nobody in Merefen has ever seen strike what they have, which is not a bell and is not nothing, and the fair copy says only that there is no bell in that county and there has never been one and nobody there has the word for what it is that they do in a market town in a fen on a Monday. He does not ring it.** The list that came down the line at the fifth hour is **four hundred and six words, itemised, every line a figure or a date or a trade, and it is the county's answer to the fourth of its own terms and the first thing that county has ever sent that anybody here can check.** It carries the supersession, the 254 and its board, the thirteen men, the standing water, the re-laid parapet, the rule, the woman's four lines, and **the county's own figure about itself — two thousand nine hundred and six tiles fired on the roll of persons of a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths against two thousand four hundred and fifty people, put in one line by the county for the first time in nineteen years, with the sentence that anybody who wants to do that arithmetic is welcome to come and do it on a bridge, and the two thousand four hundred and fifty is never in a sum with a Crown's four thousand or a served schedule's seven hundred and twenty anywhere in this batch.** **The 456 does not appear in Chapter 100 in any form and may not be scaled into any count of tiles being ground.** And in the middle of a column of prices, **with no rule above it and no rule below it**, in the hand of a man with a ledger: one man of this county has not been spoken to since the ninth week of a room at the assizes, he is at a kiln, the clerk of the roll does not know which kiln and neither does he, **the mark on his tile agrees with the entry and both are not either**, his name is in three books, and it is entered as a number and not as a name because the second of the county's own terms is forty-one words long and the county has kept it. She reads every line in fifty minutes and writes nothing, enters it against no person with the hour and no opinion, goes down it a second time line by line, and **the arithmetic is sound**, and finds nothing to argue with, and finds one thing she cannot put down, **and it is not asking her anything and there is no instrument in this kingdom that can make it ask her anything.** Then the page with the four lines on it from the twenty-second of Fallowmonth, looked at for a minute and a half with no lamp, and the entry: **the same entry, entered a second time, against nobody, with the hour, in a hand that has stopped asking — a number, and a county, and an hour, nothing else against it** — and then, on the same page, **that the not asking has been entered seven times this month and this is the eighth, and that a keeper of records enters the not writing because the not writing is the part that is a decision, and that this is the last time this office will enter that sentence in a year that has needed it, and that the day this office wants to stop entering it is not going to be the day anything has changed, it is going to be the day nobody has asked anything for a long time, and that she will not know the day it happened.** **The volume's last movement is a man of fifty-eight in a room above a kiln, four hundred miles off, who fired the tiles in that kiln last spring and does not know which kiln he fires: alive, employed, a fire in his hearth on the nights the county can see it from a road, not spoken to since the ninth week of a room at the assizes, his name in three books and all three right about him, and no instrument in two countries that will do one thing about it. The woman who reads for a living has written the whole of it down with the hour on it, against nobody, in a day-book over a weigh-house, in a room with a door on a lane, four pence a year, and there is no name on the line and there is no opinion on the line, and the pen is down.** **And the tile on the coping of the second span at the Ninebridge is not touched, not looked at and not explained in Chapter 100 or anywhere in this volume, and no character names the device on it.** It is named once in this batch, in Chapter 99, in a room four hundred miles from it, as a thing a keeper has known about for ten months because a man off a road told her he did not know what it was, and as a thing the county's own books do not mention, and the chapter's last line about it is that a thing you do arithmetic on is not a thing you have looked at.

253. **Cast, and what was spent, and the guardrails, in one place.** **Two new names first named in prose in Batch 0010 — Alish Renn, 30, a second copyist at the Bellweather Claims Archive; Ansel Tarrant, twenty-one years in the counting house of the tolls of a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists — and four slots unfilled, as the card allows.** **The volume total is now eighteen of eighteen, and the unfilled slots are twelve of twelve, and eighteen and twelve is thirty, which is the cap.** Bel is named and is a child and does not count. Bram Ottery is a returned face and is not a new name. Edric Hunn is not a Volume 02 name and **his name is not written at all in this batch.** **Four people in this batch are addressed by trade and are not named, and all four deliberately**: the kiln clerk, who is the man with the ledger and whose name is withheld on the page by design and may not be given in this volume; the woman who fires at the Kilnholt kiln; the woman of about sixty on three hearths whose fourth column says *wife*; and the man who buys a roll of nine hundred and forty-one hearths for a farthing of salt, who is named by trade as the salt merchant's brother. Everyone else in the batch is already on the page or addressed by trade: Lysa Fenn, Reeve Aldis Fenn, the clerk of the county council, the guild's man with the levels, the bench-clerk, a shipwright of nineteen years' membership, Halven, Sarn Oyelaran, Pei Tarrow, the man with the mallet, the toll-taker who carried the basket, and the boy of fifteen at the bench-board. **Nobody in the batch dies. Nobody is killed. No arch goes down and no glass breaks. The only person who is referred to in the past tense is the man who put the tile on the coping of the Ninebridge, who has been dead since the eighth of the last rolling, and he is a thing that happened before the book.** No Ashmaterial figure is added to a Bellweather or a Merefen figure anywhere; **2,906, 2,450, 941 and 254 are the only Ash quantities in the batch and they appear in Chapters 98, 99 and 100 as the county's own figures, and the subtraction of 456 is nowhere performed and nowhere scaled**; 941 appears in Chapters 99 and 100 as the county's own figure about itself.

254. **The Batch 0010 second review repair pass, in one place, and what a later writer must not undo.** **A review of the ten chapters found five blockers, ten major findings and a set of nits, and every one of them is applied. No plot beat was removed, no chapter renumbered, no card broken, no date the batch is built on moved, and no controller file touched.** The batch is **33,163 words** after this pass, against 32,842 before it, and the per-chapter column is **3,607 / 3,313 / 3,060 / 3,235 / 3,364 / 2,969 / 3,266 / 3,205 / 3,315 / 3,829**, which foots. **The five blockers, and each of them was a sentence that had been put into a state file as canon, which is why the state files had to be corrected in the same pass.** **(1) Chapter 96 said a real-world month twice** — *August of last year* for the disclosure Halven wrote, and *in August* for the sentence he said in the room. **Canon is the fifth of Embermonth, YR 312**, and the sentence in the room was said **on the eighteenth**, the day he came up. The disclosure's own date is on the page at Chapter 96's third paragraph. **(2) Chapter 92 said *it is the fifth of Wolfmonth* in a chapter that opens on the fifth of Greenmonth.** The canon that four files carry is right and the sentence was garbled: **she noticed the change on the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth, which was the seventh day of the promise, and she wrote the day down on the fifth of Wolfmonth, the morning he came up out of that county, and the promise is two hundred and twenty-three days old at the fifth of Greenmonth. A later writer may not "correct" the fifth of Wolfmonth into the fifth of Greenmonth: it is the day she wrote, not the day she is speaking.** **(3) Chapter 95's four leaves contradicted themselves four times** — the leaves were given on the page as a reading, an entry, a minute of the hall and a schedule of the nine county cells, and the map given in Mara's mouth was a purchase, an argument and a column of eleven counties, of which the eleven counties is Chapter 99's document and may not appear here. **The structure is now fixed and may not be re-ordered: leaf one is the reading, leaf two is the entry in the house's own book, leaf three is the minute of the hall with the clerk's opinion entered on it, and leaf four is a schedule, which is the only one of the four that is a number and is a copy of the index's nine lines and so breaks the first condition on the wrapper in the plainest way in the book.** **(4) Chapter 95 named Edric Hunn once, in Aren's mouth, and five state files and the Volume 02 close prompt said he is not named anywhere in Chapters 91–100.** **The name is now out of the chapter, and the guardrail is true on the page: his name is not written in Chapters 91–100 and the join is not stated once, and the Volume 02 close prompt's correction on this point is now correct and is binding on Volume 03.** **(5) Chapter 91 attached the five-day error to the wrong crossing** — it hung the sentence on *the twenty-sixth of Longlight*, which is the Volume 01 crossing whose error is **minus fifteen days**, and the five days belong to **Crossing A, the ninth of Embermonth to the fifth of Wolfmonth, twenty-six days of absence.** The chapter now says the ninth of Embermonth and gives both subtractions in the open, and the notebook's own departure date is corrected from the first of Hearthmonth to **the fourth of Hearthmonth at the fourth hour**, which is the date that makes eighty-eight days foot to the second of Greenmonth. **The ten major findings.** (a) **Chapter 97's gateway sentence was attributed to Tovan Kest on the ninth of Embermonth at eleven o'clock at night; the sentence is Chapter 77's, on the fourth of Hearthmonth, at a gate, with his hand on a rope, in daylight** — and the ninth of Embermonth at eleven o'clock at night in a gateway on Weir Street is **Reeve Aldis Fenn's**, per Chapter 77. **Tovan has carried the other sentence a hundred and ten days** — the fourth of Hearthmonth to the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth — and not eleven weeks, and the yard's fortnight has held for a hundred and ten days and not a hundred. (b) **Chapter 93's first count was dated the twelfth of Wolfmonth and expired six days before its returnable day; canon is the twelfth of Greenmonth, YR 312, on her desk by the fifteenth, returnable on or before the ninth of Frostmonth, and it expired on the twenty-first of Wolfmonth, eighteen days before that day**, which is Chapter 73's own arithmetic and now agrees with the two later sentences in Chapter 93 that date the construction to the twenty-first of Wolfmonth. (c) **Chapter 94: the magistrate arrives at the fifth hour and not the third, she walked the eleven miles out of Merefen and not four, and the four miles are the officer's own walk from the post; and Dain is at her desk on the sixteenth of that month, not the third, against a post of three days either way.** (d) **Chapter 100 no longer lifts Chapter 90's sentences.** The entry into the day-book is rewritten, and **the count of nine in four years is said once in the batch, in Chapter 100 in the form Chapter 90 used it, and the second entry carries the second time since Fallowmonth instead of a second count of the same kind.** (e) **The bell's own description — a band of forty-one rivets and a dull red seam in the metal — is back on the page in Chapter 100**, where the card put it and where four state files already said it was. (f) **Chapter 92's list is four days old in the room and not a week and a half**, the bench dates no longer carry a false clause about which two days the ninth of that month falls between, and the day she says she will not write the old reason again is distinguished from the new thing she says out loud two paragraphs later. (g) **Chapter 100 names the day the list comes down — the twenty-ninth — instead of saying *on a named day* and naming none, gives the list's own four hundred and six words against the last thing down that line at four hundred words and about four hundred and nine people, and dates the county's supersession to the twentieth of a month this county does not use**, so that the fair copy is six days old in Merefen on the twenty-sixth and nine on the twenty-ninth, against a post of three days either way. (h) **The two Ash costed columns are unchanged and both foot at forty-eight pence to the mark**: the Chapter 97 bill at 1,626 + 372 + 216 + 204 = 2,418 pence = fifty marks one shilling and sixpence, and Chapter 98's re-grounding at 13 × 5 × 5 = 325 and 9 × 30 = 270, total 595 pence = twelve marks one shilling and seven pence. (i) **Prose tics: the batch's *about n seconds/minutes* constructions are down from twenty-seven to ten, all ten of them load-bearing, and the sentence *the not writing is the part that is a decision* is now said verbatim three times** — Chapter 92's first entry, Chapter 95's last line, and the volume's own last entry in Chapter 100 — **and is varied in Chapter 92's second, Chapter 96, Chapter 98 and Chapter 99, and the phrase is not spent.** (j) **A stray *x* at the head of a line in Chapter 93 and two pairs of stacked section breaks in Chapters 92, 94 and 98 are gone.** **Recorded rather than changed:** the yard's four days in Chapter 97 are the four entries of the fifth, seventh and eighth of Wolfmonth plus one dated the day he came back, and **they are not tidied into one because they are not consecutive**; Chapter 70's "eight weeks" for the same scour is still Batch 0007's inherited error and is still for the close to decide; and the eleven miles a toll-taker carried a basket in Chapter 95 is left standing because no file settles the distance between the Old Toll and the weigh-house.

## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 100

- **Aren and Mara Vey: the breach, and it is not repaired, and it is not going to be.** **This is the series' Volume 2 relationship milestone and it has now been paid for in full and left open on purpose.** Eleven months of him reporting every number he has and of her not asking, and then **a morning in which she finds the cost with a date — two dates in his account given in the main-world frame, twice, without his noticing — and does not ask him, and enters the finding with the hour, and enters that she did not ask, and enters why she did not ask, and the why is the change and not the reason: a protection is a thing done to somebody, and this is a thing she is doing, and a wound she is choosing not to open is worse than a question.** She tore up a sheet with his county's year on it rather than convert two ends into a middle, and told him why, and that act is the closest thing to intimacy either of them has had in four years and it is also the moment the working trust breaks. He wants her to write it down and she refuses, because a date in her own hand is a document about a man, and the refusal is a professional judgement and not a coldness and he knows it. **What is on the page between them at the close: four documents, and the fourth is new** — a notebook in a coat with a morning in it; a day-book over a weigh-house with an entry made a second time against nobody; a letter in a guild hall over a wharf with two holders one of whom is not a member of any bench; the four lines copied in her fair hand six months ago; **and a sheet of paper with twelve months on it that she wrote out and tore up in front of him.** Neither of them has told the other a lie about a number. Both of them have now decided something about the other without saying it, and she has entered the deciding twice and he has entered the not-telling once, and **the volume ends with the two of them in the same county four hundred yards apart and neither of them going to ask.** **Volume 03 does not repair it. Volume 04's chosen intimacy is bought with it.**
- **Aren and Tovan Kest: the sentence is spent and the estrangement is not healed.** Eleven years and eleven months of it, and this batch spends the one thing both of them have been circling since the ninth of Frostmonth. **Chapter 91 opens it in a room, not a yard, and Tovan watches five seconds of his brother doing six or seven things of an ordinary Tuesday morning in the wrong order and says nothing at all about any of them, and goes out and shuts the door for the second time in four years.** **Chapter 97 spends it in a yard, in his own words, once, and may not be improved on: I am not going to re-teach you your own brother** — with the fairness that makes it unarguable, because he can still do all of it, and because telling him the order would only give him Toban's order, and in about a year a thing in a yard that is not a morning. **The damage is permanent in this volume and it is specific: the sentence he said to his brother at a gate with his hand on a rope on the fourth of Hearthmonth, so that on the day he said nothing his brother would not think it was his brother, has been carried a hundred and ten days and found worse than the one he has just said, because that one was about a thing his brother would lose and this one is about a thing he is not going to do — and a man can be held to a thing he is not going to do.** Nobody in that yard has the word brother in a scene. The yard is a fortnight and Tarrow is still in it and the fourth of Hearthmonth cost nobody a man, and **the one thing that has demonstrably not been ruined in this volume is the yard, and the yard is the only relationship in it that is currently getting better.** Pei Tarrow is in it, and the boy of fifteen copies the empty space under a number on a bill as well as the number, because that is what the boy does.
- **Aren and the hand of the Ninebridge: over four hundred miles and holding.** **Nothing of theirs happens in this batch and the batch does not need it to.** She is named three times in the ten chapters and is not named on the page in any of them: once in a room, as the woman who carried a half tile north in her bag and put it in at the north end, once in a fair copy, as the woman who made the second tile and has never been asked a question in sixteen years, and once in a fair copy, as the person who keeps the road at the far end of a line and strikes a coping at the fourth hour and a half on a Monday because that is the day the far end keeps. **She is still a hand and not a keeper, and the word *the hand* is the county's, and she was asked about the road and not asked as a person in the ninth month of the last block and nothing in this batch changes it.** The last thing he wrote about her is still the Chapter 85 notebook line about the lie and its price, and the tile on the coping is still not touched, not looked at and not explained, and it has been on that coping for forty-one years and its maker is dead.
- **Aren and Lysa Fenn: they have met again and it was about a figure, and neither of them enjoyed it and neither of them said so.** He is a road warden with a vote on a question that is not about him and she is a magistrate whose best argument for nine years has been that a court is not a person, **and in Chapter 93 she says out loud in a room that somebody is going to make it a person in about four years and that the first county to build one will be a woman of forty-five who is right about the mechanism and not be a madman, and he is not in that room and does not know it was said.** In Chapter 91 he asks her one question about the Sedge Reach and gets the answer that there will be a number and nobody who can check it, and in Chapter 100 the county has paid six pence for a check. **The two instruments she made this month and the two instruments he is closest to are exact opposites — a court that can be sued and a holding that cannot be served — and neither of the two of them ever learns what the other one did.**
- **Aren and Sabin Dain: one conversation, in a gateway, in the rain, and it is the volume's mirror.** **He asks her about the *Keeper* column and she refuses to discuss it, and when he does not press she says thank you, and that is the whole of their relationship in Volume 02.** She is right about the count, right about the archive, right that a filing room with a woman's name in a provincial file is a thing a person can be ruined with, **and she is a woman with a form and a good filing system and a horror of unnamed things, which is Mara Vey's position mirrored, and in Volume 04 she joins an order that has learned to remove names rather than complain about them, and a reader who was paying attention at Chapter 55 has seen this already.** She asked to be recorded and was recorded, and a witness is a figure in a book that answers a question for as long as the book is dry, and she is glad of that, and she has said it will not help her at all. **She does not touch the marginal instruction and does not notice it, and no character in this batch does, and Volume 03 is where he learns what it is being read as.**
- **Aren and Halven: an hour and a quarter, and no question.** **A man came two hundred miles on his own money to be in a room and nobody asked him one thing, and that is the closest thing to an acquittal available in this county, and both of them know it, and he asked for it to be entered and it was entered against a person, and the reason there is a page cannot be entered.** He wanted one document in the world in which it says he did it, and the document exists, and it is in a press in a fen county, and it is a copy, and the man in it is a registrar and not a keeper, and he will be called back in Volume 11 and will accept restitution and archive labour and be barred from office for ever, and the reason he can be is this afternoon.
- **Mara Vey and the eight entries of the not writing.** **She has entered the not writing eight times in the month of Greenmonth and the last of them is the last line of the volume's day-book, and the sentence has not changed in four years and a half and she is at the end of what it can do.** The standing entries this month: the two wrong dates and that she did not ask (92); the county's year written out and torn up (92); the empty county cell in an index leaf, an hour of an afternoon and only the hour entered (92); the second count and what it does (93); the disclosure of a man she has four opinions about and the not writing of all four (96); the third post she has not signed and the reason (96); the two documents about two counties put side by side and not joined (98); the eighth ruled line, third look, not filled, wanting entered and reason not given (98); the brine weights, two lines five days apart in one week of Fallowmonth, read together, the old reason written out and then the new one because the reason has changed (98); the four lines and the fair copy not joined (98); a name out of a copy entered against a person with a claim number against nothing (99); the column of eleven counties entered as a question (99); the list, line by line, and one thing she cannot put down (100); and the entry, a second time, against nobody, with the hour (100). **She has not asked him one question about the line nine of a list about a man at a kiln in four months, and there is no instrument in this kingdom that can make her ask, and she is at four pence a year, and there are two unserved books in the building and one of them is a press with a half tile in it that weighs a shade over ten ounces and is the account of nobody.**
- **Mara Vey and the four hundred and fifty miles of a person she cannot be shown.** **Ostry Vaile, 58, alive, employed, correct in three books, does not know which kiln he fires, not spoken to since the ninth week of a room at the assizes.** He is on the page in this batch four times and is a number every time: a line with no rule above it in a fair copy, a hearth and a trade and a fourth column, thirteen men out with the number written down, and a bell rung at the far end of a line by two pairs of hands that are not his. **She has read the line four times, entered the fact against no person with the hour, not built the roll, not joined it to anything, and written down four times that the not writing is the part that is a decision — and the volume ends on the eighth of those, in a hand that has stopped asking, and the last line of the volume is not a sentence about him.** What nobody in either country has: that a man who softens cannot be witnessed, and a call is a thing you note down, and a room with a table and a book in it is where a thing like that ends up, and that the ninth of Greenmonth is a Tuesday and a market day, and that the lowest water of the year is the twenty-ninth of it, and that the twenty-eighth is a Sunday.

## Canon established by the Volume 02 close

**How to use this section.** Items 1–254 are the record of Volume 01, of the Volume 02 plan, and of Chapters 51–100, including Batch 0010's second review repair pass, which is item 254. **Items 255 onward are the record of the Volume 02 close, which is `outline/volumes/volume-02-close.md`, and `outline/volume-03.md` is written against them. Nothing in 255 onward renumbers or re-derives an earlier item; where this close disagrees with an earlier item it says so in the item, and the earlier item's shape survives.**

255. **The Volume 02 close, and what it audited, and what it did not touch.** The close is `outline/volumes/volume-02-close.md`, the same species as the Volume 01 close, and it is read before `outline/volume-03.md`. It audits Volume 02 as **five blocks against the outline's cards rather than against its intentions**, from the forty-eight numbered deviations and eight date corrections recorded in the five batch entries in `state/batch-summaries.md` — six deviations plus eight date corrections for Batch 0006, and seven, eleven, twelve and twelve for Batches 0007, 0008, 0009 and 0010 — and **Batch 0006 has no prompt directory and never had one, so its entry is the only surviving record of the plan for Chapters 51–60.** **No chapter prose was changed, no chapter was renumbered, no plot beat was moved, `outline/ending.md` was not touched, and no controller file was written.**

256. **The volume's argument, as it landed, and the two places its own last ten chapters contradict it.** Volume 02's answer — arrived at by a bridgewright in a shed in four days, by a kiln-worker in nineteen seconds, and independently by a keeper of records in about a minute — is **a record a person can contradict is a record of that person, a record that cannot be contradicted is a claim about that person, and what cannot be taken from you is being asked.** **It is contradicted twice on purpose and both contradictions are the argument's own consequences.** A county council made the Bellweather Claims Archive **a body corporate with a door, a number and the capacity to sue and to be sued** on the ninth of Greenmonth, on a form whose printed space for a thing's *name* was filled and whose printed space for its *purpose* was left empty, and **nobody in the room said the sentence, the chapter did not say it, and a yard said it three times in the same words to one person at a time — a body corporate can be sued, a keeper can only be dismissed.** And the second count was answered with a figure, **one thousand six hundred and nine**, taken lawfully out of a day-book; **the figure cannot expire and the instrument is spent the moment it is answered**, and the office's answer to a figure is a bigger figure. **The volume therefore does not resolve its own argument and is not written to.**

257. **The Stage 3 decision, its cost, and the shape of holding Stage 2 in Volume 03.** `outline/series.md`'s power ladder gives **the Second Door to Volumes 2–3 and Threadkeeper to Volumes 4–6**, and `bible/power-system.md` names Threadkeeper's third ability as a limited forced-connection cut. **Stage 3 is not reached in Volume 03 and must not be.** **The cost of not reaching it is the volume's climax:** a cut is exactly what a man is told to use on a failing water route, somebody says so to him in a room in Block 3, he refuses, and the reason given is a date and a community that lost contact for ever after a cut on a forged witness — the Volume 06 plant, held — **and what he does instead is hold a failing span with his body and comes up with a permanent limp acquired before the return stitch closes.** **The Second Door's own qualification's second clause is met in Block 4 and is the volume's most expensive act: he holds three routes for the middle of the volume and the County of Ash is closed — by a four-line notice down the line, on a scheduled day, itemised, checkable, sent by the woman who keeps the road at that end at the request of a bench, agreed to by him in writing, and not lost.** **At the close of Volume 03 he holds two routes: Bellweather-Living and the Hearth Marches**, which is the series' resolution line for that volume.

258. **The Volume 03 time ledger, and Rainmonth's length, now fixed.** Volume 03 runs from **the thirtieth of Greenmonth, YR 313 — the day after Chapter 100, which was the twenty-ninth — to the twenty-fourth of Hearthmonth, YR 313**, being **two hundred and thirty-four days elapsed, two hundred and thirty-five inclusive, just under eight months**, against the ledger's eight. **Rainmonth's length is fixed by this close at thirty days**, from the thirty-day month the whole calendar has been built on and from the audited chain in item 147, because Volume 03 runs forward past it; **every month in the Volume 03 stretch is thirty days and that is binding on every later volume**, and **Mudmonth's length is still unfixed and no volume needs it yet.** The chain is built forward from Chapter 100's own last line, **the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, YR 313 a Monday**: Greenmonth 30 Tue · Longlight 1 Wed · Rainmonth 1 Fri · Harvestmonth 1 Sun · Fallowmonth 1 Tue · Embermonth 1 Thu · Wolfmonth 1 Sat · Frostmonth 1 Mon · Hearthmonth 1 Wed, and **the volume's last day, the twenty-fourth of Hearthmonth, is a Friday and a market day.** The two crossings: **out the twenty-second of Rainmonth and back the second of Harvestmonth, ten days' absence against a nine-day path interval, one day of fever, three markets lost; out the fifth of Fallowmonth at the fourth hour of a market day and back the second of Hearthmonth, one hundred and seventeen days' absence against thirteen months, three days of fever, thirty-three markets lost.** Errors against the printed three-week warning: **minus eleven days — the warning is longer than the absence, and it is the first crossing in this case to come back inside it — and ninety-six days**, ratios of about minus one and about four fifths, **so the ratio and not the figure is the thing the volume is about, and one hundred and twenty-seven days of absence in two hundred and thirty-four is a little over half** and thirty-six of seventy-seven market days are lost, **and the printed figure has not changed and the office still cannot say whose figure it is and no character in Volume 03 describes the warning as having failed.**

259. **The Hearth Marches, the boundary oath, the eight counties, and the finding that makes the arithmetic stop working.** A boundary in the Marches is made of water, so **the man who holds the ditch and the man who holds the oath have been the same person for a hundred and forty years** — the words are *the bound* and *the ditchman* and they have been one word for that long — and the oath has been renewed in public, on a stone, in a year anybody there can name, by men who are still alive, **and every renewal moves the ditch a hand's width, and the water table has risen under four villages and the salt has come up out of the ground it was always in, and nobody has poisoned anything.** The volume's question is the one `outline/volume-02.md` §11 hands over — *why are the Culling Order's records concentrated around agricultural oaths?* — **and the answer is small and true and is reached by two people in a room out of a column: an oath renewed in public by men who are still alive is the only durable consent a rural county can produce, and also the only document in the countryside that names a body, and a body can be served, and that is why the records concentrate where the oaths are. It is not because oaths cause anything. It is because an oath is a name.** And **the arithmetic stops working because the eight stones are not eight of the same instrument** — some fix a boundary between two fields and some fix a *count*, a boundary between two people's numbers of seed or two holdings' shares of a watercourse — **and a column of counties is not a cause, and the three counties with nothing in the leaf are the three where nobody survived to swear anything.**

260. **The private-keeper clause as a plant, its Volume 03 beat, and the two things it may not become.** The clause has been printed in an office's hand on the reverse of Form R.C. 4 since the first of Embermonth, YR 312, and **no character in Volume 02 mentions it, refers to it, explains it or notices it**, and Chapter 94 was written specifically so that it could not be noticed. **Volume 03 is where Aren learns what it is being read as, and he learns it out of a file and not out of a person: the clause is not a law, it is a practice, and the practice has already been used — an entry in a *Keeper* column on a village's own paper relied on by an office, and a man thereby made answerable for a road he keeps and cannot refuse.** **And it points at him: his own entry — *Aren Kest, road warden, in the roll of the Merefen bridge guild, and no other office* — is the sentence being read as a keeper credential in a provincial file, and the officer who wrote it refused to write the word *none* in a box headed *State the office, if any* on purpose, on the ground that a man who writes *none* has conceded that there ought to be one.** **It may be printed, quoted in a return, cited in a file and refused about in a gateway. It may not be put in anybody's mouth as an explanatory sentence, and Sabin Dain may not be the person who explains it and may not change her want.** And **Dain offers, in Volume 03, a road warden's rank and a sealed personal record in exchange for closing the eastern paths** — a **warden's** rank being a command rank and not a custody, and a sealed personal record being a record he cannot see, which is the one thing he has spent two volumes refusing to make. **The offer was drafted before he entered the county; the recruitment is revealed by her, in writing, because she is a woman with a filing system and a horror of unnamed things, and she says it is not going to work and has not said so to her own office.**

261. **The three rules, entered in a day-book in Volume 03, and what they are.** Three rules against the office and against no person, in a fair copy, with the hour: **(1)** a cost is entered before it is incurred, in the book of the person who pays it and not the book of the person who decides it, and an entry made before is not a promise and is not a confession; **(2)** a thing nobody dissents about is entered as though somebody had dissented, with the name and the hour, and a keeper who enters a dissent on behalf of a person is making a record and not a record of a person and is not to do it; **(3)** an authority is entered with the words that made it, and an authority nobody can name the source of is entered as a habit, **and a habit is a thing a county does to itself when nobody is looking.** **Rule three's final clause is a sentence out of a fair copy of a county bench's minute four hundred miles off — *we did not lose our register, we lost our refusal, a register is what you have instead* — and neither of the two of them credits it to anybody, and the keeper of records has the four lines copied in her own hand and does not say it.** **The rules are a procedure built around a concealment and not an end to it, and the volume must say so on the page in a document and not in a scene.**

262. **The corpus of documents at the close of Chapter 100, in one place, and the promise that it is the volume's whole material.** Sixteen things, every one on a page, none of them a mystery, all of them on the table for Volume 03: the Old Toll crossing in trust in abeyance with the tolls suspended, a dressed block and forty yards of tarred hemp, **no glass and no third seat**; **the Sedge Reach mended in a fortnight, the scour a washtub from the sixth of Mudmonth, and the county's surveyor having paid the guild's man with the levels six pence out of a box for a written report and told nobody**; the bench of nine asked three times and saying yes three times, with a fourth coming, and the shortest minute in the roll; **the Bellweather Claims Archive as a body corporate with a door and a number since the ninth of Greenmonth, two unserved books, and a press with a half tile in it that weighs a shade over ten ounces and is the account of nobody**; **the trust's account for one hundred and fourteen days of absence, fifty marks one shilling and sixpence, with a fifth line carrying no figure at all, and the line now has six pence of report against it**; **the two dated Crown forms with a figure in each and no name in either, being eleven and one thousand six hundred and nine**; the instruction of the ninth of Longlight in force and superseded by nothing; **Halven's four paragraphs and the endorsement numbered 4 of the season that cannot be withdrawn, and the press register's gap with no name against it**; **the county column of a schedule of dispositions, eleven counties, eight stones, entered as a question**; **the index leaf with press four, die eleven, two impressions and a county cell ruled and empty**; **Alis Merrow's four-line argument and the four-leaf purchase at a farthing of salt, and the buyer who is the salt merchant's brother and does not know what he has bought**; **the fifth of the County of Ash's five terms, on a tile and in a book, forty-one words in the second term, and the road is not there**; the bell on the Old Toll abutment with its band of forty-one rivets and its dull red seam; the two eighth ruled lines, in different rooms, in different chapters, in front of different people; and **a tile on a coping of the second span at the Ninebridge, a hand's width, with a rope on it and no yoke, whose maker is dead.** **And a keeper of records and a road warden who are the only two people in the world who can each be held to a document about the other, four hundred yards apart, and neither of them going to ask.**

263. **THE PROMISE'S AGE IS WRONG BY THIRTY DAYS, AND THE SHAPE IS RIGHT.** This close audited a figure that the Batch 0010 repair pass put into five state files as canon, and found it wrong. **Chapter 92 says the promise of the twenty-second of Fallowmonth, YR 312 is "two hundred and twenty-three days" old at the fifth of Greenmonth, YR 313. It is one hundred and ninety-three days.** Counted from the anchor in item 147 and the thirty-day month and nothing else: eight days remain in Fallowmonth after the twenty-second, then six months of thirty — Embermonth, Wolfmonth, Frostmonth, Hearthmonth, Goatmonth, Thawmonth — and then five days of Greenmonth, which is **193**. **The weekday chain agrees with 193 and not with 223:** Fallowmonth 22 is a Monday and Greenmonth 5 is a Friday, which is one hundred and ninety-three days at four days over a week, and two hundred and twenty-three is not a whole number of weeks off that pair. **Decision: the prose figure is an error and the shape is canon — she noticed on the seventh day of the promise, wrote the day down on the fifth of Wolfmonth, and the lateness is in the months since the writing and not in the noticing — because the shape is the series' Volume 2 relationship milestone and the number is not. The repair is one number in one chapter and this close writes no prose; it is recorded as an open item for the first Volume 03 phase. No Volume 03 figure may be derived from the promise's age. Item 238's figure is superseded on this point only; the rest of item 238 stands.**

264. **Chapter 70's "eight weeks" for the Sedge Reach scour stands, and is a known defect.** The scour is a washtub from the sixth of Mudmonth and Chapter 70 is the fifth of Wolfmonth, so most of a year is the truth and the chapter's figure is wrong. **It stands**, for three reasons: one reader could catch it and only by doing arithmetic across a month boundary; **the corrected figure is on the page twice in the volume's own last ten chapters, in a book of its own, in the guild's man's own hand with the hour on it**; and a repair would mean editing a chapter no phase owns, for a fact the volume's last block already gets right. **Carried as a known defect. Do not re-derive it and do not treat it as a fresh error.**

265. **Petch's dates: the chapter's, not item 161's.** Item 161 and the Batch 0006 entry record that Rowan Petch went out on the eighth at the first hour and came back on the ninth's. **Chapter 72 has him out on the eighth at the first hour, three days on the road, and back and filing on the twelfth, home by the fifteenth.** The chapter's dates are internally consistent, are the ones on the page, and **are canon**. Item 161's reading is not reachable again by a town four days off by post and is not contradicted in substance.

266. **The five blanks of Volume 02, and the rule that holds them apart.** **The fifth space headed *struck* with no sixth** (Ch 66, Ash — ruled ninety-four years ago with a knife and never noticed); **the county cell in the ninth line of an index of outgoing writs** (Ch 58, Ch 92 — with no space on the leaf for a reason and there never was); **four ruled cells at the foot of a provincial shelf register** (Ch 79); **six lines in a county's book with nothing against them and no reason on them** (Ch 79, Ch 80, Ash); **the eighth ruled line in the served schedule of the eighth of Fallowmonth** (Ch 58, Ch 98 — still empty, looked at three times, the reason for leaving it empty copied in the hand of a county boy of nineteen who asked whether it would still be on the sheet in a year, and it is). **No two of the five are in one room, in one chapter or in one sentence anywhere in Volume 02, no character in either country says any two of them are the same, and no later volume may put them together.** A sixth blank — the space on a form for a thing's *name*, left empty on the county council's resolution — is a different kind of thing: it was left empty by accident, by a clerk in a hurry, in about a minute.

267. **The method defect the close found, and the standing rule it produces.** In the last ten chapters of Volume 02 **two defects that had been dealt with earlier in the volume came back, and both were introduced by state files rather than by prose:** a real-world month name spoken twice in Chapter 96, and **the forbidden sum of the Crown's four thousand and the served schedule's seven hundred and twenty performed in Chapter 98, in the middle of the batch's own guardrail paragraph.** Both were found by review passes and both are corrected on the page, and the second pass then found **five sentences that the first pass had put into a state file as canon and that the prose did not say.** **The meta-language defect did not come back:** there is no narrator-voice reference to the book outside a physical object in Chapters 91–100, the three in the block are diegetic, *Culling Order* is twice in the whole manuscript and *custodian* not at all in Volume 02. **The rule that follows: a later phase states a figure, checks it, and records the check. A defect that is recorded is a fact about the book; a defect that is inherited silently becomes a canon with a footnote. And a state file is the least reliable document in this repository — take the fact from it and then go and look at the page.**

268. **What Volume 02 did badly, in short, because the last two volumes will be written against it.** **It became a book about forms.** The Crown's method is documentary and that is correct and it is why the volume works, but from about Chapter 95 the best pages are pages in which documents are read and checked, and the human event in most of them is a woman deciding not to write something. **It repeated one rhythm about forty times** — a chapter ends on somebody declining to do a thing for a craft reason nobody expected — **and a reader trained by Volume 01 to watch for the refusal is, by Chapter 80, watching for the refusal instead of reading the page.** **Nine months of Ash in ten chapters used about nine features of a county that has fifteen.** And **it opened two threads and left them to their own schedules**, which is correct discipline and a long wait. **Inherit on purpose: the refusal, the not-here, the road that does not open against the road that fails, the refusal to total, to file the brine weights and to write the one sentence the whole case wants, four kinds of holding, the ordinary labour, and the rule that a reason is a thing believed and a minute is a thing set. Correct on purpose: make the argument next through a record that is given away lawfully, on a form, with a receipt, and read back to the man it is about.**

269. **The next phase, and the only prompt that exists for it.** The next phase is **the Volume 03 Batch 0011 prompt, Chapters 101–110, and that phase creates its own prompt; this close creates none.** No Chapters 101–110 prompt and no second volume-close prompt has been created anywhere in `workspace/`, and the five Volume 02 batch prompt directories keep their prompts and gain their `.done` marker from the controller and not from the writer. `state/phase-ledger.json` is controller-owned, is stale by design, and is not what dispatch reads, and nothing in this close touched it.

## Canon established by Batch 0011 (Volume 03, Chapters 101–110, prose on the page)

**How to use this section.** Items 1–254 are Volume 01, the Volume 02 plan, and Chapters 51–100. Items 255–269 are the Volume 02 close. **Items 270 onward are Batch 0011, the first block of Volume 03, and they are the canon Chapters 111–150 are written against. Nothing in this section re-derives an earlier item; where it disagrees it says so in the item.**

270. **The block, in one place.** Ten chapters, **31,648 words**, all ten days main world, **no crossing in either direction, no path scene, no System display, no gate, no stage claimed, no revelation, no mechanism explained.** POVs Aren ×6, Mara ×2, Tovan ×2, Lysa Fenn ×1, with the road warden inside Chapters 104 and 106. **Two new names and four slots unfilled, as the cast plan allows: Thessa Wend and Oswin Rill. The volume total is two of seven and the unfilled slots are eighteen of twenty-three.** Coll Ferrow and Delya Munn are **not named in this batch** and are Batch 0012's; the guild's first hand is *the first hand of the Marches bridge guild* and the woman who renews the stone is *the woman who renews the stone* throughout. **Nobody dies, nobody is killed, no arch goes down, no glass breaks, the tile on the coping is not touched or looked at or named, the eighth ruled line is not looked at, the brine weights are not mentioned or filed, no Ash figure is on the page in any form, and *Culling Order*, *custodian* and *Gatehouse* do not appear and the marshal's name is not written.**

271. **THE COLUMN OF ELEVEN COUNTIES, AND THE HEADING THAT IS NOT ON THE COPY.** **Eleven counties, eight with a boundary oath renewed in public on a stone in a year anybody there can name, three with a dash. The arithmetic is the same on the fourth of Longlight as it was on the twenty-seventh of Greenmonth and Aren Kest gets eight, and this time he is in the room.** Two new facts on the page: **a list is a thing you can refuse and a column is a thing you get put in** (a list has a person in it and the person knows and can say the whole of the line; a column has a county in it and the county does not know, and a person can be asked and a court cannot be sued and ground with a stone on it can do nothing but be in the column), **and the column has no heading on the copy**, because the copy is a copy of a copy of a copy and the foot of a page does not carry the head of a column; the head is on a parent leaf in a creditor's press in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, and a creditor's press is a thing that can be asked and not a thing that can be made to answer. **The heading is the volume's open question and it is not answered in this block.** The day-book entry is **about the arithmetic and not about the not writing** and is the first such entry in months: *the not entering is the part of it that is a decision, and I have entered that this time it was a decision about a column and not about a person, and the difference is that a column is not anybody's business and a person is.* **The finding that a column of counties is a list of the places where an office can find a body is Block 4's and may not be stated before Chapter 141.**

272. **THE MARCHES DELEGATION: WHO CAME, WHEN, WHY, AND WHAT THEY SAID.** **Came up the hill on the fourth of Longlight at first light, five days earlier than the day the council's clerk had named, and the delegation said they had been on the road eleven days in a waggon with one river crossing, from a county four hundred miles off, and the post counted from the ninth twice.** **Composition: eleven of the council — nine householders and two officers, the woman who renews the stone and the man who keeps the minute — plus two of the Marches bridge guild, who came with the council and not under it, plus Oswin Rill, who is not on the council and came because the council would not go four hundred miles without him.** **They spoke first, before anybody had asked them a question, and refused outside command in seven sentences, and said the words *we are not a rescue* and *we are not asking you to believe that*.** They were in Merefen until the **eighteenth**; the guild's first hand stayed two weeks longer to agree a rate and left after the twenty-third. **They came because the fourth column of a return has a man in it who says he did not put himself there, and he was in the hall** — the delegation's one question, and their own words that they will take the wage question home and lose it. **The bench conceded nothing, voted on nothing, which is a first in the roll, and entered that the delegation had spoken before it was asked anything.**

273. **THE SIX TERMS, FIXED WORDING, AND THEY ARE THE SAME SIX IN CHAPTER 114.** Printed in full off a four-foot board of lath and lime in chalk in Chapter 104 and **a later writer may not re-word a line of them**: *First. The road is not to be made to carry a person. Second. No name is to be read out on the far side of the line by anybody from the north, and no name is to be written in the line's book at the north end. Third. The north is not to send anybody who can be asked to come and look. Fourth. The stone is not to be renewed in the north's presence. Fifth. The road is kept by a hand at each end and a witness at each end, and the witness at the Marches' end is chosen by the Marches. Sixth. The line is not to be opened on a day the Marches have not named.* **Term four is the price and term six is the door, and that is the batch's own gloss and it is entered by the keeper of records in a marginal note against term six: *a condition of this office, and the office does not know what it is a condition of.* The board cannot be copied out of, and a true copy of the six lines is now in the day-book over the weigh-house, in the only book in that office that cannot be served, and it is the first document out of a place that had agreed to be checked and had not been asked to.**

274. **THE GUILD'S FOUR TERMS, WHICH ARE BETTER THAN ANYTHING THE NORTH HAS BROUGHT, AND THE ONE THAT IS NOT EXPLAINED.** **The first hand of the Marches bridge guild is nineteen men, one of them his brother-in-law, and he states his terms before he is asked, and they are: (1) we are going to do our own work, and we cut a ditch in a day and a half that your craft would plan for a fortnight and a season, out of a book of water and a memory, and we have done it four times in nine years and twice it was wrong; (2) the water in a ditch is somebody's job and not a county's mood, we have asked a wage three times, twice of a landlord and once of our own bench, and **I do not want a stranger's money, I want the same nineteen men all year at a rate they can refuse**; (3) **a stranger's labour is taken at the guild's rate and is paid in the county's coin at the crossing and not in goods**, because they have four hundred miles of road between them and anybody who wants to argue about the price of a bushel, and they learned that in a waggon; (4) **we do not work with a stranger's oath in our water**, and there is a word in this county for a man who can make a ditch do what he wants instead of what the water wants, and he is not welcome, and he will not be told what the word is.** **Term four is a guild trade rule about ditches. It is not the cut, it does not name a cut, it is not explained by anybody, and it is not a plant for anything except the guild's own reasonableness. The cut is named, refused, dated and not explained in Chapters 124–126 and not before.**

275. **THE FORM IS WHAT BROUGHT THE DELEGATION, AND THE PRINTED EXAMPLE IS AREN'S OWN WORDS.** **The mechanism of the whole volume is now on the page in a shed and in a day-book: the office's worked example of how a county is to fill a *Keeper* column where no office of keeper is found is a real name in a real guild, printed at the foot of every form of the R.C. series since the first of Embermonth, YR 312, and a clerk in the Marches copied it into the *Person having knowledge* column of a return about a river, and the office relied on the *Keeper* column instead, which is a seed-carter who never filled it in.** The office did not have to do a single thing: *it printed a form with a real name and a real guild on it, at the foot, as the way of doing the thing, and a clerk four hundred miles off did the rest on a wet day with a quill.* **This is the volume's answer to its own question arriving early and in the wrong shape, and the answer is not finished here.** The return's five columns are fixed and are the same in every chapter they appear in: *Object. Date. Person having knowledge. Keeper. How often.* The *Object* is the Water, and the ditch system of the four lower villages, and the standing water in the low ground at Sallow Marsh. The *Date* is given in the Marches' own frame and is never converted. The *How often* line reads, in the county's own hand: *twice a day, and the same man has not read this line before he wrote it.* **The box headed *State the office, if any* is empty on the page and has always been empty, and a later writer may not fill it: Chapter 51 is canon and the reason is Dain's, and a filled box destroys the hole.**

276. **OSWIN RILL'S TWO HANDS, AND THE ONE THING NOBODY HAS EVER READ.** **The fair copy of the whole leaf came up the hill on the fourth of Longlight and is held in the press of the Bellweather Claims Archive, entered entire, un-copied, because the whole value of the document is one man's hand on the back of it and no other hand anywhere.** The front: an office's marginal note in nine words — *Fourth column relied upon as the office of record for the object. Return forwarded. No further returnable date.* The back, in his own hand, dated two years before the return: *I do not keep this. I have not kept this. I did not put my name in the fourth column. I told the man it was wrong and he said it had to be full or the office would fill it. Put it back and tell them I said so. — O. Rill.* **A record a person can contradict is a record of that person; a record that cannot be contradicted is a claim; the fourth column is a claim and the back of the leaf is a record, and this is the whole of two volumes' argument on one leaf, and the office that holds the leaf has a column headed Keeper and has never turned the leaf over.** The leaf has **two** ruled cells at the foot with nothing in them, deliberately not four: item 266's blank of Chapter 79 is the only four-cell foot in the manuscript and may not be duplicated. **The reason the leaf exists at all is a grandmother: a copyist of thirty-one years who was taught that a whole leaf means the cells at the foot as well, and a copyist who leaves the blanks off is a copyist who has made an argument.**

277. **THE FOUR DATES OF THE LEAF AND THE LETTER, FIXED.** **Ivet Salter's letter is dated the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, YR 313, and arrives on the first of Longlight — three days in the post, which is the figure she has said out loud in a filing room for a year. Mara Vey's request goes back on the second. The fair copy of the leaf was sent on the first, unasked, and arrived on the fourth — nine days before Chapter 105 and twenty-three days before Chapter 109. The two crossed in the bag and neither of the two women will ever know which of them was in a hurry.** The flat line — *a register answers what was entered on the shelf and nothing else* — is **repeated once in this volume, in a letter of the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, and is not improved on, and this is the only time after Chapter 79 that it is spoken.** **It is not to be spoken a third time in this volume.**

278. **THE OFFER: WHAT IT IS, WHAT IT IS WORTH, AND THAT HE HAS TOLD NOBODY.** **Dated the ninth of Longlight, four days in the post against an expectation of three. A road warden's rank in the eastern counties — a command and not a custody, the grade given to a man who is answerable to a person and not to a community, which may be held by a man nobody appointed, because the office has found that a man appointed by a community may refuse — eleven marks a year, a district, a clerk, the right to send men; and a sealed record of himself, kept by the office, produced to any body that asks the office a question about him and never produced to him, *because a record a person cannot see is a record a person cannot contradict.* For both of the above, **the eastern paths**, a category in the office that is older than he is and is not a count. Made out three years ago, held open since, **the number of persons to whom it has been made out is not stated on the page.** **He does not know what the eastern paths are and cannot refuse them, and the answer he has sealed is that.** **He has told nobody about it, and the list of the six he has told nothing to is in his notebook and is the volume's cost.** Dain's third sheet is her own, dated separately so that the two do not have one date between them, and says: the offer was drafted three years ago and she found the drawer in the ninth month of last year and recognised the man in it from a description in a file; she is sending it to him and not to his bench; **she does not believe it is going to work and she has not said so to her own office and is not going to**; and her reason, which is an office that cannot get a name will get a figure and an office that cannot get a figure will get a rank, and a man who takes the rank is a man who can be asked where a road is, and when he cannot say the office will have a man bound by a record he has never read, and she has watched it work on two men and one of them was a registrar. **The refusal is not in this block. It is in Chapters 148–150, in a document, and it is not to be made in a scene anywhere in this block or the next.**
