Nothing has been asked of him and he has asked for nothing, and the room is worse for both.**
- **Aren and Coll Ferrow:** trust without employment, and Ferrow now tells him when he is wrong. Ferrow lets the mechanism belong to Thessa, measures nine days honestly, calls Gace dangerous in the right sense, gives the standing argument twice and is heard once, and on the stair says *you were right and you have spent it*, which is the first time in this volume anybody has told Aren that a true thing of his has a price. The guild remains better than the north at water, and Ferrow says the correct amount of sleep a man gets after marking a wall he had no business marking.
- **Aren and the minute-man:** the closest thing in the county to a working relationship, and it is made of the book. Twenty-two years of facts and never a decision; Aren asks him to hold a question and the man says he cannot keep one and never had to; he states the entering-a-silence problem himself, before anybody asked him to; he enters the first silence of his career on Aren's word alone and it is true; he refuses the four gateway answers because he did not hear them from the people who gave them, and says a man who writes what he has not heard has stopped being a keeper and started being a clerk; and he ends the block with a bucket under a window he has never entered.
- **Aren and Delya Munn:** right about history, wrong about soil, never called a villain. She names the working openly and takes his unexplained refusal openly; she answers a child's question in a gateway with the truth and it is the county's most load-bearing unwritten sentence; the second half about soil is still unsaid by anyone.
- **Aren and Thessa Wend:** the centre of the batch. She says the mechanism, gives nine lines and the bank leaf, tells the drowning once, and is not thanked; he asks the volume question badly and carries her answer; neither asks the other for book or bank.
- **Aren and Ordmar Gace / Aled Rhys:** new and clean. Gace gives the volume its turn; Rhys gives it the word refused without heat and freight truth in nine sentences; neither votes and neither is owed anything. Gace has now been thanked twice for refusing him flat and has gone away owing nothing, and Aren's note is that such a man comes back with something that is not a term and not a form.
- **Aren and the woman of sixty on three hearths / the woman of sixty-six in the vault:** heard and unasked. One hears through boards that a list to be helped differs from a list to be struck, and her earlier warning is referred to and not re-told. **The other is now something more: she gave him a question nobody had a right to ask a stranger, he asked leave and she gave it on conditions and kept the measure in her head, and there are three marks on the outside of her wall that she has not decided about and will decide in about a year, and the two of them are the only keepers of a record neither of them can read out of a book.**
- **Aren and the man of sixty-eight on the roll / the boy of eleven from a white village:** one afternoon, two households, and both of them have cost him something. The old man gave him the truth once for nothing and will not have the smudge corrected and his four true words are in nobody's book by Aren's own decision. The boy was sent to ask whether being asked would put his household on a list, was given a true answer about a column and a pen, and went down the lane frightened, and the answer he carried back is not the answer that was sent for.
- **Aren and the yard's four men:** working, and now spoken for. The drawknife man holds a line with him and asks nothing about anchors, tells him plainly that a landing that will not take is refused and no reasons are read out to the tide, gives him the labour and the fire and the fen stories, and at the end of the block tells him he will take a sill as evidence and not as a promise, and puts him on the staging at seven. **Aren is on the staging at seven.**

## Canon established by Batch 0014 (Volume 03, Chapters 131–140, prose on the page)

323. **THE MECHANISM, STATED ONCE, BY THE GUILD, IN CHAPTER 131, AND IT IS A HEAD, A TROUGH, AND A POND.** A boundary here is a channel, the channel is a ditch, a ditch has a head, and **the head of that ditch has not been at the trough since before Ferrow's father was born, and the trough is dug to a level and does not walk.** The oath has been sworn at the trough for a hundred and forty years because a trough is the one place on that boundary where a man can stand and look at the water and know where the line is, **and the boundary has gone downstream a hand's width a year for a hundred and forty years because the top of a bank cut the same depth in the same line comes down a hand's width every winter and the man who renews fills it in on the side it came down on and then swears to the place it has got to. So the oath has been sworn eleven yards from the truth every year since the first one and nobody has ever once looked down.** A ditch whose head is not at the trough does not take the trough's water and has not for a hundred and forty years, so the water comes over the boundary at the trough and stands against the low side of the bank, and a pond against a bank on a mile of boundary is a water table a mile wide, and a water table a mile wide under four villages is the salt coming up out of ground that always had it in it. **A later writer may not restate this in other words and may not build a beat on it. It is said once, by the guild's first hand, in Chapter 131, and the four hundred and forty yards, the eleven yards, the hundred and forty years, the hand's width, the mile and the trough are its fixed figures.** It is consistent with Chapter 122 (Thessa's bank leaf and her account of the pond at eleven yards) and with Chapter 127 (the flood line on the chapel corner), and a later writer may not present the hand's width as a separate and competing explanation.

324. **THE GUILD'S WATERWORKS, PROVED ON THE PAGE AND NOT IN A STRANGER'S MOUTH, AND ITS TWO ERRORS.** No drawing, because **a drawing of water is a picture of something that has already gone and it is going to go again before the ink is dry**, and a claim can be looked at again and a person cannot, and in nine years the drawing wins because it is paper and he is a person. The record of a day's work on a boundary is the man who held it telling the next one and the stone, and Ferrow calls that a bad method and the only one in this county that has ever worked. Three pegs on the old bank's shoulder, because a bank that has been moved a hand's width a year for a hundred and forty years has the line in its shoulder and **you find that with a boot**. The line came down two fingers to the left; **it is the same error the same first hand made eleven years earlier on the same system and he is wrong the same way twice, and the reason is that a man brings a new trench down to meet an old one from the side he is standing on, and the left is where the spade goes in easy.** A leaf is not a survey: Thessa's leaf is a record and his line is a decision, and a decision made in a day on a wet bank is two fingers out and always in the direction of the man's own comfort. **The expectation of a finger and a half by the middle of the next sowing and a hand by the fourth year is entered in the guild's book in nineteen words with the hour against it, and Ferrow's sentence for it is that a thing you expect to be true in a year is worth more in a book than a thing you found out in a day.** The guild's four stops are the thing the north's plan has not got: a plan with no stop has no last day and cannot be footed.

325. **THE FLOOD, AND WHY IT CAME, IN CHAPTER 134, IN NINE SENTENCES, BY THE FIRST HAND.** The Water did not change course. **It came back into the only bed it has ever had**, and the evidence is a line of dead alders along the old bend **whose youngest is older than the bound**, because alders die when a bank goes over on them and a line of them is a bank that went over all at once in a year long before anybody remembers. The new bank is a hundred and forty feet of stuff thrown up out of a trench three weeks before, at the downstream end, and **a bank that has not been through a winter is not a bank, it is a heap, and the river does not have to beat a hundred and forty years, it has to beat three weeks and a wet month, and it would rather do it at four in the morning in the wrong part of the year.** The only thing that can be done in four hours is to put the deck where the old bed is, and hold it, and let the Water go over it instead of round it. **Ferrow knew in the sixth rolling and told Aren alone, gave him the date and not the reason, and did not tell the council, and the reason is that a guild which says out loud what a deck is for is a guild which has decided the deck is a flood deck, and a council that expects a flood is a council with a plan, and this county has never had a plan for anything except a boundary.** Nobody blames anybody. The line in the lane is *it had done exactly what it was cut to do.* No villain, no curse, no named person, and the salt is not named as one.

326. **THE CAUSEWAY, ITS COST, AND WHAT IT WAS FOR. 704 pence, footed in Chapter 133.** A hundred and sixteen feet of elm deck on four bays across the old bend four hundred yards above the market town, a handrail on the upstream side only, four days' notice before the ground stopped taking footings, nineteen men plus the four of a fen yard and a stranger, worked in all four days. **Nineteen men at eight pence is 152 pence the day; four days is 608; elm and oak off the guild's own stacks at what the stacks would fetch is 96; 608 + 96 = 704 pence, which is fourteen marks, two shillings and eightpence.** The two ends cost the fourth day, because a causeway is four hundred yards of road that happens to be over water in one place, and the ends were made eleven feet wide with an oak sill and the deck set eleven inches above the sill, **and not one line of that is in any book.** It stood eleven men for one hour and forty minutes, then went three feet sideways in the time it takes to say a word because the bar was through the upstream rail, and then went the length of itself. **It saved the near side of the vault and nothing else, and Block 5's span is a different thing and is re-cut twice in the wrong place, and neither of them is a failure of character: the schedule is the antagonist.**

327. **NINE MINUTES, AND THE THREE PLACES IT COMES FROM, AND THE ONE PERSON WHO WILL NOT HAVE IT IN A BOOK.** A boy of fifteen went in off the deck at about the sixth hour and a half of the second day of the flood, about forty yards, in a foot and a half of Water off a bend at a mile and a half. **The man at the drawknife went in off the near bank, head up, feet down, one arm, in about nine seconds, and had another eleven to get him to the bank, and he gave no name, was not asked for one by anybody including Aren, and is not named in any book in that county, and knows that this is the only thing he has there.** The count is nine and it comes from three independent places and none of them is a record: **the woman of sixty-six rang the chapel bell nine times and stopped, and has said in a yard to Ferrow that she will not have the number of times she rang a bell in a book; Ferrow's watch said eight minutes in the half of it he felt it and something else after; a boatman who was in the water off the bend from about half past five put the gap at nine because he was in twice and counted.** The boy said four minutes and did not know how he knew. The council's minute carries nine and no name, and gives the reason no name is entered. **Nobody in the volume has been killed and nobody dies, and the salt is the villain of Volume 03 and is not a person and is not named as one by anybody.** Thessa Wend writes one line in her own column that is not a reading of a post, and bookmarks it, and shows it to nobody.

328. **THE MARKS, FOUND TWICE, AND NOT READ.** In the sixth rolling, in a wet week, a man of forty-one with a lantern puts the lantern through the floor inside the third course, goes down on his hands and knees, and stands up on a floor three feet off the ground that is dry because the middle is on gravel and the far end is not. **In the seventh rolling, with a foot of water in the room, a different man does the same thing and the only person he ever tells is Aren, on a causeway deck, in about nine seconds.** The water does not read the marks and cannot. Ferrow asks one question in a yard, whether the mark was low, and goes on shingling, and the guild's book and the council's minute both have no line about it. Chapter 136 reads them in daylight with the keeper present, which is not reading them, and **the keeper keeps the measure in her head, decides nothing, and says she will decide in the spring** (item 320's condition holds and is not discharged). A later writer may not copy the marks into any record, may not measure them into anything, may not have the keeper change her mind in this volume, and may not let the water read them.

329. **SIAN PUGH, NAMED ONCE, IN CHAPTER 136, IN A YARD, BY THE MAN AT THE DRAWKNIFE.** He asks her for her name because he is going to have to say it in a room in nine months and has found that he cannot, and she says it once: **Sian Pugh.** It is spoken once by one person and is not spoken again by anybody in that county, and the following morning a woman at a sluice asks the minute-man which Pugh keeps the seed, and he says there is a Pugh who keeps the seed and does not use it much and does not want to be asked, and the woman says that was a foolish thing to want, and he agrees and enters neither of them. **Volume 03's name budget is now seven of seven with twenty-three of twenty-three slots unfilled, the cap is closed, and no new face may be added to this volume after Chapter 140 (§14).** The keeper who allowed the marks in Chapter 127 is this woman and is not a second keeper, and the question of whether the name is spent is closed and may not be re-opened.

330. **THE COMMAND, THE WITHDRAWAL BROKEN IN PUBLIC, AND THE NINE SECONDS.** Chapter 137, eighth rolling, the fourth hour of the second week: **he came up the outside stair with a bar and a timetable and asked the council to put its hand to nine days of the water.** Ferrow named the withdrawal out loud in front of eleven people before Aren had spoken — that he had entered in his own book that he would bring nothing else to that chamber unless a person on it asked him for it by name (item 321) — and Munn asked whether it was true, and he said it was, and he said in front of the room that nobody had asked him, and he gave no reason, and the minute-man entered the whole of it with the hour against no person, including the sentence that the entering of a question rather than a fact is a decision and the reason the minute-man was proud of it. **The withdrawal's condition was therefore not met and the command is a breaking of it and not a fulfilment of it, and the room saw it being broken.** The minute-man then asked the one question that decided the rolling — *who is this to be, in the book* — and the answer was under the council's name and no man's. Munn stood up, which she does not do, gave the reason, and made the keeper find a form in Ferrow's book that has been in the county since a landlord asked the council to put a ditch on his account nineteen years ago, and **the keeper read the form out in about nine seconds in the voice he uses for facts, and there was no argument, and eleven were present, and it is entered as a fact and not as a defeat: *The council declines, and the guild will do it.*** The reason is minuted in Munn's own words and is not summarised, at the slate woman's asking that it be kept in the same hand and the same book and the same run of the page and not on a separate leaf, because a separate leaf is a thing a person can take away.

331. **THE PLAN THAT WAS ACCEPTED INSTEAD, THE CONCESSION, AND THE COST, IN CHAPTER 138.** The guild's plan was written in the twelfth rolling, before Aren arrived, and is keyed to the water and not to the hour, with four stops and **no work at all in the last three days of the nine**, and the reason in the nineteenth line is that the bank above the pond is coming down. **It is better than Aren's in three places and he said so out loud in the room at his own request and named the three places**, and the load-bearing one is that a sheet keyed to the hour has no last day and cannot be footed, and a stop has to be a mark on a board and a mark on a board is a reading, and he had a reading and made it into an hour. **Its cost, footed in the room: nineteen at eight pence is 152 pence a day and the four men of the yard at the guild's rate are 32 between them, 152 + 32 = 184 a day; six worked days of 184 is 1,104 pence, which is twenty-three marks exactly, and Ferrow says it lands clean because the plan says stop.** The hand-over is four minutes in the county's own form and the minute reads: *the causeway and the waterworks above the market town are the guild's, at the guild's terms, and the council does not order the work; and a stranger is of no council and holds no office in this county and may not be given one by this council, and he asked to be put under the council's hand and was not, and the reason entered by the council is the reason entered above and is not shortened.* The fair copy went on the board under the bench where anybody coming up two flights may read it and the keeper read all of it aloud, **and the room made a space for the woman of about sixty on three hearths, who came into that room for the first time in two rollings.** **The cost of the eighth rolling lives in a document and not in a scene: a line in a good hand in a book that cannot be served, in a county with no office, no writ, no bell and no schedule.**

332. **THE FOUR MEN ON THE COUNCIL'S ACCOUNT, AND THE COLUMN WITH NOTHING IN IT.** The crew's ninety working days ran out in the fourth week of the seventh rolling and the four men of the fen yard stayed: the work was not finished, a yard's rule is that the yard does not leave a job, and a man who has come four hundred miles does not walk back over a river in spate. They are on the council's account at the guild's rate and cannot be paid and cannot be footed, **and the minute-man enters four men of a yard, a date, and the word *none* in a money column, and says that a man who is not being paid is not a debt but a favour, and that this county has never had a favour in it in nineteen years that anybody can point at.** It is the first line in nineteen years in either book with nothing in a money column, and it is in two books, and Ferrow says that in nine years somebody will take the two and put them side by side and say the county has a debt of four men it cannot name. **A man of fifty-two asks to be entered as not having sworn anything, and is put in the guild's book and not the council's, with a date and no hour, because a trade cannot be made to answer for them and a bench can.** He is the Block 5 gateway voice (§8 Ch 144) and that beat is unspent.

333. **THE FOUR-LINE NOTICE, ITEMISED, CHECKABLE, CONTAINING NO NAME, AND ITS FOURTH LINE.** Arrived in the third week of the ninth rolling with a carter's boy and a basket of onions. **Line one: entered at the fourth hour on a day this county keeps, in the county's own book, and sent because the second of the county's terms says no name is to be read out on the far side of the line by anybody from the north and no name is to be written in the line's book at the north end, and this contains no name. Line two: that the line is not kept and is not to be kept again, and the hand at that end is released from it and is not to be sought. Line three: that the book at that end is shut and is kept shut and is not to be asked for, and that nothing sent north is to be treated by anybody as a return. Line four: that the second of the county's terms is given back to the man it was written for, whole, in the county's own words, and that the county has kept it for nineteen years, and does not know what the man is called and does not intend to be told, and that the line at that end is the whole of what this county has to say to him.** It is a refusal of him and it is correct: he came to hold a road to a person, the terms say no name, the county has kept them for nineteen years and the north has not, **and the only name in the arrangement is in a file four hundred miles off in a column headed *Keeper*, written by a clerk off the printed example at the foot of a form, in an office that did not send anybody, saying *and no other office*.** The sender is the woman who keeps the road at that end and is a copyist and not the author; the author is a bench of nine in a long room in the clay county, and the room and the hour are on the copy so that anybody who doubts it can go and stand there and look. **The forty-one words of the second of the terms are referred to and not reprinted, and Aren reads the first three lines out in the county's own copy and does not say the fourth, in front of nine people, and gives his reason** (items 290 and 334).

334. **OUTLINE DISCREPANCY, RECORDED AND NOT EDITED. `outline/volume-03.md` §8 Block 4 Ch 139 says the notice was sent because *the fifth of five terms* says no name is to be written in the line's book at the north end. Canon is the *second* of the county's five terms, and the fifth is the hand-and-witness term.** The chapter follows the canon and the outline is not edited by this batch. **A later writer may not use the fifth term for the notice, may not re-word the second of the terms, and may not print the terms again in a form that puts a word count on the page.** The term is forty-one words long on the page of Chapters 77, 79, 90 and 100 and those chapters do not agree with a count of the printed line; that is an inherited property of the whole manuscript's second term and this batch refers to it rather than reproducing it, which is the one safe handling. **The notice also may not be described as a message and may not be treated by anybody as a return** (its third line), and `scheduled day` remains barred.

335. **THE CALL, THE ANSWER, AND THE ONE VOICE LINE IN THE BLOCK.** He called the second route once, at the fourth hour, with a hand at each end and a date and the hour and a witness at each end who is not the man who keeps that road, and the witness was the man at the drawknife. **The answer was the notice: four lines, in a fair hand, a bench that asked for it, and no hand, no witness and no date at the far end, and the notice says there is not to be a hand, and it is correct, and it is the answer, and it is not a refusal and it is not nothing.** Nobody said the word about a road not opening; a county that cannot refuse anything has no word for a road that is not there, a thing that is not there cannot be looked for, and the keeper did not enter it and was asked by nobody (item 292 holds). He writes in his own book that he was not surprised, in those words, because a man who is not surprised has already counted the cost. **Above that entry, in a smaller hand with no hour against it, in words he did not write and cannot account for: *A road needs a living hand.*** That is the block's only Gatehouse-voice line, it is unattributed, it is in his own notebook, and it is the same line the manuscript has carried since Chapter 77. At the close of the block he holds two routes, Bellweather-Living and the Hearth Marches; **the County of Ash is closed in writing, itemised, checkable, and not lost, and the qualification's second clause is met.**

336. **THE RETURN OF THE COLUMN, THE BENCH'S ANSWER, AND THE COUNTING HOUSE'S HEAD.** The fair copy of the column of eleven counties came back down the same line in the same basket, wrapped in oiled paper, **addressed to a shelf and not to a man**, with a wrapper's line from the copyist saying so and saying she would like to know that. **Its cost is on the wrapper and is footed: two men on the road at thirty-two pence a day for three days is 32 + 32 + 32 = 96 pence, and sheet, wax and the boy's copying at the office's rate is 6 pence, and 96 + 6 = 102 pence, and it is 102 pence of a bench's money.** The same figure as Chapter 111's cost of sending it out (item 301) and the two are not the same money. **The column still has no head, and the copy says so in four words, because the head is on a parent leaf in a press in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists and no instrument in this kingdom will get it.** Under the column is the answer of the bench of nine in the clay county, asked for by the keeper who sent the copy out and unprompted, and it is the first thing a path-side bench has ever sent four hundred miles to a county that is not on any map, without being asked to by anybody in that county.

337. **THE FINDING, IN TWO PEOPLE IN A ROOM, OUT OF A COLUMN, AND IT IS THIS BLOCK'S.** Arrived in the cold room under the tithe barn in about two hours, in three pieces, each of which one of the two men already had. **The eight are not eight of one instrument: six of the eight fix a share of a watercourse and two fix a share of a sowing, and the column says only *a long field, a boundary, a season, a stone renewed in public* in a registrar's six words, and a registrar's hand is a hand that writes a shape, and a shape is a category, and a category is the only reason a column has a count in it. The three with a dash are counties where there was nobody left to do the swearing, and a dash is not a zero, and this county, with about sixty households on a roll and no mark against them, would be one of the three.** **And the finding, which is not a cause and is not a prophecy: an oath renewed in public by men who are still alive is the only durable consent a rural county can produce, and it is the only document in the countryside that names a body, and a body can be served, and that is why the records gather where the oaths are. Not because oaths cause anything. Because an oath is a name.** A column of counties is not a cause and no Crown's man explains it. **The Marches is the sixth of the eight, and a claim about a share of a watercourse kept for a hundred and forty years by men who are still alive does not sit still: the share is fixed and the watercourse is not, and after a hundred and forty years the share is eleven yards off the water it is a share of.** He cannot get eight of eleven a fifth time in eight rollings and four rooms and two sides of a line, and the reason is in his own book with the hour. **This finding is spent here and nowhere earlier, and a later writer may not state it before this point in any form, may not improve on it, and may not attach it to any county other than the six of the eight.**

338. **THE ROLL, THE SECOND COLUMN, AND WHY THE KEEPER WILL NOT PUT THE FINDING IN IT.** The keeper refuses to enter the finding in the roll and gives the reason aloud, twice, and does not improve on it: **a roll of this county is the only thing in the county a person can be put in front of, and there is a rule older than him and older than the stone that nothing goes in the roll that is not a household of this county, and a second column is where a person goes to be got at.** The roll had a second column once, in the year before he came, and there were four names in it for four years, and the four names were men who had asked to have a second thing recorded, and they are not in the roll now and the two men before him took the column out, and one of them told him why on the stairs in his first week. **He has been there twenty-two years, has never once wanted to put anything in that column, and has wanted to put something in it four times.** The finding goes in the book with the hour against nobody, and it is the longest entry in that book and the only one in twenty-two years that has a finding in it and no person in it. **And in the same block the woman of about sixty on three hearths asks, once, for an entry that names nobody and is about a class of persons: that a woman on three hearths who is not on the oath is not a person this bench asks things of, and that the next one will be on three hearths for forty-one years and will be standing in the rain outside that window and will not know to come in.** It is entered and read back, and it is the first entry in that book with a thing in it that a person asked to have put there about a class of persons and not about herself.

339. **THE GATEWAY AFTER THE FLOOD, AND WHAT IT WAS ACTUALLY ABOUT.** Ferrow says in the ninth rolling that four of them told a boy in a gateway in the second rolling that there was no list, and that in five rollings half of four villages would sign whatever was read out to them because the council was right and not because they were deceived. **It has taken seven rollings instead of five and it has not been a list of households to be helped. It has been four lines with no name on it, and there is nothing at the top of it to sign.** He then says the gateway is still standing, because the answer in it has not been contradicted by anything, and that a county which answers a child in a gateway and is then proved right for seven rollings about a different thing has not become harder to find. **The four answers are uncontradicted, unimproved, in no book, and the county has not learned it was wrong about a list; this is the plant Block 5 stands on and a list of households to be helped arrives in Chapters 141–143, **and the difference between that and a list of persons to be struck is UNSPENT: it is not said anywhere in Chapters 131–140, `outline/volume-03.md` §14 assigns the one sentence to the woman of sixty-six who keeps the seed vault, and it is Batch 0015's to say for the first time and not to improve on. Corrected at the Batch 0014 review, which found the first pass of this item and of the Batch 0015 prompt both asserting it as already delivered when `list` occurs four times in the whole block and all four are Ferrow's (Ch 137:61, Ch 139:47, Ch 139:71 twice) about a list that does not exist.**

340. **FIGURES AND DEVICE COUNTS FOR THE BATCH.** **35,318 words**, per chapter 4316 / 3952 / 3156 / 2975 / 3959 / 2943 / 2978 / 2906 / 3724 / 4409, **re-counted from the page after the review repair pass, which added eight words to Chapter 134 and two to Chapter 136, and re-counted again at the Batch 0015 review repair pass, which removed two duplicated passages from Chapter 138 and took 242 words out of that chapter, so that the block is 242 words shorter than the 35,560 recorded in the first pass of this item and in the Batch 0014 summary.** Four `---` rules, in Chapters 134, 135, 136 and 139, and **zero** fully-bolded standalone lines, against ceilings of six and twenty. No System panel and one Gatehouse-voice line, in Chapter 139, unattributed, in a smaller hand, in his own notebook. **Money foots four times and the fair copy's cost foots once, five columns, all in pence, all shown on the page before a total is said: 480, 704, 592, 1,104 and 102.** No banned sum, no 4720 in any form, no Ash figure on the page, no 456 in any form, no Marches figure set beside an Ash or a Merefen one, no hearth figure anywhere, no Hushwater pairing, and **no main-world month name, no real-world month name and no main-world weekday anywhere in the ten chapters in any register including a notebook and a minute.** `scheduled day` is not used. The tile on the coping is not touched, not looked at, not named and its device is named by nobody. The fourth time anything was named in this volume in prose is Sian Pugh, once. **The string `cut` occurs four times in the block and none of them is the Stage 3 working** (a bank cut to a depth, marks cut in a wall, a ditch that did what it was cut to do, marks cut into stone); the working stands once in Chapter 124 and is not re-derived anywhere in Chapters 131–140. Filler counts for the next writer's baseline: `nodded` 0, `held` 14, `wrote` 22, `stood` 19, `plain` 3, `counted` 7, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0, **`the whole of` 3 (Ch 134:65, Ch 137:73, Ch 139:31; entered as 2 in three state files on the first pass and corrected at the Batch 0014 review)**, `about N seconds` 3. The word `cut` is counted here because a later phase will grep it.

341. **THE BATCH 0014 REVIEW REPAIR PASS, IN ONE PLACE, AND WHAT A LATER WRITER MUST NOT UNDO.** A review of Chapters 131–140 returned **ten findings: three HIGH, three MEDIUM, two LOW–MEDIUM and two LOW.** **Every prose finding is applied and no plot beat, no card beat and no state claim was reversed; the four state and prompt findings are applied so that Batch 0015 does not inherit them.** Applied to the page, three edits: **(1) `chapter-0140.md` line 127 said the county he was sitting in was *the sixth of the six*, in the same chapter whose line 67 says *the sixth of the eight* and in the same sentence that counts six water shares and two sowing shares; the eight is fixed by the finding itself, item 337 fixes *the sixth of the eight*, and line 127 is now *the sixth of the eight*.** **The source of the slip is identified and a later writer must know it, because the two phrases are both in the book and they mean different things: *the sixth of the six* is the sixth of the county's six terms, spoken in Chapter 118 — *And the sixth of the six is this: the line is not to be opened on a day the Marches have not named* — and it is a rule about a named day. *The sixth of the eight* is the volume's finding in Chapter 140, and the eight is eight stones, six of water and two of sowing. **The two must never be confused, and the phrase *the sixth of the six* now belongs to Chapter 118 and the terms alone.** **(2) `chapter-0136.md` line 37 contradicted itself inside one speech — *I have been in this county six rollings … I have had that on purpose for seven rollings* — in a chapter dated in the eighth rolling three times and whose own line 41 says *the only time in seven rollings*; the first figure is now seven, and the second clause now reads *since the first of them* so that the speech does not say the same number twice.** **(3) The Merefen coda at the foot of `chapter-0134.md` claimed that the only two documents to have come into that room out of the Marches were *a fair copy of a column of eleven counties and a four-line sheet with no name at the top of it*, and the four-line sheet is the County of Ash notice, which arrives in the market town in the ninth rolling (Ch 139:3) and is sent from the north, so it could not have been on a Merefen shelf in the seventh rolling.** **The clause is now *the only two documents this office holds about that county are a fair copy of a column of eleven counties and a whole leaf of returns with a printed figure of three weeks at the head of them and ninety-six days of error under it*,** which uses the two figures already established in the same paragraph, keeps the plural, keeps *both of them are on a shelf, and that I am the shelf*, and invents nothing. **Nothing else in Chapters 131–140 was touched.** Applied to state and prompt: **`the whole of` is 3 and not 2** (item 340, batch-summary deviation 8, `state/current.md`; the page has Ch 134:65, Ch 137:73 and Ch 139:31, and this is the figure a later grep gets); **the one-sentence list line is recorded as unspent** (item 339 and the Batch 0015 prompt, at both places it stood); **Pugh's three-part want is recorded as unspent and carried into the Batch 0015 prompt**, which had named her only as *the woman of sixty-six who keeps the seed vault* with no want attached; and the Batch 0015 prompt's heading no longer says a review had run before it had. **Judged and recorded rather than changed: the review's sixth finding, that *Toban's* in the Batch 0015 prompt line 69 is a typo for *Tovan*.** It is not a typo and the prompt now uses *Tovan's* only because lines 29 and 45 of the same prompt use *Tovan* and an internal inconsistency in one document is worth removing: **the outline itself writes *Toban's sentence* in §10, and *Toban's* is this manuscript's established colloquial for Tovan Kest and his yard, standing in Chapters 53, 60, 71, 76, 77, 102 and 117 of Volumes 02 and 03, so no prose is affected and the colloquial is untouched where it belongs, which is in the yard's mouth and not in a state file.**

342. **THE CHAPTER 136 ROLLING SHIFT, RECORDED AS A CARD DEVIATION.** The Batch 0014 prompt assigned Chapters 134–136 to the seventh rolling; **Chapter 136 is dated in the eighth, at its lines 9, 47 and 83**, and the day map absorbed it without listing it. The reason is on the page: the marks are read in daylight on the tenth day after the water went down (Ch 136:1), the flood cost is put up in the fourth week of the eighth rolling (Ch 136:47), and Chapter 135 ends with the Water still in the old bed. **The block therefore runs fifth, sixth, seventh, eighth and ninth across ten chapters, with Chapters 134 and 135 in the seventh and Chapter 136 in the eighth, and the eight and ninth rollings are each a single chapter, 138 and 139–140.** A later writer may not move Chapter 136 back into the seventh rolling without moving the flood cost with it, and **the count of rollings a character says they have been in the county is seven at the eighth rolling and eight at the ninth** (item 331, and the two figures in Chapters 136 and 140 as corrected at the review).

343. **PUGH'S WANT, AND THE PART OF IT THAT IS ON THE PAGE.** `outline/volume-03.md` §14 gives the Batch 0014 name three things: **that the seed be in nine jars and not in a wall, that she wants to be the last keeper of a seed room and not the first keeper of a seed house, and that she does not want the cooperative's thanks.** **None of the three is said anywhere in Chapters 131–140, and the first pass of this state file and of the Batch 0015 prompt recorded the name as spent and the want as delivered, which was wrong; corrected at the review.** What is on the page is the decision she has given herself and has not carried out: **nine jars went up onto a chapel step at the ninth hour in the order they had stood on the shelf (Ch 135:15), she is the last of them to leave the room, and at the end of it she says what she gave herself a year to decide has decided about the deciding and not about the thing — *Ask me in the spring* (Ch 135:103).** **The three-part want is therefore unspent and is Batch 0015's to say, once, in her own words, and the name cap being closed here means the beat has nowhere else to go.** The three marks are a different thing and are not part of it: they are on the outside of the wall, they are read by nobody, and the deciding about them may not be forced in either direction.

344. **THE BATCH 0014 LENGTH, JUDGED AT THE REVIEW AND NOT CHANGED.** The block is **35,318 words** against a prompt ceiling of 2,800–3,800 a chapter, **and was 35,560 when this item was written, the difference being 242 words of duplicated passages removed from Chapter 138 at the Batch 0015 review repair pass and not a word of compression.** **Chapters 131, 132, 135 and 140 exceed it** at 4,316 / 3,952 / 3,959 / 4,409. The first pass disclosed this in the batch summary and in `state/current.md` rather than concealing it, and **the review confirmed it as a real deviation and as transparency rather than concealment.** It is not compression: **no chapter is under 2,906 words, every chapter carries a completed scene, and the four that are long are the four that carry the mechanism, the flood, the rescue and the finding.** It is not padding and it is not trimmed, because trimming four long chapters to a ceiling would cost the four load-bearing scenes and buy a shorter file. **Recorded here so that Batch 0015 is not told to hit a number it cannot hit honestly, and so that no writer reads Batch 0014's length as the house figure.** The house figure for the next block is set by `outline/volume-03.md` §17's time budget and not by this block's word count.

## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 140

- **Aren and Mara:** unchanged and unrepaired, and not in these ten chapters except for one four-line coda in Chapter 134 in a room over a weigh-house, undated. The ninth not-asking stands and no tenth is entered. She reads the printed three weeks against ninety-six days of error, the bell is rung and nothing at the far end is struck, she enters the hour against nothing, and she writes under it that this office is now keeping a column about a road in a county that is not on any map, in a room where nobody works, and that the only two documents that have ever come into that room out of that county are a fair copy of a column of eleven counties and a four-line sheet with no name at the top of it, and that both of them are on a shelf, and that she is the shelf. **She does not know that the column has come back, that the bench in the clay county answered a question she asked, or that a keeper in a county four hundred miles off has entered a finding about eleven counties in a stitch-bound roll that cannot be served.** The fourth concealment and the reply in the bag for the provincial town both stand untouched, and Dain's offer is neither accepted nor refused and is not refused in writing until Chapters 148–150.
- **Aren and Tovan:** unchanged and unhealed. The yard's four men are in the county on its own calendar, on the council's account, unfootable, and are the best crew in it. The drawknife man went into the Water after a boy of fifteen, gave no name, was not asked for one, and has spent seven rollings in a county where nobody has ever asked him anything and has said in a guild room and in a lane that he swore nothing. No brother language is used and the spent sentence is not re-said, improved, or alluded to as retractable. **Each brother keeps a page the other does not know and the yard's book has a line in nineteen words with a date and no hour and no brother in it.** Batch 0015 must not answer the Chapter 145 question in a state file, a summary, or an assertion.
- **Aren and the hand of the Ninebridge:** unchanged and now one route lighter. The bell was rung and nothing at the far end of it was struck, entered in Merefen against nothing. The four-line notice is from the woman who keeps that end, who is a copyist and not the author, and the author is a bench of nine in the clay county and the room and the hour are on the copy. **The tile on the coping is not touched, not looked at, not named, and its device is named by nobody, and it is now beyond a road that has been closed in four lines of writing by the only people with the right to close it.**
- **Aren and the Marches council:** refused him in nine seconds, in the county's own form, in front of eleven people, and entered the refusal as a fact and not a defeat, and refused him correctly, and it refused a stranger's timetable while taking a guild's plan written before he arrived. **The cost of it is a line in a fair copy on a board under the bench: a stranger is of no council and holds no office in this county and may not be given one by this council, and he asked to be put under the council's hand and was not.** The room saw him break his own entered decision in public to take the command at all, and the breaking is in the book with the hour against no person. **He now has no instrument with that council, nothing to withdraw from, and a bar he is not entitled to.**
- **Aren and Coll Ferrow:** the closest working relationship in the county, and it is made of a bar and a book and nothing else. Ferrow let the mechanism belong to Thessa in the field and told the Water was coming on a morning in the seventh rolling and was right by about four hours, and gave him a date and not a reason and said he was going to lose the argument about the four words in the man's notebook. He made him say in public that he had put three claims in a room where he had promised none. He costed his own plan in the room and made the room's argument for him. He says on the stair that they are the sixth of the eight and that he has been the sixth for nineteen years and did not know, and that he would have asked two others and would not have asked the clay county, because a man who decides about a thing he has been keeping for nineteen years has become an office. **And he says that Gace's proposal is the least dangerous thing in this county, and that he has been calling it dangerous for four rollings, and that he will take that back in a room and not in a gateway, and has not told Gace yet.** The guild remains better than the north at water and the north's sheet is on the minute as a thing a man conceded at his own request with the three places named.
- **Aren and the minute-man:** the closest thing in the county to a working relationship, and it is made of the book. He asked the question that decided the eighth rolling and it was the right one. He entered the breaking of a man's own decision as a fact and a question as a decision, and said the entering was the point. He refused to enter a document with no name at the top of it and entered the refusal and the reason and called it the first contradiction in twenty-two years and the one he is proudest of, and then said out loud that it is the only thing this county has produced that an office four hundred miles off cannot get at, and that he has spent twenty-two years wishing this room were harder to find. He refused to put the finding in the roll and gave the reason twice and did not improve on it. He read a fair copy aloud in a room and made a space for a woman who came in out of the rain. He has now entered the second silence of his career, and it is the easier one, because somebody did something and he does not know who.
- **Aren and Thessa Wend:** the centre of the block and still unasked and still asking. He went to the weir on the ninth day and asked her whether the two inches were hers or the board's, and she told him the board is a painted foot lower than the ground it stands in and was a good decision made by a man who is dead, and that if the mark moves the mark is wrong and if the mark does not move the water does, and that nobody has ever asked her which of the two she would like to be. He showed her the notebook and shut it and did not open it, and she watched him not open it, and told him his decision was the right decision in the wrong county. She gave him nine readings and would not give him the book, and at the end of the flood her board was four inches under the winter mark and the pond had gone down two inches and the salt had not moved. **They have now not asked each other the same thing four times in eight rollings and neither has ever written any of the four down, and it is the only piece of work in the account that neither of them can produce.**
- **Aren and Delya Munn:** right about the history and wrong about the soil, and never called a villain, and now the only person in the county who has said in public that the four gateway answers are the county's own unwritten record. She refused his timetable on a form that has been in the county since a landlord asked for a ditch on his account nineteen years ago, and the refusal is correct because a bench that can be made to answer for a thing is a bench that has begun to govern, and she has kept nineteen years of not governing. She stood up, which she does not do, and gave the reason in her own words so that the minute could not shorten it, and she knows she will have to stand in a field in the fourth week and not say that nine men are saying true words for the first time in a hundred and forty years. **The second half about the soil is still unsaid by anybody in the volume.**
- **Aren and Sian Pugh:** she gave him a question nobody had a right to ask a stranger, he asked leave and she gave it on conditions, and he put three marks on the outside of her wall. **In the eighth rolling she read them in daylight with him, and the marks were exactly where they were, and the one that works is the low one, and neither of them can produce the record of it.** She told him his decision about the chamber was the right decision in the wrong county. He told her in a wet week that a man in a flooded room with a lantern is not looking for a floor to stand on, and in a flood a second man stood on that floor and told nobody, and told a man with a bar in nine seconds. He asked, once, for the number, and did not get it, and she said then do not ask. **He is the only person outside a room of eleven who knows what the three marks are for and she is the only person who has decided to keep them, and she has not decided anything about the rest, and the man at the drawknife said her name in a yard in the eighth rolling and nobody in that county has said it since.**
- **Aren and the man of sixty on three hearths / the man of sixty-eight on the roll / the boy of eleven from a white village / the boy who reads the gauge / the man of fifty-two of the fen yard:** five people the county has never asked anything and has now, in one way each, answered. The woman of sixty asked, once, for an entry about a class of persons and not about herself, and it is in the book, and it is the only thing she has ever asked this council for. The man of sixty-eight's four true words are still in nobody's book by Aren's own decision, and the silence is still entered. The boy of eleven is not in this block and the four answers are unchanged. The boy who reads the gauge is fifteen, has read two inches over the winter mark and then four inches under it, and has never been asked what it means and has told a stranger what the still thing is. The man of fifty-two has asked to be entered as not having sworn anything, and is in the guild's book with a date and no hour.
- **The yard's four men and the guild's nineteen:** the only relationship in the book getting better, and it got better again by costing the yard's four men a contract they are not being paid under and by a man going into a river nobody has thanked. The crew is whole and is on the council's account and cannot be footed, and the guild is out 480 + 704 + 592 pence on three jobs, one of which it did not get paid for, and its first hand has now told the ninth rolling's finding to a stranger in a doorway and said that the man who went into the water is the only person in that county nobody can name.

## Canon established by Batch 0015 (Volume 03, Chapters 141–150, prose on the page)

345. **THE OATH BROKEN IN PUBLIC, ON A DAY NAMED IN ADVANCE, WITH A RELEASE, A CONSEQUENCE AND NO OFFICE BEHIND IT, AND ENTERED AS A THING THAT CAN HAPPEN AGAIN.** Chapter 141: the council takes the proposal of the third rolling and adds to it, and **a day is named in advance for the breaking of the oath and the release, and the day is the fourth week of the twelfth rolling, and the day is not moved, and the reason the day is not moved is that a man knowing the day is the whole of the instrument.** Chapter 143: nine men who renew the stone swear the renewal in the field before the trough, **and for the first time in a hundred and forty years what they swear is true, because the head of the ditch is at the trough, and Delya Munn stands in the field and does not tell them and does not tell the room, and she has carried that since the second rolling.** Munn reads the release and then says that the stone stands and is the only evidence this county has that anybody ever agreed with anybody, and that breaking an oath in public on a day named in advance honours the stone more than keeping it into ruin, and **a thing that cannot be broken again is a thing that has been kept so long it has stopped meaning anything.** The consequence is the list, and the four villages go on it whole and not by household and not by fault. **A later writer may not lift the oath, may not have a bench absolve it, may not have an office take it, and may not write the breaking as a one-time act: the whole of the design is that it can be done again.**

346. **THE LIST, AND THE DIFFERENCE, AND THE ONE SENTENCE, SAID ONCE BY THE KEEPER OF THE SEED VAULT.** Chapter 142: the list is made three weeks before the day, in a room, with nobody sent to anybody. **The form has three columns and none of them is a name: roll number, the household's own mark, what the household would be helped with, and a fourth ruled line for a household that would rather say its need out loud.** It is keyed to the roll, the roll is the only book in that county a person can be put in front of, and **the minute-man enters the knowing at the head of the list in the council's hand: that the two books are in one room, that a man with both can make a household into a person, and that this council has done it knowing and enters the knowing here so that it is the first thing anybody reads and not the last.** **Twenty-nine of fifty-eight households put a mark, and one took a mark off, and the man of sixty-eight is the first and his mark is a thumb and not the flood-year smudge on the roll and he will not give a name.** The one sentence, said once, by the woman of sixty-six who keeps the seed room, in about four seconds, and not improved on, and the minute-man's pen does not move: **a list of households to be helped you can take back, and a list of persons to be struck you cannot, because there was never anybody at the other end of it to take it back to.** It was unspent at the start of this batch (item 339). **A later writer may not repeat it, may not improve it, may not attribute it to anybody else, and may not have any character say which of the two things is better.** The four gateway answers are not contradicted, improved on or entered anywhere, and **the county has not learned that it was wrong about a list and does not learn it in this block.**

347. **THE FOUR GATEWAY ANSWERS PAID, AND NOT BY DECEPTION.** Ferrow said in the ninth rolling that in five rollings half of four villages would sign whatever was read out to them because the council was right and not because anybody had deceived them. **It has taken eight rollings and the number is twenty-nine of fifty-eight, and the room is told in Chapter 142 that not one of the twenty-nine was deceived: the thing that produced the marks was a board with three columns and none of them a name, a household's own thumb in wet chalk, and four voices in a gateway telling a child the truth eight rollings earlier.** Ferrow's own second half is on the page: a county that has been right in a gateway and is then shown a board with a thumbhole in it is a county whose people do not need to be frightened into a thing they have already agreed with, and **a thumb on a board is a household saying it would like seed, and a household that would like seed is a household that will still be on white ground in four years if the water does not come down, and he is certain of that and has no idea what to do with it.**

348. **THE FOUR TRUE WORDS, AND THE MAN OF SIXTY-EIGHT'S MARK, AND NEITHER ENTERED.** The man of sixty-eight is the first mark on the list, in wet chalk, in front of witnesses, and he says the smudge on the roll is his and is not a good one and he is not having it argued about. **His four true words remain in nobody's book by Aren's own decision, and are not said a second time to a second person in this block, and Aren does not change his mind.** The minute-man enters nothing further; the silence of the fifth rolling stands and is not disturbed.

349. **THE KEEPER OF THE SEED ROOM'S WANT, ALL THREE PARTS, SAID ONCE, AND HER DECISION NOT TAKEN.** Chapter 142, in the stone room behind the chapel, to a man who has asked leave: **that the seed be in nine jars and not in a wall, because a wall does not let anybody take a jar out and a man in a hurry will not break a wall for nine jars and there are two answers built into that room over forty years and both of them are a man putting a thing into a wall and not being able to get it out; that she wants to be the last keeper of a seed room and not the first keeper of a seed house, because in about four years this county is going to have a door and she will be standing behind it and the first one is the one they send for; and that she does not want the cooperative's thanks, and she says it in advance so that he will not be surprised and so that if he looks surprised she will know he is managing a room and not a county.** All three were unspent (item 343). **And separately, and not part of it: the three marks on the outside of the wall are a different thing, she has decided nothing about them, and she says she will decide in the spring or she will not, and neither of those is his to hear about yet. The spring is now. A later phase may not have her decide and may not have her not decide.** The marks are read by nobody, copied into nothing, measured into nothing, and the water does not read them.

350. **THE BOUNDARIES RE-CUT IN NINE DAYS BY THE PEOPLE ON BOTH SIDES OF THEM, AND THE COST, AND WHAT THE COST MEANS.** Chapter 141, footed on a board in front of nineteen men: **nineteen of the guild at eight pence is 152 pence a day, the four of the fen yard at the guild's rate is 32, a day is 184, nine days is 184 nine times = 1,656, and ninety-six of elm off the guild's own stacks at what the stacks would fetch, and 1,656 and 96 is 1,752 pence, which is thirty-six marks and two shillings, and the county has not got it and will have it by the last day of the twelfth rolling or will not have it at all.** **And the second half of the column, which is the one to carry forward: the four hundred and forty yards of the fifth rolling cost 480 pence, and 1,752 and 480 is a difference of 1,272 pence, twenty-six marks and two shillings, and Ferrow says in the room that the difference is what it costs to do a thing twice.** Chapter 143: the work is four days before the named day and five after, and the last day of the breaking is in the account as a day on which the men of the length went to the field and did not dig, **because a plan that is worked on the day the ground stops taking a barrow is a plan written by a man who has never stopped working.** The re-cutting is done by the men of Sallow Marsh north and the men of the other three villages south, **because a boundary that is a channel cannot be re-cut by one side of it.** The two sides are four hundred yards apart for about four hours on the second evening and the old head is nine feet off the new one where they meet, and the second night the water comes up to the top of the new ditch and stops there. **The Batch 0014 columns of 480, 704, 592 and 1,104 are not re-derived in this block; they are re-stated inside a fair copy in Chapter 149 and added to, and 480 and 704 and 592 and 1,104 and 1,752 foot to 4,632 pence, ninety-six marks and two shillings.**

351. **THE PLAN HOLDS AND ONE THING DOES NOT, AND THE SCHEDULE IS THE ANTAGONIST.** Chapter 143: the plan is tested nine days after it is made, in the same season, in standing water, and it holds. **The Water came back up over the re-cut boundaries in the last night of the year because a promise that can be broken is a promise that can be tested, and a mile of new ditch took the night river in about a minute and a half, and the salt under four villages did not move an inch and the four villages were not under water at any hour of the night.** The thing that did not hold is a footbridge of two bays and five yards, elm, on two oak sills, at the trough: **cut back into a bank in the seventh rolling in four hours of flood, re-cut in the ninth rolling when the new line came to it, and re-cut once more in the tenth rolling by a crew on four days' notice in a wet week.** A later writer may not move the span, may not make it an arch, may not attribute the wrong place to anybody's carelessness, and **the elm is green and the oak is in a bed of new trench and a bank that has not been through a winter is a heap, and those are the reasons and the schedule is the reason and there is no villain in any of it.**

352. **THE SPAN, THE WORKING, AND WHY HE COULD BE ON IT FOR NINE HOURS.** Chapter 146, and it is a bridgewright's working and not a feat of strength. **A lid is not a drain.** The water has to be kept going one way instead of four, and the water going over a deck instead of round it is the reason a deck in a river is a lid and not a drain, and a man on it is not holding the water off. **He stays on the structure because staying on a structure is the trade, and he can do it for nine hours because he has been counting load points since the first chapter of his own book and nobody has ever told him to stop**, four at the near sill and two under its heel and two at the far end, counted twice to see whether he got the same ones. **He calls eleven things out loud, every one of them early, and thirty men on a bank act on his mouth and not on the wood, and if he says it wrong everybody on that bank dies of being wrong in the correct direction.** Ferrow does not ask him to go out and a first hand who is sixty and has a bar says in the guild's book in nine words that he did not ask and would have had no standing to stop him, and nineteen men and a bound and a slate woman and eleven on a bench all understand at the same moment in the dark that a stranger has just said the one sentence that makes him not a stranger for nine hours, and not one of them takes it. **Aren's own condition for going out is stated on the page: no speech, and any man who thinks he is taking over can come out and take over and he will get off inside four seconds.**

353. **ALED RHYS'S NINE SENTENCES, AND HE DOES NOT VOTE.** Chapter 146, from a boat on the north side at about the eleventh hour, to a bank and not to a room, and in the flat voice of a man paid on the day the water goes down. **A boat is a thing that cannot be stopped and that is the whole of his trade; a bridge is the only structure he knows that cannot get out of the way; a boat with too much water in it throws something off and that is the whole of seamanship; a bridge with too much water in it cannot, so a bridgewright has to decide in the morning what to throw off in the night, and the thing he throws off is people; a man on a span at night is not keeping the water off, he is keeping the deck in one piece so that the water has one way to go instead of four, and he is finding out which of the two sills is going first, and when he finds out he says it out loud, and everybody on the bank acts on his mouth and not on the wood.** Nine sentences, one vote, and nobody in that county has ever heard him vote. **A later writer may not improve the nine sentences, may not give him a vote, and may not turn him into a symbol of anything.**

354. **NOBODY DIED, AND THE NUMBER, AND WHY.** Chapter 147 opens on it and it has to be said first: **there are four hundred people in that county and about thirty of them were on a bank in the dark, and a boy of fifteen was up the bank at the second hour with a lamp in each hand and no instruction of any kind, and eleven men came down the north approach in the fourth hour with a cart of elm off the guild's own stack that they had no business cutting in a flood, and none of them came back on it. The number is thirty and the reason it is thirty is a ditch, a bank, nine days of work and a man on a lid.** The man who went into the Water after a boy of fifteen is nine days gone with the crew, **and the reason nobody in that county's job hauled up four feet of bank is that there was nobody there whose job it was, and the man who did it was a man at a sluice who was told to stand there with a bar and was not told to use it and did not use it.**

355. **THE PERMANENT LIMP, ACQUIRED ON THE FAR SIDE BEFORE THE RETURN STITCH CLOSED, AND THE PRICE OF IT.** Chapter 147, at about the fifth hour of the last night of the year. **The near sill goes two inches toward the ditch in under a minute without a sound; the near bay goes with it four inches and comes back; he has about a second and a half to decide whether to be on the far bay or the near one, and the far bay is where the bar and the line are and the far sill is the one that was sound, and he goes to the far bay and takes them with him.** The near bay then drops the other two inches and catches on the mortise of the far bay, and the drop goes through the heel of the near bay and into the right leg, and the right leg goes sideways, and what held him is that he had both hands on the far rail and he did not let go of it, because a man who lets go of a rail in a night flood is a man in the water. **About half the reaction went through the leg. The limp is the price of standing on a structure for five hours while the thing under it goes; it is the leg he had chosen to put across the failing bay rather than the sound one; nobody in the block calls it a punishment, a sacrifice or a cost of the flood, and the schedule is still the schedule.** The span held three more hours with one bay hanging four inches off the far one, **and the near bay never once gave the water a fifth place to go, and that is not luck, that is what a lid does.** He came off in four minutes, and the leg would not take him, and he got up four feet of bank with a stranger's shoulder. **A later writer may not heal it, may not make it worse for any reason other than standing on structures, and may not put it on a cause other than the working.** A gatehouse warning is not a cause of anything and no character says so.

356. **THE DRIFT, WRITTEN ON FAITH ON THE FAR SIDE AND CHECKED ON THE NEAR SIDE, WITH THE PRINTED FIGURE UNCHANGED AND NOBODY SAYING THE WORD.** Chapter 147, in the register a path-side man uses: **went out on the fifth of a month this county does not use, at the fourth hour, on a line made up in nine minutes with the hour against each of the nine; the office's printed figure is three weeks; the days are a hundred and seventeen; the error is ninety-six; the error is about four fifths of the days and not of the warning, and the warning is not what the error is over, and the path is nowhere in it; the printed figure has not changed and the office cannot say whose figure it is and he cannot either; this is written on faith on the far side of a line and will be checked on the near side, and if the two figures are not the same number then he has put a month in it twice and has earned that.** And the second paragraph: **the error is in him; it was eleven days the other way when he came in, with the warning longer than the absence; it is ninety-six days the other way now; what changed between them is the work and not the road; he wrote that down two volumes ago and has now proved it twice, and he would like it entered that he proved it the second time on a lid with one leg.** Chapter 148 checks it: **a hundred and seventeen days, a printed three weeks unchanged, twenty-one days against a hundred and seventeen, an error of ninety-six, about four fifths of the absence and not of the warning, and the path interval not in it at all and never.** No character in Chapters 141–150 describes the warning as having failed, and the drift is the widest the printed warning has ever been out. **The ratio is error over absence and never over the warning; 96/117 is about four fifths and the arithmetic is on the page in both chapters.**

357. **THE HAND-OVER, THE COUNTY'S OWN FORM, SEVEN COLUMNS, AND THE CHAPTER 145 QUESTION ANSWERED.** Item 322 is discharged, on the page, in Chapter 145. **The form is nineteen years old, came out of a landlord asking the council to put a ditch on his account, and is ruled in seven columns: what is given, to whom in the county's words, by whose hand, the day, the hour, a witness at this end, a witness at that end. **The enumeration is seven and the page said six at three places, and the count is the count: corrected at the Batch 0015 review repair pass and the form is seven columns, and the point of the count is unchanged, which is that the list is complete and therefore the two absent columns are absent on purpose.** There is no column for an office, because a form with a column for an office has been supplied with an authority, and there is no column for a vote and there never has been one, and the reason is the minute-man's sentence in his own words: a vote is a thing a man carries into a room he is standing in.** The eighth rolling is read out of the book before the thirteenth can be filled in, and the minute-man enters that the man bringing this was not asked for it, **because a man who is not asked is not to ask is a rule about carrying things up a stair, and giving is the other direction.** What is given is the causeway, the waterworks, the mile of boundary, the trough, the ditch at its head, four hundred and forty yards already opened, the new work, the list and the nine jars. **The road goes to the guild at the guild's terms, and to a yard of a fen county four hundred miles off at a yard's rate, and the name is the yard's and not his, and he is not the yard and he holds nothing in it and there is no vote of his in it.** The far-end witness is that yard, **entered as valid on a fact carried in ninety-six bushels of salt in a handcart and not on a signature**, and the minute-man says a record in another county in a cart with salt in it is worth what a cart is worth and that is better than nothing. The witness at this end is the woman of about sixty on three hearths, **because a column not filled in is a hole in a document, and because a rule with an exception in it has the exception as the person it is about, and because a person not on the oath is not a person this bench asks things of, which she asked for in the ninth rolling about a class of persons and not about herself, and which is the reason a stranger was not asked about his family in the thirteenth.** **The minute carries the hour and no name. The fair copy goes on the board under the bench where anybody coming up two flights may read it, and the name is in the fair copy and in the guild's book and in neither book of the council, and neither of the two men uses the word brother in the scene, and the word brother occurs once in the whole block and it belongs to the salt merchant's brother on a coast.**

358. **THE YARD'S NAME IN THE GUILD'S BOOK, AND A NAME IS A MAN AND A STREET IS A PLACE.** Chapter 144, in the last week of the twelfth rolling. Ferrow has wanted the yard's name in the guild's book since the fourth week of the seventh rolling and has not written it, **because the man of fifty-two said in the guild room in the eighth rolling that he was not going to be a thing in this county that somebody found out from a book, and a first hand agreed to that and has kept to it for four rollings by leaving the most useful half of it out.** The man of fifty-two gives the reason for allowing it in: the county wrote four hundred miles on a board because a cost board wants a county, and he has never written it and never said it and has twice given the answer that a yard is where they are. **The entry is the yard of Tovan Kest, Weir Street, in a fen town four hundred miles off, on a coast road where the road is a causeway, and it is spelled with a v after Ferrow gets the letter wrong once, and the street is added because a name is a man and a street is a place and a place is a thing a person can be shown and a man is not.** The reason Ferrow wants it is that on the day they go there will be a moment and it will not be a speech. **And the gateway line, said once, by the man of fifty-two, to about nine of the guild's men and nobody in particular: we are from a fen county four hundred miles off, on a coast road, and we are the best crew in that county, and there is nobody in this county who can do what we do, and I have said it in a gateway and not in a room. Nobody wrote it down.** The minute-man was not there and the slate woman was not there. A later writer may not enter that sentence in any book.

359. **THE CREW'S ACCOUNT, PAID IN PART IN KIND, AND THE REST AT NONE.** Chapter 144, footed on the guild's board: **four men at the guild's rate of eight pence is 32 pence a day; 32 nine times is 288; 288 and 288 is 656; 656 and 288 is 864 pence, which is eighteen marks exactly, and it lands clean because twenty-seven divides into a hundred and sixty-four without a remainder.** It is paid **in ninety-six bushels of salt at nine pence the bushel, and 9 pence ninety-six times is also 864 pence, and it lands because the county's rate and the county's salt are both the county's own.** **The rest of the stay, from the fourth week of the seventh rolling, is at none, and none is in two books, and Ferrow says that in nine years somebody will take the two books and put them side by side and say the county owes four men it cannot name, and the county can now name them and still cannot pay them, and the trade took a name, a street, a yard, a rate, a date and a count and got no figure.** Ferrow will not count the salt out in front of nineteen men, because a man handed his money in front of a room is a man who has been given a figure. The yard's rule is entered: **nothing that belongs to a man goes in this yard's book and only a thing set; a contract is a thing set; the salt is a thing set; the name is a thing set; the rest of it is not a thing set, because it is a figure a county in a market room agreed it could not pay, and a yard that puts that in a book has admitted a debt it cannot call in, and a book that knows what a man has decided not to do is an office.** The four men left at the start of the thirteenth rolling with the salt in a handcart, on a day the council named, which is the same instrument as naming a day for a breaking.

360. **THE YEAR ENDED, IN THE COUNTY'S WORDS, AND THE RECORD OF A SOWING IS A STONE.** Chapter 141, last hour, said by the minute-man to a room and not to the council: **there are three rollings after this one, and a year is thirteen rollings from the first sowing and a rolling is four weeks, so the year is a year and a rolling, and the year ends in the fourth week of the thirteenth and the first sowing of the next is within a week of that, and it is the first sowing in thirteen rollings this county has made without a stone being in the ground for it.** He has entered every year in that book for twenty-two years and the way he enters a year is by its stone, and in nine years somebody will come to that book to find out what was sown and there will be nothing in it, **because the record of a sowing is a stone and the stone is a boundary and a boundary is not a return.** The sowing is entered as a sowing and not as a return, in the same run of the page and the same hand, so that a man in nine years has to read two entries and not one and cannot make one line out of the other. **The tenth to the thirteenth rolling is the whole of what was left, and the year ended in this block, and the block is the end of a year and a rolling in a county that is not on any map in this kingdom.**

361. **THE FOUR WHITE VILLAGES ARE NOT CURED, THE SALT IS NOT LIFTED, AND THE FIRST CROP OFF TWO OF THEM IS A FAILURE ON A BOARD IN A KEEPER'S HAND WITH THE HOUR ON IT.** Carried on the page in Chapter 143 in a field, in Chapter 146 in a bank, and in the Marches' own account in Chapter 149. **A man at a sluice who has scraped ground for forty years: the plan holds and two of the four villages' ground is worse than it was, and the salt has not moved; the water table is still a mile wide; a hundred and forty years of a hand's width a year is a mile and a bit; a mile of water standing on the low side of a bank is a water table a mile wide; a water table comes down when the thing feeding it stops, and it comes down at the rate it went up and not any faster, and nobody in this kingdom has ever found a way to make a water table come down quicker than it went up; you have put a stop to a hundred and forty years of it being somewhere else, and a hundred and forty years of water does not go away because you have stopped moving it, it goes down, and it takes four years, and half of the county will not manage not making it worse because half of them are the reason it moved.** The flood's ground and the old white are the same white and one will grow something and the other will grow a stone, and a man in a county cannot tell them apart from where he is standing and can from where he is not. **The four villages sowed in the last week of the twelfth rolling into ground the digging finished six days before, which is wrong, and the rain came on the eighth day, and the first crop off two of the four came in the last rolling and it is a failure and it is on a board in a keeper's hand with the hour on it and the board is not a book and cannot be served.** Nothing in this volume is a victory and it is not written as one.

362. **THE RETURN, IN A GATEWAY IN WEIR STREET, IN THE RAIN, AND NOBODY LOOKED FOR HIM.** Chapter 148, at about the fourth hour of the second of Hearthmonth, YR 313, a Thursday and a market day. **The north end of that line is a yard, and a yard's gate is a place a cart goes through and is not a place a man comes up out of, and the yard was shut and the street was empty and he was not looked for.** He hears the Old Toll bell at the fourth hour and a half four hundred yards up the road and does not turn round. **Three days of fever, the second, the third and the fourth, breaking on the fifth, which is the second time in this case that a crossing has cost three; the boy of fifteen holds the tools, cannot make a fire for about an hour and a half, and carries a scythe edge down to the yard for a man who cannot lift it; the boy of the Old Toll, seventeen and still not good at it, asks the fourth time whether the man at the rooms over the cooper is carrying anything and says he will not say he is carrying nothing because that would be a lie and will not say he knows what is in it because he does not, and that it is a Monday the far end wants on and he will not waste an ask on a Friday.** **On the morning of the fourth the yard's book has a fifth line with an hour against it and nothing at all against either, written in nine seconds with a wet nib by the first hand of the yard, who has not been asked about any of the five and will not be, and the boy of fifteen asks what it is for and is told it is nothing and puts the kettle on. A yard in a fen town held one end of a line for a hundred and seventeen days because the other end of it was four hundred miles off in a country that is not on any map, and the holding was an accident of where a gate is, and a thing set went in a book, and no instrument in this kingdom can make that book answer.** **A later writer may not have Tovan told any of this, may not have him asked, and may not have the line explained to him in this volume.**
