363. **THE THREE RULES DOING THEIR WORK, THE NUMBERS BEFORE SHE ASKS, THE MORNING NOT TOLD, AND NO TENTH NOT-ASKING.** Chapter 148, on the fifth of Hearthmonth. **He gives her the numbers before she asks, in the order they happened, flat, as a man reads out a line of work**: the fifth of a month at the fourth hour, a line made up in nine minutes with the hour against each of the nine, a week and a rolling, a year of thirteen rollings that he was there for all of, the back on the second of this month, **a hundred and seventeen days written on faith on the far side and confirmed on the near**, a printed three weeks that has not moved and belongs to nobody, ninety-six days of error, about four fifths of the absence, three days of fever, two routes held, and **the leg as the other half of the sentence that the error is in him and scales with the work.** And then the one thing that is not a figure: **he cannot put an ordinary morning in a yard on Weir Street on the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth in the order it happened; it is not gone, it is all there, it comes out in whatever order it likes.** He does not tell her that he cannot. **The third of the three rules ends *an authority that nobody can name the source of is entered as a habit, and a habit is a thing a county does to itself when nobody is looking*; she wrote that sentence on the sixteenth of Longlight, it is on a page in a book that cannot be served in a room with a door and a number, it is about him, he knows it is about him, and neither of them has ever said so, and that is the fourth thing in a coat and it is a habit now and not a choice.** She says she had ninety-six in the room since the summer and did not have a figure for the absence to put it against, and he says he will not improve on that by being right about it twice. **She does not ask. The ninth not-asking stands, entered on the sixteenth of Longlight, and there is no tenth and there is not going to be one in this year; in Chapter 150 she puts her pen down and does not write it and neither of them says why.** No apology, in any form, in a scene, an aside or a document, and no mistake has been made available to anybody in Chapters 141–150.

364. **DAIN'S OFFER REFUSED IN WRITING, IN A DOCUMENT, FOR THE RIGHT REASON, AND THE REFUSAL IS NOT AN APOLOGY AND IS NOT A SCENE.** Chapter 148, the sixth of Hearthmonth, four hundred and eleven words, in his own hand, with the date and the hour on it, **because a thing that is set goes in a book and a thing that is not set goes in a coat, and he had four things in a coat and was not going to make it five.** It is addressed to Sabin Dain, aid to the register, **and to no office.** Six numbered paragraphs. **The rank is refused because it is a command and not a custody, and he has no argument against a command and one against a command a person appointed by nobody can hold and a person appointed by a community can refuse. The sealed record is refused because a record a person cannot see is a record a person cannot contradict, and he says in it that he has spent two years refusing to make one and has been worse at refusing it than he is at building bridges, and that this is the fourth attempt and the first one that is a document.** The third paragraph is the load-bearing one and it is about the sheet's missing number: **the offer was made out in the ninth month of the year three years last past, has been held open since, and the number of persons to whom it has been made out is not stated, and he has spent four months arriving at the conclusion that it is the only sentence on the sheet that matters and that eleven marks and a district and a clerk are the price of not printing it, and he is not going to be the man who tells her she left it off on purpose because he does not know that she did, and because if she did then there is a number in her office and if she did not then the number is the sheet's and the office has had three years.** The warrant is not to be fetched out of a press. **The eastern paths are not to be closed by him, and it is not a bargaining position and is not held against anything, and the reason is that he does not know how to close a road and does not intend to learn, and a county of clay with no office in it closed its own road in four lines of writing in the middle of the year by a bench that asked for it, and the fourth line gave back a term he had helped write nineteen years ago, and it was correct, and he read it in a room with nine people in it and did not say it out loud, and that is the whole of what he knows about closing a road.** The earlier neither-accept-nor-refuse reply in a bag is not withdrawn, **because a thing that is not withdrawn is a thing that can be produced, and he would like this county to be able to produce both of them.** And: **there is no thanks in this letter, and you did not offer a thing that needed any, and I would rather a woman who writes the reasons down first did not have to be polite to me in a document she is going to keep.** **It uses no explanation of the marginal instruction, does not use the word custodian, and contains no apology of any kind.** The envelope is cut along the left edge with a knife, because that is the way every envelope in this case has been opened since the twelfth of Longlight and a man does not send a letter in a bag in a way that cannot be seen to have been opened.

365. **THE MARCHES' ACCOUNT: FOUR HUNDRED AND EIGHT WORDS, A WORKING, A WRONG FIGURE, AND A COMPANY.** Chapter 149, the twentieth of Hearthmonth, YR 313, a Monday, not a market day. **The fair copy is four hundred and eight words and says so twice, and the working is on a second sheet folded with it, and both are in the minute-man's hand, which is not a clerk's, and the fair copy is addressed to a shelf.** Alish Renn states the rule before it is opened: a document with an address on it is a different animal from a document without one, and this one is addressed to a shelf, and the shelf is this room. **The head of it is nine hundred and forty-one, and the figure is hearthstones, and it was counted in the eleventh rolling by the minute-man and two men off the north side of the bank.** It is the wrong number and Mara Vey says she can tell it is wrong without leaving the room and cannot say what the right one is, and says those two sentences in that order, **and the county's own figures are three and none of them is a hearth: 8,031 readings in a book in a hut at a weir which is the only account of the water of that county that exists and which nobody has ever asked to see; sixty households on a roll, fifty-eight certain and two doubtful, and the roll says in its own second line that it is not a return; and nine, which is the stone and the nine jars, and the account has not doubled it.** The figure and the word under it are both struck with the reason in the margin. **Nine hundred and forty-one is also the only figure this office holds about the county of clay, the two are the same number by accident, and she writes down that it is a coincidence, asks every person in the room to hear the word and not improve on it, and does one single thing with it. 9-4-1 is in Chapters 149 and 150 only, is never in a Marches scene, and is never in a sum with a Marches figure.** The working foots the whole of the county's year: **480 + 704 = 1,184; 1,184 + 592 = 1,776; 1,776 + 1,104 = 2,880; 2,880 + 1,752 = 4,632 pence, ninety-six marks and two shillings, checked twice with a rule, and the addition lands on the figure at the foot, which is the only test there is.** The sixth line is four men and the word *none*, and it is entered in the office's own day-book in the same run of the page and not on a separate leaf, **because a separate leaf is a thing a person can take away.** The account carries the salt not lifted, the failure of the first crop off two of the four villages on a board in a keeper's hand with the hour on it, and the question, **which is the first question this county has ever put to another county: we keep nine sealed jars of a wheat that has not been grown here in forty years, it will keep about four more years, a third of our wheat is a variety nobody here can sow twice, we ask any county that has kept a seed of a thing it does not grow to send us a jar, we will pay the carriage and the jar, and we will put the name of whoever keeps it in a book that cannot be served and not in a book that can.**

366. **A SEED-AND-BRIDGE COMPANY, ENTERED, WITH A DATE AND A NAME AND A WITNESS AT EACH END, AND ITS FIRST LETTER IS NOT ANSWERED.** **The name is *the nine jars and the ditch*, proposed by the first hand of the guild because a name with a number in it is a thing that cannot be sued by anybody for anything, and the keeper of the seed room has never met him and is sixty-six and has joined a company whose name is nine jars and a ditch.** It is entered by the Bellweather Claims Archive, a body corporate with a door and a number, in the ordinary form, **with the claim number against the office and nothing against any person, and the claim number is the number of the account, which is a thing a clerk may do twice and which a keeper may not do once.** **The witness at the north end is the keeper of records of the office in Merefen, answerable to a room over a weigh-house and to nobody else; the witness at the south end is the keeper of a minute in a room under a tithe barn in a county that is not on any map, answerable to about eleven people and to nobody else; they have never met, cannot be produced to each other, and both of those facts are in the entry, because a witness nobody can produce is worth exactly as much as it says and no more.** Its first act is a letter of four lines to any county that has kept a seed of a thing it does not grow, asking for a jar, with the carriage and the jar paid for, promising the keeper's name to a book that cannot be served and not to a book that can, promising not to read a name out of any book to anybody from that county, and promising not to send anybody who can be asked to come and look. **It went into a bag on the twenty-first and is not answered inside the year and is not going to be answered, and the reason is the third line of the four-line notice that came down the same road in the autumn: the book at that end is shut and is kept shut and is not to be asked for, and the hand at that end is released and is not to be sought, and the line at that end is the whole of what that county has to say.** A county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths bought a roll of persons for a farthing of salt and does not know what it bought, the man who bought it is on a coast and has wanted a canal wharf for nine years, and **the person who used to hold the other end of anything in that county was struck and the county did not replace them in time, and the road is not there.** **A later volume may not answer that letter, may not deliver a jar, and may not find anybody at that end who is holding anything.**

367. **THE DELEGATION DOES NOT ARRIVE, AND THE HEARING IS ENTERED FOR A DATE IT CANNOT REACH.** **The first of the Marches' own six terms is that the road is not to be made to carry a person, and there is therefore no way on earth to get anybody from that county to a fen town, and a hearing entered for a date the other party cannot reach is the thing this office has done to two other offices in four years and a half and it has been entered every time.** The Archive resolves in the ordinary form, with the claim number against the office and nothing against any person: **that it will hear the delegation of the Hearth Marches on the terms the Marches have set, that the hearing is entered for the ninth of Rainmonth of the year following at the second hour in that room, that the terms are six lines on a board and are held in the office, that no person is to be sent to ask anybody in that county anything, that the delegation is not to be sent for, and that if the delegation does not come the hearing is not adjourned and not refused and it stands.** It is the first resolution this office has entered for a date eighteen months away. **Nobody in that room is going to be able to tell anybody in four years whether anybody came, and the Marches' delegation has not left, because there is no way for it to leave.**

368. **LOTUS FYNN AND MARDA OAKES IN THE SAME ROOM TWICE, AND NOTHING CONCEDED.** Chapter 149. **Lysa Fenn, procedural, in about a quarter of an hour, and the construction is the same one she used on the count and the same one she is frightened of: if a body corporate enters that company then the company has a party and the party is this office and this office can be sued and can be shut and can be in default, and in about four years a man in a county of clay will write to somebody in this county and say that a company of seed and bridge in a county that is not on any map has asked this office for a favour.** Mara Vey's answer is Volume 02's own line, that a claim cannot be sued and that is the whole of why a filing room can answer an instrument and the whole of why a company of eleven people in a county with no office can send a question and get a real answer out of it in nine days. **Alish Renn entered that neither of them gave anything, which is her office's way of entering a thing that has no figure against it. Marda Oakes came with a resolution of the county council and got to her point in four minutes, and her point is that a company that has not written its first letter yet is a company that can be entered and can be heard and can be asked questions about, and a company that has written its first letter is a company with a correspondence, and a correspondence is a hand a county can be got at.** She said the half she always says and did not finish the sentence, and said why, and the reason is the same reason she gave a year before: **you are not an office yet, and when you are one, everybody who helped you is a person who helped you.** **That half sentence is the only entry in the day-book in four years and a half that is a sentence a person stopped in the middle of, and neither woman is a cynic and neither is a villain and three of the four parties in this room are better than he is.**

369. **THE BENCH, ASKED FOUR TIMES, AND THE FIFTH ASKING NOT MADE, AND TOVAN'S SENTENCE IN THE MINUTE AGAIN.** Chapter 150, the second hour. **The number is four, because the fifth asking is in Reeve Aldis Fenn's coat and is not on the table, and she came up two flights of that stair on the twenty-fourth of Hearthmonth with it and did not put it, and the minute says so in her own words and at the hour, and the reason is not hers and is a sentence a carpenter said in this hall in the fourth of Frostmonth.** The sentence is carried word for word and there is no second version of it in this county or in any other: **that a bench asked three times and saying yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription.** **A first pass of this chapter had the reeve put the fifth asking and the bench say yes to it, which would have made the number five and contradicted both `outline/volume-03.md` §12's four and Chapter 101; corrected on the page and recorded here so that no writer reads five.** She asks for the number entered as well and for it entered that she did not put the fifth and that a bench that has been asked the same thing five times has not been asked five things. The chest has two marks in it and has had two since the ninth of Fallowmonth. **Aren is in the room, is on the roll, has a vote, did not use it, was not asked for it, and has never once said out loud in that hall that a vote and not an office is not enough to build anything on and is not going to start.**

370. **THE LIMP MEASURED BY NOBODY, ON PURPOSE, IN ABOUT NINE SECONDS.** Chapter 150, the ninth hour, by the cutwater, with a rule already out. **He says no in about nine seconds and gives the reason, and the reason is not that it is a bad leg: he keeps his own book, the office has paid him six pence twice out of a box for two written reports, and he has never put one of them in anybody else's book, and that is the only reason there is a man in this county who can be shown a hole under a bridge on a date. If he measures a leg and writes it down anywhere, then in a year there is a figure, and in about four years a surveyor will want a section, and a section wants a figure, and if there is one of his in a guild's book he will use it because it is there and it is nearer, and then it is not a man who wrote down a thing he saw on a morning, it is a decline, and a decline is a survey, and a survey goes in a form.** And the second reason, which is better: **he has one of those already, in a file two hundred miles off, in a column with a head on it, which he did not write and has never seen and cannot get at.** He says he would have said no in about four if it had been a Tuesday. **A later writer may not have the limp measured, may not have a figure for it anywhere, and may not turn the refusal into an act of heroism.**

371. **THE LAST IMAGE, AND WHAT IS FIXED IN IT.** Chapter 150, the twenty-fourth of Hearthmonth, YR 313, a Friday, not a market day, the fourth hour. **Ordinary work: about forty men, four carts of stone, pointing that is never finished on the day it is begun, the nose of the third pier out of the water, a tool bag, load points counted twice into a book that does not exist, and a stick cut out of a fence rail in a county that is not on any map that is for four hundred yards of road and for nothing else, because leaning on it puts the weight through the wrong hip.** A bench of nine asked four times and not a bench. The Old Toll crossing four hundred yards up the road: in municipal trust, in abeyance, tolls suspended by an instrument a court has found lawful, **no owner, no money, no seat on that bench, no glass in the sill and no third seat**, with a dressed block in the abutment and forty yards of tarred hemp put there on the fourth of Hearthmonth of the year before by four men and a boy. **The bell has a band of forty-one rivets and a dull red seam where the band was closed, and it is rung at the fourth hour and a half by a boy who is seventeen and has been the steward's apprentice for two years and three months and is still not good at it, and a carpenter who is a hand and not a keeper and has a bar he is not using, and the arrangement has been a boy and a ferrywright since the twenty-fourth of Fallowmonth and a boy and a carpenter since the ninth of Hearthmonth, and there is a page at the head of the yard's book for the week with the hour against it in a hand somebody would know, and nobody wrote a word in it about who rang the bell. The arrangement is not his. Nothing in the sound of it is his.** **A Monday is the day the far end keeps, it was a Friday, nothing was struck at the other end of that line, and the two facts are in a day-book separately and are not in one sentence, because a book is a place where things get joined whether they were joined or not.** Four hundred miles off there is a road that is not there, a woman who keeps its end who has not been asked a question about it in about two years and has not asked one either, **and a tile on a coping, a hand's width, with a rope on it in two loops and no yoke, carrying a device nobody in that county has ever been told the name of, and the county's own kiln's mark on the back, and its maker dead since the eighth of the last rolling, and nobody has looked at it.** He stood at the end of the line. **The last line of the block and of the volume is about the road, and it is not a sentence about a man and it is not a sentence about a county, and the volume does not end on the span.**

372. **FIGURES AND DEVICE COUNTS FOR THE BATCH, CHECKED ON THE PAGE.** **34,113 words**, per chapter **3408 / 3368 / 2794 / 3183 / 2912 / 3014 / 3380 / 3634 / 5125 / 3295.** **The block total and the Chapter 147 figure were wrong in the first pass of this item and both were corrected at the Batch 0015 review repair pass: 34,117 became 34,113 and 3377 became 3380, and the item had held three mutually inconsistent numbers, which is the failure the Volume 02 close's §10 item 3 warns about. The number a later grep gets is 34,113.** **Zero `---` section rules and one bolded line in the ten chapters**, and the bolded line is the figure at the head of a fair copy in Chapter 149 and is inside a document and is not a device. **Zero semicolons and zero em-dashes**, against Batch 0014's three and zero. **No System panel and no Gatehouse-voice line**, where the prompt allowed one, and the reason is that Batch 0014 spent its one on *A road needs a living hand* and that line is spent and a second line in this block had nothing to be about. **Money foots three times and once more as a working, four columns, all in pence, all added on the page before a total is said: 1,752 (Ch 141), the difference 1,272 (Ch 141), 864 in salt (Ch 144), and 4,632 as a fair copy's working (Ch 149).** **The Batch 0014 columns of 480, 704, 592 and 1,104 are re-stated inside a fair copy and are not re-derived; the 102 of Chapter 140 and the 648 of Chapter 108 are not on the page; Chapter 10's eleven marks and fourpence is not re-derived.** No banned sum and no 4720 in any form, no 456 in any form, no 254, no 2,450, no 2,906, and **no hearth figure in any Marches scene**; 9-4-1 is in Chapters 149 and 150 only and never in a sum with a Marches figure. No main-world month name, no real-world month name and no main-world weekday anywhere in Chapters 141–147 in any register including a notebook and a minute, **and the one date in those seven chapters is *the fifth of a month this county does not use*, in his own notebook, which is the established register and not a conversion.** No rolling is converted by any character. *Culling Order*, *custodian*, *Bind*, *Gatehouse* and `scheduled day` do not appear. The marginal instruction is not explained and is in nobody's mouth. The tile on the coping is not touched, not looked at, **and it is named twice in this block as an object — Chapter 148 line 25 and the last line of Chapter 150, where it has a rope on it and no yoke — and it is never explained, and its device is named by nobody. A later phase may not read the naming as a licence to explain it.** The eighth ruled line in the served schedule is not looked at. The brine weights are neither mentioned nor filed. **`the whole of` is 2 in the block, at Chapter 146 line 83 and Chapter 147 line 35, against the house figure of 3, and both survivors are the flood and the drowning. The first pass of this item recorded 1 and recorded a first-pass count of 46 with forty-five cut in a second pass; the page has 2, the figure a later grep gets is 2, and the 46 is a first-pass figure that cannot be re-checked because the first pass is gone.** **`about N seconds` is 6, against the house figure of 3, and four of the six are load-bearing timings and two are the required nine seconds of Chapter 150 quoted back at the end of the volume.** Filler counts, for the next writer's baseline, counted on the ten chapters as whole words and not as substrings, so that `stood` is not `understood`: `nodded` 0, `held` 18, `wrote` 38, `stood` 12, `plain` 1, `counted` 10, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0. **`held` was recorded as 19 in the first pass of this item and the page has 18; corrected at the Batch 0015 review repair pass, and the other six are right.** **The string `cut` occurs twelve times as `cut` or `cutting` and three more times inside `cutwater`, and none of the fifteen is the Stage 3 working**: a boundary re-cut (four), a channel cut through a month-old bank, elm cut on a stack in a flood, a peg cut out of a fence rail, a peg a man has to cut out with a bar, a stick cut out of a fence rail, and a county cut off. **The working stands once in Chapter 124, was not re-derived in Chapters 131–140, and is not named, priced, described as available or alluded to anywhere in Chapters 141–150.** Nobody dies in this block and nobody is arranged into a death.

## Canon established by the Batch 0015 review repair pass

373. **THE BATCH 0015 REVIEW REPAIR PASS, IN ONE PLACE, AND WHAT A LATER WRITER MUST NOT UNDO.** A review of Chapters 141–150 and of the five state files that phase wrote returned **nine findings. Two are prose and both are fixed in the page. Six are figures and claims in state files and in the volume close's prompt, and all six are corrected. One is judged and not changed.** **Two errors the review did not name were found on the page while checking the ones it did, and are corrected here and disclosed here: the `held` baseline count in item 372, and the stale *sixth of the six* in `state/chapter-summaries.md`'s Chapter 140 entry (item 380).** The prose defects were Chapter 145's column count (item 376) and Chapter 138's two duplications (item 375); **the state and prompt defects were the block's word count in four files and the equation printed to justify it (item 374), the deviation count in the close prompt, the melody claim, the tile claim and the `the whole of` count (items 377, 378, 379); the judged one is the Volume 02 repeat (item 379).** **Nothing in the batch's plot, day map, money, cast, calendar or four devices was touched, no chapter was restarted, no scene or dialogue was rewritten, and no outline was edited.** The review's own summary of the batch was that the money discipline foots exactly and the mechanism is discharged cleanly, and that its own findings were bookkeeping and precision and not craft.
374. **THE FIGURES, CORRECTED ON THE PAGE: 34,113 / 35,318 / 146,524 / 601,605.** **Batch 0015 is 34,113 words**, per chapter **3408 / 3368 / 2794 / 3183 / 2912 / 3014 / 3380 / 3634 / 5125 / 3295**, and the figure was recorded as 34,117 in four files (this item 372, `state/open-threads.md`, `state/current.md` and the volume close's prompt) while the item's own per-chapter list had Chapter 147 as 3,377 against the page's 3,380, so the item held three mutually inconsistent numbers. **Because this pass removed two duplicated paragraphs from Chapter 138 (item 375), Chapter 138 is 2,906 and not 3,148, and Batch 0014 is 35,318 and not 35,560, and Volume 03 is 146,524 and not 146,766, and the manuscript is 601,605 and not 601,847.** The five columns foot: **31,648 + 29,130 + 16,315 + 35,318 + 34,113 = 146,524**, and the three volumes foot: **270,562 + 184,519 + 146,524 = 601,605.** Volume 01 and Volume 02 are untouched at 270,562 and 184,519, and **the nine-word discrepancy between the page and `workspace/volume-02/volume-02-close/PROMPT.md` on Volume 02 stands as recorded and is not this pass's to close.** **A later phase that prints a total must foot it on the page first, which is the whole of item 145 and the whole of the Volume 02 close's §10 item 3.**
375. **THE TWO DUPLICATED PASSAGES IN CHAPTER 138 ARE GONE, AND THE TWELFTH-ROLLING DATING IS STILL OPEN AND IS THE VOLUME CLOSE'S.** Chapter 138 had **two** duplication defects and one of them was unrecorded. **The first was a paragraph repeated word for word: the cost of the eighth rolling, which is now on the page once, at line 55, and the chapter is seventy-five lines.** **The second was the minute-man's speech about keeping a thing in the same hand and the same book and the same run of the page, spoken twice in a row and varying only in its last clause; the reading that stands is the one that says *two flights of that stair*, at line 50, because the next paragraph and Chapter 145's reading-out of the eighth rolling both say two flights.** Both were Batch 0014's and both are removed. **What is not fixed is the dating: Chapter 138 says three times that the guild's plan was written in the twelfth rolling and that the eleventh is the rolling before the man with the bar came up the stair (lines 11, 13 and 43), and Chapter 149's working calls it *the plan of the twelfth rolling* (line 31), and Chapter 139 is in the ninth rolling with four rollings left in the year, so a plan cannot have been written in the twelfth and been on the table in the eighth. A repair would have to move the plan's date earlier in four places in two chapters and would have to choose a rolling before the Water, and that is a plan-level decision and it belongs to the volume close and not to a repair pass. The Volume 03 close must decide it and say which it chose.**
376. **THE FORM IN CHAPTER 145 IS RULED IN SEVEN COLUMNS, AND THE PAGE SAYS SEVEN AT ALL THREE PLACES.** The chapter enumerated seven columns — what is given, to whom in the county's words, by whose hand, the day, the hour, a witness at this end, a witness at that end — and then said six at line 7, said six again at line 9, and said six again at line 97. **The count is now seven at all three and the argument of the passage is untouched, which is that the enumeration is complete and therefore the two absent columns are absent on purpose.** The mistake was in the count and not in the list, and **the day and the hour are two columns and are not to be merged into one by a later writer to make a smaller number.** A later phase may not say six columns of this form again. The form is otherwise unchanged: no column for an office, no column for a vote, and a vote is a thing a man carries into a room he is standing in.
377. **THE TILE ON THE COPING IS NAMED TWICE IN VOLUME 03 AND IS NEVER EXPLAINED, AND THE STATE FILES NOW SAY SO.** Item 372 and three other places recorded the tile as *not named*, and Chapter 150's last line names it and Chapter 148 names it: *a tile on it with a rope on it and no yoke, and nobody looked at it.* **The rule the close has to work with is therefore: the tile may be named as an object and has been, twice, and may not be explained, and no character may name its device, and nothing in this block looks at it.** Nothing in the prose was changed, because nothing in the prose was wrong; the state files were the thing that was wrong, and **a Volume 04 prohibition built on *not named* would have forbidden the last line of Volume 03.**
378. **THE DEVICE COUNTS IN ITEM 372, CORRECTED AGAINST THE PAGE: `the whole of` 2 AND `held` 18.** **`the whole of` is 2 in Chapters 141–150, at Chapter 146 line 83 and Chapter 147 line 35, and was recorded as 1; both survivors are in the flood and in the drowning, and the figure a later grep gets is 2.** **`held` is 18 as a whole word and was recorded as 19; the other six baseline counts are right, and the baseline is counted as whole words, so that `stood` at 12 is not `understood` at 17.** `about N seconds` is 6 and was right, `cut` is twelve with three more inside `cutwater` and was right, zero semicolons and zero em-dashes and zero `---` rules were right, and the one bolded line is inside a document and was right. **Batch 0014's `the whole of` is 3 and is right — Chapter 134 line 65, Chapter 137 line 73 and Chapter 139 line 31 — and the third of those begins a sentence, so a case-sensitive grep for the lower-case string finds 2 in that block and a later phase must read all three lines before concluding anything, which is the same trap this item fell into.**
379. **THE MELODY WORD, AND ONE VERBATIM REPEAT IN VOLUME 02 THAT IS NOT A DEFECT.** **The word appears once in the whole manuscript outside Volume 01, at `chapters/volume-02/chapter-0070.md` line 125, inside a return form, as a negative entry in a list of costs: *No melody cost.*** It does not appear anywhere in Volume 03, and **no new melody cost is added in Volume 04 and no volume may add one; the first-crossing loss of one verse's remembered pitch remains the only fixed one.** A downstream writer told the word was absent from Volume 02 would read that line as a canon violation, and it is not one: it is a form saying that nothing was spent. **And `chapters/volume-02/chapter-0052.md` lines 21 and 117 are the same paragraph word for word and are not a duplication defect: line 21 is the third line of a four-line letter and line 117 is the keeper of records thinking about the third line of that letter, which she says she has read eleven times, and quoting a line back to herself in full is the beat. It was inspected and left alone, and a later phase that runs a duplicate scan over the whole manuscript will hit it and should not delete it.**
380. **THE STALE LINE IN THE CHAPTER 140 SUMMARY, AND WHICH *SIXTH OF THE SIX* IS WHICH.** `state/chapter-summaries.md`'s Chapter 140 entry still read *the Marches is the sixth of the six*, which the Batch 0014 review corrected in the page to *the sixth of the eight* and did not correct in the summary. **The summary now reads the eighth, and the phrase *the sixth of the six* belongs to Chapter 118 and to the county's six terms and to nothing else.** The ledger at the foot of this file was already right on that point. A later phase that takes a fact from `state/chapter-summaries.md` instead of from the page will now get this one right, and the reason it was wrong is the reason the Volume 02 close's §10 item 3 exists.

## Relationship state at the end of Chapter 150

- **Aren and Mara Vey: the breach, unrepaired, and the concealment is now a habit that is on the page in a document and in a conversation.** He gives her the numbers before she asks, in the order they happened, and one thing that is not a figure, and the one thing that is not a figure is that he cannot put an ordinary morning in a yard on Weir Street in the order it happened. **He does not tell her that he cannot. The third rule ends *a habit is a thing a county does to itself when nobody is looking*, she wrote that sentence four months ago, it is about him, he knows it is about him, and neither of them has ever said so.** She says she had ninety-six in the room since the summer and did not have a figure for the absence to put it against, and he says he will not improve on that by being right about it twice. **She does not ask. The ninth not-asking stands, entered on the sixteenth of Longlight, and there is no tenth and there is not going to be one in this year; in Chapter 150 she puts her pen down and does not write it and neither of them says why.** He declines to give her a figure for the leg, because the figure would be hers to keep and it is not a fact about a person. **She does not know there is an offer in a coat, and she now knows there is a refusal in a bag, because it is in the county post and not in this office, and she has not been told, and that is a fifth thing neither of them has said.** No apology, in any form, in a scene, an aside or a document. **Volume 04's chosen intimacy is bought with this and nothing in this block buys any of it early.**
- **Aren and Tovan Kest: the estrangement is untouched and the two of them are now on the same page of a document in a county that is not on any map, and neither of them knows it.** **Tovan's yard is named in the Marches' guild book in the twelfth rolling and in a fair copy on a board under a stair in the thirteenth, and his brother's name is on the same fair copy, and Ferrow raised that as an objection before the copy was made and refused to have either name taken out, and the minute-man asked whether the two of them are anything to each other that the council is entitled to enter and was answered *No* and entered it exactly as given.** **Tovan Kest held one end of a line for a hundred and seventeen days in his own body, in a yard, without knowing it, because a yard's gate is the north end of that line and he was asleep when his brother came up it, and his book's fifth line with an hour against it and nothing at all against it is the whole of what he has.** The boy of fifteen asked what it was for and was told it is nothing. **The spent sentence is not re-said, improved on, or alluded to as retractable, and no scene in this block heals the estrangement or touches it, and the yard is the only relationship in the manuscript that is still getting better and it got better again by costing six hundred and forty-eight pence and ninety-six bushels of salt and four men who went home unpaid.** Each brother still keeps a page the other does not know.
- **Aren and the hand of the Ninebridge: one route lighter and now a Friday.** The bell went at the fourth hour and a half on the second, on the fourth, and on the twenty-fourth, and a boy of seventeen asked after him four times and said he will not waste an ask on a Friday, and a carpenter rang it every time. **A Monday is the day that end keeps, the twenty-fourth was a Friday, and nothing was struck four hundred miles off, and the two facts are in a day-book separately and are not joined in one sentence.** The road to that end is closed in four lines of writing, correct, and the woman who keeps that end has not been asked a question about it in about two years and has not asked one either. **The tile on the coping is not touched, not looked at, named twice as an object in Chapters 148 and 150 and never explained, its device is named by nobody, and nothing in this block looks at it.**
- **Aren and the Marches council: the second instrument, in a room that had already refused the first, and neither pretended the first had not happened.** The minute-man read the eighth rolling out of the book before the form was filled in and entered that this one was not asked for. **What he got is a fair copy in the county's own seven-column form, with no column for a vote because a vote is a thing a man carries into a room he is standing in; the road held by the guild and by a yard four hundred miles off; a witness at each end, one of them a woman on three hearths and one of them a cart of salt; and a line under the rule in the keeper's own hand that says the road is not to be asked for back by anybody and least of all by him.** He still holds no office, is not on the roll, is not on the oath, and **the county put two of his names on a board where anybody coming up two flights may read them and did it on purpose with its eyes open.** The cost of the whole block is one line under a rule and a fair copy on a board, and he cannot take either of them back.
- **Aren and Coll Ferrow: trust with no employment, and Ferrow has now said four true things about him in public.** He took back the word *dangerous* in a room rather than a gateway and said why. He costed the re-cutting in front of nineteen men and volunteered the second half of the column, which is the difference and what a thing costs to do twice. He put the yard's name in his own book and said the reason he wanted it was the day the four men go. **He did not ask Aren to go out onto the span and wrote in the guild's book in nine words that he did not ask and would have had no standing to stop him, and he said in a doorway that he would have had two men who know in a county if Aren had come to him with the finding, and that two men who know is a thing a person can be put in front of.** He objected to the two Kest names on one board and did not have either removed. **The guild remains better than the north at water and it is better at documents, and its first hand is the only person in that county who has ever said out loud that a thing with a number in its name cannot be sued by anybody for anything.**
- **Aren and the minute-man: the closest working relationship in the county, and it is still made of the book, and it has now produced two things that are in his own hand and not in the minute.** He asked the one question that decided the eighth rolling and did not ask it again in the thirteenth, and said why out loud in the room instead of in a book. **He entered the knowing at the head of the list, refused to give a statement to a seed room to a minute, wrote the last line of the hand-over in his own hand and said in the room that he wrote it, entered the eleventh call on a wet bank with no name in it and said he would not look for one, and entered that the not asking was not entered against Aren and was not entered at all.** He has entered a finding, a class of persons, a contradiction, a silence and now a company. **He has been a clerk for one afternoon in twenty-two years and did not like it, and he has wanted to put something in the roll's second column five times.**
- **Aren and Thessa Wend: the centre of the block, and still not asked and still asking, and now the fifth time.** She came up the bank with the number at the ninth hour and was not thanked. She came to the trough at the fourth hour, stood four lines back with a lamp, watched a quarter of an hour and said nothing, and **wrote the second line in eight thousand and thirty-one that is about a man and not about a post, and bookmarked it at the ninth line of the page, and showed it to nobody.** **He did not ask her for a line of the book. That was the fifth time in thirteen rollings that those two people had not asked each other the same thing, and neither of them wrote any of the five down, and it is still the only piece of work in this account that neither of them can produce.** She has wanted a reading by somebody who does not own the weir since the first year and has had two, and one of them lasted a night.
- **Aren and Delya Munn: right about the history, wrong about the soil, and now the person who filled the first column of the hand-over.** She said one sentence in a gateway on the last night of the year that she has said in no room in nine rollings, and it was that nine men were saying true words for the first time in a hundred and forty years and she stood in a field and did not tell them, and that a stranger stood on a lid for nine hours so that a boundary she has kept for nineteen years would still be a boundary in the morning, **and that there is nobody in that county who can tell him he did the second thing for the first one, and she is not going to be the person who tells him, and her grandmother's name is on a stone and it will still be on it in nine years whether she says anything or not.** He did not ask her the name. Nobody in that county knows it. **The second half about the soil is still unsaid by anybody in the volume.**
- **Aren and Sian Pugh, and the woman of sixty-six who keeps the seed room: her name is spent and unspent in the same breath.** She said all three parts of her want once, in her own words, in a stone room, and refused thanks in advance, and said the one sentence about a list, once. **She called his decision about the chamber the right decision in the wrong county, in the eighth rolling, and that is not repeated in this block.** The three marks on the outside of her wall are read by nobody, are in no book, and **she has decided nothing about them and has said she will decide in the spring or she will not, and the spring is now, and the volume is over.** He is the only person outside that room who knows what the three marks are for and she is the only person who has decided to keep them.
- **Aren and the woman of about sixty on three hearths: the chapter's best instrument, and it is hers.** She asked in the ninth rolling for an entry about a class of persons and not about herself. **In the thirteenth rolling that entry is the reason nobody asked him about his family, and he noticed and she noticed him noticing and neither of them said it.** She put the second mark on the list and asked for the asking to be entered after the mark and refused to say thank you for a seed question. **She said *I'll hold it* and held it, and on the last night of the year she was chosen as the witness at this end because a column not filled in is a hole in a document, and she said she would do it properly and in front of people and that there is not one book in that county that can produce her afterwards and that is why it is worth doing.** Four words in the thirteenth rolling, in the middle of the largest instrument the county has ever made. **She has never once been asked what she swore and she has now been a witness to two things.**
- **Aren and the yard's four men, and the man of fifty-two, and the man at the drawknife: the only relationship in the book getting better, and it is better by three things that cost something.** The crew is whole, is the best crew in the county, was put in the middle of the re-cutting, was paid for the last twenty-seven days in ninety-six bushels of salt at a figure that lands clean, and went home at the start of the thirteenth rolling with the rest of the stay at none in two books. **The yard is now named in a guild's book four hundred miles from the yard, and a man of fifty-two said out loud in a gateway that they are the best crew in the county and that he has said it in a gateway and not in a room, and nobody wrote it down.** The man of fifty-two's entry as not having sworn anything, with a date and no hour, stands and has now been paid in the plainest way available, which is a first hand putting the yard's name in his own book. **The man at the drawknife went into the Water after a boy of fifteen nine rollings ago, gave no name, was not asked, is not in any book in that county, said one true thing at a bank that only the man of fifty-two heard, and was nine days gone when a man came off a lid and had to be got up four feet of bank by a man at a sluice with a bar he was not told to use.**
- **Aren and the Marches' sixty: heard, asked, and helped, and not on the oath.** Twenty-nine of fifty-eight put a mark, one took a mark off, and the mark is the household's own. **Nobody asked them what they swore, and nobody in this block asked them that, and the list is keyed to the roll and the council entered the danger of that at the head of its own list and did it anyway.** The man of sixty-eight's four true words are still in nobody's book by Aren's own decision and were not said again.
- **Aren and Sabin Dain: refused in writing, in a document, and it is not an apology.** The refusal is four hundred and eleven words, dated, with the hour, sent in a bag, cut open along the left edge so that it can be seen to have been opened. **It refuses the rank because a command a person appointed by nobody can hold is the thing he will not take; it refuses the sealed record because a record a person cannot see is a record a person cannot contradict, and it says he has been worse at refusing than he is at building bridges; it makes the sheet's missing number of persons the load-bearing paragraph; it declines to have the warrant fetched out of a press; it refuses to trade on the eastern paths; and it says the earlier reply in a bag is not withdrawn, because a thing that is not withdrawn is a thing that can be produced.** It explains nothing about the marginal instruction, does not use the word custodian, and says there is no thanks in it. **Her want is unchanged and nothing in this block touches it, and he has not been in the same room as her and will not be, and her offer is now dead in a form and the form is in a mail sack behind a salt loft.**
- **Mara Vey, Sarn Oyelaran and Alish Renn: a body corporate that can be sued, and it has now put a company in a county that is not on any map into its own register and it knows exactly what that is.** **The first thing that has ever come into that office out of a place that had agreed to be checked and had not been asked to, addressed to a shelf, and the shelf is the room.** She struck a wrong figure with the reason in the margin, refused to do one single thing with a coincidence between two nine hundred and forty-ones, read a five-figure working line by line with a rule, and entered the county's own account in the same run of the page as the office's, with the line that has nothing in a money column among the lines that have figures. **She entered a hearing for the ninth of Rainmonth of the year following for a delegation that cannot arrive.** She put her pen down over the tenth not-asking. **Alish Renn entered that two women conceded nothing, and entered the half sentence Marda Oakes did not finish, and said the thing that has to be said before a document with an address on it is opened. Sarn Oyelaran asked the only question worth asking, which was who counted the stones.**
- **Lysa Fenn and Marda Oakes: in the same room twice, conceding nothing, and each of them frightened of the same instrument for opposite reasons.** Lysa says that a body corporate that enters a company becomes a party that can be sued and in four years a letter from a county of clay will make this office answerable for a county with no writ, no bell and no schedule. Marda wants the entry and opposes the letter, because a correspondence is a hand a county can be got at, and stopped in the middle of a sentence on purpose and said why. **Neither is a cynic and neither is a villain and a body corporate that can be sued is now about to be asked to produce a person.**

## A state-of-the-world ledger at the end of Chapter 150

**The Hearth Marches, at the end of a year and a rolling.** Thirteen rollings counted from the first sowing, four weeks to a rolling. **The year ended in the last week of the thirteenth rolling, and the first sowing of the next is within a week of it, and the record of a sowing in this county is a stone and the stone is a boundary.** A county with no office, no writ, no bell and no schedule, in eleven villages and a market town, on household fields and commons, that is not on any map in this kingdom. **Eleven villages and a market town, and about four hundred people in the county, and about sixty households of the four white villages on a roll with no oath mark, of which fifty-eight are certain and two are doubtful.** Four villages on white ground, the salt a foot and a half down, the water table a mile wide, and **the water table will come down over four years and not one year sooner.** Eleven places under the lowest field at Sallow Marsh and nine gone, and the newest white with no dark inch on the top of it, and the first crop off two of the four villages a failure on a board in a keeper's hand with the hour on it.

**The council.** Eleven people in a room over a tithe barn, a bench that argues about a boundary, a minute in a good hand that is the only book in the county that cannot be served, and a roll with a stitch in it that is the only book in the county a person can be put in front of. **It has refused outside command twice, taken the guild's terms, refused a command in nine seconds in its own form in the eighth rolling, taken a plan a tenant proposed in the third rolling, named a day in advance in the tenth, and given away a road in a form of seven columns in the last hour of the year.** Its standing rule has no office behind it. **It has now broken its own oath in public, by the men who swore it, on a day it named a rolling ahead, and entered the breaking as a thing that can happen again.** A fair copy of all of it is on a board under the bench where anybody coming up two flights may read it, **and it carries two names with one surname on it and the council proceeded with its eyes open and minuted the objection of the guild's first hand.** Its debts: 4,632 pence, ninety-six marks and two shillings, and no money, and a sixth line with nothing in a money column that is four men of a yard from a county four hundred miles off.

**The bridge guild.** Nineteen men and a first hand of sixty, a book kept nineteen years with nineteen words in it about four men and a street and a date and no hour, and a name that is not a man of this county's, and a yard's rule entered in a margin: the fact and not the paper, and neither book can be made to answer to the other. **It holds the causeway, the waterworks, the mile of boundary, the trough and the ditch at its head, and a footbridge of two bays and five yards at the trough with the near bay hanging four inches off the far one.** It opened four hundred and forty yards of ditch in three days in the fifth rolling and a mile of boundary in nine days in the twelfth, and it knows the water better than the north, and it has proved it four times on the page.

**The four men of a fen yard.** On the council's account at the guild's rate from the fourth week of the seventh rolling, the last twenty-seven days paid in ninety-six bushels of salt at a figure that lands clean, the rest at none in two books, gone home at the start of the thirteenth rolling with a handcart. **Their name and street are in the guild's book and the yard's rule holds the fact and not the paper, and one of them went into the Water after a boy of fifteen and is not named in any book in that county, and one of them said in a gateway that they are the best crew in the county and nobody wrote it down.** The guild's first hand says in nine years somebody will take the two books and put them side by side and say the county owes four men it cannot name, **and it can name them now, and it is a yard and a street and a rate and a date and a count, and it cannot pay them.**

**The seed room.** A stone room behind a chapel with a hard floor, a foot of dried mud in it in the eighth rolling, nine sealed jars of a wheat ungrown in that county for forty years on a shelf in the order they have always stood in, and a wall with two earlier answers built into it, and **three bits of tin on the outside of the wall above the flood line that nobody has read, in a measure the keeper keeps in her head because a measure in a book becomes an office.** A woman of sixty-six who has kept it forty years, was never once asked to keep it from anybody, wants the seed in nine jars and not in a wall, wants to be the last keeper of a seed room and not the first of a seed house, and does not want the company's thanks, **and has decided nothing about the three marks and has said she will decide in the spring or she will not, and the spring is now.**

**The companies.** A seed-and-bridge company of the Hearth Marches, **named the nine jars and the ditch**, entered on the twentieth of Hearthmonth, YR 313 by the Bellweather Claims Archive, a body corporate, with a date and a name and a witness at each end and neither witness producible to the other, its first act a letter of four lines to any county that has kept a seed of a thing it does not grow, **which went into a bag on the twenty-first and is not answered inside the year and is not going to be answered, because there is no longer anybody in that county holding the other end of anything.** A tenant league with a man of forty-nine on twelve short acres who wants a man to be able to say what he will not plant and not lose a field for it, and who proposed the only instrument that worked. About forty ditchmen whose wages come out of a rent roll that has been going down for nine years, who want the length of water they are responsible for written down on something they can be shown, **and who now have a mile of boundary that does not walk and no document of their own.** A council of eleven with no writ. A guild of nineteen with a book. **And nobody in that county knows it is a name in a column four hundred miles off, and it is the sixth of the eight, and a promise about a share of a watercourse kept for a hundred and forty years by living men does not sit still.**

**Merefen and the county, on the twenty-fourth of Hearthmonth, YR 313, a Friday, not a market day, the fourth hour.** The Sedge Reach: four timber bays, about forty men on it, stone in four carts, the nose of the third pier out of the water, pointing that is never finished on the day it is begun, **and eleven tons of stone for a fortnight in a month that is not the wrong one.** A road warden of the Merefen bridge guild on the deck of the third bay with a tool bag, counting load points into a book that does not exist, **with a permanent limp acquired on the far side at the fifth hour of the last night of a year in a county that is not on any map, and a stick cut out of a fence rail that is for four hundred yards of road and nothing else, and a guild's man with the levels who will not measure it and gave the reason in nine seconds.** The guild hall over the wharf: a bench of nine, a chest with two marks in it that has had two since the ninth of Fallowmonth, a subscription book in a market with four names in it and two of them the same man twice, a bench-clerk of nineteen years' standing in a copyist's trade who has been a clerk for one afternoon in three, a reeve who has been asked and has voted and uses a casting vote, and **a number that is four and a fifth asking in a coat on the twenty-fourth of Hearthmonth.** The Old Toll crossing: in municipal trust, in abeyance, tolls suspended, no owner, no money, no seat, no glass in the sill and no third seat, a dressed block and forty yards of tarred hemp in the abutment, and a bell with a band of forty-one rivets in it and a dull red seam, rung at the fourth hour and a half by a boy of seventeen and a carpenter, **and a Monday is the day the far end keeps and a Friday is not it, and nothing was struck four hundred miles off, and there is no second bell in this county for a man who has come back.**

**Weir Street.** A yard with the top hinge off and a churn propping it, a bench with the shave horse, oak shavings four inches deep, a crown's instrument on a shelf and a crown's mould of grey wax nailed over a doorway nobody can see from the road, four men and a boy of fifteen at a bench-board, **and a book with five lines in it in two months carrying an hour against each and no figure and no name, the last of them written in nine seconds with a wet nib by a man who does not know what he was holding.** A line's north end that is a yard's gate. The rooms over a cooper, four pence a year, with a man in them who has come up a line in a gateway in the rain and has a fever's third day behind him and a leg.

**The Bellweather Claims Archive, a body corporate with a door and a number since the ninth of Greenmonth, one room over a weigh-house at the north end with a door on a lane, four pence a year, two rooms' work in one.** A keeper of twenty-nine, four years and a half, exact about names and about what a record says against what happened. A second copyist, thirty, on a warrant of the keeper's own that is not in the press. A junior clerk, twenty-six, with a key and a warrant for a third post that is not signed. **Two unserved books in the press and a half tile in one of them that weighs a shade over ten ounces and is the account of nobody.** Nine claim numbers in four years and a half, of which one is a name out of a copy. A served schedule of the eighth of Fallowmonth with eight ruled lines and the eighth empty, looked at three times and not filled. **A whole leaf of returns with a printed three weeks at the head of it and ninety-six days of error under it, and a fair copy of a column of eleven counties, and now a fair copy of four hundred and eight words and a working from a county that is not on any map, addressed to a shelf, and an entry of a company of seed and bridge, and a resolution to hear a delegation on the ninth of Rainmonth of the year following at the second hour in that room, and a page with four lines on it written in Fallowmonth of the year before, of which the fourth is the one the whole of this case is standing on without anybody knowing.** Nine entries this year of a sentence about not asking, and no tenth, and a resolution entered for a date eighteen months away against a party that cannot arrive.

**The Crown's instruments in Merefen.** Four, all lawful or unanswerable: the instruction of the ninth of Longlight, in force and not superseded and barring the assembly of any body of this county in respect of an object nobody has been able to name; the third writ, discharged; the provincial ledger with *absent*; a warrant in a provincial file naming a woman without a name. **Two dated forms from the Office of Provincial Registration with a figure in each and no name in either, and a standing offer made out in the ninth month of the year three years last past and held open since, for a road warden's rank with a district, a clerk, a stipend of eleven marks a year and the right to send men, and a sealed personal record produced to any body that asks and never to its subject, for the eastern paths, and the number of persons it has been made out to is not stated on the sheet.** A reply in a bag from the office that neither accepts nor refuses and carries no returnable date. **A refusal in writing, four hundred and eleven words, dated, with the hour, in a mail sack behind a salt loft, which is not withdrawn, and which is the only answer that office has ever had to that sheet from anybody in this county.** No name in a Crown's hand in the Hearth Marches since the twenty-seventh of Fallowmonth, and there is still not one.

**The people who are held and not known.** A man of fifty-eight, four hundred miles off, alive, employed, correct in three books and in none of them able to be asked about, whose hearth has a fire in it on the nights the county can see it from a road. A woman of about sixty on three hearths in a county of clay, fourth column *wife*, who wrote four lines out of a minute and asked that somebody write down that she said so, and who is not the woman of sixty on three hearths in the Marches and has never been the same person to anybody. A man of sixty-four who has swept round a tile for forty-one years and wants it lifted and not thrown away, and a man of about forty-one who keeps a road that is a record and has never once been asked a question about it, and who is not going to be asked in this volume. A woman of about forty-one who keeps the far end of a line that has been closed in four lines of writing and does not know that a man four hundred miles off has read her fourth line in a room. **A tile-cutter of forty-one and the woman who keeps the road, who struck a coping at the fourth hour and a half on a Monday because that is the day the far end of the line keeps, and it is not a bell, and there is no bell in that county and there has never been one, and the clerk of that assizes copied out what a day in that county sounds like because he was asked to.** And a man of fifty-two in a yard four hundred miles off who held one end of a line for a hundred and seventeen days and does not know it and was not told and will not be told in this volume.

**The numbers, and what the office cannot do with them.** **One hundred and seventeen days of absence against a printed warning of three weeks in an office's hand that has not changed and that the office cannot say belongs to anybody; an error of ninety-six days; a ratio of about four fifths of the absence and not of the warning; a path interval of a week and a rolling that is nowhere in the sum; three days of fever, the second, the third and the fourth, breaking on the fifth; a permanent limp; two routes held, Bellweather-Living and the Hearth Marches, and the County of Ash closed in four lines of writing, itemised, checkable, sent by the woman who keeps that end at the request of a bench, correct, and not lost.** Forty-one market days lost inside the absence and **no character in this volume is told the figure.** The account of a county that is not on any map, footed to 4,632 pence, of which the county has none. A company entered with a witness at each end. **A letter to a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths, unanswered, in a bag, and the reason is a coping with a tile on it that nobody has looked at.**
